Module 1: Descriptive Statistics & Probability Foundations · 34 questions

Quartile convention used throughout this module (as in CS-1): the k-th quartile is at position \(k(n+1)/4\) in the sorted data, with linear interpolation between neighbours when the position is fractional. Sample variance uses divisor \(n - 1\); population variance uses \(N\).

3.A Descriptive Statistics (1.1)

L1 · FoundationP1.1

Classify the variable

For each variable state (i) qualitative or quantitative, (ii) if quantitative, discrete or continuous, and (iii) the level of measurement (nominal, ordinal, interval, ratio).

(a) Postal PIN code of a customer
(b) Hotel star rating (1★ to 5★)
(c) Temperature of a server room in kelvin
(d) Temperature of the same room in °C
(e) Calendar year of birth
(f) Number of failed login attempts in a day
(g) Page-load time in milliseconds
(h) T-shirt size S / M / L / XL

A number is not automatically quantitative. Ask: can you meaningfully subtract the values? Does zero mean "none of the quantity"?

Variable Type Level
(a) PIN code Qualitative (numbers are only labels) Nominal
(b) Star rating Qualitative, ordered Ordinal (the gap from 1★ to 2★ need not equal the gap from 4★ to 5★)
(c) Kelvin Quantitative, continuous Ratio (0 K is absolute zero, so 300 K really is twice 150 K)
(d) °C Quantitative, continuous Interval (0 °C is arbitrary, so ratios are meaningless)
(e) Year of birth Quantitative, discrete as recorded Interval (year 0 is a convention, so "2000 is twice 1000" is meaningless)
(f) Failed logins Quantitative, discrete Ratio
(g) Load time Quantitative, continuous Ratio
(h) T-shirt size Qualitative Ordinal

Key insight: The level of measurement is decided by which operations are meaningful (\(=\), \(<\), \(-\), \(\div\)), not by whether the value is written as a number. The same physical quantity (temperature) can be interval or ratio depending on the scale.

tests: 1.1.1 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP1.2

True or false, with a reason

(a) The sum of deviations from the median is always zero.
(b) A data set can have more than one mode.
(c) Appending one extremely large value to a data set always changes the median.
(d) A standard deviation can be negative when most values lie below the mean.
(e) Variance is measured in the same units as the data.
(f) \(\text{SD} = 0\) if and only if all observations are equal.
(g) The mode can be computed for nominal data, but the mean cannot.
(h) Computing the variance of a sample with divisor \(n\) (instead of \(n - 1\)) tends to underestimate the population variance.

For (c), try \(\{1, 2, 2, 3\}\) with 100 appended.

(a) False. The deviations sum to zero about the mean:

\[ \sum (x - \bar{x}) = \sum x - n\bar{x} = 0 \]

About the median they generally do not.
(b) True. Data can be bimodal or multimodal.
(c) False. \(\{1, 2, 2, 3\}\) has median 2, and \(\{1, 2, 2, 3, 100\}\) also has median 2. The median moves by at most one position, and its value may not change at all.
(d) False. SD is the square root of an average of squares, so \(\text{SD} \ge 0\).
(e) False. Variance is in squared units. The SD is in the original units.
(f) True. \(\sum (x - \bar{x})^2 = 0\) forces every \(x = \bar{x}\).
(g) True. Labels such as "O+" or "B−" can be counted but not added.
(h) True. Deviations measured from \(\bar{x}\) are on average smaller than deviations from \(\mu\). See P1.13.

Key insight: Each measure has an algebraic property (balance point, position, frequency) that decides what it is sensitive to. Learn that property instead of memorising a list.

tests: 1.1.2, 1.1.4 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP1.3

Pick the right centre

Choose the most appropriate measure of central tendency and justify it in one line.

(a) Blood groups of 500 donors.
(b) Responses on a 5-point Likert scale (Strongly disagree … Strongly agree).
(c) Annual household income in a city, where a few billionaires are present.
(d) Heights of adult men in a large, roughly bell-shaped sample.
(e) A network manager wants "the number of server failures per day that happens most often".

Check the level of measurement first, then the skewness.

(a) Mode. The data are nominal, so it is the only valid measure.
(b) Median (or mode). The data are ordinal, so order is meaningful but distances are not, and averaging codes 1 to 5 assumes equal gaps.
(c) Median. The data are heavily right-skewed, and the mean is pulled up by extreme values.
(d) Mean. The data are symmetric with no outliers. The mean uses all the data and has the smallest sampling variability.
(e) Mode. The question literally asks for the most frequent value.

Key insight: Choose the measure using two questions: (1) What does the scale allow? (2) Is the distribution skewed or does it have outliers?

tests: 1.1.2, 1.1.3 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP1.4

Full summary of a small data set

Times (minutes) taken by a data pipeline on 10 consecutive nights, rounded to the nearest minute:

12, 15, 11, 18, 15, 20, 14, 15, 17, 13

Find (a) mean, median, mode; (b) range, and range using real limits; (c) sample variance and SD; (d) population variance and SD if these 10 nights are the whole population of interest; (e) \(Q_1\), \(Q_3\), IQR, QD.

Sort first. With \(n = 10\), the quartile positions are 2.75 and 8.25. Data rounded to the nearest minute have real limits \(\pm 0.5\).

Sorted: 11, 12, 13, 14, 15, 15, 15, 17, 18, 20

(a) Mean \(= 150/10 = \mathbf{15}\); median \(= (15 + 15)/2 = \mathbf{15}\); mode = 15 (appears 3 times).
(b) Range \(= 20 - 11 = \mathbf{9}\). With real limits: \(20.5 - 10.5 = \mathbf{10}\).
(c) Sample variance and SD:

\[ SS = \sum (x - 15)^2 = \mathbf{68}, \qquad s^2 = \frac{68}{9} = \mathbf{7.556}, \qquad s = \mathbf{2.749} \]

(d) \(\sigma^2 = 68/10 = \mathbf{6.8}\); \(\sigma = \mathbf{2.608}\).
(e) Quartiles by interpolation:

\[ Q_1 \ (\text{position } 2.75) = 12 + 0.75(13 - 12) = \mathbf{12.75}, \qquad Q_3 \ (\text{position } 8.25) = 17 + 0.25(18 - 17) = \mathbf{17.25} \]

IQR = 4.5; QD = 2.25.

Key insight: Mean = median = mode here, which suggests a roughly symmetric distribution. The only difference between (c) and (d) is the divisor, and the next problems explain why that matters.

tests: 1.1.2, 1.1.4, 1.1.5 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP1.5

Grouped (frequency) data

Code review of 40 modules recorded the number of bugs per module:

Bugs \(x\) 0 1 2 3 4 5
Modules \(f\) 6 10 12 8 3 1

Find the mean, median, mode, sample variance and sample SD.

Mean \(= \sum fx / \sum f\). For the median, find the 20th and 21st values using cumulative frequencies.

\(\sum f = 40\), \(\sum fx = 0 + 10 + 24 + 24 + 12 + 5 = 75\), so mean = 1.875.
Cumulative frequencies are 6, 16, 28, … The 20th and 21st values both fall in \(x = 2\), so the median = 2.
Mode = 2 (\(f = 12\)).
Sample variance and SD:

\[ \sum f(x - 1.875)^2 = 62.375, \qquad s^2 = \frac{62.375}{39} = \mathbf{1.599}, \qquad s = \mathbf{1.265} \]

(The population version is \(62.375/40 = 1.559\).)

Key insight: A frequency table is a compressed list. Every formula works with each \(x\) weighted by its \(f\). Here mean < median = mode, which indicates a slight right tail (the rare modules with 4 or 5 bugs).

tests: 1.1.2, 1.1.4 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP1.6

Quartiles, fences and outliers

Response sizes (KB) of 11 API calls: 21, 25, 27, 28, 30, 31, 33, 34, 36, 38, 62.

(a) Give the five-number summary.
(b) Compute IQR and QD.
(c) Compute the inner fences (\(1.5 \cdot \text{IQR}\)) and outer fences (\(3 \cdot \text{IQR}\)).
(d) Classify 62 as not an outlier, a potential (mild) outlier, or an extreme outlier.

\(n + 1 = 12\), so the quartiles sit exactly at positions 3, 6 and 9.

(a) Min 21, \(Q_1\) 27, median 31, \(Q_3\) 36, max 62.
(b) IQR = 9, QD = 4.5.
(c) Fences:

\[ \text{Inner: } 27 - 13.5 = \mathbf{13.5} \text{ and } 36 + 13.5 = \mathbf{49.5}, \qquad \text{Outer: } 27 - 27 = \mathbf{0} \text{ and } 36 + 27 = \mathbf{63} \]

(d) \(62 > 49.5\) but \(62 < 63\), so it is a potential (mild) outlier, not an extreme one. In the box plot, the upper whisker stops at 38 and 62 is plotted as a separate point.

Key insight: Fences are built from quartiles, which an outlier cannot move much. An outlier test based on \(\text{mean} \pm 2 \cdot \text{SD}\) is weaker because the outlier inflates the SD it is being judged by.

tests: 1.1.5 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP1.7

Reading skew from summary measures

(a) For a moderately skewed distribution, mean = 52 and median = 48. Estimate the mode and state the direction of skew.
(b) Another distribution has mode = 75 and median = 70. Estimate the mean and state the direction of skew.
(c) A distribution has mean = median = mode = 100. What can and cannot be concluded?

\(\text{Mode} \approx 3 \cdot \text{Median} - 2 \cdot \text{Mean}\).

(a) Applying the rule:

\[ \text{Mode} \approx 3(48) - 2(52) = \mathbf{40} \]

Mean > median > mode, so the distribution is positively (right) skewed.
(b) Solving for the mean:

\[ 75 = 3(70) - 2 \cdot \text{Mean} \;\Rightarrow\; \text{Mean} \approx \mathbf{67.5} \]

Mean < median < mode, so the distribution is negatively (left) skewed.
(c) The distribution is consistent with symmetry, but this does not prove it is normal, or even symmetric in every respect (the tails could differ). It says nothing about spread.

Key insight: The mean is pulled toward the long tail and the mode sits at the peak, so their order shows the direction of skew. The 3-median rule is empirical and only approximate.

tests: 1.1.3 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP1.8

Two branches, two box plots

A bank measured lunchtime waiting times (minutes) for 15 customers at each of two branches.

Branch A: 3.1, 4.2, 2.8, 5.0, 4.6, 3.9, 6.2, 4.1, 3.5, 4.8, 0.9, 5.4, 4.4, 3.7, 5.9
Branch B: 7.2, 5.1, 8.4, 6.0, 9.3, 4.8, 7.7, 6.6, 10.8, 7.0, 5.6, 6.3, 8.9, 12.9, 6.8

(a) Give the five-number summary for each branch.
(b) Identify any outliers using the \(1.5 \cdot \text{IQR}\) rule.
(c) Describe the shape of each distribution using the box plot geometry and mean vs median.
(d) Write a three-sentence comparison for the bank manager.

With \(n = 15\), the quartile positions are 4, 8 and 12. Compare the distances \((Q_2 - Q_1)\) and \((Q_3 - Q_2)\).

(a) A: 0.9, 3.5, 4.2, 5.0, 6.2 (mean 4.167). B: 4.8, 6.0, 7.0, 8.9, 12.9 (mean 7.56).
(b) A: IQR = 1.5, fences 1.25 and 7.25, so 0.9 is a (low) outlier. B: IQR = 2.9, fences 1.65 and 13.25, so there are no outliers (12.9 is close to the fence).
(c) A: \(Q_2 - Q_1 = 0.7\) and \(Q_3 - Q_2 = 0.8\), so the box is nearly symmetric, with one unusually fast customer. Mean (4.17) \(\approx\) median (4.2). B: \(Q_2 - Q_1 = 1.0\) and \(Q_3 - Q_2 = 1.9\), with a long upper whisker and mean \(7.56 >\) median 7.0, so B is right-skewed.
(d) Customers at Branch A wait about 3 minutes less on a typical day (median 4.2 vs 7.0). Branch B also has much more spread (IQR 2.9 vs 1.5, SD 2.20 vs 1.32) and occasional very long waits. B's problem is both a higher typical wait and less predictability.

Key insight: Comparing two groups needs location, spread and shape together. Box plots show all three at once.

tests: 1.1.5, 1.1.3, 1.1.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP1.9

How much does one outlier matter?

Delivery times (days) for 10 orders: 4, 5, 5, 6, 6, 7, 7, 8, 9, 31.

(a) Compute the mean, median, sample SD and IQR.
(b) Use the fences to decide whether 31 is an outlier.
(c) Recompute all four statistics without 31 and give the percentage change of each.
(d) Which two statistics would you report to customers, and why?

With \(n = 10\), the quartile positions are 2.75 and 8.25. With \(n = 9\), they are 2.5 and 7.5.

(a) Mean = 8.8, median = 6.5, \(s = \mathbf{7.94}\), \(Q_1 = 5\), \(Q_3 = 8.25\), IQR = 3.25.
(b) Upper fence:

\[ 8.25 + 1.5(3.25) = \mathbf{13.125} \]

so 31 is an outlier (it is beyond even the outer fence of 18).
(c) Without 31: mean 6.33 (−28%), median 6.0 (−8%), \(s\) 1.58 (−80%), IQR 2.5 (\(Q_1\) 5, \(Q_3\) 7.5; −23%).
(d) Report the median and IQR. These describe the typical customer experience and are barely affected by one exceptional order. Investigate the 31-day order separately, since it may be a lost parcel or a data-entry error.

Key insight: SD squares the deviations, so it is the most fragile of these statistics. One point out of 10 inflated it fivefold. Robust statistics (median, IQR) exist for exactly this situation.

tests: 1.1.2, 1.1.4, 1.1.5 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP1.10

Combining two groups

Section 1 (\(n_1 = 30\)) scored mean 62 with sample SD 8. Section 2 (\(n_2 = 20\)) scored mean 70 with sample SD 10. Find the mean and the sample SD of all 50 students combined. Explain why the combined SD is not simply the average of 8 and 10.

Total SS = (within-group SS) + (between-group SS), where \(SS_i(\text{within}) = (n_i - 1)s_i^2\) and \(\text{between} = \sum n_i(\bar{x}_i - \bar{x})^2\).

Combined mean:

\[ \bar{x} = \frac{30 \cdot 62 + 20 \cdot 70}{50} = \mathbf{65.2} \]

Within SS \(= 29(64) + 19(100) = 1856 + 1900 = 3756\).
Between SS:

\[ 30(62 - 65.2)^2 + 20(70 - 65.2)^2 = 307.2 + 460.8 = 768 \]

Total SS = 4524, so:

\[ s^2 = \frac{4524}{49} = \mathbf{92.33}, \qquad s = \mathbf{9.61} \]

The combined SD (9.61) is larger than a simple average of 8 and 10 because the two group means differ. Spread between the groups adds to the spread within them.

Key insight: Total variation = within-group + between-group. This is the identity on which ANOVA (Module 4) is built.

tests: 1.1.2, 1.1.4 · L3 Application
L2 · ComputationP1.11

Shortcuts and learning checks

(a) A sample of 6 scores has \(\sum x = 48\) and \(\sum x^2 = 420\). Find SS, \(s^2\), \(s\) and the population variance \(\sigma^2\) (treating the 6 as a population).
(b) A sample of 5 scores has \(SS = 40\). What is the sample variance?
(c) Heights are recorded to the nearest cm. The shortest is 152 cm and the tallest is 181 cm. Give the range using real limits.

\(SS = \sum x^2 - (\sum x)^2/n\).

(a) Using the computational form:

\[ SS = 420 - \frac{48^2}{6} = 420 - 384 = \mathbf{36}; \quad s^2 = \frac{36}{5} = \mathbf{7.2}; \quad s = \mathbf{2.683}; \quad \sigma^2 = \frac{36}{6} = \mathbf{6} \]

(b) \(s^2 = 40/4 = \mathbf{10}\).
(c) \(181.5 - 151.5 = \mathbf{30}\) cm.

Key insight: Calculators and code use the computational form \(\sum x^2 - (\sum x)^2/n\). P1.14 shows it is algebraically identical to \(\sum (x - \bar{x})^2\).

tests: 1.1.4 · L2 Computation
L4 · AnalysisP1.12

The mean as a balance point and best guess

(a) Prove that \(\sum (x_i - \bar{x}) = 0\) for any data set.
(b) Prove that the function \(g(c) = \sum (x_i - c)^2\) is minimised at \(c = \bar{x}\), and that its minimum value is SS.
(c) Show that \(g(c) = SS + n(\bar{x} - c)^2\). What does this say about the "cost" of using a wrong centre?
(d) (Discussion) Which value of \(c\) minimises \(\sum \lvert x_i - c \rvert\)? Why does this explain the median's robustness?

For (b), differentiate with respect to \(c\). For (c), write \(x_i - c = (x_i - \bar{x}) + (\bar{x} - c)\) and expand.

(a) Summing the deviations:

\[ \sum (x_i - \bar{x}) = \sum x_i - n\bar{x} = n\bar{x} - n\bar{x} = \mathbf{0} \]

(b) Setting the derivative to zero:

\[ g'(c) = -2\sum (x_i - c) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; \sum x_i = nc \;\Rightarrow\; \mathbf{c = \bar{x}} \]

\(g''(c) = 2n > 0\), so this is a minimum, and \(g(\bar{x}) = \sum (x_i - \bar{x})^2 = SS\).
(c) Expanding the square:

\[ \sum \left[(x_i - \bar{x}) + (\bar{x} - c)\right]^2 = \sum (x_i - \bar{x})^2 + 2(\bar{x} - c)\sum (x_i - \bar{x}) + n(\bar{x} - c)^2 \]

The middle term is 0 by (a), so \(g(c) = SS + n(\bar{x} - c)^2\). The penalty for a wrong centre grows quadratically with its distance from \(\bar{x}\).
(d) The median minimises the sum of absolute deviations. The absolute loss grows only linearly, so a far-away point pulls the minimiser by one rank position rather than by its full distance.

Key insight: The mean is the least-squares estimate of the centre. The same "minimise squared error" idea becomes least-squares regression (Module 5) and MSE in forecasting (Module 6).

tests: 1.1.2, 1.1.4 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP1.13

Why divide by n − 1?

The population is \(\{2, 4, 6\}\), with \(N = 3\).

(a) Compute the population variance \(\sigma^2\).
(b) List all 9 ordered samples of size \(n = 2\) drawn with replacement. For each, compute \(SS/n\) and \(SS/(n - 1)\).
(c) Average each estimator over the 9 samples. Which one is unbiased?
(d) Algebraic proof: using \(\sum (x_i - \bar{x})^2 = \sum (x_i - \mu)^2 - n(\bar{x} - \mu)^2\), \(E[(x_i - \mu)^2] = \sigma^2\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(\bar{x}) = \sigma^2/n\), show that \(E[SS] = (n - 1)\sigma^2\).

For a sample of 2, \(SS = (a - b)^2/2\).

(a) \(\mu = 4\), so:

\[ \sigma^2 = \frac{4 + 0 + 4}{3} = \mathbf{8/3 \approx 2.667} \]

(b) The pairs with equal values have \(SS = 0\). The pairs (2,4), (4,2), (4,6), (6,4) have \(SS = 2\). The pairs (2,6), (6,2) have \(SS = 8\).
(c) Average of \(SS/2\) = 1.333 (biased low, exactly half of \(\sigma^2\)). Average of \(SS/1\) = \(2.667 = \sigma^2\), so the \(n - 1\) version is unbiased.
(d) Taking expectations:

\[ E[SS] = n\sigma^2 - n \cdot \frac{\sigma^2}{n} = \mathbf{(n - 1)\sigma^2} \]

so \(E[SS/(n - 1)] = \sigma^2\).

Key insight: \(\bar{x}\) is computed from the same sample, so it sits closer to the data than \(\mu\) does. Dividing by \(n - 1\) (the degrees of freedom) corrects this optimism. Degrees of freedom appear again in \(t\), \(\chi^2\) and ANOVA.

tests: 1.1.4 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP1.14

Deriving the computational formula

(a) Prove that \(\sum (x_i - \bar{x})^2 = \sum x_i^2 - (\sum x_i)^2/n\).
(b) Verify it on the P1.4 data (\(\sum x = 150\), \(\sum x^2 = 2318\)).
(c) Explain why the computational form can lose accuracy on a computer when the values are like 1,000,000.1, 1,000,000.2, … and what practitioners do instead.

Expand \((x_i - \bar{x})^2\) and use \(\sum x_i = n\bar{x}\).

(a) Expanding:

\[ \sum (x_i^2 - 2x_i\bar{x} + \bar{x}^2) = \sum x_i^2 - 2\bar{x} \cdot n\bar{x} + n\bar{x}^2 = \sum x_i^2 - n\bar{x}^2 = \mathbf{\sum x_i^2 - (\sum x_i)^2/n} \]

(b) Substituting:

\[ 2318 - \frac{150^2}{10} = 2318 - 2250 = \mathbf{68} \]

which matches P1.4.
(c) Both \(\sum x^2\) and \((\sum x)^2/n\) are huge, nearly equal numbers, and subtracting them loses most of the significant digits (catastrophic cancellation). Practitioners shift the data first (subtract any value near the mean; variance is shift-invariant) or use Welford's one-pass algorithm.

Key insight: Two formulas can be equal in exact arithmetic and still behave differently in floating point. Variance being unchanged by a shift is what makes the fix legitimate.

tests: 1.1.4 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP1.15

What transformations do to summaries

Daily maximum temperatures (°C) over one week: 18, 22, 25, 21, 24, 30, 19.

(a) Compute the mean, median, sample SD and IQR in °C.
(b) Convert to °F (\(F = 1.8C + 32\)). Predict the four statistics in °F without recomputing from the raw data, then check.
(c) A faulty sensor adds an eighth reading of 45 °C. Recompute the four statistics in °C. Which changed most? Is 45 an outlier by the \(1.5 \cdot \text{IQR}\) rule?
(d) State the general rule: for \(y = ax + b\), how do the mean, median, SD, variance and IQR transform?

Adding a constant shifts location but not spread. Multiplying scales both.

(a) Mean 22.71, median 22, \(s\) 4.071, IQR 6 (\(Q_1 = 19\), \(Q_3 = 25\) at positions 2 and 6).
(b) Mean \(1.8(22.71) + 32 = \mathbf{72.89}\); median 71.6; \(s\) \(1.8(4.071) = \mathbf{7.327}\); IQR \(1.8(6) = \mathbf{10.8}\). Direct computation agrees.
(c) With 45: mean 25.5 (+2.79), median 23 (+1), \(s\) 8.73 (more than double), IQR 9.25 (\(Q_1\) 19.5, \(Q_3\) 28.75). The upper fence is

\[ 28.75 + 1.5(9.25) = 42.625 < 45 \]

so 45 is an outlier. The SD changed most.
(d) For \(y = ax + b\): mean and median become \(a \cdot (\text{stat}) + b\); SD and IQR become \(\lvert a \rvert \cdot (\text{stat})\), with no shift; variance becomes \(a^2 \cdot (\text{variance})\).

Key insight: Unit conversion is harmless and predictable, but a single bad reading is not. When a sensor or data join fails, robust statistics give an early warning: the median moved by 1 while the SD doubled.

tests: 1.1.2, 1.1.4, 1.1.5 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP1.16

Reading a describe() table like a data scientist

df.describe() on a customer data set produced:

income (₹ '000/yr) age (yrs)
count 5000 5000
mean 68 38
std 95 12
min 0 −3
25% 22 29
50% 41 37
75% 78 46
max 2400 142

(a) What is the direction of skew of income? Give two pieces of evidence from the table.
(b) Compute the \(1.5 \cdot \text{IQR}\) and \(3 \cdot \text{IQR}\) upper fences for income. Is the maximum a mild or an extreme outlier?
(c) A junior analyst writes "most incomes lie within mean ± 1 SD". Criticise this.
(d) Which values in the age column are impossible rather than merely unusual? How should impossible values be treated differently from genuine outliers?
(e) Which centre and spread would you put in an executive summary for income? Suggest a transformation for modelling.

Compare the mean with the median, and the gaps \(Q_2 - Q_1\) and \(Q_3 - Q_2\). Compute \(\text{mean} - \text{SD}\).

(a) Strongly right-skewed. Mean (68) > median (41). \(Q_3 - Q_2 = 37 > Q_2 - Q_1 = 19\). The max (2400) is about 25 SDs above the mean.
(b) IQR = 56. The fences:

\[ 1.5 \cdot \text{IQR}: \ 78 + 84 = \mathbf{162}, \qquad 3 \cdot \text{IQR}: \ 78 + 168 = \mathbf{246} \]

2400 is an extreme outlier.
(c) \(\text{Mean} - \text{SD} = 68 - 95 = \mathbf{-27}\), which is a negative income. SD-based ranges assume rough symmetry. For skewed, non-negative data they are meaningless. Use quantiles instead (the middle 50% of incomes lie between 22 and 78).
(d) Age −3 and 142 are impossible. These are data-quality errors and should be set to missing or corrected from the source, not kept as outliers. Genuine outliers (for example a real income of 2400) are valid data and should be kept, with a robust method or a separate investigation. The age fences (3.5 and 71.5) would also flag legitimate 75-year-olds, so fences flag points for review and do not decide what gets deleted. Income min = 0 needs a check too (is it unemployed, or missing coded as 0?).
(e) Report the median (₹41k) and IQR (₹56k). For modelling, use \(\log(\text{income} + 1)\), which compresses the right tail.

Key insight: The first job in EDA is reading a summary table for shape and data quality. Skewness, impossible values and outliers each need a different response.

tests: 1.1.3, 1.1.4, 1.1.5, 1.1.2 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP1.17

Three traps

(a) Class X scored 50, 50, 50, 50, 50. Class Y scored 10, 30, 50, 70, 90. A report says "the classes performed identically (same mean, median)". Quantify what the report misses.
(b) "Yesterday was 10 °C, today is 20 °C, so today is twice as hot." Evaluate this using kelvin.
(c) Commute times of 20 employees: ten take about 10 minutes (they live nearby) and ten take about 50 minutes (they live in the suburbs). The HR report says "the typical commute is 30 minutes". What is wrong, and what should be reported instead?

(a) needs a spread measure. (b) needs a scale with a true zero. (c) needs the shape.

(a) SD of X = 0. SD of Y:

\[ s_Y = \sqrt{\frac{4000}{4}} = \mathbf{31.62} \]

Y's results range from failing to excellent, so the central tendency is the same but the classes are very different.
(b) \(283.15\ \text{K} \to 293.15\ \text{K}\) is a ratio of 1.035, only 3.5% more thermal energy. °C is an interval scale, so ratios are meaningless.
(c) The distribution is bimodal. Almost no one commutes 30 minutes, and the mean describes a non-existent person. Report the two modes or clusters (about 10 and about 50 minutes) or show a histogram.

Key insight: A single number never describes a distribution. Before trusting a summary, check the spread, check the scale, and check how many modes there are.

tests: 1.1.1, 1.1.2, 1.1.4 · L5 Mastery

3.B Probability Foundations (1.2)

L1 · FoundationP1.18

Sample space and event algebra

A fair coin is tossed three times. Let \(A\) = "at least two heads" and \(B\) = "first toss is a tail".

(a) Write \(S\).
(b) List \(A\), \(B\), \(A \cap B\), \(A \cup B\), \(A^c\).
(c) Verify De Morgan's law \((A \cup B)^c = A^c \cap B^c\) by listing both sides.
(d) Compute \(P(A)\), \(P(B)\) and \(P(A \cap B)\). Are \(A\) and \(B\) independent?

\(\lvert S \rvert = 2^3 = 8\), all equally likely.

(a) \(S = \{\text{HHH, HHT, HTH, HTT, THH, THT, TTH, TTT}\}\).
(b) \(A = \{\text{HHH, HHT, HTH, THH}\}\); \(B = \{\text{THH, THT, TTH, TTT}\}\); \(A \cap B = \{\text{THH}\}\); \(A \cup B\) = all outcomes except HTT (7 outcomes); \(A^c = \{\text{HTT, THT, TTH, TTT}\}\).
(c) \((A \cup B)^c = \{\text{HTT}\}\). \(A^c \cap B^c = A^c \cap \{\text{HHH, HHT, HTH, HTT}\} = \{\text{HTT}\}\). ✓
(d) The probabilities:

\[ P(A) = \mathbf{1/2}, \quad P(B) = \mathbf{1/2}, \quad P(A \cap B) = \mathbf{1/8} \ne 1/4 \]

so \(A\) and \(B\) are dependent. A first tail makes "at least two heads" harder to achieve.

Key insight: Events are sets, and probability is computed on those sets. Writing out \(S\) for small experiments builds the intuition used later for large ones.

tests: 1.2.1, 1.2.2 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP1.19

True or false: probability vocabulary

(a) If two events are mutually exclusive and each has positive probability, they are independent.
(b) Independent events can occur together.
(c) \(P(A \cup B) \le P(A) + P(B)\) for any events.
(d) If \(A \subset B\) then \(P(A) \le P(B)\).
(e) The complement of "at least one head in 3 tosses" is "at most one head".
(f) \(A\) and \(A^c\) are mutually exclusive and exhaustive.
(g) If \(P(A) = 0\), then \(A\) can never happen.

For (g), think ahead to a continuous random variable such as an exact height of 170.000… cm.

(a) False. \(P(A \cap B) = 0 \ne P(A)P(B) > 0\), so they are dependent. Learning that \(A\) happened tells you \(B\) did not.
(b) True. Independence concerns information, not overlap. Two coin tosses can both be heads.
(c) True. By the addition rule:

\[ P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cap B), \qquad P(A \cap B) \ge 0 \]

(d) True. \(B = A \cup (B \setminus A)\), and the axioms give \(P(B) = P(A) + P(B \setminus A)\).
(e) False. The complement is "no heads".
(f) True.
(g) False in general. In a continuous model, every single exact value has probability 0 but some value does occur. (For finite, equally likely sample spaces it is true.)

Key insight: "Mutually exclusive" is a statement about sets (no overlap). "Independent" is a statement about probabilities (multiplication). The two are different questions.

tests: 1.2.3, 1.2.4, 1.2.6 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP1.20

Which assignments are legal?

An experiment has four mutually exclusive, exhaustive outcomes \(A\), \(B\), \(C\), \(D\). Which assignments are permissible?

(a) 0.25, 0.30, 0.15, 0.30
(b) 0.4, 0.3, 0.2, 0.2
(c) 0.5, 0.6, −0.1, 0
(d) \(1/3\), \(1/4\), \(1/6\), \(1/4\)
(e) \(3/8\), \(1/4\), \(1/8\), \(1/8\)

Check two things: every value \(\ge 0\), and the total \(= 1\).

(a) Permissible (sum 1).
(b) Not (sum 1.1).
(c) Not (a negative value, even though the sum is 1).
(d) Permissible:

\[ \frac{4}{12} + \frac{3}{12} + \frac{2}{12} + \frac{3}{12} = 1 \]

(e) Not (sum \(7/8\)).

Key insight: The axioms are the complete test of validity. The same check is applied to PMFs in Module 3.

tests: 1.2.3 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP1.21

Two dice

Two fair dice are thrown. Find the probability that the sum is

(a) a prime number;
(b) at least 10;
(c) neither 7 nor a double.

Count favourable outcomes out of 36. In (c), check whether "sum 7" and "double" can overlap.

(a) Sums 2, 3, 5, 7, 11 have \(1 + 2 + 4 + 6 + 2 = 15\) outcomes, so \(15/36 = 5/12\).
(b) Sums 10, 11, 12 have \(3 + 2 + 1 = 6\) outcomes, so \(6/36 = 1/6\).
(c) A sum of 7 (6 outcomes) and a double (6 outcomes) cannot occur together, because a double has an even sum. So:

\[ P = 1 - \frac{12}{36} = \mathbf{2/3} \]

Key insight: "Neither \(A\) nor \(B\)" \(= 1 - P(A \cup B)\). When \(A\) and \(B\) are mutually exclusive, the overlap term vanishes.

tests: 1.2.3, 1.2.4 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP1.22

Addition rule, every variant

On an e-commerce site, \(P(\text{user views a recommendation}, A) = 0.55\), \(P(\text{user uses search}, B) = 0.35\), and \(P(A \text{ or } B) = 0.70\). Find \(P(\text{both})\), \(P(\text{exactly one})\), \(P(\text{neither})\) and \(P(A \text{ only})\).

Draw a Venn diagram and fill in the intersection first.

\[ P(A \cap B) = 0.55 + 0.35 - 0.70 = \mathbf{0.20} \]

\(P(\text{exactly one}) = P(A \cup B) - P(A \cap B) = \mathbf{0.50}\).
\(P(\text{neither}) = 1 - 0.70 = \mathbf{0.30}\).
\(P(A \text{ only}) = 0.55 - 0.20 = \mathbf{0.35}\).

Key insight: Once the intersection is known, every region of a two-event Venn diagram follows.

tests: 1.2.4 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP1.23

Counting-based probabilities

(a) A committee of 6 is chosen at random from 7 men and 5 women. Find \(P(\text{at least 3 women})\).
(b) A lot of 15 chips has 4 defective. Three chips are chosen at random without replacement. Find \(P(\text{none defective})\), \(P(\text{at least one defective})\) and \(P(\text{exactly one defective})\).

The total number of committees is \(\binom{12}{6}\). Split "at least 3" into the cases 3, 4 and 5.

(a) Summing the cases:

\[ \frac{\binom{5}{3}\binom{7}{3} + \binom{5}{4}\binom{7}{2} + \binom{5}{5}\binom{7}{1}}{\binom{12}{6}} = \frac{350 + 105 + 7}{924} = \frac{462}{924} = \mathbf{0.5} \]

(b) \(\binom{15}{3} = 455\). Then:

\[ P(\text{none}) = \frac{\binom{11}{3}}{455} = \frac{165}{455} = \mathbf{0.3626} \]

\(P(\text{at least one}) = \mathbf{0.6374}\).

\[ P(\text{exactly one}) = \frac{\binom{4}{1}\binom{11}{2}}{455} = \frac{220}{455} = \mathbf{0.4835} \]

Key insight: For "at least one", the complement is almost always the shortest route. These counts are the hypergeometric model, which is the without-replacement relative of the Binomial (Module 3).

tests: 1.2.5 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP1.24

Joint probability table

Subscribers of a streaming service are cross-classified by plan and main device:

Mobile Laptop TV
Basic 0.12 0.08 0.05
Standard 0.15 0.15 0.10
Premium 0.08 0.12 0.15

(a) Find all marginal probabilities.
(b) \(P(\text{Premium and TV})\); \(P(\text{Premium and not TV})\).
(c) \(P(\text{Premium or TV})\).
(d) \(P(\text{neither Basic nor Mobile})\).
(e) \(P(\text{exactly one of Basic, Mobile})\).
(f) Are "Premium" and "TV" independent? Interpret this for the marketing team.

Row and column sums give the marginals. Independence requires the cell value = row marginal \(\times\) column marginal.

(a) Basic 0.25, Standard 0.40, Premium 0.35; Mobile 0.35, Laptop 0.35, TV 0.30.
(b) 0.15; \(0.35 - 0.15 = \mathbf{0.20}\).
(c) \(0.35 + 0.30 - 0.15 = \mathbf{0.50}\).
(d) \(1 - (0.25 + 0.35 - 0.12) = \mathbf{0.52}\).
(e) \(0.25 + 0.35 - 2(0.12) = \mathbf{0.36}\).
(f) Testing independence:

\[ P(\text{Premium}) \, P(\text{TV}) = 0.35 \times 0.30 = 0.105 \ne 0.15 \]

so they are dependent. Premium and TV occur together more often than chance would predict, so TV viewers are a natural target for Premium upgrades.

Key insight: A two-way table contains every joint, marginal and (in Module 2) conditional probability. Independence can be read cell by cell by comparing each cell with the product of its margins.

tests: 1.2.7, 1.2.4, 1.2.6 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP1.25

Mutually exclusive vs independent: find p

\(P(A) = 0.3\), \(P(A \cup B) = 0.65\) and \(P(B) = p\).

(a) For what \(p\) are \(A\) and \(B\) mutually exclusive?
(b) For what \(p\) are they independent?
(c) Can any \(p\) make them both mutually exclusive and independent? Explain.

ME gives \(P(A \cap B) = 0\). Independence gives \(P(A \cap B) = 0.3p\).

(a) \(0.65 = 0.3 + p\), so \(p = 0.35\).
(b) Under independence:

\[ 0.65 = 0.3 + p - 0.3p \;\Rightarrow\; 0.7p = 0.35 \;\Rightarrow\; \mathbf{p = 0.5} \]

(c) Both conditions together require \(0.3p = 0\), so \(p = 0\). But then \(P(A \cup B) = 0.3 \ne 0.65\). No.

Key insight: The same union probability gives different answers under the two assumptions, which shows numerically that they are different concepts.

tests: 1.2.6, 1.2.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP1.26

Reliability with independent components

(a) A primary drive fails with probability 0.02, and each of two backup drives fails with probability 0.05. All failures are independent. Data are lost only if all three fail. Find \(P(\text{data survive})\).
(b) A service needs two microservices in series (both must work), each 95% available. This pair runs in parallel with a fallback monolith that is 90% available. Find the system availability.

Series: multiply the availabilities. Parallel: the system fails only if every branch fails.

(a) Multiplying the independent failures:

\[ P(\text{loss}) = 0.02 \times 0.05 \times 0.05 = 0.00005, \qquad P(\text{survive}) = \mathbf{0.99995} \]

(b) Series pair availability \(= 0.95^2 = 0.9025\). Then:

\[ \text{System} = 1 - (1 - 0.9025)(1 - 0.90) = 1 - 0.0975 \times 0.1 = \mathbf{0.99025} \]

Key insight: For "at least one works", compute the complement "all fail". Independence turns that complement into a product. Correlated failures, such as a shared power supply, would break this calculation.

tests: 1.2.6, 1.2.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP1.27

Contradicting witnesses

Two independent annotators label the same image. A is correct 75% of the time and B is correct 90% of the time. For a binary label, what is the probability that they disagree?

Disagreement = (A right and B wrong) or (A wrong and B right). These two cases are mutually exclusive.

\[ P = 0.75(0.10) + 0.25(0.90) = 0.075 + 0.225 = \mathbf{0.30} \]

Key insight: The answer combines the two rules: independence lets you multiply within each case, and mutual exclusivity lets you add across cases. Inter-annotator agreement in ML labelling uses exactly this calculation.

tests: 1.2.4, 1.2.6 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP1.28

Proofs about independence

(a) Prove that if \(A\) and \(B\) are mutually exclusive with \(P(A) > 0\) and \(P(B) > 0\), they cannot be independent.
(b) Prove that if \(A\) and \(B\) are independent, then \(A\) and \(B^c\) are independent, and \(A^c\) and \(B^c\) are independent.
(c) Using only the three axioms, prove \(P(A^c) = 1 - P(A)\) and \(P(\varnothing) = 0\).

(b): \(A = (A \cap B) \cup (A \cap B^c)\) is a disjoint union. (c): \(S = A \cup A^c\).

(a) ME gives \(P(A \cap B) = 0\), but \(P(A)P(B) > 0\), so \(P(A \cap B) \ne P(A)P(B)\). Dependent.
(b) For \(A\) and \(B^c\):

\[ P(A \cap B^c) = P(A) - P(A \cap B) = P(A) - P(A)P(B) = P(A)(1 - P(B)) = P(A)P(B^c) \ \checkmark \]

Also:

\[ P(A^c \cap B^c) = 1 - P(A \cup B) = 1 - P(A) - P(B) + P(A)P(B) = (1 - P(A))(1 - P(B)) = P(A^c)P(B^c) \ \checkmark \]

(c) \(A\) and \(A^c\) are disjoint with union \(S\), so \(P(A) + P(A^c) = P(S) = 1\). Taking \(A = S\) gives \(P(\varnothing) = 1 - 1 = 0\).

Key insight: Independence passes to complements: if knowing \(A\) tells you nothing about \(B\), it tells you nothing about "not \(B\)" either. This result justifies the "\(1 - \prod (1 - p_i)\)" formula for "at least one".

tests: 1.2.6, 1.2.3 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP1.29

Three-event inclusion–exclusion

Of 120 students, 60 take Python (\(P\)), 50 take Statistics (\(S\)) and 40 take ML (\(M\)). Also 20 take \(P\) and \(S\), 15 take \(P\) and \(M\), 10 take \(S\) and \(M\), and 5 take all three. A student is chosen at random.

(a) Derive the three-event inclusion–exclusion formula and use it to find \(P(\text{at least one course})\).
(b) Find \(P(\text{exactly one course})\), \(P(\text{exactly two})\) and \(P(\text{all three})\), and check that they add up to (a).
(c) Derive the formula \(P(\text{exactly one of } A, B) = P(A) + P(B) - 2P(A \cap B)\) for two events.

Fill the Venn diagram from the centre outward.

(a) Inclusion–exclusion:

\[ P(P \cup S \cup M) = \sum P(\text{single}) - \sum P(\text{pairs}) + P(\text{triple}) = \frac{60 + 50 + 40 - 20 - 15 - 10 + 5}{120} = \frac{110}{120} = \mathbf{0.9167} \]

(b) Only \(P = 60 - 20 - 15 + 5 = 30\), only \(S = 25\), only \(M = 20\), so exactly one \(= 75/120 = \mathbf{0.625}\). Exactly two \(= (15 + 10 + 5)/120 = \mathbf{0.25}\). All three \(= 5/120 = \mathbf{0.0417}\). The total is \(110/120\). ✓
(c) Adding the two "only" regions:

\[ \text{Exactly one} = (A \text{ only}) + (B \text{ only}) = [P(A) - P(A \cap B)] + [P(B) - P(A \cap B)] = \mathbf{P(A) + P(B) - 2P(A \cap B)} \]

Key insight: Inclusion–exclusion alternates adding and subtracting so that every region is counted exactly once. The Venn-diagram fill gives a built-in check.

tests: 1.2.4, 1.2.2 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP1.30

How large or small can an intersection be?

(a) If \(P(A) = 0.7\) and \(P(B) = 0.6\), find the smallest and largest possible values of \(P(A \cap B)\) and of \(P(A \cup B)\). Describe the configuration that achieves each extreme.
(b) Prove the general bound \(\max(0, P(A) + P(B) - 1) \le P(A \cap B) \le \min(P(A), P(B))\).
(c) \(A\) and \(B\) are mutually exclusive and \(A \cup B = S\). Find the maximum of \(P(A) \cdot P(B)\).

\(P(A \cup B) \le 1\) gives the lower bound. \(A \cap B \subset A\) gives the upper bound.

(a) \(P(A \cap B) \in\) \([0.3, 0.6]\). The minimum 0.3 occurs when the union fills \(S\). The maximum 0.6 occurs when \(B \subset A\). Correspondingly, \(P(A \cup B) \in\) \([0.7, 1.0]\). (Independence would give 0.42, inside the range.)
(b) From the addition rule:

\[ 1 \ge P(A \cup B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A \cap B) \;\Rightarrow\; P(A \cap B) \ge P(A) + P(B) - 1 \]

and \(P(A \cap B) \ge 0\) always. \(A \cap B \subset A\) and \(A \cap B \subset B\) give the upper bound.
(c) \(P(A) + P(B) = 1\). The product \(p(1 - p)\) is maximised at \(p = 1/2\), giving \(1/4\).

Key insight: Marginals alone do not determine a joint probability; they only bound it. Extra information (independence, or a measured overlap) is needed to pin it down. This is why ML models need joint data, not just per-feature rates.

tests: 1.2.4, 1.2.3, 1.2.6 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP1.31

Do spam features look independent?

A corpus of 1000 emails contains 250 spam and 750 ham.

contains "offer" contains suspicious link contains both
Spam (250) 150 100 60
Ham (750) 150 100 30

(a) Over all 1000 emails, are "offer" (\(O\)) and "link" (\(L\)) independent?
(b) Within spam only, are \(O\) and \(L\) independent? Within ham only?
(c) Explain how the answers to (a) and (b) can differ. Why does this matter for the Naive Bayes classifier in Module 2?

Compute \(P(O)\), \(P(L)\) and \(P(O \cap L)\) separately in each population.

(a) Over all emails:

\[ P(O) = \frac{300}{1000} = 0.3, \quad P(L) = 0.2, \quad P(O \cap L) = \frac{90}{1000} = 0.09 \ne 0.06 \]

so they are dependent overall.
(b) Spam: \(0.6 \times 0.4 = 0.24 = 60/250\), so they are independent within spam. Ham: \(0.2 \times 0.1333 = 0.0267 \ne 30/750 = 0.04\), so they are not independent within ham.
(c) Both features are more common in spam, so the class label creates an association between them in the pooled data even when there is none within a class. Naive Bayes needs independence given the class (conditional independence), not overall independence. In this data the assumption holds for spam but is violated for ham.

Key insight: Independence depends on the population you condition on. Mixing subgroups can create or destroy associations, which is the root of Simpson's-paradox-type effects.

tests: 1.2.6, 1.2.7 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP1.32

The "at least one" trap in experimentation

A data team runs 20 independent A/B tests on features that truly have no effect. Each test has a 5% chance of a false positive.

(a) Find \(P(\text{at least one false positive})\).
(b) What is the smallest number of tests for which this probability exceeds 0.5?
(c) If each test instead uses a threshold of \(0.05/20\) (Bonferroni), find \(P(\text{at least one false positive})\) across the 20 tests.

\(P(\text{at least one}) = 1 - P(\text{none}) = 1 - 0.95^n\).

(a) Using the complement:

\[ 1 - 0.95^{20} = \mathbf{0.6415} \]

(b) \(0.95^{13} = 0.513\) and \(0.95^{14} = 0.488\), so \(n = 14\).
(c) With the Bonferroni threshold:

\[ 1 - (1 - 0.0025)^{20} = \mathbf{0.0488} \]

which is below 5%.

Key insight: Small per-event risks compound quickly over many independent trials. This basic probability rule is the reason multiple-testing corrections exist, and it reappears in hypothesis testing (Module 4).

tests: 1.2.4, 1.2.6 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP1.33

Is the data even consistent?

(a) A survey reports: 70% of users like feature A, 60% like feature B, and 20% like neither. Find the percentage that like both, and confirm the numbers are consistent.
(b) A dashboard reports: \(P(\text{churn}) = 0.6\), \(P(\text{complaint}) = 0.5\), \(P(\text{churn and complaint}) = 0.05\). Show that these numbers cannot all be correct.
(c) A model outputs class probabilities 0.62, 0.31 and 0.12 for three mutually exclusive classes. What is wrong, and what is the standard fix?

Every union must satisfy \(\max(P(A), P(B)) \le P(A \cup B) \le 1\).

(a) \(P(A \cup B) = 0.8\), so:

\[ P(\text{both}) = 0.7 + 0.6 - 0.8 = \mathbf{0.5}\ \mathbf{(50\%)} \]

This is \(\le \min(0.7, 0.6)\), so the data are consistent.
(b) Applying the addition rule:

\[ P(\text{union}) = 0.6 + 0.5 - 0.05 = \mathbf{1.05 > 1} \]

which is impossible. The intersection must be at least 0.1 (P1.30).
(c) The probabilities sum to 1.05, violating the axioms. Renormalise by dividing each by the total (0.590, 0.295, 0.114), or better, produce the outputs with a \(\operatorname{softmax}\).

Key insight: Checking the axioms catches broken pipelines, survey errors and uncalibrated model outputs.

tests: 1.2.3, 1.2.4 · L5 Mastery

3.C Module 1 Capstone

L5 · MasteryP1.34

Latency audit: from raw data to probabilities

An SRE team logged 12 request latencies (ms) on each of two servers.

Server A: 110, 95, 120, 105, 130, 98, 102, 115, 400, 108, 112, 100
Server B: 140, 150, 135, 160, 145, 155, 138, 148, 152, 142, 158, 147

(a) Give the five-number summary, IQR and \(1.5 \cdot \text{IQR}\) / \(3 \cdot \text{IQR}\) fences for each server, and classify any outliers.
(b) Compute the mean and median of A with and without the outlier. A manager compares means only and concludes "A is barely faster than B". Is this fair?
(c) Compute the sample SD of A (with and without the outlier) and of B. Which server is more predictable once glitches are excluded?
(d) Pool all 24 requests. Let \(S\) = "latency > 125 ms" and \(A\) = "served by server A". Find \(P(S)\), \(P(A)\), \(P(S \cap A)\) and \(P(S \cup A)\). Are \(S\) and \(A\) independent? Are they mutually exclusive?
(e) Two of the 24 requests are drawn at random without replacement. Find \(P(\text{both are slow})\).
(f) Write a two-sentence recommendation.

With \(n = 12\), the quartile positions are 3.25 and 9.75. With \(n = 11\), they are 3 and 9.

(a) A: 95, 100.5, 109, 118.75, 400; IQR 18.25; inner fences 73.125 and 146.125; outer fences 45.75 and 173.5, so 400 is an extreme outlier. B: 135, 140.5, 147.5, 154.25, 160; IQR 13.75; inner fences 119.875 and 174.875, so there are no outliers.
(b) A: mean 132.9 (median 109) with the outlier, and mean 108.6 (median 108) without it. B: mean = median = 147.5. Comparing means (132.9 vs 147.5) understates A's advantage, because one glitch inflates A's mean by about 24 ms. The medians show a typical A request is about 38 ms faster. Not fair.
(c) \(s_A = \mathbf{84.7}\) with the outlier and 10.35 without; \(s_B = \mathbf{7.91}\). Excluding the glitch, B is slightly more predictable (lower SD), while A is faster.
(d) Slow requests: 2 from A (130 and 400) and 12 from B, so:

\[ P(S) = \frac{14}{24} = \mathbf{0.583}, \quad P(A) = \mathbf{0.5}, \quad P(S \cap A) = \frac{2}{24} = \mathbf{0.083} \]

\[ P(S \cup A) = \frac{12}{24} + \frac{14}{24} - \frac{2}{24} = \mathbf{1} \]

(every B request is slow). \(P(S)P(A) = 0.292 \ne 0.083\), so they are dependent. They are not mutually exclusive, since they share 2 requests.
(e) Counting pairs:

\[ \frac{\binom{14}{2}}{\binom{24}{2}} = \frac{91}{276} = \mathbf{0.330} \]

(f) Server A gives a lower typical latency (median 109 vs 147.5 ms), but its rare extreme spikes such as 400 ms need a root-cause fix. Prefer A, add monitoring of the 99th percentile, and report medians and IQRs rather than means in the SLA dashboard.

Key insight: Descriptive statistics and probability are one workflow. You summarise the data robustly, turn the summaries into events, and the empirical probabilities of those events (including independence checks) drive the decision.

tests: 1.1.2, 1.1.3, 1.1.4, 1.1.5, 1.2.3, 1.2.4, 1.2.6, 1.2.5 · L5 Mastery
Module 2: Conditional Probability, Bayes' Theorem & Naive Bayes · 35 questions

Covers tree nodes 2.1.1–2.1.5 and 2.2.1–2.2.8 (CS-3, CS-4, W-1, W-2).

4.A Conditional & Total Probability (2.1)

L1 · FoundationP2.1

True or false — the rules of conditioning

\(A\) and \(B\) are events with \(P(A) > 0\) and \(P(B) > 0\). Mark each statement True or False and justify it in one line.

(a) \(P(A \mid B) = P(B \mid A)\) for all events \(A\) and \(B\).
(b) \(P(A \mid A) = 1\).
(c) \(P(A \mid B) + P(A^c \mid B) = 1\).
(d) \(P(A \mid B) + P(A \mid B^c) = 1\).
(e) If \(A \subset B\), then \(P(B \mid A) = 1\).
(f) \(P(A \cap B) \le P(A \mid B)\).

Conditioning on \(B\) makes \(B\) the new sample space. Ask which statements stay inside one sample space and which mix two.

(a) False.

\[ P(A \mid B) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)} \quad \text{and} \quad P(B \mid A) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(A)} \]

They are equal only when \(P(A) = P(B)\).
(b) True. \(\frac{P(A \cap A)}{P(A)} = 1\).
(c) True. Given \(B\), the events \(A\) and \(A^c\) partition the reduced sample space.
(d) False. The two terms are conditioned on different sample spaces (\(B\) and \(B^c\)). P2.13 gives a counter-example where the sum is 1.22.
(e) True. \(A \subset B\) gives \(A \cap B = A\), so \(P(B \mid A) = \frac{P(A)}{P(A)} = 1\).
(f) True. \(P(A \cap B) = P(A \mid B) \cdot P(B)\), and \(P(B) \le 1\).

Key insight: The only thing that changes under conditioning is the denominator, which is the new sample space. Complements behave normally inside one conditional world. Nothing forces probabilities from two different conditional worlds to add up.

tests: 2.1.1, 2.1.4 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP2.2

Translate the English into notation

Let \(D\) = "patient has the disease" and \(T\) = "test is positive". Write each phrase as a probability expression. Do not compute anything.

(a) "Among diseased patients, 92% test positive."
(b) "Of those who test positive, 30% actually have the disease."
(c) "3% of the population both has the disease and tests positive."
(d) "The test wrongly flags 6% of healthy people."
(e) "If the test is negative, the chance of disease is 0.1%."

The group named after "among", "of those who" or "if" is the condition. It goes to the right of the bar.

(a) \(P(T \mid D) = 0.92\), the sensitivity.
(b) \(P(D \mid T) = 0.30\), the positive predictive value.
(c) \(P(D \cap T) = 0.03\), a joint probability with no conditioning.
(d) \(P(T \mid D^c) = 0.06\), the false-positive rate (\(1 - \text{specificity}\)).
(e) \(P(D \mid T^c) = 0.001\).

Key insight: Most Bayes errors come from translation, not algebra. Phrases (a) and (b) sound alike but describe different conditional probabilities. Mixing them up is the base-rate fallacy, which Part 4.B examines.

tests: 2.1.1, 2.1.3 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP2.3

With or without replacement?

A box holds 5 red and 5 blue chips. Two chips are drawn. Let \(R_1\) = "first chip red" and \(R_2\) = "second chip red".

(a) With replacement: are \(R_1\) and \(R_2\) independent? Give \(P(R_2 \mid R_1)\) and \(P(R_2)\).
(b) Without replacement: answer the same questions.
(c) Without replacement, what is \(P(R_2)\) when you are not told the first draw? Explain why it is not "smaller than 1/2".

For (c), use total probability over the two possible first draws.

(a) Independent. \(P(R_2 \mid R_1) = 5/10 = 0.5 = P(R_2)\).
(b) Dependent. \(P(R_2 \mid R_1) = 4/9 \approx 0.444\), while \(P(R_2) = 0.5\).
(c)

\[ P(R_2) = P(R_1) \cdot \tfrac{4}{9} + P(R_1^c) \cdot \tfrac{5}{9} = 0.5 \cdot \tfrac{4}{9} + 0.5 \cdot \tfrac{5}{9} = \mathbf{0.5} \]

The second draw is equally likely to be any of the 10 chips. The first draw changes the probability only when you know its result.

Key insight: Sampling without replacement makes the draws dependent. The unconditional probability of the second draw is unchanged, because dependence shows up only once you condition on something.

tests: 2.1.4 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP2.4

Two dice, two directions

Two fair dice are thrown. Let \(A\) = "sum \(\ge 9\)" and \(B\) = "at least one six".

(a) Find \(P(B \mid A)\).
(b) Find \(P(A \mid B)\).
(c) Use the asymmetry between (a) and (b) to explain the "reduced sample space" idea.

List the outcomes in \(A\) and the outcomes in \(B\), then count \(A \cap B\).

\(A\) has 10 outcomes: sum 9 has 4, sum 10 has 3, sum 11 has 2, sum 12 has 1. \(B\) has 11 outcomes. \(A \cap B = \{(3,6),(6,3),(4,6),(6,4),(5,6),(6,5),(6,6)\}\) has 7 outcomes.

(a)

\[ P(B \mid A) = \frac{7}{10} = \mathbf{0.70} \]

(b)

\[ P(A \mid B) = \frac{7}{11} \approx \mathbf{0.636} \]

(c) Both answers share the numerator \(\lvert A \cap B \rvert = 7\). They differ only in the reduced sample space they divide by: 10 outcomes in \(A\), 11 in \(B\).

Key insight: \(P(B \mid A)\) and \(P(A \mid B)\) share a numerator and differ in the denominator. That one fact is the whole content of Bayes' theorem.

tests: 2.1.1 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP2.5

Conditional probabilities from a contingency table

A survey of 200 employees records department and whether each prefers work-from-home (WFH):

Department WFH = Yes WFH = No Total
Engineering 60 20 80
Sales 25 45 70
Support 15 35 50
Total 100 100 200

One employee is chosen at random. Find:
(a) \(P(\text{Yes} \mid \text{Engineering})\)
(b) \(P(\text{Engineering} \mid \text{Yes})\)
(c) \(P(\text{Sales} \cap \text{No})\)
(d) \(P(\text{No} \mid \text{not Engineering})\)
(e) Are "Engineering" and "prefers WFH" independent?

For a conditional probability, divide the cell by the total of the row or column you are conditioning on.

(a) \(60/80 = \mathbf{0.75}\)
(b) \(60/100 = \mathbf{0.60}\)
(c) \(45/200 = \mathbf{0.225}\)
(d)

\[ \frac{45 + 35}{70 + 50} = \frac{80}{120} = \mathbf{0.667} \]

(e) \(P(\text{Yes}) = 0.5\) but \(P(\text{Yes} \mid \text{Eng}) = 0.75\), so they are not independent. Engineers lean towards WFH.

Key insight: In a table, a conditional probability is a row or column percentage. Independence means every row has the same proportions as the overall margin.

tests: 2.1.3, 1.2.6 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP2.6

Chain rule without replacement

A lot of 20 chips has 4 defectives. Three chips are drawn one at a time without replacement. Find:
(a) \(P(\text{all three defective})\)
(b) \(P(\text{first two good, third defective})\)
(c) \(P(\text{exactly one defective})\). Compute it two ways: with the chain rule over the 3 orderings, and with combinations.

\(P(A \cap B \cap C) = P(A) \cdot P(B \mid A) \cdot P(C \mid A \cap B)\). Every draw changes the composition of the lot.

(a)

\[ \left(\frac{4}{20}\right)\left(\frac{3}{19}\right)\left(\frac{2}{18}\right) = \frac{24}{6840} \approx \mathbf{0.00351} \]

(b)

\[ \left(\frac{16}{20}\right)\left(\frac{15}{19}\right)\left(\frac{4}{18}\right) = \frac{960}{6840} \approx \mathbf{0.1404} \]

(c) Chain rule: each of the 3 positions for the defective has probability \(960/6840\), so \(3 \times 960/6840 = \mathbf{0.4211}\). Combinations:

\[ \frac{\binom{4}{1}\binom{16}{2}}{\binom{20}{3}} = \frac{4 \cdot 120}{1140} = \mathbf{0.4211} \]

The two methods agree.

Key insight: The chain rule and counting with combinations are the same calculation. The chain rule follows the order of the draws, and the combination count adds up all the orders at once.

tests: 2.1.2 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP2.7

Total probability with three suppliers

A laptop maker gets batteries from suppliers A, B and C in the proportions 50%, 30% and 20%. Their failure rates in the first year are 1%, 2% and 5%. Find the probability that a randomly chosen laptop's battery fails in the first year. Draw the tree diagram.

The suppliers partition the sample space. Add \(P(\text{supplier}) \times P(\text{fail} \mid \text{supplier})\) over the branches.

\[ P(F) = 0.5(0.01) + 0.3(0.02) + 0.2(0.05) = 0.005 + 0.006 + 0.010 = \mathbf{0.021} \]

Key insight: The overall failure rate is a weighted average of the supplier rates, weighted by each supplier's share. Supplier C supplies only 20% but contributes almost half of all failures (0.010 out of 0.021).

tests: 2.1.5 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP2.8

Loan defaults by age (fill the table, then condition)

A bank's 6,000 loans are classified by age group and default status. Some cells are missing:

Young Middle-aged Senior Total
Default = No 1200 2790 1410 ?
Default = Yes ? ? ? ?
Total 1500 3000 1500 6000

(a) Complete the table.
(b) Find \(P(\text{default} \mid \text{young})\) and \(P(\text{young} \mid \text{default})\).
(c) Find \(P(\text{no default} \mid \text{middle-aged})\) and \(P(\text{middle-aged} \mid \text{no default})\).
(d) Which group has the highest default rate? Which group contributes the most defaulters? Is default independent of age?

Fill each missing cell by subtracting from the column total. A "rate" conditions on the age group. A "share of defaulters" conditions on default.

(a) Default = Yes: Young 300, Middle 210, Senior 90, total 600. Default = No total: 5400.
(b) \(P(D \mid \text{Young}) = 300/1500 = \mathbf{0.20}\). \(P(\text{Young} \mid D) = 300/600 = \mathbf{0.50}\).
(c) \(P(\text{No} \mid \text{Middle}) = 2790/3000 = \mathbf{0.93}\). \(P(\text{Middle} \mid \text{No}) = 2790/5400 \approx \mathbf{0.517}\).
(d) Young has both the highest default rate (20%, against 7% and 6%) and the largest share of defaulters (50%). The overall default rate is 600/6000 = 10%, and the group rates differ from it, so default is not independent of age.

Key insight: "Rate within a group" and "share of a group among the cases" answer different business questions: who is risky, and where the losses come from. They are \(P(D \mid G)\) and \(P(G \mid D)\).

tests: 2.1.3, 2.1.4, 1.2.7 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP2.9

Departures and arrivals

At an airport, 88% of flights depart on time, 82% arrive on time, and 78% do both.

(a) A flight departed on time. Find the probability it arrives on time.
(b) A flight arrived on time. Find the probability it departed on time.
(c) Are on-time departure and on-time arrival independent? What is the direction of the association?
(d) A flight departed late. Find the probability it still arrives on time.

For (d), \(P(\text{Arr} \cap \text{Dep}^c) = P(\text{Arr}) - P(\text{Arr} \cap \text{Dep})\).

(a) \(0.78/0.88 \approx \mathbf{0.886}\)
(b) \(0.78/0.82 \approx \mathbf{0.951}\)
(c)

\[ P(\text{Dep}) \cdot P(\text{Arr}) = 0.88 \times 0.82 = 0.7216 \ne 0.78 \]

so they are dependent. The association is positive: \(P(\text{Arr} \mid \text{Dep}) = 0.886 > P(\text{Arr}) = 0.82\).
(d) \(P(\text{Arr} \cap \text{Dep}^c) = 0.82 - 0.78 = 0.04\), and \(P(\text{Dep}^c) = 0.12\), so

\[ P(\text{Arr} \mid \text{Dep}^c) = \frac{0.04}{0.12} \approx \mathbf{0.333} \]

Key insight: Positive dependence means one event's probability rises when the other occurs. Notice that \(P(\text{Arr} \mid \text{Dep}) + P(\text{Arr} \mid \text{Dep}^c) = 1.22\), which shows directly why P2.1(d) is false.

tests: 2.1.1, 2.1.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP2.10

Two-stage urn: tree diagram, then reverse

Urn I holds 4 red and 6 blue balls. Urn II holds 5 red and 3 blue balls. One ball is moved at random from Urn I to Urn II. Then one ball is drawn from Urn II.

(a) Find \(P(\text{ball drawn from Urn II is red})\).
(b) The ball drawn is red. Find the probability that the transferred ball was red.

After the transfer, Urn II has 9 balls. Its red count is 6 if a red ball was moved and 5 otherwise.

(a)

\[ P(R) = \left(\frac{4}{10}\right)\left(\frac{6}{9}\right) + \left(\frac{6}{10}\right)\left(\frac{5}{9}\right) = 0.2667 + 0.3333 = \mathbf{0.60} \]

(b)

\[ P(\text{transfer red} \mid R) = \frac{0.2667}{0.60} \approx \mathbf{0.444} \]

The prior for "transferred red" was 0.40. Seeing a red ball raised it to 0.444.

Key insight: Total probability runs forward along the tree. Bayes runs backward along the same tree and reuses the same branch products.

tests: 2.1.5, 2.2.1 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP2.11

Solving for an unknown share (inverse total probability)

Company A makes 40% of the tools on the market. Companies B and C make the other 60%. The non-defective rates are 97% for A, 94% for B and 98% for C. What share must B and C each have so that a randomly chosen tool is non-defective with probability exactly 0.96?

Let B's share be \(x\), so C's share is \(0.6 - x\). Write the total-probability equation and solve for \(x\).

\[ \begin{aligned} 0.4(0.97) + 0.94x + 0.98(0.6 - x) &= 0.96 \\ 0.388 + 0.588 - 0.04x &= 0.96 \\ x &= 0.40 \end{aligned} \]

B: 40%, C: 20%. Check: \(0.388 + 0.376 + 0.196 = 0.96\).

Key insight: Total probability is a linear equation in the mixture weights. If the target rate is given, you can solve for the unknown weight. This is a 1-D version of estimating mixing coefficients, which comes back in GMMs (6.7).

tests: 2.1.5 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP2.12

Proof: positive association runs both ways

Let \(P(A) > 0\) and \(P(B) > 0\).
(a) Prove that \(P(A \mid B) > P(A)\) if and only if \(P(B \mid A) > P(B)\).
(b) Prove that if \(P(A \mid B) > P(A)\), then \(P(A \mid B^c) < P(A)\), assuming \(P(B^c) > 0\).
(c) State in plain words what (a) and (b) mean for "evidence".

Write both inequalities in terms of \(P(A \cap B)\). For (b), \(P(A)\) is a weighted average of \(P(A \mid B)\) and \(P(A \mid B^c)\).

(a)

\[ \begin{aligned} P(A \mid B) > P(A) &\iff \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)} > P(A) \\ &\iff P(A \cap B) > P(A)P(B) \\ &\iff \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(A)} > P(B) \\ &\iff P(B \mid A) > P(B) \end{aligned} \]

Each step multiplies or divides by a positive number, so the chain holds in both directions. \(\blacksquare\)

(b) By total probability,

\[ P(A) = P(A \mid B)P(B) + P(A \mid B^c)P(B^c) \]

So \(P(A)\) is a weighted average of \(P(A \mid B)\) and \(P(A \mid B^c)\) with weights that sum to 1. If one term is above the average, the other must be below it. \(\blacksquare\)

(c) If \(B\) makes \(A\) more likely, then \(A\) makes \(B\) more likely. Association is symmetric. Also, evidence that supports a hypothesis when observed must count against it when absent. You cannot have evidence that supports \(A\) both when it appears and when it does not.

Key insight: The direction of dependence is symmetric (both sides reduce to \(P(A \cap B)\) vs \(P(A)P(B)\)). The size of the effect is not, because \(P(A \mid B) \ne P(B \mid A)\) in general.

tests: 2.1.1, 1.2.6 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP2.13

Conditional probability is a probability measure

Fix an event \(B\) with \(P(B) > 0\) and define \(Q(A) = P(A \mid B)\).
(a) Prove that \(Q\) satisfies Kolmogorov's three axioms.
(b) Deduce that \(Q(A \cup C) = Q(A) + Q(C) - Q(A \cap C)\) and \(Q(A^c) = 1 - Q(A)\).
(c) Use the numbers in P2.9 to show that \(P(A \mid B) + P(A \mid B^c)\) can exceed 1. Explain why this does not contradict (b).

For countable additivity: if the \(A_i\) are disjoint, so are the sets \(A_i \cap B\).

(a)

  • Non-negativity: \(Q(A) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)} \ge 0\).
  • Normalisation: \(Q(S) = \frac{P(B)}{P(B)} = 1\).
  • Additivity: for disjoint \(A_i\),

\[ Q\left(\bigcup A_i\right) = \frac{P\left(\bigcup (A_i \cap B)\right)}{P(B)} = \frac{\sum P(A_i \cap B)}{P(B)} = \sum Q(A_i) \quad \blacksquare \]

(b) Every theorem that follows from the axioms holds for any function satisfying them. The addition rule and the complement rule therefore hold for \(Q\). \(\blacksquare\)

(c)

\[ P(\text{Arr} \mid \text{Dep}) + P(\text{Arr} \mid \text{Dep}^c) = 0.886 + 0.333 = \mathbf{1.22 > 1} \]

This does not contradict (b). The complement rule applies to the event, inside one measure: \(P(\text{Arr} \mid \text{Dep}) + P(\text{Arr}^c \mid \text{Dep}) = 1\). Changing the condition gives a different measure.

Key insight: Conditioning on a fixed event \(B\) gives a full probability model, so every rule of probability holds "given \(B\)". You may complement the event. You may not complement the condition.

tests: 2.1.1, 1.2.3 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP2.14

Simpson's paradox: when total probability reverses a verdict

Two hospitals report survival after the same surgery:

Low-risk patients High-risk patients
Hospital A 95 of 100 survive 720 of 900 survive
Hospital B 810 of 900 survive 70 of 100 survive

(a) Compute each hospital's overall survival rate.
(b) Compute each hospital's survival rate within each risk group. Which hospital is better?
(c) Explain the reversal with the law of total probability.
(d) Compute a "fair" comparison that applies both hospitals' stratum rates to a common 50/50 mix of patients. What does a data scientist learn from this before reporting an aggregate metric, for example model accuracy across user segments?

\(P(S) = P(S \mid \text{Low})P(\text{Low}) + P(S \mid \text{High})P(\text{High})\). The hospitals have very different \(P(\text{High})\).

(a) A: 815/1000 = 81.5%. B: 880/1000 = 88.0%. B looks better.

(b)

Low-risk High-risk
A 95% 80%
B 90% 70%

A is better in both groups.

(c) Each overall rate is a weighted average of the stratum rates. A's weights are 0.1 low-risk and 0.9 high-risk. B's are 0.9 and 0.1. A's overall rate is pulled towards its high-risk rate (80%), and B's towards its low-risk rate (90%). The difference in patient mix outweighs the difference in quality.

(d) With a 50/50 mix:

\[ A = 0.5(0.95) + 0.5(0.80) = \mathbf{0.875}, \qquad B = 0.5(0.90) + 0.5(0.70) = \mathbf{0.80} \]

A is better.

Key insight: An aggregate metric is a total-probability mixture, so it depends on the mix as much as on the stratum performance. A model can beat a baseline in every segment and still lose on the overall average if the segment sizes differ. Always ask what the weights are.

tests: 2.1.5, 2.1.3 · L5 Mastery

4.B Bayes' Theorem (2.2.1–2.2.4)

L1 · FoundationP2.15

Name the parts, and spot what is missing

"A rapid test detects 90% of infected people. 3% of the population is infected. Asha tests positive. What is the chance she is infected?"

(a) Identify the prior, the likelihood, the evidence and the posterior, both in notation and in words.
(b) Explain why the posterior cannot be computed from the information given. Which single number is missing?

The evidence \(P(+)\) needs both branches of the tree: infected and not infected.

(a)

  • Prior: \(P(I) = 0.03\), the belief before the test.
  • Likelihood: \(P(+ \mid I) = 0.90\), how well the hypothesis predicts the data.
  • Evidence: \(P(+) = P(+ \mid I)P(I) + P(+ \mid I^c)P(I^c)\), the normaliser.
  • Posterior: \(P(I \mid +)\), the updated belief.

(b) The false-positive rate \(P(+ \mid I^c)\) is missing, so \(P(+)\) cannot be computed. Without it the posterior could be anywhere from 0.03 to 1.

Key insight: A hit rate alone does not make a test useful. The posterior depends on how differently the evidence behaves under the competing hypotheses, which is the likelihood ratio (see P2.24).

tests: 2.2.2 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP2.16

Which machine made the defective?

Machines \(M_1\)–\(M_4\) produce 25%, 35%, 30% and 10% of a factory's output. Their defect rates are 2%, 1.5%, 3% and 5%.
(a) Find \(P(\text{defective})\).
(b) A defective item is found. Find the posterior probability for each machine and check that they sum to 1.
(c) Find \(P(\text{not } M_3 \mid \text{defective})\).
(d) Find \(P(M_3 \mid \text{non-defective})\).
(e) \(M_4\) makes only 10% of the output. Why is its posterior about \(2\times\) its prior?

\(\text{Posterior} \propto \text{prior} \times \text{likelihood}\). Compute the four products first.

Products \(P(M_i)P(D \mid M_i)\): 0.0050, 0.00525, 0.0090, 0.0050.

(a) \(P(D) = 0.02425\)

(b) Posteriors: \(M_1 \approx \mathbf{0.206}\), \(M_2 \approx \mathbf{0.216}\), \(M_3 \approx \mathbf{0.371}\), \(M_4 \approx \mathbf{0.206}\). Sum = 1.

(c) \(1 - 0.371 \approx \mathbf{0.629}\)

(d)

\[ \frac{0.30 \times 0.97}{0.97575} \approx \mathbf{0.298} \]

(e) \(M_4\)'s defect rate is 5%, about \(2\times\) the average rate (\(0.05/0.02425 \approx 2.06\)). For each machine,

\[ \frac{\text{posterior}}{\text{prior}} = \frac{P(D \mid M_i)}{P(D)} \]

Key insight: Bayes multiplies the prior by a factor of likelihood/average likelihood. A machine with a small share but a high defect rate can account for a large share of the blame.

tests: 2.2.1, 2.2.3 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP2.17

Binary communication channel

A channel sends 1s with probability 0.45 and 0s with probability 0.55. A transmitted 0 is received correctly with probability 0.92, and a transmitted 1 with probability 0.97.
(a) Find \(P(\text{1 received})\).
(b) Find \(P(\text{1 sent} \mid \text{1 received})\).
(c) Find \(P(\text{0 sent} \mid \text{0 received})\).
(d) Find the overall probability of a transmission error.
(e) The decoder reads "received symbol = sent symbol". Is this the MAP decision for each received symbol?

An error happens on two branches: 1 sent and 0 received, or 0 sent and 1 received.

(a)

\[ 0.45(0.97) + 0.55(0.08) = 0.4365 + 0.044 = \mathbf{0.4805} \]

(b) \(0.4365/0.4805 \approx \mathbf{0.908}\)
(c)

\[ \frac{0.55(0.92)}{0.5195} = \frac{0.506}{0.5195} \approx \mathbf{0.974} \]

(d) \(0.45(0.03) + 0.55(0.08) = \mathbf{0.0575}\)
(e) Yes. Given "1 received", the posterior for 1 is \(0.908 > 0.5\). Given "0 received", the posterior for 0 is \(0.974 > 0.5\). The identity decoder therefore picks the most probable sent symbol in both cases.

Key insight: Decoding is Bayesian classification: you choose the hypothesis with the largest posterior. If the channel were noisy enough or the prior skewed enough, MAP decoding could disagree with the received symbol.

tests: 2.2.1, 2.2.3, 2.1.5 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP2.18

The taxi-cab witness (base-rate fallacy)

In a city, 85% of cabs are Green and 15% are Blue. A cab was in a hit-and-run at night. A witness says it was Blue. Tests show the witness identifies colours correctly 80% of the time under the same conditions, whatever the true colour.
(a) Most people answer "80%". Find the actual \(P(\text{Blue} \mid \text{witness says Blue})\).
(b) How accurate must the witness be before "Blue" becomes more likely than not?
(c) Explain in one sentence why (a) is below 50% even though the witness is "80% reliable".

\(P(\text{says Blue}) = 0.15(0.8) + 0.85(0.2)\).

(a)

\[ P(B \mid \text{"B"}) = \frac{0.12}{0.12 + 0.17} = \mathbf{0.414} \]

(b) The posterior exceeds 0.5 when \(0.15a > 0.85(1 - a)\), that is when \(a > 0.85\). The witness must be better than 85% accurate.

(c) Green cabs are so common that the witness's mistakes on Green cabs (0.17 of all cases) outnumber the correct "Blue" reports on Blue cabs (0.12 of all cases).

Key insight: The 80% is \(P(\text{"Blue"} \mid \text{Blue})\). The question asks for \(P(\text{Blue} \mid \text{"Blue"})\). When one hypothesis is rare, a small error rate on the common hypothesis produces more false reports than the rare hypothesis produces true ones.

tests: 2.2.3, 2.2.2 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP2.19

Medical screening: the surprisingly low PPV

A disease has prevalence 0.5%. A screening test has sensitivity \(P(+ \mid D) = 98\%\) and specificity \(P(- \mid D^c) = 95\%\).
(a) Find \(P(+)\).
(b) Find the positive predictive value \(\text{PPV} = P(D \mid +)\).
(c) Find the negative predictive value \(\text{NPV} = P(D^c \mid -)\).
(d) Build the expected \(2 \times 2\) table for 10,000 people screened: TP, FN, FP, TN. Use it to explain the answer to (b) without formulas.
(e) What would you tell a patient who has just tested positive?

10,000 people at 0.5% prevalence means 50 have the disease and 9,950 do not.

(a)

\[ P(+) = 0.005(0.98) + 0.995(0.05) = 0.0049 + 0.04975 = \mathbf{0.05465} \]

(b)

\[ \text{PPV} = \frac{0.0049}{0.05465} \approx \mathbf{0.0897} \]

about 9%.
(c) \(\text{NPV} = 0.94525/0.94535 \approx \mathbf{0.99989}\)

(d)

Test + Test − Total
Diseased TP = 49 FN = 1 50
Healthy FP = 497.5 TN = 9452.5 9950

About 546 people test positive. Only 49 of them are diseased, so \(49/546 \approx 9\%\).

(e) "A positive screen raises your risk from 0.5% to about 9%, roughly an 18-fold increase. It is far from a diagnosis. A confirmatory test is the next step." A negative result is very reassuring.

Key insight: A test that is 98% sensitive and 95% specific still has \(\text{PPV} \approx 9\%\) when the disease is rare, because the 5% false-positive rate applies to the large healthy group. In ML terms, high recall and a low false-positive rate do not guarantee high precision under class imbalance.

tests: 2.2.3, 2.1.5 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP2.20

Four companies (exam style)

A product is made by companies A, B, C and D, which supply 15%, 35%, 30% and 20% of the market. Their defect rates are 4%, 2%, 3% and 5%.
(a) What percentage of the product on the market is defective?
(b) A defective item is selected. Find the probability it came from A.
(c) Find the probability that a defective item was not made by B.
(d) Find the probability that a defective item was not made by D.
(e) A non-defective item is selected. Find the probability it came from C.

For (c) and (d), use the complement of the posterior: \(P(B^c \mid \text{Def}) = 1 - P(B \mid \text{Def})\).

Products: A 0.006, B 0.007, C 0.009, D 0.010.

(a) \(P(\text{Def}) = \mathbf{0.032}\), i.e. 3.2%.
(b)

\[ P(A \mid \text{Def}) = \frac{0.006}{0.032} = \mathbf{0.1875} \]

(c) \(P(B^c \mid \text{Def}) = 1 - 0.007/0.032 = 1 - 0.21875 = \mathbf{0.78125}\)
(d) \(P(D^c \mid \text{Def}) = 1 - 0.3125 = \mathbf{0.6875}\)
(e)

\[ P(C \mid \text{ND}) = \frac{0.30 \times 0.97}{0.968} \approx \mathbf{0.3006} \]

Key insight: Posteriors over a partition behave like any probability distribution, so complements and unions of hypotheses work as usual. For (e), the evidence "non-defective" barely shifts the prior (\(0.30 \to 0.3006\)), because the item is non-defective under every hypothesis with probability between 0.95 and 0.98. Weak evidence produces a small update.

tests: 2.2.1, 2.2.3 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP2.21

Prove Bayes' theorem and that posteriors sum to 1

Let \(E_1, \dots, E_n\) partition \(S\) with \(P(E_i) > 0\), and let \(A\) be an event with \(P(A) > 0\).
(a) Prove that \(P(A) = \sum_j P(E_j)P(A \mid E_j)\). State which axiom you use.
(b) Prove that \(P(E_i \mid A) = \dfrac{P(E_i)P(A \mid E_i)}{\sum_j P(E_j)P(A \mid E_j)}\).
(c) Prove that \(\sum_i P(E_i \mid A) = 1\).
(d) Explain why, in a Naive Bayes classifier, you can compare the unnormalised scores \(P(E_i)P(A \mid E_i)\) without computing \(P(A)\).

\(A = A \cap S = A \cap \left(\bigcup E_j\right) = \bigcup (A \cap E_j)\), and these pieces are disjoint.

(a) \(A = \bigcup_j (A \cap E_j)\) by the distributive law. The pieces are disjoint because the \(E_j\) are. Additivity (Axiom 3) gives \(P(A) = \sum P(A \cap E_j)\). The multiplication rule gives

\[ P(A \cap E_j) = P(E_j)P(A \mid E_j) \quad \blacksquare \]

(b) By definition, \(P(E_i \mid A) = \frac{P(E_i \cap A)}{P(A)}\). Replace the numerator using the multiplication rule and the denominator using (a). \(\blacksquare\)

(c)

\[ \sum_i P(E_i \mid A) = \frac{\sum_i P(E_i)P(A \mid E_i)}{\sum_j P(E_j)P(A \mid E_j)} = 1 \]

The numerator and denominator are the same sum. \(\blacksquare\)

(d) \(P(A)\) is the same positive constant for every hypothesis. Dividing by it preserves the ordering, so

\[ \arg\max_i P(E_i \mid A) = \arg\max_i P(E_i)P(A \mid E_i) \]

You need \(P(A)\) only if you want calibrated probabilities. In that case, normalise the scores to sum to 1, using (c).

Key insight: The denominator in Bayes' theorem is the sum of all the numerators. That is why posteriors sum to 1 and why classifiers can skip the denominator.

tests: 2.2.1, 2.2.2, 2.1.5 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP2.22

Sequential updating equals batch updating

Use the test from P2.19 (prevalence 0.005, sensitivity 0.98, false-positive rate 0.05). A patient takes the test twice. The two results are conditionally independent given disease status, and both are positive.
(a) Sequential: update the prior with the first positive result. Then use that posterior as the new prior and update with the second.
(b) Batch: compute \(P(D \mid +, +)\) in one step using \(P(+,+ \mid D) = 0.98^2\) and \(P(+,+ \mid D^c) = 0.05^2\).
(c) Prove algebraically that (a) and (b) always agree under conditional independence.
(d) Why would the answers differ if both tests shared the same cause of false positives, for example cross-reacting antibodies?

Write the posterior after step 1 as \(\frac{P(D)P(+_1 \mid D)}{P(+_1)}\). Then the second update multiplies by \(P(+_2 \mid D, +_1) = P(+_2 \mid D)\).

(a) After the first positive: 0.0897. After the second:

\[ \frac{0.0897(0.98)}{0.0897(0.98) + 0.9103(0.05)} \approx \mathbf{0.6588} \]

(b)

\[ \frac{0.005(0.9604)}{0.005(0.9604) + 0.995(0.0025)} \approx \mathbf{0.6588} \]

Same answer.

(c) Sequential:

\[ P(D \mid +_1, +_2) = \frac{P(+_2 \mid D, +_1)\,P(D \mid +_1)}{P(+_2 \mid +_1)} \]

Conditional independence gives \(P(+_2 \mid D, +_1) = P(+_2 \mid D)\), so the numerator is \(\frac{P(+_2 \mid D)P(+_1 \mid D)P(D)}{P(+_1)}\).
The denominator is \(P(+_2 \mid +_1) = \frac{P(+_1, +_2)}{P(+_1)}\).
The \(P(+_1)\) terms cancel, leaving

\[ \frac{P(D)P(+_1 \mid D)P(+_2 \mid D)}{P(+_1, +_2)} \]

which is the batch formula. \(\blacksquare\)

(d) If false positives share a cause, \(P(+_2 \mid D^c, +_1)\) is much larger than 0.05. The second positive then carries far less new information, and multiplying likelihoods as if the tests were independent overstates the posterior. This is the naive-Bayes "double counting" problem (see P2.33).

Key insight: Under conditional independence, today's posterior is tomorrow's prior, and the order and grouping of the evidence do not matter. That property is what makes Bayesian learning incremental.

tests: 2.2.2, 2.2.5 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP2.23

MAP vs ML hypothesis

A coin comes from one of three machines:

  • \(H_1\): fair, \(p = 0.5\), prior 0.90
  • \(H_2\): \(p(\text{Heads}) = 0.8\), prior 0.08
  • \(H_3\): \(p(\text{Heads}) = 0.2\), prior 0.02

You toss it 5 times and observe H H T H H.
(a) Compute the likelihood \(P(\text{data} \mid H_i)\) for each hypothesis. Which is the ML hypothesis?
(b) Compute the posteriors. Which is the MAP hypothesis?
(c) Suppose instead you observe \(n\) heads in a row. What is the smallest \(n\) for which the MAP hypothesis becomes \(H_2\)?
(d) When are the MAP and ML hypotheses guaranteed to coincide?

Likelihood of a specific sequence: \(p^4(1 - p)^1\). MAP = \(\arg\max\) of prior \(\times\) likelihood.

(a) Likelihoods: \(H_1\) 0.03125, \(H_2\) 0.08192, \(H_3\) 0.00128. ML = \(H_2\).

(b) Prior \(\times\) likelihood: 0.028125, 0.006554, 0.0000256. Posteriors \(\approx\) 0.810, 0.189, 0.0007. MAP = \(H_1\).

(c) MAP becomes \(H_2\) when

\[ 0.08(0.8)^n > 0.90(0.5)^n \iff 1.6^n > 11.25 \]

so \(n > 5.15\) and \(n = 6\). Check: at \(n = 5\) the posteriors are 0.518 vs 0.482; at \(n = 6\) they are 0.401 vs 0.599.

(d) When the prior is uniform over the hypotheses, the prior is a constant factor and MAP = ML. Also, as data accumulate the likelihood dominates any fixed prior with no zero entries, so MAP tends to ML.

Key insight: ML asks which hypothesis best explains the data. MAP asks which hypothesis is most believable given the data and prior knowledge. A strong prior needs strong evidence to overturn it. Laplace smoothing in Naive Bayes is a mild prior of this kind.

tests: 2.2.4, 2.2.2 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP2.24

Odds form of Bayes and the likelihood ratio

Bayes' theorem can be written as posterior odds = prior odds \(\times\) likelihood ratio (LR), with \(\text{LR}^+ = \frac{P(+ \mid D)}{P(+ \mid D^c)}\).
(a) Derive the odds form from the standard formula.
(b) For the P2.19 test, compute \(\text{LR}^+\) and \(\text{LR}^- = \frac{P(- \mid D)}{P(- \mid D^c)}\).
(c) Use the odds form to recompute the PPV at prevalence 0.5%. Then compute the PPV for the same test in a specialist clinic where prevalence is 30%.
(d) How many independent positive results are needed at prevalence 0.5% before \(P(D \mid \text{all positive})\) exceeds 0.95?
(e) A vendor says their new test is "99% accurate". Explain to a manager why you also need the LR and the base rate before buying it.

Divide \(P(D \mid +)\) by \(P(D^c \mid +)\). The \(P(+)\) terms cancel. Independent tests multiply their LRs.

(a)

\[ \frac{P(D \mid +)}{P(D^c \mid +)} = \frac{P(+ \mid D)P(D)/P(+)}{P(+ \mid D^c)P(D^c)/P(+)} = \frac{P(D)}{P(D^c)} \times \frac{P(+ \mid D)}{P(+ \mid D^c)} \quad \blacksquare \]

(b) \(\text{LR}^+ = 0.98/0.05 = \mathbf{19.6}\). \(\text{LR}^- = 0.02/0.95 \approx \mathbf{0.021}\).

(c) At 0.5%: prior odds = \(0.005/0.995 = 0.005025\). Posterior odds = 0.0985, so \(\text{PPV} \approx 0.0897\), matching P2.19. At 30%: prior odds = 0.4286. Posterior odds = 8.40, so \(\text{PPV} \approx 0.894\).

(d) We need

\[ 0.005025 \times 19.6^k > 19 \]

so \(k > 2.77\) and \(k = 3\). After 2 positives \(P \approx 0.659\); after 3, \(P \approx 0.974\).

(e) "Accuracy" mixes sensitivity and specificity and ignores prevalence. What matters is \(\text{LR}^+\) (how much a positive shifts the odds) and the prior odds in the population where the test is used. The same test gives \(\text{PPV} \approx 9\%\) in a screening population and \(\approx 89\%\) in a specialist clinic.

Key insight: In odds form Bayes becomes a single multiplication. The likelihood ratio belongs to the test, and the prior odds belong to the population. Keeping them separate removes most base-rate confusion.

tests: 2.2.2, 2.2.3, 2.2.4 · L5 Mastery

4.C Naive Bayes Classifier (2.2.5–2.2.8)

L1 · FoundationP2.25

Conditional independence: concept check

Mark each statement True or False and justify it.
(a) "Thunder is conditionally independent of Rain given Lightning" means \(P(\text{Thunder} \mid \text{Rain}, \text{Lightning}) = P(\text{Thunder} \mid \text{Lightning})\).
(b) Naive Bayes assumes the features are independent of each other.
(c) Naive Bayes is called "naive" because it uses Bayes' theorem without a prior.
(d) Under the naive assumption, \(P(X_1, X_2, X_3 \mid Y) = P(X_1 \mid Y) \cdot P(X_2 \mid Y) \cdot P(X_3 \mid Y)\).
(e) Bernoulli NB is suitable for word-presence features, and Multinomial NB for word counts.
(f) Gaussian NB assumes every continuous feature is normally distributed within each class.

Pay close attention to the words "given the class" in (b).

(a) True. Once you know Lightning occurred, also learning about Rain does not change the probability of Thunder.
(b) False as stated. NB assumes the features are independent given the class. They may be strongly dependent unconditionally (see P2.30).
(c) False. It uses priors. "Naive" refers to the conditional-independence assumption.
(d) True. This is the factorisation that defines NB.
(e) True.
(f) True. Each feature gets one Gaussian per class.

Key insight: "Independent" and "conditionally independent" are different assumptions, and neither implies the other. NB makes only the second one.
