tests: 2.2.5, 2.2.8 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP2.26

The zero-frequency problem

(a) In a spam filter, the word "invoice" never appears in the training spam emails. Explain what NB does with a new email that contains "invoice" plus 20 strongly spammy words.
(b) What does Laplace (add-1) smoothing change in the numerator and in the denominator? Why must the denominator change too?
(c) Is smoothing important when a word appears 5,000 times in a class? When it appears 0 or 1 times?

Each conditional distribution \(P(\text{word} \mid \text{class})\) must still sum to 1 over the vocabulary after smoothing.

(a) \(P(\text{"invoice"} \mid \text{Spam}) = 0\), so the whole product for Spam is 0 whatever the other 20 words say. The email is classified Not-Spam with "certainty". One unseen word vetoes all the other evidence.

(b) Numerator: count + 1. Denominator: class total + \(V\), where \(V\) is the vocabulary size or the number of values of a categorical attribute. Adding 1 to each of \(V\) counts adds \(V\) to the total, and the probabilities must still sum to 1.

(c) At 5,000 occurrences the change is negligible: \(\frac{5000+1}{N+V}\) is about \(5000/N\). At 0 or 1 occurrences the change is large. Smoothing matters exactly where the data are thin.

Key insight: A zero estimate from a finite sample says "never seen", not "impossible". Laplace smoothing is a mild prior that keeps every probability positive.

tests: 2.2.7 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP2.27

Categorical Naive Bayes by hand

# Outlook Wind Play
1 Sunny Weak Yes
2 Sunny Strong No
3 Rainy Weak Yes
4 Rainy Strong No
5 Sunny Weak Yes
6 Rainy Weak No
7 Sunny Strong Yes
8 Rainy Strong No
9 Sunny Weak Yes
10 Rainy Weak Yes

(a) Build the prior and the likelihood tables \(P(\text{Outlook} \mid \text{Play})\) and \(P(\text{Wind} \mid \text{Play})\).
(b) Classify \(X = (\text{Rainy}, \text{Strong})\) and give the normalised posterior.
(c) Classify \(X = (\text{Sunny}, \text{Weak})\) and give the normalised posterior.
(d) Estimate \(P(\text{Play} = \text{Yes} \mid \text{Rainy}, \text{Strong})\) directly from the table, with no naive assumption. Does NB agree?

There are 6 Yes rows and 4 No rows. Count each attribute value separately within each class.

(a) Priors: \(P(\text{Yes}) = 0.6\), \(P(\text{No}) = 0.4\).

Yes No
Sunny 4/6 1/4
Rainy 2/6 3/4
Weak 5/6 1/4
Strong 1/6 3/4

(b)

\[ \text{Yes: } 0.6 \times \tfrac{2}{6} \times \tfrac{1}{6} = 0.0333, \qquad \text{No: } 0.4 \times \tfrac{3}{4} \times \tfrac{3}{4} = 0.225 \]

Classify No, with \(P(\text{No} \mid X) = 0.225/0.2583 \approx \mathbf{0.871}\).

(c)

\[ \text{Yes: } 0.6 \times \tfrac{4}{6} \times \tfrac{5}{6} = 0.3333, \qquad \text{No: } 0.4 \times \tfrac{1}{4} \times \tfrac{1}{4} = 0.025 \]

Classify Yes, with \(P(\text{Yes} \mid X) \approx \mathbf{0.930}\).

(d) Rows 4 and 8 are the (Rainy, Strong) rows, and both are No, so the direct estimate is \(P(\text{Yes} \mid X) = 0\). NB gives 0.129 and agrees on the class, but it is less extreme.

Key insight: NB smooths over combinations of features that are rare in the data by combining one-feature evidence. That is useful with many features, where exact combinations are almost never seen. It also explains why NB's probabilities are usually less extreme or less accurate than its class decisions.

tests: 2.2.6 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP2.28

Text Naive Bayes with Laplace smoothing

Training documents:

Text Tag
"great match today" Sports
"great goal great game" Sports
"the match was close" Sports
"the election was close" Politics
"great speech today" Politics
"the vote count" Politics

Classify "great close game" with Multinomial NB.
(a) Count the total words per class and the vocabulary size \(V\).
(b) Without smoothing, compute both class scores. What goes wrong?
(c) With add-1 smoothing, compute \(P(\text{word} \mid \text{class})\) for the three query words, the two scores and the normalised posterior.
(d) A test document contains the word "stadium", which never appeared in training. What does standard practice do with it, and why?

The priors are 1/2 each. Smoothed

\[ P(w \mid c) = \frac{\text{count}(w,c) + 1}{N_c + V} \]

(a) Sports: \(N = 11\) words (great \(\times 3\), match \(\times 2\), today, goal, game, the, was, close). Politics: \(N = 10\) words (the \(\times 2\), election, was, close, great, speech, today, vote, count). \(V = 12\) distinct words.

(b) Sports: \(3/11 \times 1/11 \times 1/11 \approx 0.00225\). Politics: "game" never appears, so the score is 0. One missing word rules Politics out completely.

(c) Sports: great 4/23, close 2/23, game 2/23. Politics: great 2/22, close 2/22, game 1/22.

\[ \text{Sports score} = \tfrac{1}{2} \times \tfrac{16}{12167} \approx 6.58 \times 10^{-4}, \qquad \text{Politics score} = \tfrac{1}{2} \times \tfrac{4}{10648} \approx 1.88 \times 10^{-4} \]

\(P(\text{Sports} \mid \text{doc}) \approx 0.778\). Classify Sports.

(d) It is ignored (dropped). It has no estimate in either class, and with smoothing it would add the same factor \(\frac{1}{N_c+V}\) to both classes, which is nearly uninformative. Some implementations reserve an "unknown" token instead.

Key insight: Smoothing changes the question from "has this word ever been seen in class \(c\)?" to "how often, relative to everything we have seen?". The decision becomes robust to a single gap in the training data.

tests: 2.2.6, 2.2.7, 2.2.8 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP2.29

Laplace smoothing for categorical attributes

# Colour Size Class
1 Red Small A
2 Red Large A
3 Green Small A
4 Red Small A
5 Blue Large B
6 Green Large B
7 Blue Small B
8 Green Large B

Colour has 3 values and Size has 2. Classify \(X = (\text{Blue}, \text{Small})\).
(a) Without smoothing.
(b) With add-1 smoothing, where the denominator is (class count + number of values of that attribute).
(c) Interpret the change from (a) to (b). Is a "100% certain" answer from 4 examples per class justified?

Smoothed \(P(\text{Blue} \mid A) = \frac{0 + 1}{4 + 3}\).

(a) A: \(P(\text{Blue} \mid A) = 0\), so the score is 0. B: \(0.5 \times 2/4 \times 1/4 = 0.0625\). B with posterior 1.0.

(b)

\[ A: 0.5 \times \tfrac{1}{7} \times \tfrac{4}{6} \approx 0.0476, \qquad B: 0.5 \times \tfrac{3}{7} \times \tfrac{2}{6} \approx 0.0714 \]

B with posterior 0.60.

(c) The class decision does not change, but the confidence drops from 100% to 60%. With 4 examples per class, a single unseen value (Blue in A) is weak evidence of impossibility, so 0.60 is a more honest posterior.

Key insight: For categorical attributes, the "\(V\)" in Laplace smoothing is the number of possible values of that attribute, not the vocabulary of the whole dataset. Smoothing mostly fixes overconfidence, and it occasionally changes a decision.

tests: 2.2.6, 2.2.7 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP2.30

Conditional independence and independence: two counter-examples

(a) Conditionally independent but not independent. A bag holds one fair coin and one coin with \(P(H) = 0.9\). You pick a coin at random and toss it twice. Show that the tosses are independent given the coin, and dependent unconditionally.

(b) Independent but not conditionally independent. \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent fair coin tosses. Let \(Z = 1\) if exactly one of them is Heads. Show that \(X\) and \(Y\) are not independent given \(Z = 1\).

(c) Which of the two situations makes Naive Bayes's assumption correct? In the second situation, what goes wrong if you treat \(X\) and \(Y\) as conditionally independent given the class \(Z\)?

For (a), compute \(P(H_1)\) and \(P(H_1 \cap H_2)\) by total probability over the coin.

(a) Given the coin, the tosses are separate physical events, so \(P(H_1 H_2 \mid \text{coin}) = p^2\). Unconditionally:

\[ \begin{aligned} P(H_1) &= 0.5(0.5) + 0.5(0.9) = 0.7, \quad \text{so } P(H_1)P(H_2) = 0.49 \\ P(H_1 \cap H_2) &= 0.5(0.25) + 0.5(0.81) = \mathbf{0.53 \ne 0.49} \end{aligned} \]

The tosses are dependent: a first Head suggests the biased coin, which makes a second Head more likely.

(b) Given \(Z = 1\), the only outcomes are HT and TH, each with probability \(\frac{1}{2}\). So \(P(X=H \mid Z=1) = P(Y=H \mid Z=1) = \frac{1}{2}\), but

\[ P(X=H, Y=H \mid Z=1) = \mathbf{0 \ne \tfrac{1}{4}} \]

Once you know \(Z\), learning \(X\) fixes \(Y\). This is the "explaining away" effect.

(c) NB's assumption holds in (a), where the class (coin) is a common cause and the features are its symptoms. In (b), the class is determined jointly by both features (the XOR pattern). Each feature alone carries no information about \(Z\): \(P(Z=1 \mid X=H) = \frac{1}{2}\). NB therefore cannot learn this concept at all.

Key insight: NB works when the class is a common cause of the features. It fails when the class is defined by an interaction between features. Correlated features in the data do not automatically break NB, but interactions do.

tests: 2.2.5, 1.2.6 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP2.31

Why "naive" makes learning possible: counting parameters

There are \(n\) binary features and a binary class \(Y\).
(a) Count the free parameters needed to specify \(P(Y)\) and the full joint \(P(X_1, \dots, X_n \mid Y)\) for both classes, with no assumptions.
(b) Count the parameters under the Naive Bayes assumption.
(c) Evaluate both for \(n = 30\). Comment on how many training examples you would need for each.
(d) Show that the NB estimate \(\hat{P}(X_i = 1 \mid Y = c) = \frac{\text{number of class-}c\text{ examples with } X_i = 1}{N_c}\) is the maximum-likelihood estimate of a Bernoulli parameter.

A distribution over \(2^n\) outcomes has \(2^n - 1\) free parameters. For (d), maximise \(p^k(1-p)^{N-k}\).

(a)

\[ 1 + 2(2^n - 1) \]

(b) \(1 + 2n\), which is one Bernoulli parameter per feature per class, plus the prior.

(c) Full joint: 2,147,483,647. NB: 61. With 30 features, most of the \(2^{30} \approx 10^9\) feature combinations would never appear in any realistic training set. NB needs only enough data to estimate 61 frequencies.

(d) For one feature in class \(c\), with \(k\) "ones" out of \(N_c\) examples, the log-likelihood is

\[ k \ln p + (N_c - k) \ln(1 - p) \]

Setting the derivative \(\frac{k}{p} - \frac{N_c - k}{1 - p}\) to 0 gives \(\hat{p} = k/N_c\). \(\blacksquare\) (This links to MLE in 4.5.)

Key insight: The naive assumption trades some bias for a huge reduction in variance: the parameter count drops from exponential to linear in \(n\). That trade is why NB works well on high-dimensional text with little data. Training NB by counting is maximum-likelihood estimation.

tests: 2.2.5, 2.2.6 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP2.32

Gaussian Naive Bayes

A fruit sorter uses two continuous features: weight \(w\) (g) and diameter \(d\) (cm). Within each class, each feature is modelled as normal:

Class Prior \(w\): mean, SD \(d\): mean, SD
Orange 0.7 150, 20 7.5, 0.6
Grapefruit 0.3 300, 40 11.0, 1.0

A fruit has \(w = 220\) g and \(d = 9.0\) cm.
(a) Compute the z-score of each feature under each class.
(b) Compute the four normal density values and the two unnormalised scores. Classify the fruit and give its posterior.
(c) The prior favours Orange 70:30. Why does Grapefruit still win?
(d) Gaussian NB uses densities, which can exceed 1. Is that a problem for the classifier?

\(N(x \mid \mu, \sigma) = \dfrac{\exp(-z^2/2)}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\). Working in logs is easier.

(a) Orange: \(z_w = 3.5\), \(z_d = 2.5\). Grapefruit: \(z_w = -2.0\), \(z_d = -2.0\).

(b) Densities: Orange \(w\) \(4.36 \times 10^{-5}\), \(d\) 0.0292. Grapefruit \(w\) \(1.35 \times 10^{-3}\), \(d\) 0.0540.
Scores:

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{Orange: } & 0.7 \times 4.36\text{e-}5 \times 0.0292 \approx 8.9 \times 10^{-7} \\ \text{Grapefruit: } & 0.3 \times 1.35\text{e-}3 \times 0.0540 \approx 2.19 \times 10^{-5} \end{aligned} \]

Grapefruit, with \(P \approx 0.961\).

(c) The fruit is 3.5 SD away from a typical orange in weight. A normal density falls off like \(\exp(-z^2/2)\), so the orange likelihood (\(\approx 1.27 \times 10^{-6}\)) is about \(57\times\) smaller than the grapefruit likelihood (\(\approx 7.29 \times 10^{-5}\)), which outweighs the 7/3 prior ratio.

(d) No. Densities are only ever compared as likelihoods. Normalising the scores produces valid posterior probabilities.

Key insight: Gaussian NB scores each feature by its standardised distance from the class mean. Because the log-likelihood is quadratic in \(z\), a single extreme feature can dominate the decision. That makes Gaussian NB sensitive to outliers and to features measured on scales with very different spreads.

tests: 2.2.8, 2.2.6, 3.2.4 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP2.33

Underflow, log-probabilities and calibration

(a) A document has 500 words, each with \(P(w \mid c) \approx 0.001\). What is the product of the likelihoods? Can it be stored as a 64-bit double, whose smallest normal value is about \(10^{-308}\)?
(b) Rewrite the NB decision rule in log space. Redo P2.28(c) in logs and recover the posterior with the log-sum-exp trick.
(c) Calibration. A spam email contains "free" and "FREE!!!". In the data the two words always occur together, and each alone has a likelihood ratio of 4 for spam. With prior odds 1:1, what is the true posterior \(P(\text{spam} \mid \text{both})\), and what does NB report?
(d) Explain why NB rankings, such as the ROC/AUC ordering, can still be good when its probabilities are badly calibrated.

\(\log\left(\prod p_i\right) = \sum \log p_i\). For (c): the two words are really one piece of evidence, but NB multiplies its LR twice.

(a) \(0.001^{500} = \mathbf{10^{-1500}}\). This underflows to 0 in double precision, so both classes would score 0.

(b) Decision rule:

\[ \hat{c} = \arg\max_c \left[\log P(c) + \sum_i \log P(w_i \mid c)\right] \]

From P2.28: \(\log \text{score}(\text{Sports}) \approx -7.327\), \(\log \text{score}(\text{Politics}) \approx -8.580\).

\[ \text{Posterior} = \frac{\exp(-7.327 - m)}{\exp(-7.327 - m) + \exp(-8.580 - m)} \]

with \(m = \max = -7.327\), giving 0.778, as before.

(c) True posterior: odds \(1 \times 4 = 4\), so 0.80. NB: odds \(1 \times 4 \times 4 = 16\), so \(\approx 0.941\). NB is overconfident.

(d) Redundant features push NB scores towards 0 or 1 by a monotone distortion of the true posterior. A monotone transformation keeps the ordering of examples, so threshold-free metrics like AUC can stay high. If calibrated probabilities are needed, apply post-hoc calibration such as Platt scaling or isotonic regression.

Key insight: Work in logs to avoid underflow. Treat NB probabilities as scores and calibrate them before using them as risks. The naive assumption counts correlated evidence more than once.

tests: 2.2.6, 2.2.8 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP2.34

Prior shift between training and deployment

A spam classifier was trained on a deliberately balanced set (50% spam). For one email it outputs \(P_{\text{train}}(\text{spam} \mid x) = 0.90\). In production, only 10% of email is spam.
(a) Assume the class-conditional likelihoods \(P(x \mid \text{class})\) are the same in training and production. Show how to correct the posterior for the new prior, and compute the corrected value.
(b) Under the corrected model, what training-posterior threshold corresponds to "flag if production posterior > 0.5"?
(c) Recompute (a) for a phishing-heavy inbox where spam is 70%.
(d) Why do data scientists often oversample the rare class in training, and what must they remember at deployment?

Posterior odds = prior odds \(\times\) LR. Recover the LR from the training odds, then multiply by the new prior odds.

(a) Training odds \(= 0.9/0.1 = 9 = \text{LR} \times 1\), so \(\text{LR} = 9\). Production odds \(= 9 \times (0.1/0.9) = 1\). Corrected \(P = 0.50\). In general:

\[ P_{\text{new}} \propto P_{\text{train}}(c \mid x) \times \frac{\pi_{\text{new}}(c)}{\pi_{\text{train}}(c)} \]

normalised over the classes.

(b) Production odds \(> 1\) requires training odds \(> 9\), i.e. \(P_{\text{train}} > 0.90\).

(c) Odds \(= 9 \times (0.7/0.3) = 21\), so \(P \approx 0.955\).

(d) Oversampling gives the model enough rare-class examples to learn the likelihood structure. It also inflates the prior, so the deployed posteriors must be rescaled by the true prior ratio, or the threshold moved accordingly. Otherwise the system will flag far too many items.

Key insight: A classifier's posterior is prior \(\times\) likelihood. When the base rate changes, the likelihood stays the same and the prior must be updated. This is Bayes' theorem used as a deployment tool.

tests: 2.2.2, 2.2.6 · L5 Mastery

4.D Module 2 Capstone

L5 · MasteryP2.35

Capstone: a fraud-flagging rule from first principles

A card network sees 0.2% fraudulent transactions. Two binary signals are available. Assume they are conditionally independent given the class.

\(P(\text{signal} \mid \text{Fraud})\) \(P(\text{signal} \mid \text{Legit})\)
\(F_1\) = foreign merchant 0.60 0.05
\(F_2\) = night-time (00:00–05:00) 0.70 0.20

(a) Total probability: what fraction of all transactions are at foreign merchants?
(b) Find \(P(\text{Fraud} \mid F_1 \text{ and } F_2 \text{ both present})\).
(c) Find \(P(\text{Fraud} \mid F_1 \text{ present}, F_2 \text{ absent})\) and \(P(\text{Fraud} \mid \text{neither present})\).
(d) Rule R1: "flag if both signals are present". On 1,000,000 transactions, give the expected TP, FP, precision (= PPV) and recall.
(e) Rule R2: "flag if at least one signal is present". Give the same four quantities.
(f) Explain why the precision of R1 equals the answer to (b).
(g) The fraud team can review 20,000 flags per million transactions. Which rule is feasible? What would you change if the naive assumption fails because legitimate travellers also tend to transact at night abroad?

Under conditional independence, \(P(F_1, F_2 \mid \text{Fraud}) = 0.6 \times 0.7 = 0.42\) and \(P(F_1, F_2 \mid \text{Legit}) = 0.05 \times 0.20 = 0.01\). Use absent-signal probabilities \(1 - p\) where needed.

(a) \(P(F_1) = 0.002(0.6) + 0.998(0.05) = \mathbf{0.0511}\)

(b)

\[ \frac{0.002(0.42)}{0.002(0.42) + 0.998(0.01)} = \frac{0.00084}{0.01082} \approx \mathbf{0.0776} \]

(c) \(F_1\) present, \(F_2\) absent:

\[ \frac{0.002(0.6)(0.3)}{0.00036 + 0.998(0.05)(0.8)} \approx \mathbf{0.0089} \]

Neither present:

\[ \frac{0.002(0.4)(0.3)}{0.00024 + 0.998(0.95)(0.8)} \approx \mathbf{0.00032} \]

(d) R1: \(\text{TP} = 2000 \times 0.42 = \mathbf{840}\), \(\text{FP} = 998{,}000 \times 0.01 = \mathbf{9{,}980}\), precision \(\approx\) 7.8%, recall = 42%.

(e) R2: \(P(\text{at least one} \mid \text{Fraud}) = 1 - 0.4(0.3) = 0.88\), so TP = 1,760. \(P(\text{at least one} \mid \text{Legit}) = 1 - 0.95(0.8) = 0.24\), so FP = 239,520. Precision \(\approx\) 0.73%, recall = 88%.

(f) Precision = \(P(\text{Fraud} \mid \text{flagged})\). R1 flags exactly the event "\(F_1\) and \(F_2\)", so its precision is \(P(\text{Fraud} \mid F_1, F_2)\), which is (b).

(g) R1 produces 10,820 flags, which is feasible. R2 produces 241,280, which is not. If the signals are positively correlated among legitimate users, \(P(F_1, F_2 \mid \text{Legit})\) exceeds 0.01. R1 then gets more false positives, and the NB posterior in (b) overstates the fraud risk. The remedy is to estimate \(P(F_1, F_2 \mid \text{class})\) jointly from data, or to add an interaction feature such as "foreign at night".

Key insight: This capstone uses every node of the module. Total probability gives volumes, Bayes gives the risk per flag, conditional independence makes the likelihoods tractable, and base rates explain why precision stays low even with strong signals. Choosing a decision rule means trading precision against recall within the review capacity available.

tests: 2.1.5, 2.2.1, 2.2.3, 2.2.5, 2.2.6 · L5 Mastery
Module 3a: Random Variables & Joint Distributions · 29 questions

Tree nodes covered: 3.1.1–3.1.7 (CS-5, W-2). The bottleneck skills that come back again and again are: finding \(k\) so that a pmf or pdf is valid, computing \(\operatorname{Var}(X) = E[X^2] - (E[X])^2\), using \(P(X = a) = 0\) for continuous variables, getting marginals by summing or integrating, and checking independence in every cell.

5.A Discrete RVs: PMF, Expectation, Variance, CDF (3.1.1–3.1.5)

L1 · FoundationP3a.1

What exactly is a random variable?

Two coins are tossed. Let \(X\) = number of heads.

(a) Write the sample space \(S\) and the value \(X\) assigns to each outcome. Why is \(X\) called a function, and what are its domain and range?
(b) Classify each of these as a discrete or continuous random variable: (i) number of failed logins in an hour; (ii) the time until the next failed login; (iii) the proportion of 50 tested chips that are defective; (iv) a person's exact blood-alcohol concentration; (v) the number of tokens in a randomly chosen sentence.

A random variable gives a real number to each outcome. Discrete means the possible values can be listed (counted). Continuous means any value in an interval is possible.

(a) \(S = \{HH, HT, TH, TT\}\), and \(X(HH) = 2\), \(X(HT) = X(TH) = 1\), \(X(TT) = 0\). \(X\) is a function \(X: S \to \mathbb{R}\). Its domain is \(S\) and its range is \(\{0, 1, 2\}\). Several outcomes can map to the same number (HT and TH both map to 1). That is how

\[ P(X = 1) = \frac{2}{4} \]

arises.

(b) (i) Discrete, (ii) Continuous, (iii) Discrete: it takes the values \(0, \frac{1}{50}, \ldots, 1\), which is a finite list even though they are fractions. (iv) Continuous, (v) Discrete.

Key insight: Whether a variable is discrete or continuous depends on whether its values can be listed. A fraction can still be discrete. A random variable converts outcomes that may not be numbers into numbers, and that is what allows us to compute means and variances.

tests: 3.1.1 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP3a.2

Is this a legitimate PMF?

For each assignment, decide whether it is a valid probability mass function and give the reason.

(a) \(p(x) = 0.25\) for \(x = 1, 2, 3, 4\)
(b) \(p(1) = 0.4\), \(p(2) = 0.3\), \(p(3) = 0.2\), \(p(4) = 0.2\)
(c) \(p(1) = 0.5\), \(p(2) = 0.6\), \(p(3) = -0.1\), \(p(4) = 0\)
(d) \(p(x) = \frac{x}{6}\) for \(x = 1, 2, 3\)
(e) \(p(x) = \frac{x - 2}{5}\) for \(x = 1, 2, 3, 4\)
(f) \(p(x) = \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^x\) for \(x = 1, 2, 3, \ldots\) (all positive integers)

There are two conditions, and both must hold: every \(p(x) \ge 0\), and \(\sum p(x) = 1\). For (f), use the sum of a geometric series.

(a) Valid: this is the discrete uniform distribution with \(n = 4\).
(b) Invalid: the probabilities sum to 1.1.
(c) Invalid: one probability is negative. The sum is 1, but that does not rescue it.
(d) Valid: \(\frac{1}{6} + \frac{2}{6} + \frac{3}{6} = 1\), and every value is \(\ge 0\).
(e) Invalid: \(p(1) = -0.2\), which is negative, and the sum is 0.4.
(f) Valid: every term is \(> 0\), and the sum is

\[ \frac{1/2}{1 - 1/2} = 1. \]

A pmf can have infinitely many values.

Key insight: Check nonnegativity and the total of 1 separately. A sum of 1 does not make up for a negative value, as (c) shows.

tests: 3.1.2 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP3a.3

True or false — and why?

(a) The expected value \(E[X]\) must be one of the values \(X\) can take.
(b) \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\) can be negative when \(X\) takes negative values.
(c) For any random variable, \(F(x) = P(X \le x)\) is non-decreasing, with \(F(-\infty) = 0\) and \(F(+\infty) = 1\).
(d) For a discrete random variable, \(P(X < 3)\) and \(P(X \le 3)\) are always equal.
(e) If \(X\) is measured in metres, then \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\) is also measured in metres.

Think about a fair die for (a). Variance is an average of squares.

(a) False. For a fair die, \(E[X] = 3.5\), and the die can never show 3.5. Expectation is a long-run average, not a value you will observe.
(b) False. It averages squares, so always

\[ \operatorname{Var}(X) = E[(X - \mu)^2] \ge 0. \]

It equals 0 only when \(X\) is constant.
(c) True. These are the defining properties of a CDF.
(d) False. They differ by \(P(X = 3)\), which can be positive for a discrete random variable. For a continuous random variable they are equal.
(e) False. \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\) is in \(\text{metres}^2\). The standard deviation is in metres.

Key insight: Most mistakes in this module come from forgetting one of these properties. The "\(<\) vs \(\le\)" distinction only matters for discrete random variables.

tests: 3.1.3, 3.1.4, 3.1.5 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP3a.4

Find k, then mean, variance and CDF

The number of retries \(X\) needed by a network packet has pmf \(p(x) = kx\) for \(x = 1, 2, 3, 4\).

(a) Find \(k\).
(b) Find \(E[X]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\).
(c) Write the CDF \(F(x)\) for all real \(x\) and use it to find \(P(X \le 2.5)\) and \(P(X \ge 2)\).

\(\sum kx = 10k = 1\). Find \(E[X^2]\) first, then use \(\operatorname{Var} = E[X^2] - (E[X])^2\).

(a) \(k = \frac{1}{10}\). The pmf values are 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.4.
(b) The mean and second moment are:

\[ E[X] = \frac{1 \cdot 1 + 2 \cdot 2 + 3 \cdot 3 + 4 \cdot 4}{10} = \mathbf{3}, \qquad E[X^2] = \frac{1 + 8 + 27 + 64}{10} = 10 \]

So \(\operatorname{Var}(X) = 10 - 9 = 1\) and \(\text{SD} = 1\).
(c) \(F(x) = 0\) for \(x < 1\); 0.1 for \(1 \le x < 2\); 0.3 for \(2 \le x < 3\); 0.6 for \(3 \le x < 4\); 1 for \(x \ge 4\).
\(P(X \le 2.5) = F(2.5) =\) 0.3. \(P(X \ge 2) = 1 - F(1) =\) 0.9.

Key insight: The CDF of a discrete random variable is a step function defined for every real \(x\). Between two support points it stays flat, which is why \(F(2.5) = F(2)\).

tests: 3.1.2, 3.1.3, 3.1.4, 3.1.5 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP3a.5

The fair die as a full distribution

\(X\) is the face shown by a fair six-sided die.

(a) Write the pmf.
(b) Compute \(E[X]\), \(E[X^2]\), \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\) and \(\text{SD}(X)\).
(c) Find \(P(2 < X \le 5)\) using the CDF only.

\(\sum x = 21\) and \(\sum x^2 = 91\). \(P(a < X \le b) = F(b) - F(a)\).

(a) \(p(x) = \frac{1}{6}\) for \(x = 1, \ldots, 6\) (discrete uniform).
(b) \(E[X] = \frac{21}{6} =\) 3.5. \(E[X^2] = \frac{91}{6} \approx 15.1667\).

\[ \operatorname{Var} = \frac{91}{6} - 12.25 = \mathbf{\frac{35}{12} \approx 2.9167} \]

and \(\text{SD} \approx 1.708\).
(c) \(F(5) - F(2) = \frac{5}{6} - \frac{2}{6} =\) \(\frac{1}{2}\).

Key insight: \(P(a < X \le b) = F(b) - F(a)\) holds for every random variable. For a discrete one, you must decide carefully whether the endpoints are included.

tests: 3.1.2, 3.1.3, 3.1.4, 3.1.5 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP3a.6

From a CDF back to the PMF

The number of bugs \(X\) found in a code review has CDF:

\(F(x) = 0\) for \(x < 0\); 0.2 for \(0 \le x < 1\); 0.5 for \(1 \le x < 2\); 0.8 for \(2 \le x < 3\); 1 for \(x \ge 3\).

(a) Recover the pmf.
(b) Find \(E[X]\).
(c) Find \(P(X > 2)\), \(P(X \ge 2)\) and \(P(1 \le X < 3)\).

The height of each jump in \(F\) is the probability at that point: \(p(a) = F(a) - F(a^-)\).

(a) \(p(0) = 0.2\), \(p(1) = 0.3\), \(p(2) = 0.3\), \(p(3) = 0.2\).
(b) \(E[X] = 0.3 + 0.6 + 0.6 =\) 1.5.
(c) The three probabilities are:

\[ P(X > 2) = 1 - F(2) = \mathbf{0.2}, \quad P(X \ge 2) = 1 - F(1) = \mathbf{0.5}, \quad P(1 \le X < 3) = p(1) + p(2) = \mathbf{0.6} \]

Key insight: The CDF and the pmf carry the same information. You go from pmf to CDF by summing, and from CDF to pmf by taking the jump heights.

tests: 3.1.5, 3.1.2, 3.1.3 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP3a.7

Two unknowns: use both constraints

A systems manager models daily server failures \(X\) with this pmf:

\(x\) 0 1 2 3 4
\(P(X = x)\) 0.10 \(a\) 0.30 \(b\) 0.10

Long-run logs show that the mean number of failures is 2.1 per day.

(a) Find \(a\) and \(b\).
(b) Find \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\) and \(\text{SD}(X)\).
(c) Find \(P(1 < X \le 3)\).
(d) Find the probability that at least one failure occurs.

There are two unknowns, so you need two equations: \(\sum p = 1\) and \(\sum x \cdot p = 2.1\).

(a) From \(\sum p = 1\): \(a + b = 0.5\). From the mean:

\[ a + 0.6 + 3b + 0.4 = 2.1 \;\Rightarrow\; a + 3b = 1.1 \]

Solving: \(b = 0.3\) and \(a = 0.2\).
(b) \(E[X^2] = 0.2 + 1.2 + 2.7 + 1.6 = 5.7\), so

\[ \operatorname{Var} = 5.7 - 2.1^2 = \mathbf{1.29} \]

and \(\text{SD} \approx 1.136\).
(c) \(P(X = 2) + P(X = 3) =\) 0.6.
(d) \(1 - P(X = 0) =\) 0.9.

Key insight: Each piece of information you are given about a distribution, such as its total or its mean, is one equation. Count the unknowns against the equations before you start.

tests: 3.1.2, 3.1.3, 3.1.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP3a.8

Is the dice game fair?

Player A rolls two fair dice. If the roll is a double, A wins ₹30. If the sum is 7, A wins ₹12. Otherwise A pays ₹6.

(a) Let \(G\) be A's gain. Find the distribution of \(G\) and \(E[G]\). Is the game fair?
(b) Find \(\text{SD}(G)\).
(c) Keep the two prizes fixed. What loss \(L\) in the "otherwise" case would make the game fair?

There are 6 doubles and 6 outcomes with sum 7, and these sets do not overlap. The remaining 24 outcomes are losses. A game is fair when \(E[G] = 0\).

(a) \(P(G = 30) = \frac{6}{36}\), \(P(G = 12) = \frac{6}{36}\), \(P(G = -6) = \frac{24}{36}\).

\[ E[G] = \frac{180 + 72 - 144}{36} \]

\(=\) ₹3 per game. The game is not fair: it favours A.
(b) The second moment is

\[ E[G^2] = \frac{6 \cdot 900 + 6 \cdot 144 + 24 \cdot 36}{36} = 198 \]

so \(\operatorname{Var} = 198 - 9 = 189\) and \(\text{SD} \approx\) ₹13.75.
(c) Set

\[ \frac{180 + 72 - 24L}{36} = 0 \]

which gives L = ₹10.50.

Key insight: Fairness is a statement about the expected value only. The SD (₹13.75, much larger than the ₹3 edge) shows that a single game is mostly luck, and the edge only shows up over many games.

tests: 3.1.3, 3.1.4 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP3a.9

Prove \(\operatorname{Var}(aX + b) = a^2 \operatorname{Var}(X)\), then use it

(a) Starting from the definition \(\operatorname{Var}(Y) = E[(Y - E[Y])^2]\), prove \(E[aX + b] = aE[X] + b\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(aX + b) = a^2\operatorname{Var}(X)\).
(b) Also prove the shortcut \(\operatorname{Var}(X) = E[X^2] - (E[X])^2\).
(c) A city's daily temperature \(C\) (°C) has mean 20 and SD 2. Find the mean, SD and variance of \(F = 1.8C + 32\) (°F).
(d) Explain in one line why \(\operatorname{Var}(-X) = \operatorname{Var}(X)\), and why adding ₹100 to every salary does not change the salary variance.

Use linearity of expectation, \(E[\sum] = \sum E\), constants factor out. Then \((aX + b) - (a\mu + b) = a(X - \mu)\).

(a) \(E[aX + b] = \sum (ax + b)p(x) = a\sum x p(x) + b\sum p(x) = a\mu + b\). Then

\[ \operatorname{Var}(aX + b) = E[(aX + b - a\mu - b)^2] = E[a^2(X - \mu)^2] = \mathbf{a^2\operatorname{Var}(X)} \]

(b) The shortcut follows by expanding the square:

\[ E[(X - \mu)^2] = E[X^2] - 2\mu E[X] + \mu^2 = E[X^2] - \mu^2 \]

(c) \(E[F] = 1.8 \cdot 20 + 32 =\) 68 °F. \(\text{SD}(F) = 1.8 \cdot 2 =\) 3.6 °F. \(\operatorname{Var}(F) =\) 12.96 °F\(^2\).
(d) \(\operatorname{Var}(-X) = (-1)^2\operatorname{Var}(X)\). Adding a constant moves every value and the mean by the same amount, so every deviation stays the same.

Key insight: The additive part \(b\) changes location but not spread. The multiplier \(a\) scales the SD by \(\lvert a \rvert\) and the variance by \(a^2\). This is why standardisation \(Z = \frac{X - \mu}{\sigma}\) always gives mean 0 and variance 1.

tests: 3.1.3, 3.1.4 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP3a.10

Derive the mean and variance of the discrete uniform distribution

Let \(X\) be uniform on \(\{1, 2, \ldots, n\}\).

(a) Using \(\sum k = \frac{n(n+1)}{2}\) and \(\sum k^2 = \frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}\), prove \(E[X] = \frac{n+1}{2}\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(X) = \frac{n^2 - 1}{12}\).
(b) Check your result for \(n = 6\) against P3a.5.
(c) A random digit \(D\) is uniform on \(\{0, 1, \ldots, 9\}\). Find \(E[D]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(D)\) without redoing any sums.

For (c), \(D = X - 1\) where \(X\) is uniform on 1..10. Use P3a.9.

(a) \(E[X] = \frac{1}{n} \cdot \frac{n(n+1)}{2} = \frac{n+1}{2}\). \(E[X^2] = \frac{(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}\). Then

\[ \operatorname{Var} = \frac{(n+1)(2n+1)}{6} - \frac{(n+1)^2}{4} = \frac{(n+1)[2(2n+1) - 3(n+1)]}{12} = \frac{(n+1)(n-1)}{12} = \mathbf{\frac{n^2 - 1}{12}} \]

(b) \(n = 6\) gives mean 3.5 and variance \(\frac{35}{12}\). ✓
(c) With \(n = 10\): \(E[X] = 5.5\) and \(\operatorname{Var} = \frac{99}{12} = 8.25\). Since \(D = X - 1\), \(E[D] = 4.5\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(D) = 8.25\).

Key insight: Once you have derived a result in general, you can use it for every case. A shift changes the mean but not the variance.

tests: 3.1.2, 3.1.3, 3.1.4 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP3a.11

Should the company sell this extended warranty?

A company sells a 2-year laptop warranty for ₹2,500. Over the 2 years, a laptop has no fault with probability 0.85, a minor repair costing ₹2,000 with probability 0.10, and a replacement costing ₹30,000 with probability 0.05.

(a) Find the expected cost \(C\) per warranty and the expected profit per warranty.
(b) Find \(\text{SD}(C)\). Explain why one warranty is a very risky "bet" for the company.
(c) The company sells 1,000 independent warranties. Find the expected total profit and the SD of the total. (Use \(\operatorname{Var}(\sum C_i) = \sum \operatorname{Var}(C_i)\) for independent claims.)
(d) What is the break-even price? A manager says: "Most customers never claim, so we could price the warranty at ₹1,000." Use the numbers to argue against this.

The SD of a sum of \(n\) independent identical variables is \(\sqrt{n} \cdot \text{SD}\), so it grows more slowly than the mean, which grows like \(n\).

(a) \(E[C] = 0.10 \cdot 2000 + 0.05 \cdot 30000 =\) ₹1,700. Expected profit = ₹800 per warranty.
(b) The second moment and variance are:

\[ \begin{aligned} E[C^2] &= 0.10 \cdot 4 \times 10^6 + 0.05 \cdot 9 \times 10^8 = 4.54 \times 10^7 \\ \operatorname{Var} &= 4.54 \times 10^7 - 1700^2 = 4.251 \times 10^7 \end{aligned} \]

so \(\text{SD} \approx\) ₹6,520. For a single warranty the SD is about eight times the expected profit. One replacement claim wipes out the profit from many policies.
(c) Expected total profit = ₹8,00,000. SD of total cost \(= 6520 \cdot \sqrt{1000} \approx\) ₹2,06,180. The expected profit is now about 3.9 SDs above zero, so a loss is unlikely.
(d) Break-even price = ₹1,700, the expected cost. At ₹1,000 the expected profit is \(-\)₹700 per policy. "Most customers never claim" ignores the magnitude of the rare replacement claims, and the expectation weights each outcome by its size.

Key insight: Expected value sets the price, and variance measures the risk. Pooling many independent risks is what makes insurance viable, because the relative risk \(\text{SD}/\text{mean}\) falls like \(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\). This is the same reason averages are stable, which you will see again with the standard error and the CLT.

tests: 3.1.3, 3.1.4 · L5 Mastery

5.B Continuous RVs: PDF & CDF (3.1.6)

L1 · FoundationP3a.12

Densities are not probabilities

\(X\) is continuous with pdf \(f\). True or false, with a reason:

(a) \(P(X = 2) = f(2)\).
(b) \(P(1 \le X \le 3) = P(1 < X < 3)\).
(c) Since \(f\) is a probability, \(f(x) \le 1\) for every \(x\).
(d) The total area under \(f\) over its support must equal 1.
(e) If \(F\) is the CDF, then \(f(x) = F'(x)\) wherever \(F\) is differentiable.

For a continuous variable, probability is area under \(f\). A single point has zero width.

(a) False. \(P(X = 2) = 0\). \(f(2)\) is a density, which means probability per unit length.
(b) True. The endpoints carry zero probability.
(c) False. \(f\) can be larger than 1. For example, \(\text{Uniform}(0, 0.25)\) has \(f = 4\) (see P3a.18).
(d) True.
(e) True.

Key insight: A density is probability per unit of \(x\). Only an integral over an interval gives a probability.

tests: 3.1.6 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP3a.13

\(f(x) = kx^2\) on [0, 3]

Let \(f(x) = kx^2\) for \(0 \le x \le 3\), and 0 elsewhere.

(a) Find \(k\).
(b) Find \(E[X]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\).
(c) Find \(P(1 < X < 2)\).
(d) Find the CDF and the median.

\(\int_{0}^{3} x^2\,dx = 9\). The median \(m\) solves \(F(m) = 0.5\).

(a) \(9k = 1\), so \(k = \frac{1}{9}\).
(b) The first two moments are:

\[ E[X] = \int \frac{x^3}{9}\,dx = \frac{81}{36} = \mathbf{2.25}, \qquad E[X^2] = \int \frac{x^4}{9}\,dx = \frac{243}{45} = 5.4 \]

So \(\operatorname{Var} = 5.4 - 5.0625 = 0.3375\) (\(= \frac{27}{80}\)).
(c) \(\frac{2^3 - 1^3}{27} =\) \(\frac{7}{27} \approx 0.2593\).
(d) \(F(x) = \frac{x^3}{27}\) on [0, 3]. Solving \(m^3 = 13.5\) gives \(m \approx 2.381\).

Key insight: The density rises over [0, 3], so most of the mass sits near 3. The median (2.381) is above the mean (2.25). This is left skew: the long tail points toward 0.

tests: 3.1.6 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP3a.14

A linear density on [0, 2]

A delivery delay \(X\) (hours) has pdf \(f(x) = k(2x + 1)\) for \(0 \le x \le 2\), and 0 elsewhere.

(a) Find \(k\).
(b) Find \(E[X]\), \(E[X^2]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\).
(c) Find \(P(0.5 < X < 1.5)\) and \(P(X = 1)\).

\(\int_{0}^{2} (2x + 1)\,dx = \left[x^2 + x\right]_{0}^{2} = 6\).

(a) \(k = \frac{1}{6}\).
(b) The moments and variance are:

\[ \begin{aligned} E[X] &= \frac{1}{6}\int (2x^2 + x)\,dx = \frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{16}{3} + 2\right) = \mathbf{\frac{11}{9} \approx 1.222} \\ E[X^2] &= \frac{1}{6}\left(8 + \frac{8}{3}\right) = \mathbf{\frac{16}{9}} \\ \operatorname{Var} &= \frac{16}{9} - \frac{121}{81} = \mathbf{\frac{23}{81} \approx 0.284} \end{aligned} \]

(c) \(\frac{1}{6}\left[x^2 + x\right]\) from 0.5 to 1.5 \(= \frac{1}{6}(3.75 - 0.75) =\) 0.5. \(P(X = 1) = 0\).

Key insight: Every "find \(k\)" problem uses the same step: force the total area to be 1. Each moment after that is just \(\int x^r f(x)\,dx\).

tests: 3.1.6 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP3a.15

Piecewise (triangular) density — exam style

A sensor's response time \(X\) (ms) has pdf

\(f(x) = kx\) for \(0 \le x < 2\); \(f(x) = k(4 - x)\) for \(2 \le x \le 4\); \(f(x) = 0\) elsewhere.

(a) Find \(k\).
(b) Find \(E[X]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\).
(c) Find \(P(1 < X < 3)\) and \(P(X > 3.5)\).
(d) Write the CDF on each piece.

The graph is a triangle with base 4 and height \(2k\). Its symmetry gives the mean without integrating. For Var, integrate each piece separately.

(a) Area \(= \frac{1}{2} \cdot 4 \cdot 2k = 4k = 1\), so \(k = \frac{1}{4}\).
(b) By symmetry about \(x = 2\), \(E[X] = 2\). Then

\[ E[X^2] = \int_{0}^{2} \frac{x^3}{4}\,dx + \int_{2}^{4} \frac{x^2(4 - x)}{4}\,dx = 1 + \frac{11}{3} = \frac{14}{3} \]

So \(\operatorname{Var} = \frac{14}{3} - 4 = \frac{2}{3} \approx 0.667\).
(c) \(P(X \le 1) = \int_{0}^{1} \frac{x}{4}\,dx = \frac{1}{8}\). By symmetry \(P(X \ge 3) = \frac{1}{8}\), so \(P(1 < X < 3) = \frac{3}{4}\).

\[ P(X > 3.5) = \int_{3.5}^{4} \frac{4 - x}{4}\,dx = \mathbf{\frac{1}{32} \approx 0.03125} \]

(d) \(F(x) = 0\) for \(x < 0\); \(\frac{x^2}{8}\) on [0, 2); \(1 - \frac{(4 - x)^2}{8}\) on [2, 4]; 1 for \(x > 4\). Check: \(F(2) = \frac{1}{2}\) from both pieces. ✓

Key insight: With a piecewise pdf, integrate each piece over its own range, and check that the CDF pieces meet at the boundary. Using symmetry and geometry first can save a lot of algebra.

tests: 3.1.6, 3.1.5 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP3a.16

Given the CDF, find everything

The time \(X\) (min) to process a batch job has CDF \(F(x) = 0\) for \(x < 0\); \(F(x) = \frac{x^3}{8}\) for \(0 \le x \le 2\); \(F(x) = 1\) for \(x > 2\).

(a) Find the pdf.
(b) Find \(E[X]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\).
(c) Find the median.
(d) Find \(P(0.5 < X < 1.5)\).
(e) Find \(P(X > 1.5 \mid X > 1)\).

\(f = F'\). For (e), compute \(\frac{P(X > 1.5)}{P(X > 1)}\).

(a) \(f(x) = \frac{3x^2}{8}\) on [0, 2], and 0 elsewhere.
(b) \(E[X] = \int \frac{3x^3}{8}\,dx =\) 1.5. \(E[X^2] = \int \frac{3x^4}{8}\,dx = 2.4\), so \(\operatorname{Var} = 0.15\).
(c) \(\frac{m^3}{8} = 0.5\) gives \(m = 4^{1/3} \approx\) 1.587.
(d) \(\frac{1.5^3 - 0.5^3}{8} =\) 0.40625.
(e) The conditional probability is a ratio of tails:

\[ \frac{1 - 3.375/8}{1 - 1/8} = \frac{0.578125}{0.875} \approx \mathbf{0.6607} \]

Key insight: With the CDF in hand, every interval probability is a subtraction, and conditional probabilities are ratios of tail probabilities. You only need to differentiate when you want moments.

tests: 3.1.5, 3.1.6 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP3a.17

Why \(P(X = a) = 0\), and why \(f\) can exceed 1

(a) Write \(P(a \le X \le a + h) = \int_{a}^{a+h} f(x)\,dx\). Assume \(f\) is continuous at \(a\) and argue that \(P(X = a) = 0\). Then state what \(f(a)\) measures, using this integral.
(b) \(X \sim \text{Uniform}(0, 0.25)\). Write its pdf and show that it is a valid density even though \(f(x) = 4 > 1\).
(c) Give an example of a density that is unbounded (\(f \to \infty\) near a point) and still integrates to 1.

For small \(h\), \(\int_{a}^{a+h} f \approx f(a) \cdot h\). For (c), try \(f(x) = \frac{c}{\sqrt{x}}\) on \((0, 1)\).

(a) The probability of a point is squeezed to zero:

\[ 0 \le P(X = a) \le P(a \le X \le a + h) \approx f(a) \cdot h \to 0 \text{ as } h \to 0 \]

so \(P(X = a) = 0\). This also shows \(f(a) \approx \frac{P(a \le X \le a + h)}{h}\): probability per unit length.
(b) \(f(x) = 4\) on [0, 0.25]. \(f \ge 0\), and the area is \(4 \times 0.25 = 1\), so it is valid.
(c) \(f(x) = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{x}}\) on \((0, 1)\):

\[ \int_{0}^{1} \frac{dx}{2\sqrt{x}} = \left[\sqrt{x}\right]_{0}^{1} = 1 \]

yet \(f \to \infty\) as \(x \to 0^+\).

Key insight: Treat \(f\) the way you treat physical density (mass per volume), which can be large while total mass is finite. Only the area matters. This is why likelihoods for continuous data (in MLE and GMMs) can be larger than 1.

tests: 3.1.6 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP3a.18

Exponential lifetimes: derive the CDF and the memoryless property

A disk's lifetime \(X\) (years) has pdf \(f(x) = \lambda e^{-\lambda x}\) for \(x \ge 0\). (This extends the course's continuous examples.)

(a) Show that \(f\) is valid, and derive \(F(x) = 1 - e^{-\lambda x}\).
(b) Derive \(E[X] = \frac{1}{\lambda}\) (integrate by parts).
(c) Prove the memoryless property \(P(X > s + t \mid X > s) = P(X > t)\).
(d) The mean lifetime is 4 years. Find \(P(X > 6)\). Given that a disk has already lasted 3 years, find the probability that it lasts another 6 years.

\(P(X > x) = e^{-\lambda x}\). Conditional probability is \(\frac{P(X > s + t)}{P(X > s)}\).

(a) \(\int_{0}^{\infty} \lambda e^{-\lambda x}\,dx = 1\), and

\[ F(x) = \int_{0}^{x} \lambda e^{-\lambda u}\,du = 1 - e^{-\lambda x} \]

(b) Integrating by parts:

\[ \int_{0}^{\infty} x\lambda e^{-\lambda x}\,dx = \left[-xe^{-\lambda x}\right]_{0}^{\infty} + \int_{0}^{\infty} e^{-\lambda x}\,dx = \frac{1}{\lambda} \]

(c) The ratio of tails is

\[ \frac{e^{-\lambda(s+t)}}{e^{-\lambda s}} = e^{-\lambda t} = P(X > t) \]

(d) \(\lambda = 0.25\), so \(P(X > 6) = e^{-1.5} \approx\) 0.2231. By the memoryless property, the answer to the second question is also 0.2231.

Key insight: Under an exponential model, a component that has survived is as good as new, so its age carries no information. Whether this assumption is realistic should be checked. Disks that wear out are not memoryless.

tests: 3.1.5, 3.1.6, 3.1.3 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP3a.19

Which error distribution would you rather your model have?

A house-price model's error \(e\) (in ₹ lakh) has pdf \(f(e) = k(9 - e^2)\) for \(-3 \le e \le 3\). A competing model has error uniform on \([-3, 3]\).

(a) Find \(k\).
(b) For both models, compute \(E[e]\), \(\operatorname{Var}(e)\) (this is the MSE), \(P(\lvert e \rvert > 2)\), and \(E\lvert e \rvert\) (this is the MAE).
(c) Both models have the same range of errors and both are unbiased. Which would you deploy, and why? What does this say about judging a model by its worst-case error alone?

\(\int_{-3}^{3} (9 - e^2)\,de = 36\). Use symmetry: \(E\lvert e \rvert = 2\int_{0}^{3} e \cdot f(e)\,de\).

(a) \(k = \frac{1}{36}\).
(b) Model 1: \(E = 0\); \(\operatorname{Var} = \frac{9}{5} = 1.8\); \(P(\lvert e \rvert > 2) = \frac{4}{27} \approx 0.148\); \(E\lvert e \rvert = \frac{9}{8} = 1.125\).
Uniform model: \(E = 0\); \(\operatorname{Var} = 3\); \(P(\lvert e \rvert > 2) = \frac{1}{3}\); \(E\lvert e \rvert = 1.5\).
(c) Model 1 is better. It has 40% lower MSE, 25% lower MAE, and less than half as many large errors (\(\lvert e \rvert > 2\)), even though both models have the same worst case of \(\pm 3\).

Key insight: The shape of the error distribution matters, not only its range or its mean. MSE is the variance of an unbiased error. The integrals in this problem are the population versions of the MSE and MAE you will compute in forecasting (6.3).

tests: 3.1.6, 3.1.3, 3.1.4 · L5 Mastery

5.C Joint, Marginal, Conditional Distributions & Independence (3.1.7)

L1 · FoundationP3a.20

Joint-distribution concept check

True or false, with a reason:

(a) In a joint pmf table, the row sums give the marginal pmf of the row variable.
(b) If \(P(X = x, Y = y) = P(X = x)P(Y = y)\) holds for one cell, then \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent.
(c) If the support of \((X, Y)\) is the triangle \(0 < y < x < 1\), then \(X\) and \(Y\) cannot be independent.
(d) If \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent, then \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) = 0\).
(e) If \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) = 0\), then \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent.

Independence is a condition on every cell. Ask yourself what you know about \(Y\) once you know \(X = 0.9\) in (c).

(a) True. You sum the other variable out.
(b) False. The product rule must hold for all \((x, y)\).
(c) True. The support is not a rectangle, so knowing \(X\) changes the possible range of \(Y\) (\(Y < X\)). Independence would require the support to be a product set.
(d) True. \(E[XY] = E[X]E[Y]\).
(e) False. See P3a.25: covariance only measures linear association.

Key insight: Independence is a strong, all-cell condition. Zero covariance is a weaker condition. A support that is not a rectangle is a quick way to spot dependence.

tests: 3.1.7 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP3a.21

Marginals, a conditional, and an independence check

\(X = 1\) if a user is on mobile and 0 otherwise. \(Y\) = the number of pages viewed. The joint pmf is:

\(Y = 1\) \(Y = 2\) \(Y = 3\)
\(X = 0\) 0.10 0.20 0.10
\(X = 1\) 0.15 0.30 0.15

(a) Find both marginals.
(b) Find \(P(X = 1 \mid Y = 2)\).
(c) Are \(X\) and \(Y\) independent? Check every cell.

Compare each cell with (row total) \(\times\) (column total).

(a) \(P(X = 0) = 0.4\), \(P(X = 1) = 0.6\). \(P(Y = 1, 2, 3) = 0.25, 0.50, 0.25\).
(b) The conditional probability is

\[ \frac{0.30}{0.50} = \mathbf{0.6} \]

which equals \(P(X = 1)\).
(c) \(0.4 \times 0.25 = 0.10\) ✓, \(0.4 \times 0.5 = 0.20\) ✓, \(0.4 \times 0.25 = 0.10\) ✓, \(0.6 \times 0.25 = 0.15\) ✓, \(0.6 \times 0.5 = 0.30\) ✓, \(0.6 \times 0.25 = 0.15\) ✓. They are independent.

Key insight: Under independence, every row of the joint table is proportional to the \(Y\)-marginal, so conditioning on \(X\) changes nothing. The proportional rows are visible directly in the table.

tests: 3.1.7 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP3a.22

Two machines' defects — the full joint-pmf workout

\(X\) = defectives from Machine A in a shift (0 or 1). \(Y\) = defectives from Machine B (0, 1 or 2).

\(Y = 0\) \(Y = 1\) \(Y = 2\)
\(X = 0\) 0.30 0.20 0.10
\(X = 1\) 0.10 0.15 0.15

(a) Verify that this is a valid joint pmf and find the marginals.
(b) Find \(E[X]\), \(E[Y]\), \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\), \(\operatorname{Var}(Y)\) and \(E[XY]\).
(c) Find \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) = E[XY] - E[X]E[Y]\) and the correlation \(\rho\).
(d) Find the conditional distribution of \(Y\) given \(X = 1\), and also \(E[Y \mid X = 1]\) and \(E[Y \mid X = 0]\).
(e) Are \(X\) and \(Y\) independent? Give a physical interpretation.
(f) Find \(P(X + Y \ge 2)\).

\(E[XY] = \sum xy \cdot p(x, y)\). Only the cells where \(X = 1\) and \(Y \ge 1\) contribute.

(a) All entries are \(\ge 0\) and sum to 1. \(P(X = 0, 1) = 0.6, 0.4\). \(P(Y = 0, 1, 2) = 0.40, 0.35, 0.25\).
(b) \(E[X] = 0.4\). \(E[Y] = 0.85\). \(\operatorname{Var}(X) = 0.24\). \(E[Y^2] = 1.35\), so \(\operatorname{Var}(Y) = 0.6275\). \(E[XY] = 0.15 \cdot 1 + 0.15 \cdot 2 = 0.45\).
(c) The covariance and correlation are:

\[ \operatorname{Cov} = 0.45 - 0.34 = \mathbf{0.11}, \qquad \rho = \frac{0.11}{\sqrt{0.24 \cdot 0.6275}} \approx \mathbf{0.283} \]

(d) \(Y \mid X = 1\) takes the values 0, 1, 2 with probabilities 0.25, 0.375, 0.375. \(E[Y \mid X = 1] = 1.125\). \(E[Y \mid X = 0] \approx 0.667\).
(e) \(P(0, 0) = 0.30\), but \(P(X = 0)P(Y = 0) = 0.24\), so they are dependent. Defects tend to occur together, which suggests a shared cause such as a bad raw-material batch or a humid shift.
(f) \(P(0, 2) + P(1, 1) + P(1, 2) =\) 0.40.

Key insight: A conditional mean that changes with \(X\) (\(0.667 \to 1.125\)) is what dependence looks like in practice, and the positive covariance summarises it as a single number.

tests: 3.1.7, 3.1.3, 3.1.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP3a.23

Joint pdf \(f(x, y) = k(x + y)\) on the unit square

\((X, Y)\) has joint pdf \(f(x, y) = k(x + y)\) for \(0 \le x \le 1\) and \(0 \le y \le 1\).

(a) Find \(k\).
(b) Find the marginals \(f_X\) and \(f_Y\).
(c) Find \(P(X < 0.5, Y > 0.5)\) and \(P(X + Y < 1)\).
(d) Find \(E[X]\), \(E[XY]\), \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)\) and \(\rho\).
(e) Are \(X\) and \(Y\) independent?
(f) Find the conditional density \(f(y \mid x)\), and \(P(Y > 0.5 \mid X = 0.25)\).

\(f_X(x) = \int_{0}^{1} (x + y)\,dy = x + \frac{1}{2}\). For \(P(X + Y < 1)\), the inner integral is over \(y\) from 0 to \(1 - x\).

(a) \(\iint (x + y) = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2} = 1\), so \(k = 1\).
(b) \(f_X(x) = x + \frac{1}{2}\) and \(f_Y(y) = y + \frac{1}{2}\), by symmetry.
(c) The rectangle probability is

\[ \int_{0}^{0.5} \int_{0.5}^{1} (x + y)\,dy\,dx = \mathbf{\frac{1}{4}} \]

\(P(X + Y < 1) =\) \(\frac{1}{3}\).
(d) \(E[X] = \frac{7}{12}\). \(E[XY] = \frac{1}{3}\). \(\operatorname{Cov} = \frac{1}{3} - \frac{49}{144} = -\frac{1}{144}\). Then

\[ \operatorname{Var}(X) = \frac{5}{12} - \frac{49}{144} = \frac{11}{144}, \qquad \rho = \mathbf{-\frac{1}{11} \approx -0.091} \]

(e) \(\left(x + \frac{1}{2}\right)\left(y + \frac{1}{2}\right) \ne x + y\). For example, at \((0, 0)\) the product is \(\frac{1}{4}\) but \(f = 0\). So they are dependent.
(f) \(f(y \mid x) = \frac{x + y}{x + 1/2}\). At \(x = 0.25\):

\[ \int_{0.5}^{1} \frac{0.25 + y}{0.75}\,dy = \mathbf{\frac{2}{3}} \]

Key insight: \(f(x, y) = x + y\) cannot be written as \(g(x) \cdot h(y)\), so the variables are dependent, even though the dependence is weak (\(\rho \approx -0.09\)). The conditional density is the joint density divided by the marginal, which is the same rule \(P(A \mid B) = \frac{P(A \cap B)}{P(B)}\) applied to densities.

tests: 3.1.7, 3.1.6 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP3a.24

A factorisable joint density

\(f(x, y) = kxy\) for \(0 < x < 1\) and \(0 < y < 2\), and 0 elsewhere.

(a) Find \(k\).
(b) Find the marginals and show that \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent.
(c) Find \(P(X < 0.5, Y > 1)\) in two ways: using the joint pdf, and using independence.
(d) Find \(P(Y < 2X)\) and \(P(X + Y < 1)\).
(e) Find \(E[XY]\) and confirm that \(E[XY] = E[X]E[Y]\).

The support is a rectangle and the formula factorises, so independence follows. For (d), you still need double integrals, because the regions are not rectangles.

(a) \(k \cdot \frac{1}{2} \cdot 2 = 1\), so \(k = 1\).
(b) \(f_X(x) = 2x\) on \((0, 1)\) and \(f_Y(y) = \frac{y}{2}\) on \((0, 2)\). Their product is \(xy = f\), so \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent.
(c) Joint: \(\int_{0}^{0.5}\int_{1}^{2} xy\,dy\,dx = \frac{3}{16}\). Independence:

\[ P(X < 0.5) \cdot P(Y > 1) = \frac{1}{4} \cdot \frac{3}{4} = \mathbf{\frac{3}{16}} \]

(d) The region needs the joint integral:

\[ P(Y < 2X) = \int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{2x} xy\,dy\,dx = \int 2x^3\,dx = \mathbf{\frac{1}{2}} \]

\(P(X + Y < 1) =\) \(\frac{1}{24} \approx 0.0417\).
(e) \(E[X] = \frac{2}{3}\) and \(E[Y] = \frac{4}{3}\), so \(E[XY] = \frac{8}{9}\). ✓

Key insight: Independence lets you split rectangular events into products. Events that tie the variables together, such as \(Y < 2X\), still need the joint integral, even when \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent.

tests: 3.1.7 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP3a.25

Uncorrelated but completely dependent; when does variance add?

(a) Prove that \(E[X + Y] = E[X] + E[Y]\) for any \(X\) and \(Y\). Then prove \(\operatorname{Var}(X + Y) = \operatorname{Var}(X) + \operatorname{Var}(Y) + 2\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)\), and state exactly when the variances simply add.
(b) Let \(X\) be uniform on \(\{-1, 0, 1\}\) and \(Y = X^2\). Show \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) = 0\). Then show that \(X\) and \(Y\) are not independent.
(c) For the same \(X\) and \(Y\), compute \(\operatorname{Var}(X + Y)\) using the formula in (a), and check it by finding the distribution of \(X + Y\) directly.

\(E[XY] = E[X^3]\). Compare \(P(Y = 0 \mid X = 0)\) with \(P(Y = 0)\).

(a) \(E[X + Y] = \sum\sum (x + y)p(x, y) = \sum x\, p_X(x) + \sum y\, p_Y(y)\). This needs no assumption. Expanding gives

\[ E\left[\left((X - \mu_x) + (Y - \mu_y)\right)^2\right] = \operatorname{Var}X + \operatorname{Var}Y + 2\operatorname{Cov} \]

The variances add exactly when \(\operatorname{Cov} = 0\), which happens in particular when \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent.
(b) \(E[X] = 0\) and \(E[XY] = E[X^3] = 0\), so \(\operatorname{Cov} = 0\). But \(P(Y = 0 \mid X = 0) = 1\) while \(P(Y = 0) = \frac{1}{3}\), so they are dependent. \(Y\) is a deterministic function of \(X\).
(c) \(\operatorname{Var}(X) = \frac{2}{3}\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(Y) = \frac{2}{3} \cdot \frac{1}{3} = \frac{2}{9}\), so \(\operatorname{Var}(X + Y) = \frac{8}{9}\). Directly: \(X + Y\) takes the values 0, 0, 2, each with probability \(\frac{1}{3}\). The mean is \(\frac{2}{3}\), \(E[S^2] = \frac{4}{3}\), and

\[ \operatorname{Var} = \frac{4}{3} - \frac{4}{9} = \frac{8}{9} \]

✓

Key insight: Linearity of expectation always holds. Variance adds only when covariance is zero. Covariance and correlation detect only linear dependence, so a perfect U-shaped relationship can have \(r = 0\) (see 5.2).

tests: 3.1.7, 3.1.3, 3.1.4 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP3a.26

Uniform on a triangle — dependence, conditionals and \(E[Y \mid X]\)

\((X, Y)\) is uniform on the triangle \(0 < y < x < 1\), so \(f(x, y) = k\) there.

(a) Find \(k\).
(b) Find \(f_X\) and \(f_Y\), and show that \(X\) and \(Y\) are dependent.
(c) Find \(f(y \mid x)\). Name this distribution and find \(E[Y \mid X = x]\).
(d) Verify the tower property \(E[Y] = E[E[Y \mid X]]\).

The area of the triangle is \(\frac{1}{2}\). For a fixed \(x\), \(y\) ranges over \((0, x)\).

(a) \(k = 2\).
(b) \(f_X(x) = 2x\) on \((0, 1)\) and \(f_Y(y) = 2(1 - y)\) on \((0, 1)\). Their product \(4x(1 - y) \ne 2\), so they are dependent.
(c) \(f(y \mid x) = \frac{2}{2x} = \frac{1}{x}\) on \((0, x)\), which is \(\text{Uniform}(0, x)\). So \(E[Y \mid X = x] = \frac{x}{2}\).
(d) Both sides give the same value:

\[ E[Y] = \int_{0}^{1} 2y(1 - y)\,dy = \frac{1}{3}, \qquad E\left[\frac{X}{2}\right] = \frac{2/3}{2} = \frac{1}{3} \]

✓

Key insight: A joint density that is constant can still describe dependent variables, because the support is what couples them here. The conditional mean \(E[Y \mid X]\) is a function of \(X\), and averaging it over \(X\) gives back \(E[Y]\).

tests: 3.1.7, 3.1.6 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP3a.27

A confusion matrix is a joint distribution

A fraud model is evaluated on transactions. \(Y\) is the truth (1 = fraud) and \(\hat{Y}\) is the prediction (1 = flagged). The joint pmf is:

\(\hat{Y} = 1\) \(\hat{Y} = 0\)
\(Y = 1\) 0.18 0.02
\(Y = 0\) 0.08 0.72

(a) Find both marginals and interpret each in ML language.
(b) Express recall, precision and accuracy as conditional or joint probabilities and compute them.
(c) Are \(Y\) and \(\hat{Y}\) independent? What would independence mean for this model? Compute the accuracy of a "random" classifier that is independent of \(Y\) but has the same flag rate.
(d) A missed fraud costs ₹500 and a false alarm costs ₹20. Find the expected cost per transaction of this model, of "never flag", and of "flag everything". Which is cheapest? Why is accuracy the wrong metric here?

Recall = \(P(\hat{Y} = 1 \mid Y = 1)\). Precision = \(P(Y = 1 \mid \hat{Y} = 1)\). Under independence, each cell is the product of its marginals.

(a) \(P(Y = 1) = 0.20\): this is the class prior, or fraud prevalence. \(P(\hat{Y} = 1) = 0.26\): this is the model's flag rate.
(b) The three metrics are:

\[ \text{Recall} = \frac{0.18}{0.20} = \mathbf{0.90}, \quad \text{Precision} = \frac{0.18}{0.26} \approx \mathbf{0.692}, \quad \text{Accuracy} = P(Y = \hat{Y}) = 0.18 + 0.72 = \mathbf{0.90} \]

(c) \(0.20 \times 0.26 = 0.052 \ne 0.18\), so they are dependent, which is good. Independence would mean the prediction carries no information about the truth. A random classifier with a 26% flag rate would have accuracy \(0.052 + 0.80 \times 0.74 =\) 0.644.
(d) Model: \(0.02 \cdot 500 + 0.08 \cdot 20 =\) ₹11.6. Never flag: \(0.20 \cdot 500 =\) ₹100. Flag everything: \(0.80 \cdot 20 =\) ₹16. The model is cheapest. "Never flag" reaches 80% accuracy and is still the most expensive option, because accuracy treats every error as having the same cost.

Key insight: Every evaluation metric is a marginal, joint or conditional probability from this one \(2 \times 2\) joint distribution. Expected cost is \(E[g(Y, \hat{Y})]\) under that distribution, and that is the quantity a business cares about. Precision as \(P(Y = 1 \mid \hat{Y} = 1)\) is the Bayes posterior from Module 2.

tests: 3.1.7, 3.1.3 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP3a.28

The conditional mean is the best predictor

\(X\) is a study-effort level (1 = low, 2 = medium, 3 = high) and \(Y\) is an exam score. The joint pmf is:

\(Y = 40\) \(Y = 60\) \(Y = 80\)
\(X = 1\) 0.15 0.10 0.05
\(X = 2\) 0.10 0.20 0.10
\(X = 3\) 0.02 0.08 0.20

(a) Find \(E[Y \mid X = x]\) for \(x = 1, 2, 3\), and also \(E[Y]\).
(b) Predictor A ignores \(X\) and always predicts \(E[Y]\). Predictor B predicts \(E[Y \mid X]\). Compute the MSE of each. (The MSE of A is \(\operatorname{Var}(Y)\). The MSE of B is \(E[\operatorname{Var}(Y \mid X)]\).)
(c) Show numerically that \(\operatorname{Var}(Y) = E[\operatorname{Var}(Y \mid X)] + \operatorname{Var}(E[Y \mid X])\). What fraction of the variance of \(Y\) does knowing \(X\) "explains"?
(d) What is the MSE of always predicting 60? Why must it be at least \(\operatorname{Var}(Y)\)?

For any constant \(c\), \(E[(Y - c)^2] = \operatorname{Var}(Y) + (E[Y] - c)^2\).

(a) \(E[Y \mid X = 1] \approx 53.33\), \(E[Y \mid X = 2] = 60\), \(E[Y \mid X = 3] = 72\). \(E[Y] = 61.6\).
(b) \(\operatorname{Var}(Y) = 4040 - 61.6^2 =\) 245.44 (MSE of A). The conditional variances are 222.22, 200 and 149.33, so

\[ E[\operatorname{Var}(Y \mid X)] = 0.3 \cdot 222.22 + 0.4 \cdot 200 + 0.3 \cdot 149.33 = \mathbf{191.47} \]

(MSE of B).
(c) \(\operatorname{Var}(E[Y \mid X]) =\) 53.97, and

\[ 191.47 + 53.97 = 245.44 \]

✓ Knowing \(X\) explains \(\frac{53.97}{245.44} \approx\) 22% of the variance.
(d) \(245.44 + (61.6 - 60)^2 =\) 248.0. Among all constants, the mean minimises squared error, and any other constant adds the squared bias.

Key insight: \(E[Y \mid X]\) is the best possible predictor under squared loss. Regression (Module 5) tries to estimate it from data, and the "fraction explained" here is the population version of \(r^2 = \text{SSR}/\text{SST}\). The decomposition of variance in (c) is the same idea that underlies ANOVA.

tests: 3.1.7, 3.1.3, 3.1.4 · L5 Mastery

5.D Module 3a Capstone

L5 · MasteryP3a.29

Orders, returns and the profit distribution

A small online shop gets \(X\) orders per hour (0, 1 or 2) and \(Y\) returns per hour (0 or 1). A return can only happen when there is an order. The joint pmf is:

\(Y = 0\) \(Y = 1\)
\(X = 0\) \(k\) 0
\(X = 1\) \(3k\) \(k\)
\(X = 2\) \(2k\) \(2k\)

(a) Find \(k\), both marginals, and the CDF of \(X\).
(b) Find \(E[X]\), \(E[Y]\), \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\), \(\operatorname{Var}(Y)\), \(E[XY]\), \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)\) and \(\rho\).
(c) Are \(X\) and \(Y\) independent? Name the cell that settles the question immediately, and explain the business reason.
(d) Find \(P(Y = 1 \mid X = 2)\) and \(E[Y \mid X = x]\) for every \(x\). How does the return rate per order behave?
(e) Each order earns ₹50 and each return costs ₹80, so hourly profit is \(G = 50X - 80Y\). Find \(E[G]\) and \(\text{SD}(G)\) using \(\operatorname{Var}(aX + bY) = a^2\operatorname{Var}X + b^2\operatorname{Var}Y + 2ab \cdot \operatorname{Cov}\).
(f) A junior analyst ignores the covariance term. What SD do they report, and is their risk estimate too high or too low? Explain the sign.

The entries sum to \(9k\). In (f), with \(a = 50\) and \(b = -80\), the cross term is \(2(50)(-80)\operatorname{Cov}\), which is negative when \(\operatorname{Cov} > 0\).

(a) \(k = \frac{1}{9}\). \(P(X = 0, 1, 2) =\) \(\frac{1}{9}, \frac{4}{9}, \frac{4}{9}\). \(P(Y = 0, 1) =\) \(\frac{2}{3}, \frac{1}{3}\). \(F_X\): 0 for \(x < 0\); \(\frac{1}{9}\) on [0, 1); \(\frac{5}{9}\) on [1, 2); 1 for \(x \ge 2\).
(b) \(E[X] = \frac{4}{3}\). \(E[Y] = \frac{1}{3}\). \(\operatorname{Var}(X) = \frac{4}{9}\). \(\operatorname{Var}(Y) = \frac{2}{9}\). \(E[XY] = \frac{5}{9}\). \(\operatorname{Cov} = \frac{5}{9} - \frac{4}{9} = \frac{1}{9}\). \(\rho \approx 0.354\).
(c) Dependent. \(P(X = 0, Y = 1) = 0\), while \(P(X = 0)P(Y = 1) = \frac{1}{27} > 0\). With no order there can be no return.
(d) \(P(Y = 1 \mid X = 2) = \frac{1}{2}\). \(E[Y \mid X] = 0, \frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{2}\) for \(X = 0, 1, 2\). The returns per order are 0, \(\frac{1}{4}\) and \(\frac{1}{4}\), so the per-order return rate is constant at \(\frac{1}{4}\) once there is at least one order.
(e) \(E[G] = 50\left(\frac{4}{3}\right) - 80\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) =\) ₹40. Then

\[ \operatorname{Var}(G) = 2500\left(\frac{4}{9}\right) + 6400\left(\frac{2}{9}\right) - 8000\left(\frac{1}{9}\right) = \frac{14800}{9} \approx 1644.4 \]

so \(\text{SD} \approx\) ₹40.55.
(f) Without the covariance term,

\[ \operatorname{Var} = \frac{22800}{9} \approx 2533.3 \]

and \(\text{SD} \approx\) ₹50.33, which overstates the risk. More orders bring more revenue and also more returns, so the two terms partly offset each other. The cross term \(2ab \cdot \operatorname{Cov}\) is negative because \(a > 0\), \(b < 0\) and \(\operatorname{Cov} > 0\).

Key insight: This one table needs every skill in the module: validity, marginals, the CDF, moments, covariance, conditionals, independence, and the variance of a linear combination. In finance and operations, ignoring covariance gives the wrong risk estimate, sometimes too high and sometimes too low. Its sign tells you which.

tests: 3.1.2, 3.1.3, 3.1.4, 3.1.5, 3.1.7 · L5 Mastery
Module 3b: Standard Distributions, Sampling, CLT & Estimation · 44 questions

Covers tree nodes 3.2.* (CS-6) and 3.3.* (CS-7, W-3 confidence-interval part).

Critical values used throughout (from scipy.stats): \(z_{0.05} = 1.645\), \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\), \(z_{0.01} = 2.326\), \(z_{0.005} = 2.576\). t-values are stated where used.

6.A Bernoulli & Binomial (3.2.1–3.2.2)

L1 · FoundationP3b.1

Is it binomial?

For each situation, say whether \(X\) is (exactly or approximately) binomial. If it is not, name the condition that fails.

(a) \(X\) = number of heads in 10 tosses of a fair coin.
(b) A box holds 12 chips, 3 of them defective. You draw 4 chips without replacement. \(X\) = number of defectives drawn.
(c) \(X\) = number of tosses needed to get the first head.
(d) An app has 2 million users. You pick 500 at random. \(X\) = number who click a banner whose click-through rate is 3%.
(e) \(X\) = number of rainy days in a given week of the monsoon.

Check four things: \(n\) is fixed, each trial has two outcomes, \(p\) is the same on every trial, and the trials are independent.

(a) \(\text{Binomial}(10, 0.5)\).
(b) Not binomial. Drawing without replacement from a small box changes \(p\) after every draw, and the draws are dependent. \(X\) is hypergeometric.
(c) Not binomial. \(n\) is not fixed, because \(X\) itself counts the trials. \(X\) is geometric.
(d) Approximately \(\text{binomial}(500, 0.03)\). The draws are technically without replacement, but \(n/N = 0.00025\) is so small that \(p\) is effectively constant.
(e) Doubtful. Rain on consecutive days is positively correlated, so independence fails. \(p\) may also drift during the week.

Key insight: Choosing the distribution is a modelling decision. Check the four conditions before you write \(\binom{n}{x}\). "Without replacement" breaks the binomial only when the sample is a sizeable fraction of the population. This is the same 5% rule that governs the finite-population correction.

tests: 3.2.2 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP3b.2

Bernoulli basics

A spam filter flags each email independently with probability 0.3. Let \(X = 1\) if an email is flagged and \(X = 0\) otherwise.
(a) Give \(E[X]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\).
(b) For which \(p\) does a Bernoulli variable have the largest variance? What is that variance?
(c) True or false: "A Bernoulli RV is a binomial RV with \(n = 1\)."

\(\operatorname{Var} = p(1 - p)\). Treat it as a quadratic in \(p\).

(a) \(E[X] = 0.3\), \(\operatorname{Var}(X) = 0.3 \times 0.7 = 0.21\).
(b) \(p = 0.5\), maximum variance 0.25. The derivative of \(p - p^2\) is \(1 - 2p\), which is zero at \(p = 0.5\).
(c) True.

Key insight: A binary outcome is most uncertain at \(p = 0.5\). That is why sample-size formulas for proportions use \(p = 0.5\) as the worst case when \(p\) is unknown (see P3b.40).

tests: 3.2.1 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP3b.3

Direct binomial computation

A multiple-choice quiz has 8 questions with 4 options each. A student guesses every answer. Let \(X\) = number correct. Find:
(a) \(P(X = 2)\), (b) \(P(X \le 1)\), (c) \(P(X \ge 2)\), (d) mean and variance of \(X\).

Use \(p = 1/4\). For (c), take the complement of (b).

(a)

\[ \binom{8}{2} (0.25)^2 (0.75)^6 = 28 \times 0.0625 \times 0.17798 = \mathbf{0.3115} \]

(b)

\[ (0.75)^8 + 8(0.25)(0.75)^7 = 0.1001 + 0.2670 = \mathbf{0.3671} \]

(c) \(1 - 0.3671 =\) 0.6329
(d) Mean = \(np = 2\), variance = \(npq = 1.5\)

Key insight: "At least \(k\)" questions are almost always solved by complement. Summing 7 terms for (c) works, but it is slow and easy to get wrong.

tests: 3.2.2 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP3b.4

Inverse problem: recover \(n\) and \(p\)

A binomial variable has mean 6 and variance 2.4.
(a) Find \(n\) and \(p\).
(b) Find \(P(X = n)\) and \(P(X \ge 8)\).
(c) Could a binomial variable have mean 4 and variance 5? Explain.

Divide the variance by the mean to get \(q\).

(a)

\[ q = \frac{npq}{np} = \frac{2.4}{6} = 0.4 \]

so \(p = 0.6\) and \(n = 6/0.6 =\) 10.
(b) \(P(X = 10) = 0.6^{10} =\) 0.00605. \(P(X \ge 8) = P(8) + P(9) + P(10) = 0.1209 + 0.0403 + 0.0060 =\) 0.1673.
(c) No. Variance/mean = \(q\) would equal \(1.25 > 1\), which is impossible.

Key insight: For any binomial, variance < mean. A count variable with variance \(\ge\) mean is evidence against a binomial model. This is the first diagnostic for choosing a distribution.

tests: 3.2.2 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP3b.5

Acceptance sampling (exam style)

A supplier's process produces 12% defective connectors. A buyer inspects a random sample of 15 connectors from each batch. The batch is accepted if the sample contains at most 2 defectives.
(a) What is the probability that a batch is accepted?
(b) What is the expected number of defectives in a sample?
(c) Of 20 batches received in a month, how many should the buyer expect to accept?
(d) If the supplier improves to 5% defective, will the acceptance probability go up or down? Answer without computing.

The acceptance probability is \(P(X \le 2)\) with \(X \sim \text{Bin}(15, 0.12)\). The number of accepted batches is itself binomial.

(a)

\[ P(X \le 2) = 0.88^{15} + 15(0.12)(0.88)^{14} + 105(0.12)^2(0.88)^{13} = \mathbf{0.7346} \]

(b) \(np =\) 1.8
(c) \(20 \times 0.7346 \approx\) 14.7 batches (the count of accepted batches is \(\text{Bin}(20, 0.7346)\)).
(d) Up. With a smaller \(p\), the probability mass moves toward 0 defectives.

Key insight: Two binomials are nested here: defects within a batch, and accepted batches within a month. The output of one model (0.7346) becomes the \(p\) of the next.

tests: 3.2.2 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP3b.6

Minimum number of trials

A phishing-detection probe succeeds in triggering an alert with probability 0.1 per attempt, independently. What is the smallest number of attempts \(n\) such that \(P(\text{at least one alert}) \ge 0.95\)?

Write \(1 - (0.9)^n \ge 0.95\) and take logs. Watch the inequality direction, because \(\ln 0.9 < 0\).

\[ 0.9^n \le 0.05 \Rightarrow n \ge \frac{\ln 0.05}{\ln 0.9} = 28.43 \Rightarrow n = \mathbf{29} \]

Check: \(1 - 0.9^{28} = 0.9477\) (not enough), and \(1 - 0.9^{29} = 0.9529\) ✓.

Key insight: Dividing by a negative logarithm flips the inequality, and the answer must be rounded up. This is the same pattern as the CS-6 practice problem "how many times must he fire".

tests: 3.2.2 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP3b.7

Derive Bernoulli and binomial moments

(a) For \(Y \sim \text{Bernoulli}(p)\), derive \(E[Y]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(Y)\) from the definition.
(b) Write \(X \sim \text{Bin}(n, p)\) as \(X = Y_1 + \dots + Y_n\) with independent Bernoulli \(Y_i\). Derive \(E[X] = np\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(X) = npq\). State exactly where independence is used.
(c) Use the representation to find \(\operatorname{Var}(X/n)\), the variance of the sample proportion.

Expectation is linear for any random variables. Variance adds only when the covariances are zero.

(a) \(E[Y] = 0 \cdot q + 1 \cdot p =\) \(p\). \(E[Y^2] = p\) because \(Y^2 = Y\). So \(\operatorname{Var} = p - p^2 =\) \(pq\).
(b) \(E[X] = \sum E[Y_i] =\) \(np\). This uses linearity only; independence is not needed.

\[ \operatorname{Var}(X) = \sum \operatorname{Var}(Y_i) + 2\sum_{i<j} \operatorname{Cov}(Y_i, Y_j) \]

Independence makes every covariance zero, which leaves \(npq\).
(c)

\[ \operatorname{Var}(X/n) = \frac{npq}{n^2} = \mathbf{\frac{pq}{n}} \]

so \(SE(\hat{p}) = \sqrt{pq/n}\).

Key insight: The indicator-sum trick links the binomial, the CLT and the sampling distribution of \(\hat{p}\) (3.3.6). It also shows the cost of dependence: positively correlated trials, such as the Corona-in-a-room example, inflate the variance above \(npq\).

tests: 3.2.1, 3.2.2 · L4 Analysis

6.B Poisson (3.2.3)

L1 · FoundationP3b.8

Recognising a Poisson process

(a) Which are natural Poisson models? (i) the number of typos per page of a manuscript; (ii) the number of heads in 20 tosses; (iii) the number of server crashes per month; (iv) the height of a student.
(b) Over 200 days, the number of daily customer complaints has sample mean 4.1 and sample variance 9.8. Is a Poisson model plausible? Why?

A Poisson count has no fixed upper limit, counts events in a continuum of time or space, and has variance equal to its mean.

(a) (i) and (iii) are Poisson-like counts of rare events per unit. (ii) is binomial, because \(n = 20\) is a fixed cap. (iv) is continuous, so not a count at all.
(b) Not plausible. A Poisson variable has variance = mean. Here the variance is more than twice the mean (overdispersion). This usually means the rate itself varies from day to day, for example weekdays against weekends.

Key insight: Mean = variance is the fingerprint of the Poisson. Compare the two numbers before you fit a Poisson model to real counts.

tests: 3.2.3 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP3b.9

Rescaling the rate

A helpdesk receives calls at an average rate of 3 per 10 minutes, following a Poisson process. Find:
(a) \(P(\text{exactly 2 calls in 5 minutes})\);
(b) \(P(\text{no calls in 20 minutes})\);
(c) \(P(\text{at least 4 calls in 30 minutes})\).

Rescale \(\lambda\) to the interval in the question before you use the pmf.

(a) \(\lambda = 1.5\):

\[ \frac{e^{-1.5}\, 1.5^2}{2!} = \mathbf{0.2510} \]

(b) \(\lambda = 6\): \(e^{-6} =\) 0.00248
(c) \(\lambda = 9\):

\[ 1 - [P(0) + P(1) + P(2) + P(3)] = \mathbf{0.9788} \]

Key insight: \(\lambda = \text{rate} \times \text{length of interval}\). The most common Poisson error in exams is plugging in the original rate for a different window.

tests: 3.2.3 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP3b.10

Poisson approximation to the binomial

0.5% of printed circuit boards have a solder fault. A shipment contains 400 boards.
(a) Compute \(P(\text{at most 3 faulty boards})\) exactly with the binomial.
(b) Repeat with the Poisson approximation.
(c) Are the approximation conditions from the slides (\(n \ge 100\), \(np \le 10\)) satisfied?

Use \(\lambda = np\).

(a) \(\text{Binomial}(400, 0.005)\): \(P(X \le 3) = 0.8576\)
(b) \(\text{Poisson}(\lambda = 2)\):

\[ e^{-2}\left(1 + 2 + 2 + \frac{4}{3}\right) = \mathbf{0.8571} \]

(c) Yes: \(n = 400 \ge 100\) and \(np = 2 \le 10\). The error is only 0.0005.

Key insight: When \(n\) is large and \(p\) is small, the binomial depends almost entirely on the product \(np\). That is why one parameter \(\lambda\) is enough.

tests: 3.2.3 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP3b.11

Error log monitoring (exam style)

A production service logs errors at an average of 2.4 per hour (Poisson).
(a) Find \(P(\text{more than 3 errors in a given hour})\).
(b) Find \(P(\text{no error in each of three consecutive hours})\).
(c) Find \(P(\text{exactly one error in the first half-hour and exactly one in the second half-hour})\).
(d) Given that exactly 2 errors occurred in an hour, what is the probability that one fell in each half? Comment on your answer.

Disjoint intervals of a Poisson process are independent. For (d), use the definition of conditional probability with (c) as the numerator.

(a) \(1 - P(X \le 3)\) with \(\lambda = 2.4\): 0.2213
(b) \(e^{-2.4 \times 3} = e^{-7.2} =\) 0.000747 (the same as "no errors in 3 hours")
(c)

\[ \left[1.2\, e^{-1.2}\right]^2 = \mathbf{0.1306} \]

(d)

\[ \frac{0.1306}{P(X = 2 \text{ in the hour})} = \frac{0.1306}{0.2613} = \mathbf{0.5} \]

Given the total, each error independently falls in either half with probability \(\tfrac{1}{2}\). The split is \(\text{Binomial}(2, \tfrac{1}{2})\), and \(P(1) = \tfrac{1}{2}\).

Key insight: Conditioned on the total count, Poisson events are spread uniformly over the interval, and the rate \(\lambda\) cancels out. This link between the Poisson and the binomial explains why the Poisson suits "random arrivals".

tests: 3.2.3 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP3b.12

Poisson mean, variance and the limit theorem

(a) Using \(\sum_{x \ge 0} \lambda^x/x! = e^\lambda\), show \(E[X] = \lambda\) for \(X \sim \text{Poisson}(\lambda)\).
(b) Show \(E[X(X-1)] = \lambda^2\) and deduce \(\operatorname{Var}(X) = \lambda\).
(c) Let \(X_n \sim \text{Bin}(n, \lambda/n)\). Show \(P(X_n = x) \to e^{-\lambda}\lambda^x/x!\) as \(n \to \infty\) for fixed \(x\).

In (a) the \(x = 0\) term vanishes, so cancel \(x\) against \(x!\). In (c), split \(\binom{n}{x} (\lambda/n)^x(1 - \lambda/n)^{n-x}\) into three factors.

(a)

\[ E[X] = \sum_{x \ge 1} x \frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^x}{x!} = \lambda e^{-\lambda} \sum_{x \ge 1} \frac{\lambda^{x-1}}{(x-1)!} = \lambda e^{-\lambda} e^\lambda = \boldsymbol{\lambda} \]

(b)

\[ E[X(X-1)] = \lambda^2 e^{-\lambda} \sum_{x \ge 2} \frac{\lambda^{x-2}}{(x-2)!} = \lambda^2 \]

Then \(E[X^2] = \lambda^2 + \lambda\), so \(\operatorname{Var} = \lambda^2 + \lambda - \lambda^2 =\) \(\lambda\).
(c)

\[ P = \left[\frac{n(n-1)\cdots(n-x+1)}{n^x}\right] \cdot \frac{\lambda^x}{x!} \cdot \left(1 - \frac{\lambda}{n}\right)^n \cdot \left(1 - \frac{\lambda}{n}\right)^{-x} \]

The first factor \(\to 1\), \((1 - \lambda/n)^n \to e^{-\lambda}\), and the last factor \(\to 1\). The product \(\to\) \(e^{-\lambda}\lambda^x/x!\).

Key insight: The factorial-moment trick, \(E[X(X-1)]\), is the standard way to get the variance of a discrete distribution whose pmf has \(x!\) in it. The limit in (c) is the reason the rule "\(n\) large, \(p\) small" works.

tests: 3.2.3 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP3b.13

Capacity planning for an API server

An inference API receives requests as a Poisson process at 120 per minute. Each second, the server can process up to \(c\) requests; anything beyond that is queued (an "overflow second").
(a) Find the smallest \(c\) such that \(P(\text{overflow in a given second}) \le 1\%\).
(b) Traffic doubles to 240 per minute. Find the new \(c\). Did capacity have to double?
(c) For per-minute provisioning (\(\lambda = 120\)), compare the exact Poisson answer with the normal approximation \(\lambda + 2.326\sqrt{\lambda}\).
(d) Name one real-world feature of traffic that would make this Poisson analysis over-optimistic.

Per second, \(\lambda = 2\). You need \(P(X > c) \le 0.01\). Search \(c = 4, 5, 6, \dots\)

(a) \(\lambda = 2\): \(P(X > 5) = 0.0166\) and \(P(X > 6) = 0.0045 \Rightarrow\) \(c = 6\).
(b) \(\lambda = 4\): \(P(X > 8) = 0.0214\) and \(P(X > 9) = 0.0081 \Rightarrow\) \(c = 9\). Capacity rose by 50% while traffic rose by 100%, so no, it did not have to double.
(c) Exact: smallest \(c\) is 146 (\(P(X > 146) = 0.0093\)). Normal:

\[ 120 + 2.326\sqrt{120} = \mathbf{145.5 \to 146} \]

They agree.
(d) Bursty traffic, such as diurnal peaks, retries or synchronised clients. That produces overdispersion (variance > mean), so the real tail is heavier than the Poisson tail.

Key insight: The required headroom grows like \(z\sqrt{\lambda}\), which is slower than \(\lambda\). Aggregating traffic is therefore cheaper per request, an economy of scale that follows from mean = variance. The approximation behaves well for \(\lambda = 120\) but not for \(\lambda = 2\).

tests: 3.2.3, 3.2.4 · L5 Mastery

6.C Normal & Normal Approximation (3.2.4–3.2.5)

L1 · FoundationP3b.14

Normal-curve facts

True or false, with a one-line reason:
(a) For \(X \sim N(\mu, \sigma^2)\), \(P(X = \mu)\) is the largest point probability.
(b) The normal pdf can take values greater than 1.
(c) Increasing \(\sigma\) shifts the curve to the right.
(d) About 95% of values lie within \(\mu \pm 2\sigma\).
(e) A normal variable can take negative values even when \(\mu > 0\).

A density is not a probability.

(a) False. \(P(X = \text{any point}) = 0\) for a continuous RV. The density is highest at \(\mu\).
(b) True. The peak is \(\frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}}\), which exceeds 1 when \(\sigma < 0.399\).
(c) False. \(\sigma\) changes the spread. \(\mu\) changes the location.
(d) True. The exact figure is 95.45% (\(1.96\sigma\) gives exactly 95%).
(e) True. The support is \((-\infty, \infty)\). This is one reason a normal model can be poor for strictly positive data with small mean/SD.

Key insight: Separate density from probability. Probabilities come from areas, \(F(b) - F(a)\).

tests: 3.2.4, 3.1.6 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP3b.15

Z-table drill

For \(Z \sim N(0, 1)\), find (a) \(P(Z < 1.28)\), (b) \(P(-0.5 < Z < 1.5)\), (c) \(P(\lvert Z \rvert > 2.17)\).

Use symmetry: \(P(Z < -a) = 1 - \Phi(a)\).

(a) 0.8997
(b)

\[ \Phi(1.5) - \Phi(-0.5) = 0.9332 - 0.3085 = \mathbf{0.6247} \]

(c)

\[ 2[1 - \Phi(2.17)] = 2(0.0150) = \mathbf{0.0300} \]

Key insight: (c) is exactly the form of a two-tailed p-value (Module 4): \(P(\lvert Z \rvert > \lvert z_{\text{obs}} \rvert) = 2 \times \text{one tail}\).

tests: 3.2.4 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP3b.16

Reverse lookup: percentiles

An aptitude score \(X \sim N(500, 100^2)\).
(a) Find the 90th percentile.
(b) Find the score that cuts off the top 2.5%.
(c) Find the interval that contains the middle 50% of scores (Q1 and Q3), and the IQR.

\(x = \mu + z \cdot \sigma\), where \(z\) is read backwards from the table: find the area, then read off \(z\).

(a) \(z_{0.90} = 1.2816 \Rightarrow x =\) 628.2
(b) \(z = 1.96 \Rightarrow x =\) 696.0
