(c) \(z = \pm 0.6745 \Rightarrow\) (432.6, 567.4), \(\text{IQR} \approx 134.9 = 1.349\sigma\)

Key insight: The normal IQR is about \(1.35\sigma\). This gives a robust estimate \(\sigma \approx \text{IQR}/1.35\), and it explains why the \(1.5 \cdot \text{IQR}\) fence from CS-1 sits at about \(\pm 2.7\sigma\) for normal data, flagging only about 0.7% of points.

tests: 3.2.4 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP3b.17

Warranty design (exam style)

Battery life of a phone is normally distributed with mean 36 months and SD 4 months.
(a) What fraction of batteries fail before 30 months?
(b) What fraction last between 32 and 40 months?
(c) The maker will replace at most 3% of batteries under warranty. What is the longest warranty period, in whole months, it can offer?

For (c) you need the 3rd percentile. Round the warranty in the safe direction.

(a) \(z = -1.5 \Rightarrow\) 0.0668
(b) \(z \in (-1, 1) \Rightarrow\) 0.6827
(c)

\[ z_{0.03} = -1.881 \Rightarrow x = 36 - 1.881 \times 4 = 28.48 \text{ months} \Rightarrow \mathbf{28 \text{ months}} \]

Rounding up to 29 would push replacements above 3%. At 28 months, \(P(X < 28) = \Phi(-2) = 2.28\%\).

Key insight: Reverse lookups combined with rounding in the conservative direction are a classic exam trap. The same logic applies to sample-size problems (round up) and warranty problems (round down).

tests: 3.2.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP3b.18

Recover \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) from two percentiles

In a large class, exam marks are approximately normal. 10% of students scored below 40 and 5% scored above 90. Find \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\).

Write two equations, \(40 = \mu + z_{0.10}\sigma\) and \(90 = \mu + z_{0.95}\sigma\).

\(z_{0.10} = -1.2816\) and \(z_{0.95} = 1.6449\).
Subtracting:

\[ 50 = 2.9264\sigma \Rightarrow \boldsymbol{\sigma = 17.09} \]

Then

\[ \mu = 40 + 1.2816 \times 17.09 = \mathbf{61.90} \]

Key insight: Each percentile gives one linear equation in \((\mu, \sigma)\), so two percentiles pin down the distribution. This is how analysts back out a distribution from a summary report.

tests: 3.2.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP3b.19

Normal approximation with continuity correction

30% of a bank's customers use the mobile app weekly. In a random sample of 200 customers, find \(P(55 \le X \le 70)\)
(a) with the normal approximation and continuity correction, after checking the slide condition \(np, nq \ge 15\);
(b) exactly, with the binomial. Comment.

The integer range 55 to 70 inclusive becomes \(54.5 < X < 70.5\) on the continuous scale.

\(np = 60\), \(nq = 140\), both \(\ge 15\) ✓. \(\mu = 60\), \(\sigma = \sqrt{42} = 6.481\).
(a)

\[ z_1 = \frac{54.5 - 60}{6.481} = -0.849 \quad \text{and} \quad z_2 = \frac{70.5 - 60}{6.481} = 1.620 \Rightarrow \mathbf{0.7494} \]

(b) Exact binomial: 0.7469. The difference is about 0.0025.
Without the correction you would use (55, 70) and get \(z \in (-0.77, 1.54)\), roughly 0.72. That is noticeably worse.

Key insight: Each integer \(k\) owns the interval \((k - 0.5, k + 0.5)\). Decide which integers are included first, then convert to the continuous boundaries: \(\le k \mapsto k + 0.5\), \(< k \mapsto k - 0.5\), \(\ge k \mapsto k - 0.5\), \(> k \mapsto k + 0.5\).

tests: 3.2.5 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP3b.20

Linear combinations of normals (tolerance stacking)

(a) Show that if \(X \sim N(\mu, \sigma^2)\) then \(Z = (X - \mu)/\sigma\) has mean 0 and variance 1.
(b) A shaft is made of two independent parts with lengths \(A \sim N(10, 0.02^2)\) and \(B \sim N(15, 0.03^2)\) cm. The assembly \(A + B\) must be \(25 \pm 0.08\) cm. What fraction of assemblies meet the spec?
(c) A designer says "each part is within \(\pm 3\sigma\) of its target, so the assembly is within \(\pm(0.06 + 0.09) = \pm 0.15\)." Why is this too pessimistic?

For independent parts, variances add. SDs do not.

(a) \(E[Z] = (E[X] - \mu)/\sigma = 0\). \(\operatorname{Var}(Z) = \operatorname{Var}(X)/\sigma^2 = 1\), using \(\operatorname{Var}(aX + b) = a^2\operatorname{Var}(X)\).
(b) \(A + B \sim N(25, 0.02^2 + 0.03^2)\), so \(\sigma = \sqrt{0.0013} =\) 0.03606.

\[ P\left(\lvert Z \rvert < \frac{0.08}{0.03606} = 2.219\right) = \mathbf{0.9735} \]

(c) Adding SDs assumes both parts are at their extremes together. Under independence,

\[ SD(A + B) = \sqrt{\sigma_A^2 + \sigma_B^2} = 0.036 \]

which is less than \(0.05 = \sigma_A + \sigma_B\). The \(\pm 3\sigma\) assembly band is \(\pm 0.108\), not \(\pm 0.15\).

Key insight: Variances add for independent variables. This is the same fact that later gives \(\operatorname{Var}(\bar{x}) = \sigma^2/n\) and the \(\sqrt{\sigma_1^2/n_1 + \sigma_2^2/n_2}\) in two-sample tests.

tests: 3.2.4, 3.1.4 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP3b.21

When the normal approximation fails (rare events)

A fraud model raises an alert on a legitimate transaction with probability 0.001. In a batch of 1000 legitimate transactions, an analyst wants \(P(X \ge 3 \text{ false alerts})\).
(a) Compute the exact binomial answer.
(b) Compute the Poisson approximation.
(c) Compute the normal approximation with continuity correction.
(d) Which approximation should be trusted, and what rule would have warned the analyst?

\(np = 1\). Think about the shape of the distribution when its mean is 1 and it cannot go below 0.

(a) Exact: 0.0802
(b) Poisson(1): 0.0803
(c) Normal: \(\mu = 1\), \(\sigma = 0.9995\).

\[ P(X > 2.5) = P(Z > 1.50) = \mathbf{0.0667} \]

which understates the risk by about 17%.
(d) Trust the Poisson. \(np = 1\) is far below 15, and the distribution is strongly right-skewed and piled up against 0. A symmetric normal cannot represent that.

Key insight: In ML monitoring, rare events (fraud, false alarms, failures) are where the normal approximation breaks down. Use the Poisson or the exact binomial when \(np\) is small, whatever \(n\) is.

tests: 3.2.5, 3.2.3 · L5 Mastery

6.D χ², t, F (3.2.6)

L1 · FoundationP3b.22

Which distribution is which?

Match each description with \(\chi^2\), \(t\) or \(F\):
(a) Ratio of two independent chi-squares, each divided by its degrees of freedom.
(b) Symmetric and bell-shaped, but with heavier tails than \(N(0,1)\).
(c) Sum of \(k\) squared independent standard normals.
(d) Used to compare two population variances.
(e) Used for a mean when \(\sigma\) is unknown and \(n\) is small.
(f) Defined only for \(x \ge 0\) and right-skewed (two answers).

Remember the constructions: \(\chi^2 = \sum Z^2\), \(t = Z/\sqrt{\chi^2/k}\), \(F = (\chi^2/\nu_1)/(\chi^2/\nu_2)\).

(a) \(F\) (b) \(t\) (c) \(\chi^2\) (d) \(F\) (e) \(t\) (f) \(\chi^2\) and \(F\)

Key insight: All three distributions are built from the normal. That is why tests based on them need the normal-population assumption.

tests: 3.2.6 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP3b.23

Table lookups, including the lower F tail

Find:
(a) \(\chi^2_{0.05,\,10}\), the upper 5% point with 10 df;
(b) \(t_{0.025,\,9}\);
(c) \(F_{0.05}(4, 10)\), the upper 5% point with \(\nu_1 = 4\) and \(\nu_2 = 10\);
(d) the lower 5% point of \(F(4, 10)\), using only upper-tail tables.

\(F_{\text{lower},\alpha}(\nu_1, \nu_2) = 1 / F_{\text{upper},\alpha}(\nu_2, \nu_1)\). Note the swap of the degrees of freedom.

(a) 18.307 (b) 2.262 (c) 3.478
(d)

\[ F_{0.05}(10, 4) = 5.964 \Rightarrow \text{lower point} = \frac{1}{5.964} = \mathbf{0.1677} \]

Key insight: If \(W \sim F(\nu_1, \nu_2)\) then \(1/W \sim F(\nu_2, \nu_1)\), so the order of the df matters. The course convention of putting the larger \(s^2\) in the numerator exists so that only upper tables are needed.

tests: 3.2.6 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP3b.24

Sampling distribution of \(s^2\)

The fill volume of a bottling line is \(N(\mu, \sigma^2 = 4 \text{ ml}^2)\). A sample of \(n = 10\) bottles is taken.
(a) Using \((n - 1)s^2/\sigma^2 \sim \chi^2(n-1)\), find \(P(s^2 > 7.2)\).
(b) Find the range of \(s^2\) values that would occur in the middle 90% of samples.
(c) In one sample, \(s^2 = 8.0\). What does this suggest to a quality engineer?

Convert \(s^2\) to the \(\chi^2\) scale: multiply by \(9/4\).

(a)

\[ \frac{9 \times 7.2}{4} = 16.2 \Rightarrow P(\chi^2_9 > 16.2) = \mathbf{0.0628} \]

(b) \(\chi^2_9\) has 5% and 95% points 3.325 and 16.919

\[ \Rightarrow s^2 \in \left(\frac{4 \times 3.325}{9}, \frac{4 \times 16.919}{9}\right) = \mathbf{(1.478, 7.520)} \]

(c) 8.0 lies above the 95th percentile of \(s^2\) values under \(\sigma^2 = 4\). This is evidence that the process variance has increased (a one-sample variance test).

Key insight: \(s^2\) is a random variable with its own distribution, \(\chi^2\) scaled by \(\sigma^2/(n-1)\). The \(\chi^2\) distribution is skewed, so the middle-90% band is not symmetric around \(\sigma^2 = 4\).

tests: 3.2.6, 3.3.4 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP3b.25

Why t has heavy tails, and how the distributions connect

(a) Compute \(P(\lvert T \rvert > 2)\) for \(t\) with 3, 10 and 30 df, and compare it with \(P(\lvert Z \rvert > 2)\).
(b) Explain intuitively why replacing \(\sigma\) by \(s\) makes the tails heavier, and why the effect fades as \(n\) grows.
(c) Verify numerically that \(t^2_{0.025,\,10} = F_{0.05}(1, 10)\) and that \(z^2_{0.025} = \chi^2_{0.05,\,1}\). State the general identities.

The denominator \(s\) is itself random. Sometimes it comes out small, and then the ratio blows up.

(a) df 3: 0.139; df 10: 0.073; df 30: 0.055; Normal: 0.0455. The matching critical values \(t_{0.025}\) are 3.182, 2.228 and 2.042, against 1.96.
(b)

\[ T = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu}{s/\sqrt{n}} \]

When a small sample happens to give an \(s\) much smaller than \(\sigma\), \(\lvert T \rvert\) becomes large. That adds tail mass beyond what \(Z\) would have. As \(n\) grows, \(s \to \sigma\) (consistency), the extra randomness disappears, and \(t(k) \to N(0, 1)\).
(c)

\[ 2.2281^2 = \mathbf{4.9646} = F_{0.05}(1, 10). \qquad 1.96^2 = \mathbf{3.8416} \approx \chi^2_{0.05,\,1} = 3.8415. \]

In general \(t^2(k) = F(1, k)\) and \(Z^2 = \chi^2(1)\).

Key insight: Using \(z\) when \(\sigma\) is unknown and \(n\) is small makes the CI too narrow and inflates the Type I error. These identities also explain why a two-sided t-test and a one-way ANOVA with 2 groups give identical p-values (\(F = t^2\)).

tests: 3.2.6 · L4 Analysis

6.E Sampling & CLT (3.3.1–3.3.6)

L1 · FoundationP3b.26

Vocabulary and sampling methods

(a) Classify each as a parameter or a statistic: (i) the mean income of all 4 million taxpayers; (ii) the proportion defective in today's 50 inspected units; (iii) \(\rho\) between ad spend and sales for all stores; (iv) \(s^2\) of 30 response times.
(b) Name the sampling method: (i) every 25th invoice after a random start; (ii) interview whoever walks into the mall; (iii) split users into free, basic and premium tiers and take an SRS from each; (iv) ask survey respondents to forward the survey to friends; (v) randomly pick 5 states, then 3 districts in each, then 100 households in each district.
(c) True or false: "Increasing the sample size removes bias."

Parameter goes with population; statistic goes with sample. Observed = Truth + Bias + Random error.

(a) (i) parameter \(\mu\); (ii) statistic \(\hat{p}\); (iii) parameter \(\rho\); (iv) statistic \(s^2\).
(b) (i) systematic; (ii) convenience; (iii) stratified; (iv) snowball; (v) multistage.
(c) False. A larger \(n\) shrinks the random error (sampling variation, which falls like \(1/\sqrt{n}\)). Bias from a bad design, such as convenience sampling, stays however large \(n\) is.

Key insight: A big but biased sample can be precisely wrong. A good design fixes bias; a larger \(n\) fixes variance.

tests: 3.3.1, 3.3.2, 3.3.3 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP3b.27

Stratified and systematic selection

(a) A university has 4000 undergraduates, 2500 postgraduates and 1500 PhD/staff. Allocate a stratified sample of 400 with proportional allocation.
(b) From a list of \(N = 1200\) accounts, a systematic sample of \(n = 60\) is needed. Find \(k\) and list the first four selected positions if the random start is 7.
(c) Give one danger of systematic sampling.

\(n_h = n \cdot N_h/N\), and \(k = N/n\).

(a) \(N = 8000 \Rightarrow\) 200, 125, 75
(b) \(k = 20\) \(\Rightarrow\) positions 7, 27, 47, 67, …
(c) If the list has a periodic pattern with period related to \(k\), the sample can be badly biased. For example, if accounts were entered so that every 20th is a corporate account, every sampled unit could be corporate.

Key insight: Proportional allocation reproduces the population's mix exactly, which removes the variation that would come from random imbalance between strata.

tests: 3.3.2 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP3b.28

Standard error and a sample-mean probability

The time to load a dashboard has \(\mu = 50\) ms and \(\sigma = 12\) ms. For a random sample of 36 loads:
(a) state the mean and standard error of \(\bar{x}\);
(b) find \(P(\bar{x} > 53)\);
(c) find \(P(\text{a single load} > 53)\), and explain why it differs.

\(SE = \sigma/\sqrt{n}\).

(a) \(E[\bar{x}] = 50\), \(SE = 12/6 = 2\)
(b) \(z = 1.5 \Rightarrow\) 0.0668
(c) \(z = 3/12 = 0.25 \Rightarrow P =\) 0.4013 (assuming normality). Averages vary far less than single observations, so 53 is unusual for an average but ordinary for one load.

Key insight: Always ask whether the question is about one observation (use \(\sigma\)) or about an average (use \(\sigma/\sqrt{n}\)). This decides the answer.

tests: 3.3.4 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP3b.29

Finite population correction (exam style)

A company's 500 data-entry operators have mean accuracy 72% with \(\sigma = 10\) percentage points. A random sample of 60 operators is audited without replacement.
(a) Is the FPC needed by the 5% rule?
(b) Find \(P(\bar{x} < 70)\) with and without the FPC.
(c) What does the FPC equal when \(n = N\), and why does that make sense?

\(\text{FPC} = \sqrt{\frac{N - n}{N - 1}}\), and \(SE_{\text{adj}} = (\sigma/\sqrt{n}) \cdot \text{FPC}\).

(a) \(n/N = 0.12 > 0.05 \Rightarrow\) yes.
(b)

\[ SE = \frac{10}{\sqrt{60}} = 1.291. \qquad \text{FPC} = \sqrt{\frac{440}{499}} = 0.9390, \text{ so } SE_{\text{adj}} = 1.212. \]

Without FPC: \(z = -1.549 \Rightarrow\) 0.0607. With FPC: \(z = -1.650 \Rightarrow\) 0.0495.
(c) FPC = 0. If you sample the entire population, \(\bar{x} = \mu\) exactly, so there is no sampling variability.

Key insight: Sampling a large fraction of a finite population gives more information than the infinite-population formula credits, so the SE shrinks. Ignoring the FPC here overstates the tail probability by about 20%.

tests: 3.3.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP3b.30

Sampling distribution of a proportion

40% of an e-commerce site's visitors are on iOS. For a random sample of 150 visitors:
(a) verify that the normal approximation is valid (slide rule \(nP, nQ > 15\));
(b) find \(P(\hat{p} > 0.45)\);
(c) find the sample size at which \(P(\hat{p} > 0.45)\) drops to about 0.0228 (that is, \(z = 2\)).

\(SE(\hat{p}) = \sqrt{PQ/n}\). For (c), solve \(0.05/\sqrt{0.24/n} = 2\).

(a) \(nP = 60\) and \(nQ = 90\) ✓
(b)

\[ SE = \sqrt{\frac{0.24}{150}} = 0.04, \quad z = 1.25 \Rightarrow \mathbf{0.1056} \]

(c)

\[ \sqrt{\frac{0.24}{n}} = 0.025 \Rightarrow n = \frac{0.24}{0.000625} = \mathbf{384} \]

Key insight: A proportion is a mean of 0/1 values (P3b.7), so the CLT applies to it directly. Making the same deviation 1.6 times as many SEs needed \(n\) to grow 2.56 times (\(150 \to 384\)).

tests: 3.3.6 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP3b.31

CLT on a skewed population

Service times at a bank counter are exponential with mean 4 minutes (so \(\sigma = 4\) minutes, strongly right-skewed). One teller serves 64 customers in a shift.
(a) Find the approximate probability that the total service time exceeds 280 minutes.
(b) Why is it acceptable to use the normal here even though service times are far from normal?
(c) Would you use the same method to find the probability that a single customer takes more than 10 minutes? What would you use?

The total has mean \(n\mu\) and SD \(\sigma\sqrt{n}\).

(a)

\[ \text{Mean} = 256, \quad SD = 4\sqrt{64} = 32, \quad z = 0.75 \Rightarrow \mathbf{0.2266} \]

(b) By the CLT, sums and averages of \(n = 64\) independent values are close to normal whatever the population shape, given finite variance.
(c) No. The CLT says nothing about individual observations. For one customer, use the exponential directly: \(P(X > 10) = e^{-10/4} =\) 0.0821.

Key insight: The CLT changes the distribution of the mean, not the distribution of the data. A very common error is to "normalise" single observations by appealing to the CLT.

tests: 3.3.5 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP3b.32

Derive \(E[\bar{x}]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(\bar{x})\), and explain the FPC

Let \(X_1, \dots, X_n\) be iid with mean \(\mu\) and variance \(\sigma^2\).
(a) Prove \(E[\bar{x}] = \mu\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(\bar{x}) = \sigma^2/n\).
(b) Which step fails when sampling without replacement from a finite population, and in which direction?
(c) Use (a) to explain why "you need 4 times as much data to halve the standard error".

\(\operatorname{Var}(\sum X_i) = \sum \operatorname{Var}(X_i) + 2\sum_{i<j}\operatorname{Cov}(X_i, X_j)\).

(a) \(E[\bar{x}] = (1/n)\sum E[X_i] = \mu\).

\[ \operatorname{Var}(\bar{x}) = \frac{1}{n^2}\operatorname{Var}\left(\sum X_i\right) = \frac{1}{n^2}(n\sigma^2) = \mathbf{\frac{\sigma^2}{n}} \]

because the covariances are 0 under independence.
(b) Without replacement, the draws are slightly negatively correlated: \(\operatorname{Cov}(X_i, X_j) = -\sigma^2/(N - 1)\), because drawing a large value leaves smaller ones behind. The covariance terms reduce the variance to

\[ \frac{\sigma^2}{n} \cdot \frac{N - n}{N - 1} \]

which is exactly the FPC squared.
(c) \(SE = \sigma/\sqrt{n}\), so \(SE(4n) = \sigma/(2\sqrt{n}) = SE(n)/2\).

Key insight: The \(1/\sqrt{n}\) law, the FPC and the binomial variance \(npq\) all come from the same identity about the variance of a sum.

tests: 3.3.4, 3.3.5 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP3b.33

CLT thought experiment with skewed data

Insurance claim sizes are heavily right-skewed (lognormal: most claims are small, with a few huge ones). Imagine simulating 10,000 samples for each of \(n = 5\) and \(n = 50\), and plotting a histogram of the sample means for each \(n\).
(a) Describe how the two histograms differ in centre, spread and shape.
(b) For \(n = 5\), a student builds a "95% z-interval" for the mean. Will its true coverage be above, at or below 95%, and which tail causes the problem?
(c) Does the CLT tell you how large \(n\) must be? What property of the population controls it?
(d) The dataset has 10 million claims. A colleague says "\(n\) is huge, so the claims are now normally distributed." Correct the statement.

\[ \text{Skewness of } \bar{x} \approx \frac{\text{skewness of the population}}{\sqrt{n}} \]

(a) Both histograms are centred at \(\mu\). The spread at \(n = 50\) is \(\sqrt{10} \approx 3.2\) times smaller. The \(n = 5\) histogram is still clearly right-skewed; the \(n = 50\) histogram is much more symmetric but may still have a slight right tail.
(b) Below 95%. For small \(n\), the occasional huge claim drives both \(\bar{x}\) and \(s\) up, and the skew makes the interval miss more often on one side (it tends to fall short of the true mean). Coverage falls below nominal.
(c) No. The CLT is a limit theorem. The required \(n\) grows with the population's skewness and tail heaviness. Rules such as \(n \ge 30\) are heuristics that work for mild skew.
(d) The claims stay lognormal. Only the sampling distribution of the mean (or of a sum) becomes approximately normal.

Key insight: The CLT is about statistics, not data, and its speed depends on how skewed the population is. In heavy-tailed domains (insurance, revenue per user, latency), check coverage by simulation or the bootstrap rather than relying on \(n \ge 30\).

tests: 3.3.5 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP3b.34

Designing a survey for a heterogeneous user base

A streaming platform wants to estimate mean monthly watch-hours. It has three user segments:

Segment Size \(N_h\) SD of watch-hours \(S_h\)
Casual 6000 10
Regular 3000 20
Power 1000 60

The budget is \(n = 500\) interviews.
(a) Why is SRS a poor choice here?
(b) Give the proportional allocation.
(c) The optimal (Neyman) allocation is \(n_h \propto N_h S_h\). Compute it and explain why it differs.
(d) How is the overall estimate formed from the stratum means?

Where is most of the variability? Put sampling effort there.

(a) An SRS might, by chance, under-represent the small but highly variable Power segment, which makes the estimate unstable. The strata differ systematically, so stratifying removes the between-strata variation.
(b) 300, 150, 50
(c) \(N_h S_h = 60000, 60000, 60000\), which are equal \(\Rightarrow\) \(\approx 167, 167, 166\). The Power segment is only 10% of users but contributes a third of the total variability, so it deserves more interviews than its share.
(d) Weighted average:

\[ \bar{x}_{\text{st}} = \sum \frac{N_h}{N}\bar{x}_h = 0.6\bar{x}_1 + 0.3\bar{x}_2 + 0.1\bar{x}_3 \]

Weight by population share, not by sample share. Otherwise oversampling Power users biases the estimate.

Key insight: Stratify on what drives variability, allocate sample where the variance is, and reweight by population share. The same idea underlies stratified train/test splits and importance weighting in ML.

tests: 3.3.2, 3.3.3 · L5 Mastery

6.F Point & Interval Estimation (3.3.7–3.3.8)

L1 · FoundationP3b.35

What does "95% confidence" mean?

A 95% CI for mean session length is (12.1, 14.3) minutes. True or false:
(a) There is a 95% probability that \(\mu\) lies in (12.1, 14.3).
(b) If we repeated the sampling many times, about 95% of the intervals built this way would contain \(\mu\).
(c) 95% of users have session lengths between 12.1 and 14.3 minutes.
(d) A 99% CI from the same data would be wider.
(e) If \(n\) were quadrupled (with similar \(s\)), the interval would be roughly half as wide.

\(\mu\) is a fixed number. The interval is what is random.

(a) False in the frequentist sense. Once computed, the interval either contains \(\mu\) or does not.
(b) True. This is the definition.
(c) False. The CI is about the mean, not about individuals; a range for individuals is a prediction interval, which is much wider.
(d) True. \(z\) or \(t\) gets larger.
(e) True, since width \(\propto 1/\sqrt{n}\).

Key insight: The "95%" describes the long-run success rate of the procedure. Confusing a CI for the mean with a range for individual data points is the most common misreading in industry reports.

tests: 3.3.8 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP3b.36

Properties of good estimators

Match each property (unbiased, consistent, efficient, sufficient) to its description:
(a) Its sampling distribution concentrates on \(\theta\) as \(n \to \infty\).
(b) \(E[\hat{\theta}] = \theta\).
(c) It has the smallest variance among unbiased estimators.
(d) It uses all the information the sample holds about \(\theta\).
Then say which property \(s^2 = SS/(n - 1)\) has that \(SS/n\) lacks, and which property they share.

Remember the \(n - 1\) discussion in CS-1.

(a) consistent (b) unbiased (c) efficient (d) sufficient.
\(s^2\) is unbiased and \(SS/n\) is not, since \(E[SS/n] = (n-1)\sigma^2/n\). Both are consistent, because the bias factor \((n-1)/n \to 1\).

Key insight: Unbiasedness is a finite-sample property; consistency is asymptotic. The MLE \(\hat{\sigma}^2 = SS/n\) (Module 4) is biased but consistent, which is why both conventions appear.

tests: 3.3.7 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP3b.37

z-interval for a mean (\(\sigma\) known)

A sensor's historical SD is \(\sigma = 7\) units. A sample of 49 readings has \(\bar{x} = 82.4\). Construct (a) a 95% CI and (b) a 99% CI for \(\mu\). (c) What is the margin of error in (a)?

\(SE = 7/\sqrt{49} = 1\).

(a)

\[ 82.4 \pm 1.96(1) = \mathbf{(80.44, 84.36)} \]

(b)

\[ 82.4 \pm 2.576(1) = \mathbf{(79.82, 84.98)} \]

(c) \(E = 1.96\)

Key insight: More confidence costs width. Going from 95% to 99% widens the interval by \(2.576/1.96 \approx 31\%\) at the same \(n\).

tests: 3.3.8 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP3b.38

t-interval from raw data (\(\sigma\) unknown)

Eight measurements of a coating's thickness (\(\mu\text{m}\)): 12.1, 11.6, 12.8, 12.4, 11.9, 12.6, 12.2, 11.8. Assume normality. Construct a 95% CI for the mean thickness.

\(\sigma\) is unknown and \(n\) is small, so use \(t\) with \(n - 1 = 7\) df: \(t_{0.025,\,7} = 2.365\).

\(\bar{x} =\) 12.175, \(s =\) 0.4097, \(SE = 0.4097/\sqrt{8} = 0.1449\).

\[ \text{Margin} = 2.365 \times 0.1449 = 0.3425 \Rightarrow \mathbf{(11.83, 12.52)}\ \mu\text{m} \]

With \(z\) instead, the margin would be 0.284, too narrow by about 17%.

Key insight: The choice between \(z\) and \(t\) depends on whether \(\sigma\) is known, not on whether you would like a narrower interval. With \(n = 8\), \(t\) is mandatory.

tests: 3.3.8, 3.2.6 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP3b.39

CI for a proportion and for a difference of means (exam style)

(a) In a survey, 312 of 520 customers said they were satisfied. Construct a 95% CI for the population proportion satisfied. Can management claim "a majority is satisfied"?
(b) Two delivery routes were timed. Route A: \(n_1 = 60\), \(\bar{x}_1 = 46\) min, \(s_1 = 9\). Route B: \(n_2 = 50\), \(\bar{x}_2 = 40\) min, \(s_2 = 7\). Construct a 95% CI for \(\mu_A - \mu_B\) and interpret it.

For (a), use \(\hat{p} \pm z\sqrt{\hat{p}\hat{q}/n}\). For (b), use \((\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2) \pm z\sqrt{s_1^2/n_1 + s_2^2/n_2}\); both samples are large.

(a) \(\hat{p} = 0.60\), \(SE = 0.02148\), margin = \(0.0421 \Rightarrow\) (0.558, 0.642). The whole interval is above 0.5, so yes, the data support a majority at the 95% level.
(b) Difference = 6,

\[ SE = \sqrt{\frac{81}{60} + \frac{49}{50}} = 1.526, \quad \text{margin} = 2.99 \Rightarrow \mathbf{(3.01, 8.99)} \text{ minutes} \]

The interval excludes 0, so Route A is slower on average, by somewhere between about 3 and 9 minutes.

Key insight: A CI that excludes a benchmark value (0.5, or 0) is equivalent to rejecting \(H_0\) at the matching \(\alpha\) in a two-sided test. This CI–test duality is the bridge to Module 4.

tests: 3.3.8 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP3b.40

Sample-size planning

(a) Annual medical expenses have \(\sigma \approx\) $15 (in hundreds). How many employees are needed to estimate the mean within \(\pm 3\) at 95% confidence? Within \(\pm 1.5\)?
(b) Repeat \(\pm 3\) at 99% confidence.
(c) A pollster wants a proportion within \(\pm 3\) percentage points at 95% confidence and has no prior estimate of \(p\). What \(n\) is needed?

\(n = (z\sigma/E)^2\) and \(n = z^2pq/E^2\), with worst case \(p = 0.5\). Always round up.

(a)

\[ \left(\frac{1.96 \times 15}{3}\right)^2 = 96.04 \Rightarrow \mathbf{97}. \qquad \left(\frac{1.96 \times 15}{1.5}\right)^2 = 384.16 \Rightarrow \mathbf{385}. \]

Halving \(E\) roughly quadruples \(n\).
(b)

\[ \left(\frac{2.576 \times 15}{3}\right)^2 = 165.89 \Rightarrow \mathbf{166} \]

(c)

\[ \frac{1.96^2 \times 0.25}{0.03^2} = 1067.1 \Rightarrow \mathbf{1068} \]

Key insight: Rounding 96.04 down to 96 would miss the precision target, so always round up. The worst case \(p = 0.5\) explains why national polls of about 1000–1100 people quote "\(\pm 3\%\)".

tests: 3.3.8 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP3b.41

Derive the sample-size formula and the unbiasedness of \(s^2\)

(a) Starting from the z-interval \(\bar{x} \pm z_{\alpha/2}\sigma/\sqrt{n}\), derive \(n = (z_{\alpha/2}\sigma/E)^2\) for a target margin \(E\).
(b) Show \(E[\sum(X_i - \bar{x})^2] = (n - 1)\sigma^2\), and hence that \(s^2 = SS/(n - 1)\) is unbiased.
(c) Explain in one sentence why the "lost" degree of freedom happens.

For (b), write \(\sum(X_i - \bar{x})^2 = \sum X_i^2 - n \cdot \bar{x}^2\), then use \(E[X^2] = \sigma^2 + \mu^2\) and \(E[\bar{x}^2] = \sigma^2/n + \mu^2\).

(a)

\[ E = \frac{z\sigma}{\sqrt{n}} \Rightarrow \sqrt{n} = \frac{z\sigma}{E} \Rightarrow \mathbf{n = \left(\frac{z\sigma}{E}\right)^2} \]

(b) \(E[\sum X_i^2] = n(\sigma^2 + \mu^2)\). \(E[n \cdot \bar{x}^2] = n(\sigma^2/n + \mu^2) = \sigma^2 + n\mu^2\). Subtracting gives \((n - 1)\sigma^2\), so

\[ E\left[\frac{SS}{n-1}\right] = \sigma^2 \]

(c) The deviations are measured from \(\bar{x}\), which was fitted from the same data. That forces \(\sum(X_i - \bar{x}) = 0\), so only \(n - 1\) deviations are free, and \(SS\) around \(\bar{x}\) is systematically smaller than \(SS\) around \(\mu\).

Key insight: "Degrees of freedom = \(n\) minus the number of parameters estimated" recurs in \(t\) (\(n - 1\)), \(\chi^2\) goodness of fit (\(k - 1 - m\)), ANOVA (\(n - k\)) and regression (\(n - 2\)).

tests: 3.3.7, 3.3.8 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP3b.42

Is my model really better? A CI on test accuracy

On a held-out test set of 1000 labelled images, Model A is correct on 870 and Model B on 885.
(a) Construct 95% CIs for the true accuracy of each model.
(b) A teammate says "B is 1.5 points better, ship it." Based on (a), how convincing is this?
(c) How many test images would halve the margin of error for Model A?
(d) Both models were evaluated on the same 1000 images. Why does that make the comparison in (b) conservative, and what kind of test would use this fact?

Accuracy is a proportion. Width \(\propto 1/\sqrt{n}\).

(a) A:

\[ 0.870 \pm 1.96\sqrt{\frac{0.87 \cdot 0.13}{1000}} = 0.870 \pm 0.0208 \Rightarrow \mathbf{(0.849, 0.891)} \]

B: \(0.885 \pm 0.0198 \Rightarrow\) (0.865, 0.905).
(b) Not convincing. The intervals overlap heavily, and the 1.5-point gap is smaller than either margin of error (about 2 points). Test-set noise could produce a gap like this.
(c) Four times as many: 4000 images (margin \(\approx 0.0104\)).
(d) The two models' errors on the same images are correlated, since easy images are easy for both. A paired analysis, for example McNemar's test on the images where the models disagree, removes the shared variability and has more power than comparing two independent CIs.

Key insight: A test-set metric is a statistic with an SE. In ML evaluation, check the CI or SE before treating a leaderboard gap as real. Pair the comparison whenever the models share data, as with the paired t-test in Module 4.

tests: 3.3.6, 3.3.8 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP3b.43

A/B test sizing for conversion rates

A checkout page converts at about 5%. A redesign might convert at about 6%.
(a) How many users per arm are needed to estimate each arm's conversion rate within \(\pm 0.5\) percentage points at 95% confidence (use \(p = 0.05\))?
(b) The real goal is to estimate the difference \(p_2 - p_1\) within \(\pm 0.5\) points at 95% confidence, with equal \(n\) per arm and \(p_1 = 0.05\), \(p_2 = 0.06\). Find \(n\) per arm.
(c) What \(n\) per arm would give a \(\pm 1\) point margin for the difference? State the general principle.
(d) Why are rare-conversion experiments so data-hungry compared with, say, measuring mean time-on-page?

\(\operatorname{Var}(\hat{p}_2 - \hat{p}_1) = p_1q_1/n + p_2q_2/n\).

(a)

\[ n = \frac{1.96^2 \times 0.05 \times 0.95}{0.005^2} = 7299.04 \Rightarrow \mathbf{7300} \]

(b)

\[ n = \frac{1.96^2(0.0475 + 0.0564)}{0.005^2} = 15965.7 \Rightarrow \mathbf{15{,}966 \text{ per arm}} \]

(c) 3991.4 \(\Rightarrow\) 3992 per arm. Doubling the margin cuts \(n\) by 4 (\(n \propto 1/E^2\)).
(d) The effect (1 point) is tiny compared with the noise of a binary outcome. The relative SE of \(\hat{p}\) is \(\sqrt{q/(np)}\), which is large when \(p\) is small, so many users are needed to see a small absolute change.

Key insight: Plan sample size for the quantity you will actually report, here the difference. Its variance is the sum of the two arms' variances, so it needs about twice the \(n\) per arm of a single-rate estimate.

tests: 3.3.6, 3.3.8 · L5 Mastery

6.G Module 3b Capstone

L5 · MasteryP3b.44

Support-desk analytics, end to end

Tickets arrive at an IT support desk as a Poisson process at 6 per hour during an 8-hour shift.
(a) Find the probability that no ticket arrives in the first 20 minutes of a shift.
(b) Let \(D\) = the number of tickets in a shift. Give \(D\)'s distribution and parameter(s). Find \(P(D > 60)\) exactly, and with the normal approximation plus continuity correction.
(c) Management suspects the rate has risen. Over the last 36 shifts the average was 50.1 tickets. Assuming \(\sigma = \sqrt{48}\) for daily counts, construct a 95% CI for the true mean daily count. Is it consistent with 48?
(d) How many shifts of data would be needed to estimate the mean daily count within \(\pm 1\) ticket at 95% confidence?
(e) Give two ways the Poisson assumption could fail at a real helpdesk, and say how each would show up in the data.

Rescale \(\lambda\) for each part: 20 min \(\to\) 2; one shift \(\to\) 48. For (c), use the CLT on 36 daily counts.

(a) \(\lambda = 2 \Rightarrow e^{-2} =\) 0.1353
(b) \(D \sim \text{Poisson}(48)\) (mean = variance = 48). Exact \(P(D > 60) = P(D \ge 61) =\) 0.0395. Normal:

\[ P\left(Z > \frac{60.5 - 48}{6.928} = 1.804\right) = \mathbf{0.0356} \]

This is close, though the normal slightly understates the right tail of the skewed Poisson.
(c)

\[ SE = \frac{6.928}{6} = 1.155 \Rightarrow 50.1 \pm 1.96(1.155) = \mathbf{(47.84, 52.36)} \]

The interval contains 48, so the data do not show a rise at the 5% (two-sided) level. Note that \(z = 1.82\) is borderline; a one-sided view (\(p \approx 0.034\)) would point toward a rise. Choose the test direction before looking at the data.
(d)

\[ n = \left(\frac{1.96 \times \sqrt{48}}{1}\right)^2 = 184.4 \Rightarrow \mathbf{185 \text{ shifts}} \]

(e) (1) A time-varying rate, such as a morning spike after logins: hourly counts show a pattern rather than constant \(\lambda\), and the daily variance exceeds the mean. (2) Clustered arrivals, such as one outage producing many tickets at once: overdispersion (variance > mean) and too many "zero" and "very high" hours compared with the Poisson.

Key insight: One story runs through the whole module. Choose the model (Poisson), rescale its parameter, approximate it (normal plus continuity correction), summarise repeated samples (CLT and SE), and quantify uncertainty (CI and sample size). The answer to (c) also previews Module 4: whether the result counts as significant depends on \(\alpha\) and the tail chosen in advance.

tests: 3.2.3, 3.2.5, 3.3.4, 3.3.5, 3.3.8 · L5 Mastery
Module 4: Hypothesis Testing, MLE & ANOVA · 43 questions

Covers tree nodes 4.1–4.7 (CS-9, CS-10, W-3). Critical values below are exact values from scipy, rounded. Standard ones used throughout: \(z_{0.05} = 1.645\), \(z_{0.025} = 1.960\), \(z_{0.01} = 2.326\), \(z_{0.005} = 2.576\).

7.A Testing Framework (4.1)

L1 · FoundationP4.1

Writing H0 and H1 from the wording

For each claim, write \(H_0\) and \(H_1\), name the parameter, and state whether the test is left-, right- or two-tailed.

(a) A tyre maker claims its tyres last at least 60,000 km on average. A consumer group suspects they last less.
(b) A food app says its mean delivery time is no more than 30 minutes. A regulator wants to check.
(c) An HR team wants to know whether the proportion of employees who work remotely differs from last year's 40%.
(d) A researcher believes a new drug lowers mean systolic blood pressure compared with the standard drug (\(\mu_{\text{new}}\) vs \(\mu_{\text{std}}\)).
(e) A company claims that exactly 5% of its parts are defective.

The equality (\(=\), \(\le\) or \(\ge\)) always goes in \(H_0\). \(H_1\) holds whatever the investigator is trying to establish with evidence. The direction of \(H_1\) decides the tail.

(a) \(H_0: \mu \ge 60{,}000\) vs \(H_1: \mu < 60{,}000\). Left-tailed.
(b) \(H_0: \mu \le 30\) vs \(H_1: \mu > 30\). Right-tailed.
(c) \(H_0: P = 0.40\) vs \(H_1: P \ne 0.40\). Two-tailed.
(d) \(H_0: \mu_{\text{new}} \ge \mu_{\text{std}}\) (equivalently \(\mu_{\text{new}} - \mu_{\text{std}} \ge 0\)) vs \(H_1: \mu_{\text{new}} < \mu_{\text{std}}\). Left-tailed.
(e) \(H_0: P = 0.05\) vs \(H_1: P \ne 0.05\). Two-tailed.

In (a), (b) and (e) the claim sits in \(H_0\). We can only reject the claim; we can never prove it. In (c) and (d) the investigator's research hypothesis is \(H_1\).

Key insight: The tail is set by \(H_1\), never by the sample. Choosing the tail after looking at the data is cheating, because it silently doubles \(\alpha\).

tests: 4.1 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP4.2

True or false, with a one-line reason

(a) A p-value of 0.03 means there is a 3% chance that \(H_0\) is true.
(b) "Fail to reject \(H_0\)" means the data prove \(H_0\).
(c) \(\alpha\) is the probability of rejecting \(H_0\) when \(H_0\) is actually true.
(d) If a test rejects \(H_0\) at \(\alpha = 0.01\), it also rejects at \(\alpha = 0.05\).
(e) If a test rejects \(H_0\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\), it also rejects at \(\alpha = 0.01\).
(f) Lowering \(\alpha\) (with \(n\) fixed) lowers the probability of a Type II error.
(g) A smaller p-value means a larger effect.

The p-value is a probability about the data, computed under the assumption that \(H_0\) is true.

(a) False. \(p = P(\text{data at least this extreme} \mid H_0)\). It is not \(P(H_0 \mid \text{data})\).
(b) False. It only means the evidence was insufficient. Small samples often fail to reject even when \(H_0\) is false.
(c) True. That is the definition of the Type I error rate.
(d) True. \(p \le 0.01\) implies \(p \le 0.05\).
(e) False. For example, \(p = 0.03\) rejects at 0.05 but not at 0.01.
(f) False. Lowering \(\alpha\) shrinks the rejection region, so \(\beta\) increases. The two errors trade off.
(g) False. \(p\) depends on both the effect size and \(n\). A tiny effect with huge \(n\) gives a tiny \(p\) (see P4.14).

Key insight: The p-value measures how surprising the data are if \(H_0\) were true. It is not the size of the effect and not the probability that \(H_0\) is true.

tests: 4.1 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP4.3

Type I and Type II errors in context

A bank's fraud model tests every transaction with \(H_0\): "transaction is legitimate" against \(H_1\): "transaction is fraudulent".

(a) Describe a Type I error and a Type II error in words.
(b) Which error costs the customer inconvenience? Which costs the bank money?
(c) The bank lowers its flagging threshold so that more transactions are flagged. What happens to \(\alpha\) and to \(\beta\)?
(d) In medical screening for a serious but treatable disease (\(H_0\): healthy), which error would you guard against more, and how would you set \(\alpha\)?

Type I means rejecting a true \(H_0\), which is a false alarm. Type II means missing a true \(H_1\), which is a miss.

(a) Type I: a legitimate transaction is flagged as fraud (false positive). Type II: a fraudulent transaction passes as legitimate (false negative).
(b) A Type I error inconveniences the customer (a blocked card). A Type II error costs the bank money (the fraud loss).
(c) Flagging more transactions enlarges the rejection region, so \(\alpha\) rises and \(\beta\) falls.
(d) Guard against the Type II error (a missed disease). Use a larger \(\alpha\), or a larger \(n\), to increase power, and confirm positives with a second test.

Key insight: \(\alpha\) is a business decision about the relative costs of the two errors. It is not a law of nature. This is the same threshold trade-off as precision against recall in ML.

tests: 4.1 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP4.4

Computing p-values from test statistics

Find the p-value and make a decision in each case.

(a) Left-tailed z-test, \(z = -1.75\), \(\alpha = 0.05\).
(b) Right-tailed z-test, \(z = 2.10\), \(\alpha = 0.01\).
(c) Two-tailed z-test, \(z = -2.40\). Decide at \(\alpha = 0.05\) and at \(\alpha = 0.01\).
(d) Two-tailed one-sample t-test, \(t = 2.30\), \(n = 13\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). Also give the critical value.

Left-tailed: \(P(Z \le z)\). Right-tailed: \(P(Z \ge z)\). Two-tailed: \(2 \cdot P(Z \ge \lvert z \rvert)\). For \(t\), use \(df = n - 1\).

(a) \(p = \Phi(-1.75) = \mathbf{0.0401} < 0.05\), so reject \(H_0\).

(b) \(p = 1 - \Phi(2.10) = \mathbf{0.0179} > 0.01\), so do not reject. (It would reject at 0.05.)

(c) \(p = 2\Phi(-2.40) = \mathbf{0.0164}\). Reject at 0.05; do not reject at 0.01.

(d) \(df = 12\), so

\[ p = 2 \cdot P(T_{12} \ge 2.30) = \mathbf{0.0402} \]

\(t_{0.025,\,12} = \mathbf{2.179}\) and \(2.30 > 2.179\), so reject \(H_0\).

Key insight: For the same statistic, a two-tailed p-value is exactly twice the one-tailed value. A \(t\) p-value is larger than the \(z\) p-value for the same number (\(z = 2.30\) gives two-tailed \(p = 0.0214\)), because \(t\) has heavier tails.

tests: 4.1, 4.2, 4.3 · L2 Computation
L4 · AnalysisP4.5

Type II error, power and sample size for a z-test

A filling machine should deliver \(\mu = 50\) g. The quality team tests \(H_0: \mu = 50\) against \(H_1: \mu > 50\), with \(\sigma = 8\) g known, \(n = 36\) and \(\alpha = 0.05\).

(a) Find the rejection region in terms of \(\bar{x}\).
(b) The true mean is actually 53 g. Find \(\beta = P(\text{Type II error})\) and the power.
(c) What sample size gives power 0.90 against \(\mu = 53\)?
(d) Without computing, explain how \(\beta\) changes if the true mean were 51 instead.

Fix the cut-off \(c\) from \(H_0\). Then compute \(P(\bar{x} < c)\) using the true mean 53. For \(n\), use \(n = \left[\frac{(z_\alpha + z_\beta) \cdot \sigma}{\delta}\right]^2\).

(a) \(SE = 8/\sqrt{36} = 1.333\). Reject when

\[ \bar{x} > 50 + 1.645(1.333) = \mathbf{52.19}\ \text{g} \]

(b)

\[ \beta = P(\bar{x} < 52.19 \mid \mu = 53) = \Phi\left(\frac{52.19 - 53}{1.333}\right) = \Phi(-0.605) = \mathbf{0.2725} \]

Power = 0.7275.

(c)

\[ n = \left[\frac{(1.645 + 1.282) \cdot 8}{3}\right]^2 = 60.9 \]

so \(n = 61\) (always round up).

(d) If \(\mu = 51\), the true mean is closer to 50, so the two sampling distributions overlap more. \(\beta\) increases and power falls.

Key insight: Power depends on four things: the effect size \(\delta\), \(\sigma\), \(n\) and \(\alpha\). A study with too few observations "fails to find an effect" because it was underpowered, and that is a different thing from showing there is no effect.

tests: 4.1, 4.2 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP4.6

The multiple-testing trap and p-hacking

A data scientist tests 20 independent product features for an effect on retention, each at \(\alpha = 0.05\). In truth, none of them has any effect.

(a) What is the probability that at least one test comes out "significant"?
(b) How many false positives should the scientist expect?
(c) What per-test \(\alpha\) does the Bonferroni correction prescribe to keep the family-wise error at 0.05?
(d) A colleague tried 12 different feature subsets and outlier rules and reports only the best one, with \(p = 0.03\). Is this convincing? What threshold would Bonferroni require?
(e) Name two practices that prevent p-hacking.

\(P(\text{no false positive}) = 0.95^{20}\).

(a)

\[ 1 - 0.95^{20} = \mathbf{0.6415} \]

(b) \(20 \times 0.05 = \mathbf{1}\) false positive expected.

(c) \(\alpha^* = 0.05/20 = \mathbf{0.0025}\).

(d) Not convincing. Twelve analyses were attempted, so the threshold should be \(0.05/12 = \mathbf{0.00417}\), and 0.03 is well above it. Reporting only the best of many tries inflates the real Type I error rate.

(e) Pre-register the hypothesis and the analysis plan. Correct for multiplicity (Bonferroni or FDR). Hold out data for confirmation. Report every test that was run.

Key insight: \(\alpha\) controls the error rate of one test. When many tests are run, a "significant" result is almost guaranteed. This is also why ANOVA (7.F) is used instead of many pairwise t-tests.

tests: 4.1 · L5 Mastery

7.B Z-tests (4.2)

L2 · ComputationP4.7

One-mean z-test (large sample)

A manufacturer claims its power banks last at least 500 charge cycles on average. A sample of 40 units gives \(\bar{x} = 488\) cycles and \(s = 30\). Test the claim at \(\alpha = 0.05\), and then at \(\alpha = 0.01\).

The claim "at least 500" goes in \(H_0\), so the test is left-tailed. With \(n \ge 30\), \(s\) can replace \(\sigma\).

\(H_0: \mu \ge 500\) vs \(H_1: \mu < 500\) (left-tailed).

\[ z = \frac{488 - 500}{30/\sqrt{40}} = \frac{-12}{4.743} = \mathbf{-2.530} \]

\(p = \Phi(-2.530) = \mathbf{0.0057}\).

At \(\alpha = 0.05\) the critical value is \(-1.645\), and \(-2.530 < -1.645\), so reject. At \(\alpha = 0.01\) the critical value is \(-2.326\), and we still reject.

Conclusion: there is strong evidence that the mean life is below 500 cycles, so the claim is not supported.

Key insight: Since \(p = 0.0057\), the test rejects at every \(\alpha\) above 0.0057. Reporting \(p\) is more informative than a bare "reject".

tests: 4.2 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP4.8

One-proportion z-test

Historically, 30% of a telecom company's prepaid customers churn within a year. After a loyalty programme, 138 of a random sample of 400 customers churned. Does the churn rate now differ from 30%? Use \(\alpha = 0.05\), then \(\alpha = 0.01\).

Under \(H_0\), use \(P_0 = 0.30\) in the standard error: \(SE = \sqrt{\frac{P_0 Q_0}{n}}\).

\(H_0: P = 0.30\) vs \(H_1: P \ne 0.30\). \(\hat{p} = 138/400 = 0.345\).

\[ SE = \sqrt{\frac{0.3 \times 0.7}{400}} = 0.02291, \qquad z = \frac{0.045}{0.02291} = \mathbf{1.964} \]

Two-tailed \(p = \mathbf{0.0495}\).

At \(\alpha = 0.05\): \(1.964 > 1.960\), so reject (just barely). At \(\alpha = 0.01\): \(1.964 < 2.576\), so do not reject.

Note the direction: churn appears to have risen. The programme did not reduce churn.

Key insight: A borderline p-value such as 0.0495 is fragile. Report it honestly, think about practical meaning, and replicate. Also, the test measures whether there is a difference, not whether the change was the hoped-for one.

tests: 4.2 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP4.9

Difference of two means (large samples), with CI

Delivery times (in minutes) were recorded at two warehouses:

Warehouse \(n\) \(\bar{x}\) \(s\)
North 50 32.4 6.1
South 60 29.8 7.3

At \(\alpha = 0.05\), do the mean delivery times differ? Give the hypotheses, the statistic, the critical value, the p-value, the decision, a conclusion in context, and a 95% CI for \(\mu_N - \mu_S\).

\(SE = \sqrt{\frac{s_1^2}{n_1} + \frac{s_2^2}{n_2}}\).

\(H_0: \mu_N = \mu_S\) vs \(H_1: \mu_N \ne \mu_S\).

\[ SE = \sqrt{\frac{6.1^2}{50} + \frac{7.3^2}{60}} = \sqrt{0.7442 + 0.8882} = 1.2776 \]

\[ z = \frac{32.4 - 29.8}{1.2776} = \mathbf{2.035} \]

Critical value \(\pm 1.960\). \(p = \mathbf{0.0419}\).

Reject \(H_0\). North's mean delivery time is significantly different from South's; it is higher by about 2.6 minutes.

95% CI: \(2.6 \pm 1.96(1.2776) = \mathbf{(0.10,\ 5.10)}\) minutes. Zero lies outside the interval, which agrees with the test.

Key insight: The interval shows that the true difference could be as small as 0.1 minute, which is statistically significant but perhaps operationally trivial. A CI tells you more than a reject/retain verdict.

tests: 4.2, 3.3.8 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP4.10

Difference of two proportions (pooled)

Production line A produced 24 defectives in 300 items. Line B produced 15 defectives in 350 items. At \(\alpha = 0.05\), is line A's defect rate higher than line B's?

Under \(H_0: P_A = P_B\), both samples estimate the same \(P\). Pool them: \(\hat{p} = \frac{x_1 + x_2}{n_1 + n_2}\).

\(H_0: P_A \le P_B\) vs \(H_1: P_A > P_B\) (right-tailed).

\(\hat{p}_A = 0.0800\), \(\hat{p}_B = 0.0429\). Pooled \(\hat{p} = 39/650 = 0.060\).

\[ SE = \sqrt{0.06 \times 0.94 \times \left(\frac{1}{300} + \frac{1}{350}\right)} = 0.01869 \]

\[ z = \frac{0.0800 - 0.0429}{0.01869} = \mathbf{1.988} \]

Critical value 1.645. \(p = \mathbf{0.0234}\).

Reject \(H_0\). There is evidence at the 5% level that line A has a higher defect rate.

Key insight: The SE is pooled because \(H_0\) claims the proportions are equal. Keep this value; in P4.26 you will see that \(z^2 = 3.952\) is exactly the 2×2 chi-square statistic.

tests: 4.2 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP4.11

Testing a non-zero difference (unpooled SE)

A product team claims the new sign-up page raises the sign-up rate by more than 3 percentage points. In an experiment, 120 of 1000 visitors signed up on the old page and 190 of 1000 on the new page. Test the claim at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

\(H_0\) no longer says \(P_1 = P_2\), so pooling is not justified. Use the unpooled \(SE = \sqrt{\frac{\hat{p}_1\hat{q}_1}{n_1} + \frac{\hat{p}_2\hat{q}_2}{n_2}}\).

\(H_0: P_{\text{new}} - P_{\text{old}} \le 0.03\) vs \(H_1: P_{\text{new}} - P_{\text{old}} > 0.03\).

Observed difference \(= 0.19 - 0.12 = 0.07\).

\[ SE = \sqrt{\frac{0.19 \times 0.81}{1000} + \frac{0.12 \times 0.88}{1000}} = 0.01611 \]

\[ z = \frac{0.07 - 0.03}{0.01611} = \mathbf{2.483} \]

Critical value 1.645. \(p = \mathbf{0.0065}\).

Reject \(H_0\). The lift exceeds 3 percentage points.

Key insight: Pool only when \(H_0\) states that the proportions are equal. For any other hypothesised difference, the two proportions are not assumed equal, so they are estimated separately.

tests: 4.2 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP4.12

Duality between confidence intervals and two-tailed tests

(a) For a z-test with \(\sigma\) known, prove that the two-tailed test of \(H_0: \mu = \mu_0\) at level \(\alpha\) rejects if and only if \(\mu_0\) lies outside the \(100(1 - \alpha)\%\) CI \(\bar{x} \pm z_{\alpha/2} \cdot \sigma/\sqrt{n}\).
(b) With \(n = 64\), \(\sigma = 12\) and \(\bar{x} = 103.5\), build the 95% CI. Without computing any \(z\), decide \(H_0: \mu = 100\) and \(H_0: \mu = 105\). Then confirm by computing \(z\).
(c) What kind of interval corresponds to a one-tailed test?

Start from "do not reject \(\Leftrightarrow \frac{\lvert \bar{x} - \mu_0 \rvert}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \le z_{\alpha/2}\)" and rearrange the inequality to isolate \(\mu_0\).

(a) Do not reject

\[ \begin{aligned} &\Leftrightarrow -z_{\alpha/2} \le \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \le z_{\alpha/2} \\ &\Leftrightarrow \bar{x} - z_{\alpha/2}\sigma/\sqrt{n} \le \mu_0 \le \bar{x} + z_{\alpha/2}\sigma/\sqrt{n} \\ &\Leftrightarrow \mu_0 \in \text{CI}. \end{aligned} \]

Taking the negation, the test rejects \(\Leftrightarrow \mu_0 \notin \text{CI}\). ∎

(b) \(SE = 1.5\), so the CI is \(103.5 \pm 2.94 = \mathbf{(100.56,\ 106.44)}\). \(\mu_0 = 100\) lies outside, so reject (\(z = 2.333 > 1.96\)). \(\mu_0 = 105\) lies inside, so do not reject (\(z = -1.0\)).

(c) A one-sided confidence bound, for example \(\mu > \bar{x} - z_\alpha \cdot \sigma/\sqrt{n}\) for \(H_1: \mu > \mu_0\).

Key insight: A CI is the set of all \(\mu_0\) values the test would not reject. One interval therefore answers infinitely many tests at once.

tests: 4.1, 4.2, 3.3.8 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP4.13

A/B test on click-through rate, and planning its size

An e-commerce site shows banner A (control) and banner B (variant) to 12,000 users each. A gets 480 clicks (CTR 4.0%) and B gets 552 clicks (CTR 4.6%). The hypothesis was pre-registered as "B has a higher CTR".

(a) Run the test at \(\alpha = 0.05\): hypotheses, \(z\), \(p\), decision.
(b) What is the relative lift? Why do product teams usually report it?
(c) Planning: how many users per arm are needed to detect a rise from 4.0% to 4.4% with \(\alpha = 0.05\) (one-sided) and 80% power? Use \(n = \frac{\left[z_\alpha\sqrt{2\bar{p}\bar{q}} + z_\beta\sqrt{p_1 q_1 + p_2 q_2}\right]^2}{(p_2 - p_1)^2}\).
(d) The PM checked the dashboard every day and stopped the test on the first day \(p\) dropped below 0.05. Why is that a problem?

(a) is a pooled two-proportion z-test. In (c), \(\bar{p} = 0.042\), \(z_\alpha = 1.645\) and \(z_\beta = 0.842\).

(a) \(H_0: P_B \le P_A\) vs \(H_1: P_B > P_A\). Pooled \(\hat{p} = 1032/24000 = 0.043\).

\[ SE = \sqrt{\frac{0.043 \times 0.957 \times 2}{12000}} = 0.002619, \qquad z = \frac{0.006}{0.002619} = \mathbf{2.291} \]

\(p = \mathbf{0.0110} < 0.05\). Reject \(H_0\). B has a significantly higher CTR.

(b) The relative lift is \(0.6/4.0 = \mathbf{15\%}\). It shows business impact more clearly than "0.6 percentage points".

(c) \(n \approx \mathbf{31{,}095}\) users per arm. Small absolute differences on small base rates need very large samples.

(d) This is optional stopping, or "peeking". Each look is another chance for a false positive, so the real \(\alpha\) is far above 0.05. Fix \(n\) in advance, or use sequential-testing methods.

Key insight: An A/B test is just the two-proportion z-test from CS-9. The engineering work goes into fixing \(n\) in advance and not peeking.

tests: 4.2, 4.1 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP4.14

Statistical vs practical significance

A platform's mean session length has long been 10.00 min (\(\sigma \approx 2.0\)). After a UI tweak, a sample of 40,000 sessions has \(\bar{x} = 10.02\) min.

(a) Test \(H_0: \mu = 10\) vs \(H_1: \mu \ne 10\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\).
(b) Repeat with \(n = 400\) and the same \(\bar{x}\).
(c) Compute the effect size \(d = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma}\). Is the change practically meaningful?
(d) How large must \(n\) be for a difference of only 0.002 min to be "significant" at \(\alpha = 0.05\)?

\(z = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}}\) grows like \(\sqrt{n}\), but the effect size does not change with \(n\).

(a)

\[ z = \frac{0.02}{2/200} = \mathbf{2.00} \]

\(p = \mathbf{0.0455}\), so significant.

(b)

\[ z = \frac{0.02}{2/20} = \mathbf{0.20} \]

\(p = \mathbf{0.8415}\), so not significant.

(c) \(d = 0.02/2 = \mathbf{0.01}\) standard deviations, which is negligible: about one second per session.

(d) We need

\[ \frac{0.002}{2/\sqrt{n}} \ge 1.96 \]

so \(\sqrt{n} \ge 1960\) and \(n \ge 3{,}841{,}600\).

Key insight: With enough data, any non-zero difference becomes "significant". Report effect sizes and CIs alongside \(p\), and decide with the business cost of the change in mind.

tests: 4.1, 4.2 · L5 Mastery

7.C t-tests (4.3)

L1 · FoundationP4.15

Choosing between z and t, and between paired and unpaired

For each scenario, choose one-sample z, one-sample t, two-sample z, pooled (unpaired) t or paired t, and give the df where it applies.

(a) The weights of 12 cereal boxes are compared with the label value of 500 g. \(\sigma\) is unknown.
(b) The blood pressure of 15 patients is measured before and after a drug.
(c) 200 Bengaluru and 250 Pune households are compared on mean electricity bill.
(d) The exam scores of 9 students taught online and 11 taught offline are compared. The populations are normal with similar variances.
(e) A machine with known \(\sigma = 0.4\) mm produces 10 shafts, which are tested against a target diameter.
(f) The left-eye and right-eye reaction times of the same 20 people are compared.

Also: what three assumptions does the pooled t-test make?

Two measurements on the same unit make the design paired. \(\sigma\) known, or \(n\) large, points to \(z\).

(a) One-sample t, \(df = 11\).
(b) Paired t, \(df = 14\).
(c) Two-sample z (large samples).
(d) Pooled t, \(df = 9 + 11 - 2 = 18\).
(e) One-sample z, since \(\sigma\) is known (the population must be normal because \(n\) is small).
(f) Paired t, \(df = 19\).

Pooled-t assumptions: (1) both populations are normal, (2) their variances are equal (\(\sigma_1 = \sigma_2\)), (3) the samples are independent random samples.

Key insight: Whether a design is paired depends on how the data were collected, not on the numbers. If the same units were measured twice, analyse the differences.

tests: 4.1, 4.3 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP4.16

One-sample t-test from raw data

A bottling line is supposed to fill 250 ml. Eight bottles measure:
248.2, 251.0, 247.5, 249.3, 248.8, 250.1, 246.9, 249.6 ml.
At \(\alpha = 0.05\), is the mean fill different from 250 ml? Also give the 95% CI.

Compute \(\bar{x}\) and \(s\) with divisor \(n - 1\), then use \(t\) with \(df = 7\).

\(\bar{x} = \mathbf{248.925}\), \(s = \mathbf{1.360}\), \(SE = 0.481\).

\[ t = \frac{248.925 - 250}{0.481} = \mathbf{-2.235} \]

Critical value \(t_{0.025,\,7} = \pm\mathbf{2.365}\). \(p = \mathbf{0.0605}\).

\(\lvert t \rvert < 2.365\), so do not reject \(H_0\). The evidence that the mean differs from 250 ml is insufficient at the 5% level.

95% CI: \(248.925 \pm 2.365(0.481) = \mathbf{(247.79,\ 250.06)}\) ml. It contains 250, which agrees with the test.

Key insight: A z-test with the same numbers would give a critical value of 1.96 and reject. With \(n = 8\), using \(z\) understates the uncertainty in \(s\).

tests: 4.3 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP4.17

One-sample t from summary statistics (exam style)

An EV company claims the mean wait at its battery-swap stations is no more than 6 minutes. An auditor times 16 swaps: \(\bar{x} = 6.9\) min, \(s = 1.6\) min. Assume the times are approximately normal. At \(\alpha = 0.05\), test the claim. Report the hypotheses, the statistic, the critical value, the p-value, the decision and the conclusion. Also give a 95% two-sided CI.

"No more than 6" is the claim, and it goes in \(H_0\) as \(\mu \le 6\), which makes the test right-tailed. \(df = 15\).

\(H_0: \mu \le 6\) vs \(H_1: \mu > 6\).

\[ t = \frac{6.9 - 6}{1.6/4} = \mathbf{2.25} \]

Critical value \(t_{0.05,\,15} = \mathbf{1.753}\). \(p = \mathbf{0.0199}\).

Reject \(H_0\). At the 5% level there is enough evidence that the mean wait exceeds 6 minutes, so the company's claim is not supported.

95% CI: \(6.9 \pm 2.131(0.4) = \mathbf{(6.05,\ 7.75)}\) min.

Key insight: The two-sided 95% CI has \(\alpha/2 = 0.025\) in each tail, while the one-tailed test puts all 0.05 in one tail. For a one-tailed test, the matching interval is the one-sided 95% bound \(\mu > 6.9 - 1.753(0.4) = 6.20\), which excludes 6.

tests: 4.3, 4.1 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP4.18

Unpaired (pooled) t-test

Two training methods are compared on test scores:

  • Method A (\(n = 7\)): 72, 78, 81, 69, 75, 80, 77
  • Method B (\(n = 8\)): 68, 70, 74, 65, 71, 73, 69, 66

(a) Check the equal-variance assumption with an F-test at \(\alpha = 0.05\) (two-tailed).
(b) At \(\alpha = 0.05\), do the mean scores differ? Give \(t\), df, the critical value, \(p\), the decision and the 95% CI for \(\mu_A - \mu_B\).

\(s_p^2 = \frac{(n_1 - 1)s_1^2 + (n_2 - 1)s_2^2}{n_1 + n_2 - 2}\). For the F-test, put the larger variance in the numerator.

\(\bar{x}_A = 76.0\), \(s_A^2 = 18.667\). \(\bar{x}_B = 69.5\), \(s_B^2 = 10.0\).

(a) \(F = 18.667/10 = \mathbf{1.867}\), compared with \(F_{0.025}(6, 7) = \mathbf{5.119}\). \(F\) is below the critical value, so equal variances are acceptable.

(b)

\[ s_p^2 = \frac{6 \times 18.667 + 7 \times 10}{13} = \mathbf{14.0}, \qquad SE = \sqrt{14\left(\frac{1}{7} + \frac{1}{8}\right)} = 1.936 \]

\[ t = \frac{6.5}{1.936} = \mathbf{3.357} \]

with \(df = 13\). Critical value \(\pm\mathbf{2.160}\). \(p = \mathbf{0.0052}\).

Reject \(H_0\). Method A gives a significantly higher mean score.

95% CI: \(6.5 \pm 2.160(1.936) = \mathbf{(2.32,\ 10.68)}\) points.

Key insight: Pooling the variances is only valid if the variances really are similar. The F-test in (a) is the check that the pooled t-test needs.

tests: 4.3, 4.6 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP4.19

Paired t-test

Response latency (ms) of 8 servers was measured before and after a software patch:

Server 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8
Before 112 98 130 105 121 117 99 126
After 108 97 122 104 115 116 96 119

At \(\alpha = 0.05\), did the patch reduce mean latency? Give the 95% CI for the mean reduction.

Work with \(d = \text{Before} - \text{After}\). \(H_1: \mu_d > 0\). \(df = 7\).

\(d = 4, 1, 8, 1, 6, 1, 3, 7\). \(\bar{d} = \mathbf{3.875}\), \(s_d = \mathbf{2.850}\), \(SE = 1.008\).

\[ t = \frac{3.875}{1.008} = \mathbf{3.845} \]

Critical value \(t_{0.05,\,7} = \mathbf{1.895}\). \(p = \mathbf{0.0032}\).

Reject \(H_0\). The patch significantly reduced latency.

95% CI for \(\mu_d\): \(3.875 \pm 2.365(1.008) = \mathbf{(1.49,\ 6.26)}\) ms.

Key insight: A paired test converts a two-sample problem into a one-sample t-test on the differences.

tests: 4.3 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP4.20

Why pairing helps: analysing P4.19 the wrong way

(a) Analyse the P4.19 data as if they were two independent samples (pooled t, one-tailed, \(\alpha = 0.05\)). What is the conclusion?
(b) The before and after SDs are \(s_B = 12.08\) and \(s_A = 9.93\), and their correlation is \(r = 0.986\). Verify that \(s_d^2 = s_B^2 + s_A^2 - 2r \cdot s_B \cdot s_A\).
(c) Explain in one paragraph why pairing gives so much more power here.

\(\operatorname{Var}(B - A) = \operatorname{Var}(B) + \operatorname{Var}(A) - 2\operatorname{Cov}(B, A)\).

(a) \(\bar{x}_B = 113.5\), \(\bar{x}_A = 109.625\).

\[ s_p^2 = \frac{12.08^2 + 9.93^2}{2} = 122.25, \qquad t = \frac{3.875}{\sqrt{122.25 \times 2/8}} = \mathbf{0.701} \]

with \(df = 14\). The critical value is 1.761 and \(p = \mathbf{0.247}\), so we fail to reject. The wrong analysis misses the effect.

(b)

\[ 12.08^2 + 9.93^2 - 2(0.986)(12.08)(9.93) = 145.99 + 98.55 - 236.4 \approx \mathbf{8.125} \]

That equals \(s_d^2 = 2.850^2 = 8.125\). ✓

(c) Servers differ a lot from one another (SD about 10 ms), and that variation appears in both before and after. Differencing cancels it, because the high positive correlation subtracts \(2r \cdot s_B \cdot s_A\). What remains is only the small within-server change, so the SE shrinks from 5.53 to 1.01.

Key insight: Pairing (blocking) removes unit-to-unit variation from the error term. Two-way ANOVA uses the same idea with blocks (P4.38, P4.41).

tests: 4.3, 5.1 · L4 Analysis

7.D Chi-square Tests (4.4)

L1 · FoundationP4.21

Conditions and choosing the right chi-square test

(a) Which chi-square test answers each question: goodness of fit or independence?
(i) Do customer complaints arrive equally on all 7 weekdays?
(ii) Is the choice of subscription plan associated with the customer's city?
(iii) Do the number of defects per sheet follow a Poisson distribution?
(b) True or false, with a reason: "A chi-square test can be run directly on a table of percentages."
(c) What rule about expected counts must hold, and what do you do if it fails?
(d) Give the df for (i), for (ii) with a 4 plans × 5 cities table, and for (iii) with 6 cells after pooling, when \(\lambda\) is estimated from the data.

\(df(\text{GOF}) = k - 1 - (\text{number of parameters estimated})\). \(df(\text{independence}) = (r - 1)(c - 1)\).

(a) (i) GOF. (ii) Independence. (iii) GOF.
(b) False. The \(\chi^2\) statistic scales with \(n\), so it needs raw frequencies. Percentages lose \(n\): 50% of 10 and 50% of 10,000 carry very different evidence.
(c) Every expected count should be at least 5. If not, merge adjacent categories, which reduces the df, or collect more data.
(d) (i) \(df = 7 - 1 = \mathbf{6}\). (ii) \(df = 3 \times 4 = \mathbf{12}\). (iii) \(df = 6 - 1 - 1 = \mathbf{4}\).

Key insight: Each parameter you estimate from the data makes the fit look better than it really is, so it costs one degree of freedom.

tests: 4.4 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP4.22

Expected counts and checking validity

A survey of 160 users records device type against the plan chosen:

Device \ Plan Free Basic Premium Total
Mobile 22 30 18 70
Desktop 8 40 32 80
Tablet 2 5 3 10
Total 32 75 53 160

(a) Compute all expected counts under independence, and the df.
(b) Is the \(\chi^2\) approximation valid? If not, propose a fix and give the new expected counts and df.

\[ E = \frac{\text{row total} \times \text{column total}}{n} \]

(a) Mobile: 14.0, 32.81, 23.19. Desktop: 16.0, 37.5, 26.5. Tablet: 2.0, 4.69, 3.31. \(df = (3 - 1)(3 - 1) = \mathbf{4}\).

(b) Not valid, because all three Tablet cells are below 5. Merge Tablet with Mobile into a "Handheld" row (24, 35, 21; total 80). The new expected counts are 16.0, 37.5 and 26.5 for both rows, all at least 5, and \(df = 2\).

Key insight: Merge categories in a way that makes sense (tablet is close to mobile). Never merge just to make the result significant.

tests: 4.4 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP4.23

Chi-square test of independence (3×3)

A fintech firm asks 300 customers for their preferred payment method:

Method \ Age 18–30 31–50 51+ Total
UPI 60 40 20 120
Card 30 45 35 110
Cash 10 25 35 70
Total 100 110 90 300

At \(\alpha = 0.05\), is payment preference associated with age group? Give the hypotheses, the expected table, \(\chi^2\), df, the critical value, the p-value and a conclusion. Which cells contribute most?

\(\chi^2 = \sum \frac{(O - E)^2}{E}\). Look at the individual \(\frac{(O - E)^2}{E}\) terms to see where the association lies.

\(H_0\): method and age are independent. \(H_1\): they are associated.

Expected counts: UPI 40, 44, 36. Card 36.67, 40.33, 33. Cash 23.33, 25.67, 21.

Contributions: UPI 10.0, 0.364, 7.111. Card 1.212, 0.540, 0.121. Cash 7.619, 0.017, 9.333.

\[ \chi^2 = \mathbf{36.32} \]

with \(df = 4\). Critical value \(\chi^2_{0.05,\,4} = \mathbf{9.488}\). \(p \approx \mathbf{2.5 \times 10^{-7}}\).

Reject \(H_0\). Payment preference depends on age.

The largest contributions come from young customers using more UPI and less cash, and older customers using more cash and less UPI.

Key insight: A significant \(\chi^2\) tells you that the variables are associated. The cell contributions tell you how, and that is the information a business can act on.

tests: 4.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP4.24

Goodness of fit to a uniform distribution

A help desk logged 250 tickets in one week: Mon 58, Tue 42, Wed 45, Thu 39, Fri 66. At \(\alpha = 0.05\), are tickets equally likely on every weekday?

Each expected count is \(250/5 = 50\), and no parameters are estimated.

\(H_0: P = 1/5\) for every day. \(H_1\): not all days are equally likely.

\[ \chi^2 = \frac{64 + 64 + 25 + 121 + 256}{50} = \mathbf{10.60} \]

with \(df = 4\). Critical value 9.488. \(p = \mathbf{0.0314}\).

Reject \(H_0\). Ticket volume varies by day, with Friday and Monday the heaviest.

Key insight: In a GOF test, \(H_0\) is the model and rejecting it says the model does not fit. Staffing should follow the actual daily pattern, not a flat schedule.

tests: 4.4 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP4.25

Poisson goodness of fit with an estimated parameter

Server crashes per day were counted over 100 days:

Crashes \(x\) 0 1 2 3 4 5+
Days 28 38 20 9 4 1

(a) Estimate \(\lambda\) by maximum likelihood. Treat "5+" as 5.
(b) Compute the expected frequencies. Pool cells where \(E < 5\).
(c) Test the Poisson fit at \(\alpha = 0.05\), with the correct df.

The Poisson MLE is \(\hat{\lambda} = \bar{x}\). The last cell's probability is 1 minus the sum of the others. \(df = (\text{cells after pooling}) - 1 - 1\).

(a)

\[ \hat{\lambda} = \frac{0 + 38 + 40 + 27 + 16 + 5}{100} = \mathbf{1.26} \]

(b) Probabilities for 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5+: 0.2837, 0.3574, 0.2252, 0.0946, 0.0298, 0.0094. Expected counts: 28.37, 35.74, 22.52, 9.46, 2.98, 0.94. Pool 3, 4 and 5+ into "\(\ge 3\)": \(O = 14\), \(E = 13.38\).

(c)

\[ \chi^2 = 0.005 + 0.143 + 0.281 + 0.029 = \mathbf{0.458} \]

There are 4 cells, so \(df = 4 - 1 - 1 = \mathbf{2}\). Critical value 5.991. \(p = \mathbf{0.795}\).

Do not reject. A Poisson model with \(\lambda \approx 1.26\) fits the crash counts well.

Key insight: Here MLE and GOF work together. The data are used once to fit \(\lambda\) and once to judge the fit, and the lost degree of freedom accounts for that double use.

tests: 4.4, 4.5, 3.2.3 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP4.26

For a 2×2 table, \(\chi^2\) equals \(z^2\)

(a) For the 2×2 table \([[a, b], [c, d]]\) with row totals \(r_1, r_2\), column totals \(c_1, c_2\) and grand total \(n\), the uncorrected statistic is \(\chi^2 = \frac{n(ad - bc)^2}{r_1 r_2 c_1 c_2}\). Apply it to the P4.10 data (A: 24 defective, 276 good; B: 15 defective, 335 good).
(b) Compare the result with \(z^2\) from P4.10.
(c) Explain why the two tests must agree, and which question a one-sided \(z\) can answer that \(\chi^2\) cannot.

\(\chi^2\) with 1 df is the square of a standard normal.

(a)

\[ \chi^2 = \frac{650(24 \times 335 - 276 \times 15)^2}{300 \times 350 \times 39 \times 611} = \mathbf{3.952} \]

(b) From P4.10, \(z = 1.988\) and \(z^2 = \mathbf{3.952}\). They are identical.

(c) Both test \(H_0: P_A = P_B\) with the same pooled estimate, and the square of an \(N(0, 1)\) variable is \(\chi^2_1\). The critical values agree too: \(1.96^2 = 3.841 = \chi^2_{0.05,\,1}\). Because \(\chi^2\) squares the difference, it loses the sign, so it is always non-directional. Only the z-test can answer the one-sided question "is A higher?".

Key insight: Many tests that look different are the same test written another way. See also \(F = t^2\) in P4.40.

tests: 4.4, 4.2 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP4.27

Chi-square for feature selection

For a churn model (\(n = 1000\), of whom 220 churned), two categorical features are screened:

Contract Churn Stay
Monthly 180 320
Annual 40 460
Gender Churn Stay
Male 115 385
Female 105 395

(a) Compute \(\chi^2\), the p-value and Cramér's \(V = \sqrt{\chi^2/n}\) for each feature, and say which feature is the more useful predictor.
(b) Suppose the dataset had 1,000,000 rows with exactly the same proportions in the gender table. Find \(\chi^2\), \(p\) and \(V\). What does this show about using p-values for feature ranking on big data?

Both tables have expected counts 110 for churn and 390 for stay in each row. \(\chi^2\) scales linearly with \(n\), but \(V\) does not.

(a) Contract: \(\chi^2 = \mathbf{114.22}\), \(p \approx 1.2 \times 10^{-26}\), \(V = \mathbf{0.338}\), a strong and useful feature. Gender: \(\chi^2 = \mathbf{0.583}\), \(p = \mathbf{0.445}\), \(V = \mathbf{0.024}\), no evidence of association. Contract is the better predictor.

(b) \(\chi^2 = \mathbf{582.75}\), \(p \approx 10^{-128}\), yet \(V\) is still 0.024. Gender now looks "highly significant" but is still useless for prediction.

Key insight: For feature selection, rank by effect size (Cramér's \(V\), mutual information), not by \(p\). On big data almost every feature has a tiny \(p\).

tests: 4.4 · L5 Mastery

7.E Maximum Likelihood Estimation (4.5)

L1 · FoundationP4.28

Likelihood vs probability

(a) What is the difference between \(f(x \mid \theta)\) seen as a function of \(x\) and \(L(\theta) = f(x \mid \theta)\) seen as a function of \(\theta\)?
(b) Why do we maximise \(\ln L\) instead of \(L\)?
(c) True or false: "The likelihood function integrates to 1 over \(\theta\)."
(d) State the invariance property of MLEs, and use it to get the MLE of \(\sigma\) when \(\hat{\sigma}^2 = 3.6\).
(e) What must you assume before you can write down a likelihood?

The formula is the same. What changes is which symbol is held fixed.

(a) As a function of \(x\) with \(\theta\) fixed, \(f\) is a probability (or density) model for data. As a function of \(\theta\) with the observed data fixed, it scores how plausible each \(\theta\) is.
(b) \(\ln\) is strictly increasing, so it keeps the maximiser. It turns the product \(\prod f(x_i)\) into a sum, which is easier to differentiate and numerically stable (it avoids underflow).
(c) False. \(L(\theta)\) is not a density in \(\theta\) and need not integrate to 1.
(d) If \(\hat{\theta}\) is the MLE of \(\theta\), then \(g(\hat{\theta})\) is the MLE of \(g(\theta)\). So \(\hat{\sigma} = \sqrt{3.6} = \mathbf{1.897}\).
(e) A distributional model, such as Bernoulli, Poisson or Normal. Usually you also assume the observations are i.i.d., so the joint density factorises.

Key insight: MLE asks one question: "Which parameter value makes the data we actually saw most probable?"

tests: 4.5 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP4.29

Bernoulli MLE (fraud rate)

In 20 audited transactions, numbers 2, 9 and 17 were fraudulent and the rest legitimate.

(a) Write \(L(p)\) and \(\ln L(p)\).
(b) Evaluate \(L\) at \(p = 0.10\), 0.15 and 0.20. Which is largest?
(c) Derive \(\hat{p}\) by calculus.
(d) Would \(\hat{p}\) change if you were told only "3 of 20 were fraudulent" (a binomial model)?

\(L(p) = p^3(1 - p)^{17}\).

(a) \(L(p) = p^3(1 - p)^{17}\) and \(\ln L = 3 \ln p + 17 \ln(1 - p)\).

(b) \(L(0.10) = 1.668 \times 10^{-4}\), \(\mathbf{L(0.15) = 2.130 \times 10^{-4}}\), \(L(0.20) = 1.801 \times 10^{-4}\). The largest is at \(p = 0.15\).

(c)

\[ \frac{d \ln L}{dp} = \frac{3}{p} - \frac{17}{1 - p} = 0 \]

so \(3(1 - p) = 17p\) and \(\hat{p} = 3/20 = 0.15\).

(d) No. The binomial factor \(\binom{20}{3}\) does not depend on \(p\), so the maximiser is unchanged.

Key insight: The MLE of a probability is just the sample proportion. Counting relative frequencies, as Naive Bayes does, is maximum likelihood.

tests: 4.5 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP4.30

Poisson MLE and invariance

Daily counts of defective chips over 10 days: 2, 0, 3, 1, 1, 4, 2, 0, 1, 2. Assume a Poisson model.

(a) Find \(\hat{\lambda}\).
(b) Find the MLE of \(P(\text{no defects on a day})\) and of \(P(\text{at most 1 defect on a day})\).

\(\hat{\lambda} = \bar{x}\). Then plug \(\hat{\lambda}\) into \(\frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^x}{x!}\), which is justified by invariance.

(a) \(\sum x = 16\), so \(\hat{\lambda} = 1.6\).

(b)

\[ \hat{P}(X = 0) = e^{-1.6} = \mathbf{0.2019}, \qquad \hat{P}(X \le 1) = e^{-1.6}(1 + 1.6) = \mathbf{0.5249} \]

Key insight: By invariance, the MLE of any function of \(\lambda\) is the same function evaluated at \(\hat{\lambda}\). No new optimisation is needed.

tests: 4.5, 3.2.3 · L2 Computation
L4 · AnalysisP4.31

Deriving MLEs: Poisson, exponential, and a boundary case

(a) Derive the MLE of \(\lambda\) for an i.i.d. \(\text{Poisson}(\lambda)\) sample \(x_1, \ldots, x_n\).
(b) Times between failures are modelled as exponential: \(f(t) = \lambda e^{-\lambda t}\) for \(t > 0\). The data are 12, 7, 20, 3, 15, 9 hours. Derive \(\hat{\lambda}\) in general, evaluate it, and give the MLE of the mean lifetime and of \(P(T > 10)\).
(c) For \(\text{Uniform}(0, \theta)\) with data 2.3, 4.1, 0.7, 3.8, find \(\hat{\theta}\). Explain why setting \(dL/d\theta = 0\) does not work here.

(c) \(L(\theta) = \theta^{-n}\) when \(\theta \ge \max x_i\), and 0 otherwise. Sketch \(L(\theta)\).

(a)

\[ \ln L = -n\lambda + \left(\sum x_i\right) \ln \lambda - \sum \ln x_i! \]

Setting the derivative to zero: \(-n + \sum x_i/\lambda = 0\), so \(\hat{\lambda} = \bar{x}\). The second derivative, \(-\sum x_i/\lambda^2\), is negative, so this is a maximum.

(b)

\[ \ln L = n \ln \lambda - \lambda \sum t_i \]

Setting \(n/\lambda - \sum t_i = 0\) gives \(\hat{\lambda} = 1/\bar{t}\). Here \(\bar{t} = 11\), so \(\hat{\lambda} = 0.0909\) per hour. The MLE of the mean lifetime is \(1/\hat{\lambda} = \mathbf{11}\) h, and \(\hat{P}(T > 10) = e^{-10/11} = \mathbf{0.4029}\).

(c) \(L(\theta) = \theta^{-4}\) for \(\theta \ge 4.1\) and 0 below. It decreases in \(\theta\), so it is largest at the smallest allowed \(\theta\): \(\hat{\theta} = \max x_i = 4.1\). The maximum is at a boundary of the parameter space, where the derivative is not zero.

Key insight: Calculus finds interior maxima only. Always sketch the likelihood over the allowed parameter range.

tests: 4.5 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP4.32

Normal MLEs and the bias of \(\hat{\sigma}^2\)

(a) For an i.i.d. \(N(\mu, \sigma^2)\) sample, derive \(\hat{\mu}\) and \(\hat{\sigma}^2\).
(b) Show that \(E[\hat{\sigma}^2] = \frac{(n - 1)\sigma^2}{n}\), so \(\hat{\sigma}^2\) is biased. What is the bias, and is \(\hat{\sigma}^2\) consistent?
(c) For the data 4, 7, 6, 9, 9, compute \(\hat{\mu}\), \(\hat{\sigma}^2\) (the MLE) and \(s^2\).

Use \(\sum(x_i - \mu)^2 = \sum(x_i - \bar{x})^2 + n(\bar{x} - \mu)^2\), and take expectations, with \(E[(\bar{x} - \mu)^2] = \sigma^2/n\).

(a)

\[ \ln L = -\frac{n}{2} \ln(2\pi\sigma^2) - \frac{\sum(x_i - \mu)^2}{2\sigma^2} \]

\(\partial/\partial\mu = 0\) gives \(\hat{\mu} = \bar{x}\).

\[ \frac{\partial}{\partial \sigma^2} = -\frac{n}{2\sigma^2} + \frac{\sum(x_i - \mu)^2}{2\sigma^4} = 0 \]

gives \(\hat{\sigma}^2 = \sum(x_i - \bar{x})^2/n\).

(b)

\[ E\left[\sum(x_i - \bar{x})^2\right] = E\left[\sum(x_i - \mu)^2\right] - nE[(\bar{x} - \mu)^2] = n\sigma^2 - \sigma^2 = (n - 1)\sigma^2 \]

So \(E[\hat{\sigma}^2] = (n - 1)\sigma^2/n\), and the bias is \(-\sigma^2/n\). The bias goes to 0 as \(n \to \infty\), so \(\hat{\sigma}^2\) is consistent though biased.

(c) \(\hat{\mu} = \mathbf{7}\), \(SS = 18\), \(\hat{\sigma}^2 = 18/5 = \mathbf{3.6}\) and \(s^2 = 18/4 = \mathbf{4.5}\).

Key insight: This is the formal reason for the \(n - 1\) in CS-1. The MLE is not guaranteed to be unbiased, but it is consistent, and for large \(n\) it is efficient.

tests: 4.5, 1.1.4, 3.3.7 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP4.33

MLE is the loss function: least squares and cross-entropy

(a) For the regression model \(y_i = \beta x_i + \varepsilon_i\) with \(\varepsilon_i \sim \text{i.i.d. } N(0, \sigma^2)\), show that maximising the likelihood over \(\beta\) is the same as minimising \(\sum(y_i - \beta x_i)^2\). Find \(\hat{\beta}\) and the MLE of \(\sigma^2\) for the data (1, 2.1), (2, 3.9), (3, 6.2).
(b) For binary labels \(y_i \in \{0, 1\}\) with predicted probabilities \(p_i\), show that the negative mean log-likelihood equals the binary cross-entropy \(-\frac{1}{n}\sum[y_i \ln p_i + (1 - y_i) \ln(1 - p_i)]\). Evaluate it for the fraud data of P4.29 with a constant prediction \(p = 0.15\).
(c) What loss would correspond to Laplace (double-exponential) noise?

Write out \(\ln L\) and drop every term that does not involve the parameter.

(a)

\[ \ln L = -\frac{n}{2} \ln(2\pi\sigma^2) - \frac{\sum(y_i - \beta x_i)^2}{2\sigma^2} \]

For fixed \(\sigma^2\), maximising over \(\beta\) means minimising the SSE.

\[ \hat{\beta} = \frac{\sum xy}{\sum x^2} = \frac{28.5}{14} = \mathbf{2.036} \]

\(\hat{\sigma}^2 = \text{SSE}/n = \mathbf{0.0140}\) (MLE divisor \(n\)).

(b) \(\ln L = \sum[y_i \ln p_i + (1 - y_i) \ln(1 - p_i)]\), and \(-\ln L/n\) is exactly the binary cross-entropy. With 3 frauds and \(p = 0.15\):

\[ \text{BCE} = -\frac{3 \ln 0.15 + 17 \ln 0.85}{20} = \mathbf{0.4227} \]

It is minimised at \(p = 0.15\), which is the MLE.

(c) Laplace noise has density proportional to \(e^{-\lvert \varepsilon \rvert / b}\), so the MLE minimises \(\sum \lvert y_i - \hat{y}_i \rvert\). That is the MAE / L1 loss, which is more robust to outliers.

Key insight: Choosing a loss function in ML is choosing a noise distribution. Squared loss means Gaussian noise, cross-entropy means Bernoulli, and absolute loss means Laplace.

tests: 4.5, 5.4 · L5 Mastery

7.F F-test & ANOVA (4.6–4.7)

L1 · FoundationP4.34

ANOVA concepts

(a) Why not compare 3 group means with three separate t-tests at \(\alpha = 0.05\)? Compute the family-wise error rate, assuming independence.
(b) State \(H_0\) and \(H_1\) for one-way ANOVA correctly.
(c) List the three ANOVA assumptions.
(d) If \(F\) is large, what does that say about the between-group variation compared with the within-group variation?
(e) ANOVA rejects \(H_0\). Can you conclude that all the means differ? What do you do next?

\(1 - 0.95^3\).

(a)

\[ \text{FWER} = 1 - 0.95^3 = \mathbf{0.1426} \]

That is nearly three times the nominal 5%.

(b) \(H_0: \mu_1 = \mu_2 = \mu_3\). \(H_1\): not all \(\mu_j\) are equal, meaning at least one pair differs. Writing "\(\mu_1 \ne \mu_2 \ne \mu_3\)" is wrong.

(c) Normality within each group, equal variances (homoscedasticity), and independent observations.

(d) The group means are spread out by much more than the noise within groups would explain. That is evidence that the means differ.

(e) No. It means at least one pair differs. Next, run a post-hoc test such as LSD, Tukey or Bonferroni to find which pairs.

Key insight: ANOVA tests means by comparing two variance estimates. MSTR and MSE both estimate \(\sigma^2\) under \(H_0\), but MSTR becomes inflated when the means differ.

tests: 4.7 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP4.35

F-test for two variances
