Two suppliers' bolt lengths: supplier 1 has \(n_1 = 11\) and \(s_1^2 = 24.5\ \text{mm}^2\). Supplier 2 has \(n_2 = 9\) and \(s_2^2 = 10.2\ \text{mm}^2\). At \(\alpha = 0.05\), do the variances differ?

Put the larger variance in the numerator, then compare with \(F_{\alpha/2}(n_1 - 1, n_2 - 1)\).

\(H_0: \sigma_1^2 = \sigma_2^2\) vs \(H_1: \sigma_1^2 \ne \sigma_2^2\).

\[ F = \frac{24.5}{10.2} = \mathbf{2.402} \]

with df (10, 8). Critical value \(F_{0.025}(10, 8) = \mathbf{4.295}\). Two-tailed \(p = \mathbf{0.227}\).

Do not reject. There is not enough evidence that the variabilities differ.

Key insight: With small samples, a variance ratio of 2.4 can easily arise by chance. F-tests have low power, and they are sensitive to departures from normality.

tests: 4.6, 3.2.6 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP4.36

Completing an ANOVA table

Four diets were tested on 24 subjects, 6 per diet. Fill in the table and decide at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

Source SS df MS F
Between (diets) 84 ? ? ?
Within (error) ? ? ?
Total 244 ?

\(df_{\text{between}} = k - 1\), \(df_{\text{within}} = n - k\), and \(\text{SSE} = \text{SST} - \text{SSTR}\).

df: 3, 20, 23.

\[ \text{SSE} = 244 - 84 = \mathbf{160}, \quad \text{MSTR} = \frac{84}{3} = \mathbf{28}, \quad \text{MSE} = \frac{160}{20} = \mathbf{8}, \quad F = \frac{28}{8} = \mathbf{3.5} \]

The critical value is \(F_{0.05}(3, 20) = \mathbf{3.098}\) and \(p = \mathbf{0.0345}\). Reject \(H_0\). At least one diet's mean differs.

Key insight: Both the sums of squares and the degrees of freedom split additively: \(\text{SST} = \text{SSTR} + \text{SSE}\) and \(n - 1 = (k - 1) + (n - k)\). Mean squares do not add.

tests: 4.7 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP4.37

One-way ANOVA with LSD post-hoc test

Response times (ms) under three caching strategies:

  • A: 23, 25, 21, 24, 22
  • B: 28, 26, 30, 27, 29, 28
  • C: 24, 27, 25, 26, 23

(a) Using the correction factor \(\text{CF} = G^2/n\), build the ANOVA table and test at \(\alpha = 0.05\).
(b) If \(H_0\) is rejected, use the \(\text{LSD} = t_{\alpha/2,\, n-k} \cdot \sqrt{\text{MSE}\left(\frac{1}{n_i} + \frac{1}{n_j}\right)}\) to find which pairs differ.

\(\text{SST} = \sum x^2 - \text{CF}\). \(\text{SSTR} = \sum(T_j^2/n_j) - \text{CF}\), where \(T_j\) is each group's total.

(a) Group totals: 115, 168, 125. \(G = 408\), \(n = 16\), \(\text{CF} = 408^2/16 = 10404\). \(\sum x^2 = 10504\).

\[ \text{SST} = \mathbf{100}, \qquad \text{SSTR} = \frac{115^2}{5} + \frac{168^2}{6} + \frac{125^2}{5} - 10404 = \mathbf{70}, \qquad \text{SSE} = \mathbf{30} \]

Source SS df MS F
Between 70 2 35.0 15.17
Within 30 13 2.308
Total 100 15

The critical value is \(F_{0.05}(2, 13) = \mathbf{3.806}\) and \(p = \mathbf{0.0004}\). Reject \(H_0\).

(b) \(t_{0.025,\,13} = 2.160\). Group means are A = 23, B = 28, C = 25.

  • A vs B: \(\lvert \text{diff} \rvert = 5.0\), \(\text{LSD} = 1.987\), so they differ.
  • B vs C: \(\lvert \text{diff} \rvert = 3.0\), \(\text{LSD} = 1.987\), so they differ.
  • A vs C: \(\lvert \text{diff} \rvert = 2.0\), \(\text{LSD} = 2.076\), so they do not differ (just barely).

Strategy B is significantly slower than both A and C. A and C cannot be separated.

Key insight: The ANOVA \(F\) only tells you that some difference exists. The post-hoc comparisons locate it. LSD is the least conservative post-hoc method.

tests: 4.7 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP4.38

Two-way ANOVA without replication (treatments + blocks)

Output (units per hour) of 4 machines (treatments), each run by 3 operators (blocks):

Operator \ Machine M1 M2 M3 M4
O1 15 18 14 20
O2 16 19 15 22
O3 13 17 12 19

At \(\alpha = 0.05\), test whether machines differ and whether operators differ.

\(k = 4\) treatments and \(m = 3\) blocks. \(\text{SSTR} = \sum C^2/m - \text{CF}\), \(\text{SSBL} = \sum R^2/k - \text{CF}\), \(\text{SSE} = \text{SST} - \text{SSTR} - \text{SSBL}\), and \(df_{\text{error}} = (k - 1)(m - 1)\).

\(G = 200\), \(\text{CF} = 200^2/12 = 3333.33\), \(\sum x^2 = 3434\), \(\text{SST} = \mathbf{100.67}\).

Machine totals 44, 54, 41, 61 give \(\text{SSTR} = \mathbf{84.67}\). Operator totals 67, 72, 61 give \(\text{SSBL} = \mathbf{15.17}\). \(\text{SSE} = \mathbf{0.83}\).

Source SS df MS F
Machines 84.67 3 28.22 203.2
Operators 15.17 2 7.58 54.6
Error 0.83 6 0.139
Total 100.67 11

Machines: \(F_{0.05}(3, 6) = 4.757\) and \(p \approx 2 \times 10^{-6}\). Machines differ.

Operators: \(F_{0.05}(2, 6) = 5.143\) and \(p = 0.0001\). Operators differ.

Key insight: Without the operator block, its 15.17 would have stayed in SSE, which would have been 16.0 on 8 df. Blocking removes a known source of variation from the error term, so the test becomes sharper.

tests: 4.7 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP4.39

Proving SST = SSTR + SSE

(a) Prove that \(\sum_j \sum_i (x_{ij} - \bar{x})^2 = \sum_j n_j(\bar{x}_j - \bar{x})^2 + \sum_j \sum_i (x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j)^2\).
(b) Justify each degree of freedom: \(n - 1\), \(k - 1\) and \(n - k\).
(c) Check the identity numerically on P4.37 by computing SSTR directly from the group means.

Write \(x_{ij} - \bar{x} = (x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j) + (\bar{x}_j - \bar{x})\), square it, and show the cross term is zero using \(\sum_i (x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j) = 0\).

(a)

\[ (x_{ij} - \bar{x})^2 = (x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j)^2 + (\bar{x}_j - \bar{x})^2 + 2(x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j)(\bar{x}_j - \bar{x}) \]

Summing over \(i\) within group \(j\), the cross term becomes

\[ 2(\bar{x}_j - \bar{x}) \cdot \sum_i (x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j) = 2(\bar{x}_j - \bar{x}) \cdot 0 = 0 \]

The middle term summed over \(i\) gives \(n_j(\bar{x}_j - \bar{x})^2\). Summing over \(j\) completes the proof. ∎

(b) SST uses \(n\) deviations with one constraint (they sum to 0), so \(n - 1\). SSTR uses \(k\) group means with one constraint (the weighted deviations sum to 0), so \(k - 1\). SSE has \(n\) deviations with one constraint per group, so \(n - k\).

(c) The grand mean is 25.5.

\[ \text{SSTR} = 5(23 - 25.5)^2 + 6(28 - 25.5)^2 + 5(25 - 25.5)^2 = 31.25 + 37.5 + 1.25 = \mathbf{70} \]

✓. Then \(\text{SSE} = 100 - 70 = 30\) ✓.

Key insight: The cross term vanishes for the same reason as in CS-1: deviations from a mean sum to zero. The regression identity \(\text{SST} = \text{SSR} + \text{SSE}\) works the same way.

tests: 4.7 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP4.40

With two groups, ANOVA's F equals the pooled \(t^2\)

Group 1: 10, 12, 11, 14, 13. Group 2: 15, 14, 17, 16, 18.

(a) Run a pooled two-sample t-test (two-tailed).
(b) Run a one-way ANOVA on the same data.
(c) Show that \(F = t^2\), and that the two p-values match. Explain why this must happen in general.

With \(k = 2\), MSE equals \(s_p^2\). Show that \(\text{SSTR} = \frac{(\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2)^2}{1/n_1 + 1/n_2}\).

(a) \(\bar{x}_1 = 12\), \(\bar{x}_2 = 16\), \(s_1^2 = s_2^2 = 2.5\), so \(s_p^2 = 2.5\).

\[ t = \frac{-4}{\sqrt{2.5 \times 0.4}} = \mathbf{-4.0} \]

with \(df = 8\) and \(p = \mathbf{0.00395}\).

(b) \(G = 140\), \(\text{CF} = 1960\), \(\text{SST} = 60\), \(\text{SSTR} = 40\), \(\text{SSE} = 20\). \(\text{MSTR} = 40\), \(\text{MSE} = 2.5\), \(F = 16.0\) on (1, 8) df, \(p = \mathbf{0.00395}\).

(c) \(F = 16 = (-4)^2\). In general, with \(k = 2\),

\[ \text{SSTR} = \frac{n_1 n_2(\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2)^2}{n_1 + n_2} = \frac{(\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2)^2}{1/n_1 + 1/n_2} \]

and \(\text{MSE} = s_p^2\). So \(F = \text{MSTR}/\text{MSE} = t^2\). And \(F(1, \nu)\) is the distribution of the square of a \(t(\nu)\) variable.

Key insight: ANOVA generalises the pooled t-test to \(k\) groups. When \(k = 2\) the two give identical answers.

tests: 4.7, 4.3 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP4.41

Comparing ML models across CV folds: why one-way ANOVA is wrong

Three model configurations were evaluated on the same 5 cross-validation folds (accuracy):

Config \ Fold 1 2 3 4 5
A 0.812 0.845 0.798 0.861 0.830
B 0.820 0.851 0.806 0.866 0.839
C 0.808 0.839 0.801 0.855 0.826

(a) A junior analyst runs a one-way ANOVA. Compute \(F\) and \(p\).
(b) Run a two-way ANOVA with configs as treatments and folds as blocks. Compute \(F\) and \(p\) for the configs.
(c) Explain why the conclusions differ, and which analysis is correct. Which independence assumption does (a) violate?

Some folds are simply easier than others (fold 4 is high for every config). That variation belongs to the folds, not to the error.

Config means: A = 0.8292, B = 0.8364, C = 0.8258. \(\text{SST} = 0.007072\), \(\text{SSTR} = 0.000293\).

(a) \(\text{SSE} = 0.006779\) on 12 df, so \(\text{MSE} = 0.000565\).

\[ F = \frac{0.000146}{0.000565} = \mathbf{0.259} \]

and \(p = \mathbf{0.776}\). The analyst concludes there is no difference.

(b) \(\text{SSBL}\) (folds) \(= 0.006743\) on 4 df. \(\text{SSE} = 0.0000357\) on 8 df, so \(\text{MSE} = 4.47 \times 10^{-6}\).

\[ F_{\text{config}} = \frac{0.000146}{4.47 \times 10^{-6}} = \mathbf{32.79} \]

on (2, 8) df. The critical value is 4.459 and \(p = \mathbf{0.00014}\). The configs differ significantly, and B is best on every fold.

(c) All three configs are scored on the same folds, so their accuracies are matched by fold. They are not independent samples. One-way ANOVA puts the large fold-to-fold variation into the error term, and that hides a small but consistent improvement. The blocked (two-way) design is correct. It is the \(k\)-group version of a paired t-test.

Key insight: When every model is scored on the same splits, compare them with a blocked/paired analysis. Treating them as independent throws away most of the power.

tests: 4.7, 4.3 · L5 Mastery

7.G Module 4 Capstone

L5 · MasteryP4.42

Checkout redesign: a full inference workflow

An e-commerce company randomly assigns 3000 visitors to each of three checkout designs.

Design Visitors Purchases Order value of a random sample of 20 buyers (₹): mean, SD
A (current) 3000 210 1520, 310
B 3000 255 1610, 290
C 3000 228 1495, 330

(a) Conversion, all designs: at \(\alpha = 0.05\), is conversion associated with design? Use a 3×2 \(\chi^2\) test.
(b) Order value: run a one-way ANOVA from the summary statistics at \(\alpha = 0.05\). Use \(\text{SSTR} = \sum n_j(\bar{x}_j - \bar{x})^2\) and \(\text{SSE} = \sum (n_j - 1)s_j^2\).
(c) Assumption check: an F-test on the largest vs the smallest variance (\(\alpha = 0.05\), two-tailed).
(d) Before the experiment, the team pre-registered one hypothesis: "B converts better than A". Test it (pooled z, one-tailed, \(\alpha = 0.05\)).
(e) Give the MLEs of the three conversion rates and a 95% CI for \(P_B - P_A\).
(f) Afterwards, a manager wants all three pairwise conversion comparisons. What Bonferroni threshold applies? Does B vs A survive it, one-tailed and two-tailed?
(g) Write a three-sentence recommendation for the CEO.

In (a) every expected count is 231 for purchases and 2769 for non-purchases. In (b) the grand mean is 1541.67 and \(df = (2, 57)\).

(a)

\[ \chi^2 = 1.909 + 0.159 + 2.494 + 0.208 + 0.039 + 0.003 = \mathbf{4.81} \]

with \(df = 2\). The critical value is 5.991 and \(p = \mathbf{0.090}\). Do not reject. The overall evidence of association is weak.

(b) \(\text{SSTR} = 146{,}333\) and \(\text{SSE} = 5{,}492{,}900\). \(\text{MSTR} = 73{,}167\) and \(\text{MSE} = 96{,}367\), so \(F = 0.759\) on (2, 57) df. The critical value is 3.159 and \(p = \mathbf{0.473}\). There is no significant difference in mean order value.

(c)

\[ F = \frac{330^2}{290^2} = \mathbf{1.295} \]

on (19, 19) df. The critical value \(F_{0.025}\) is 2.526 and \(p = 0.579\). The equal-variance assumption is reasonable.

(d) \(\hat{p}_A = 0.070\), \(\hat{p}_B = 0.085\), pooled 0.0775, \(SE = 0.006904\). \(z = \mathbf{2.173}\) and one-tailed \(p = \mathbf{0.0149}\). Reject \(H_0\). B converts better than A.

(e) The MLEs are \(\hat{p}_A = \mathbf{0.070}\), \(\hat{p}_B = \mathbf{0.085}\) and \(\hat{p}_C = \mathbf{0.076}\) (sample proportions). Unpooled \(SE = 0.006901\), so the 95% CI for \(P_B - P_A\) is

\[ 0.015 \pm 0.0135 = \mathbf{(0.0015,\ 0.0285)} \]

(f) The threshold is \(\alpha^* = 0.05/3 = \mathbf{0.0167}\). One-tailed, \(p = 0.0149 < 0.0167\), so it survives. Two-tailed, \(p = 0.0298 > 0.0167\), so it does not. A pre-registered directional test is a confirmatory test. Post-hoc pairwise tests are exploratory and need the correction.

(g) Design B increases conversion from 7.0% to about 8.5%, a relative lift of roughly 21%, and the pre-registered test confirms it (\(p = 0.015\)). Order values do not differ between designs, so the gain is expected to come from more buyers, not larger baskets. Roll out B, and keep monitoring, because the CI is wide (0.15 to 2.85 percentage points).

Key insight: An overall test (\(\chi^2\) or ANOVA) and a focused, pre-registered comparison can disagree. Both can be correct, because they answer different questions with different power. Decide the hypotheses before looking at the data.

tests: 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.5, 4.6, 4.7 · L5 Mastery
L3 · ApplicationP4.43

Choosing the right test

For each scenario, name the test, the statistic, the df and the tail. Do not compute anything.

  1. Is the mean IQ of 25 students (\(\sigma\) unknown) greater than 100?
  2. Is the variance of a new machine's output smaller than that of the old machine? Samples of 10 and 12.
  3. 500 voters are cross-classified by party preference (4 parties) and region (3 regions). Is there an association?
  4. The weights of 20 people before and after a 6-week diet: did the diet reduce weight?
  5. Mean yields under 5 fertilisers, 4 plots each, all plots in one field.
  6. The same 5 fertilisers, but each is applied on each of 4 different farms.
  7. Do 600 die rolls fit a fair die?
  8. Is the defect rate of supplier X (40/800) different from supplier Y (55/900)?

For each one, ask: means, proportions, variances or counts? One group or several? Paired or independent? Is \(\sigma\) known?

  1. One-sample t, \(df = 24\), right-tailed.
  2. F-test, \(F = s_{\text{old}}^2/s_{\text{new}}^2\), df (11, 9), right-tailed (\(H_1: \sigma_{\text{new}}^2 < \sigma_{\text{old}}^2\)).
  3. \(\chi^2\) independence, \(df = (4 - 1)(3 - 1) = 6\), right tail of \(\chi^2\).
  4. Paired t on \(d = \text{before} - \text{after}\), \(df = 19\), right-tailed (\(H_1: \mu_d > 0\)).
  5. One-way ANOVA, \(F\) with (4, 15) df.
  6. Two-way ANOVA without replication, with farms as blocks. \(F_{\text{treatment}}\) has (4, 12) df and \(F_{\text{block}}\) has (3, 12) df.
  7. \(\chi^2\) goodness of fit, \(df = 5\).
  8. Two-proportion z, pooled, two-tailed. Equivalently a 2×2 \(\chi^2\) with 1 df.

Key insight: Choosing the test comes before calculating it. The four questions in the hint pick the test in almost every exam problem.

tests: 4.1, 4.7 · L3 Application
Module 5: Covariance, Correlation & Regression · 35 questions

Tree nodes 5.1–5.6 (CS-11, W-4). Unless stated otherwise, "covariance" means the sample covariance with divisor \(n - 1\) (as on the CS-11 slides). Whenever the divisor choice could change your answer, the problem says so.

8.A Covariance & Covariance Matrix (5.1)

L1 · FoundationP5.1

What covariance can and cannot tell you

Mark each statement True or False and justify it in one line.

(a) \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) = 250\) shows a stronger relationship than \(\operatorname{Cov}(U, V) = 3\).
(b) If \(Y\) tends to decrease as \(X\) increases, \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) < 0\).
(c) If you convert \(X\) from metres to centimetres, \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)\) is multiplied by 100.
(d) \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, X) = \operatorname{Var}(X)\).
(e) \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) = 0\) implies that \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent.
(f) In a variance–covariance matrix, the off-diagonal entry in row \(i\), column \(j\) equals the entry in row \(j\), column \(i\).

Covariance carries the product of the two variables' units. Only its sign is scale-free.

(a) False. The magnitude of a covariance depends on units and scale, so covariances of different pairs cannot be compared. Strength has to be compared with \(r\).
(b) True. Below-mean \(x\) pairs with above-mean \(y\), so the products \((x - \bar{x})(y - \bar{y})\) are mostly negative.
(c) True. \(\operatorname{Cov}(100X, Y) = 100 \cdot \operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)\).
(d) True. \(\frac{\sum (x - \bar{x})(x - \bar{x})}{n - 1} = s^2\).
(e) False. Zero covariance means only no linear association (see P5.14). Independence implies zero covariance, but the converse does not hold.
(f) True. \(\operatorname{Cov}(X_i, X_j) = \operatorname{Cov}(X_j, X_i)\), so \(\Sigma\) is symmetric.

Key insight: Covariance gives the direction of a linear association. Its magnitude is not interpretable on its own, and that is exactly why the correlation coefficient was introduced.

tests: 5.1 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP5.2

Sample covariance by hand

A machine operator records hours of preventive maintenance per month (\(X\)) and the machine's uptime score (\(Y\)):

\(X\) 2 4 6 8 10
\(Y\) 3 7 8 12 15

(a) Compute the sample covariance and state its units.
(b) What would the population-style covariance (divisor \(n\)) be?
(c) Interpret the sign.

\(\bar{x} = 6\) and \(\bar{y} = 9\). Tabulate \((x - \bar{x})(y - \bar{y})\).

The deviations are \(d_x = -4, -2, 0, 2, 4\) and \(d_y = -6, -2, -1, 3, 6\).
The products are 24, 4, 0, 6, 24, so \(\sum d_x d_y = \mathbf{58}\).

(a)

\[ \operatorname{Cov} = \frac{58}{4} = \mathbf{14.5 \text{ hour}\cdot\text{score units}} \]

(b) \(\frac{58}{5} = \mathbf{11.6}\).
(c) The covariance is positive: more maintenance goes with higher uptime. The number 14.5 alone does not tell you whether the association is strong (\(r = 0.989\) here).

Key insight: Covariance is the average of the cross-products of deviations. A point contributes positively when it lies in the "both above mean" or "both below mean" quadrant.

tests: 5.1 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP5.3

Covariance from a joint PMF

Let \(X\) be the number of failed sensors on line A (0 or 1) and \(Y\) the number on line B (0, 1 or 2). Their joint PMF is:

\(Y=0\) \(Y=1\) \(Y=2\)
\(X=0\) 0.20 0.15 0.05
\(X=1\) 0.10 0.20 0.30

(a) Find \(E[X]\), \(E[Y]\) and \(E[XY]\).
(b) Find \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) = E[XY] - E[X]E[Y]\).
(c) Find the correlation \(\rho\).
(d) Are \(X\) and \(Y\) independent?

Only the cells with \(x = 1\) and \(y \ge 1\) contribute to \(E[XY]\).

The marginals are \(P(X=1) = 0.6\) and \(P(Y = 0, 1, 2) = 0.30, 0.35, 0.35\).

(a) \(E[X] = 0.6\), \(E[Y] = 0.35 + 0.70 = 1.05\), \(E[XY] = 1 \cdot 1 \cdot 0.20 + 1 \cdot 2 \cdot 0.30 = 0.80\).
(b) \(\operatorname{Cov} = 0.80 - 0.6 \times 1.05 = 0.17\).
(c) \(\operatorname{Var} X = 0.6 - 0.36 = 0.24\). \(E[Y^2] = 0.35 + 4(0.35) = 1.75\), so \(\operatorname{Var} Y = 1.75 - 1.1025 = 0.6475\). Then

\[ \rho = \frac{0.17}{\sqrt{0.24 \times 0.6475}} \approx \mathbf{0.431} \]

(d) No. \(\operatorname{Cov} \ne 0\) already rules out independence. For a direct check, \(P(0,0) = 0.20\) but \(P(X=0)P(Y=0) = 0.4 \times 0.3 = 0.12\).

Key insight: \(E[XY] - E[X]E[Y]\) is the probabilistic form of the sample covariance formula. A nonzero covariance proves dependence. A zero covariance would prove nothing.

tests: 5.1, 3.1.7 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP5.4

Unit changes and building a covariance matrix

(a) For a group of adults, \(\operatorname{Cov}(\text{height in cm}, \text{weight in kg}) = 42\). Find the covariance when height is in inches (1 in = 2.54 cm) and weight is in pounds (1 kg = 2.2046 lb). Does the correlation change?

(b) Three features of a dataset have \(\operatorname{Var}(X_1) = 16\), \(\operatorname{Var}(X_2) = 25\), \(\operatorname{Var}(X_3) = 9\), \(\operatorname{Cov}(X_1, X_2) = 12\), \(\operatorname{Cov}(X_1, X_3) = -6\) and \(\operatorname{Cov}(X_2, X_3) = 0\). Write the full 3×3 variance–covariance matrix \(\Sigma\) and the corresponding correlation matrix \(R\). Then answer: which pair is most strongly related, and is any pair linearly unrelated?

Converting cm to inches divides by 2.54, and converting kg to lb multiplies by 2.2046. The correlation entries are \(r_{ij} = \frac{\sigma_{ij}}{\sigma_i \sigma_j}\).

(a)

\[ \operatorname{Cov}' = 42 \times \frac{1}{2.54} \times 2.2046 \approx \mathbf{36.45 \text{ in}\cdot\text{lb}} \]

The correlation is unchanged, because both standard deviations rescale by the same factors and these cancel.

(b)

Σ = | 16  12  −6 |        R = |  1    0.6  −0.5 |
    | 12  25   0 |            |  0.6  1     0   |
    | −6   0   9 |            | −0.5  0     1   |

\(r_{12} = \frac{12}{4 \cdot 5} = 0.6\), \(r_{13} = \frac{-6}{4 \cdot 3} = -0.5\), \(r_{23} = 0\).
The strongest pair is \(X_1\)–\(X_2\) (\(r = 0.6\)). \(X_2\) and \(X_3\) have no linear relationship (\(r = 0\)).
Note that \(\operatorname{Cov}(X_1, X_2) = 12\) is larger in absolute value than \(\lvert \operatorname{Cov}(X_1, X_3) \rvert = 6\), and it also gives the larger \(\lvert r \rvert\). That agreement is a coincidence: rank strength by \(R\), never by \(\Sigma\).

Key insight: The covariance matrix holds variances on its diagonal and covariances off it. Dividing by \(\sigma_i \sigma_j\) turns it into the scale-free correlation matrix. That matrix is what you read to judge relationships, and it is what PCA on standardised data uses.

tests: 5.1, 5.2 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP5.5

Covariance under linear transformation

(a) Prove from the definition that \(\operatorname{Cov}(aX + b, cY + d) = ac \cdot \operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)\).
(b) Prove that \(\operatorname{Var}(X \pm Y) = \operatorname{Var} X + \operatorname{Var} Y \pm 2\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)\).
(c) Take \(\operatorname{Var} X = 9\), \(\operatorname{Var} Y = 16\) and \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) = 6\). Compute \(\operatorname{Var}(X + Y)\), \(\operatorname{Var}(X - Y)\), \(\operatorname{Var}(2X - 3Y)\), \(\operatorname{Cov}(2X + 3, -Y + 1)\), and the correlation between \(2X + 3\) and \(-Y + 1\).

The mean of \(aX + b\) is \(a\mu_X + b\), so the deviation is \(a(X - \mu_X)\): the shift \(b\) disappears.

(a)

\[ \begin{aligned} \operatorname{Cov}(aX + b, cY + d) &= E[(aX + b - a\mu_X - b)(cY + d - c\mu_Y - d)] \\ &= E[a(X - \mu_X) \cdot c(Y - \mu_Y)] \\ &= \mathbf{ac \cdot \operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)} \end{aligned} \]

(b) \(\operatorname{Var}(X \pm Y) = E[((X - \mu_X) \pm (Y - \mu_Y))^2]\). Expanding the square gives

\[ \mathbf{\operatorname{Var} X + \operatorname{Var} Y \pm 2\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)} \]

(c)

  • \(\operatorname{Var}(X + Y) = 9 + 16 + 12 = \mathbf{37}\)
  • \(\operatorname{Var}(X - Y) = 9 + 16 - 12 = \mathbf{13}\)
  • \(\operatorname{Var}(2X - 3Y) = 4 \cdot 9 + 9 \cdot 16 - 2 \cdot 2 \cdot 3 \cdot 6 = 36 + 144 - 72 = \mathbf{108}\)
  • \(\operatorname{Cov}(2X + 3, -Y + 1) = 2 \cdot (-1) \cdot 6 = \mathbf{-12}\)
  • \(\text{Corr} = \frac{-12}{2 \cdot 3 \cdot 4} = \mathbf{-0.5}\), which is minus the original \(\rho = \frac{6}{3 \cdot 4} = 0.5\).

Key insight: Shifts never affect covariance. Scales multiply it. Correlation keeps only the sign of the scaling, which is the formal reason \(r\) is unit-free.

tests: 5.1, 3.1.4 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP5.6

PCA by hand on a 2×2 covariance matrix

Two centred features have covariance matrix

Σ = | 5  2 |
    | 2  2 |

(a) Find the eigenvalues of \(\Sigma\) by solving \(\det(\Sigma - \lambda I) = 0\).
(b) Find the unit eigenvectors.
(c) Write the first principal component \(PC_1\) as a linear combination of \(X_1\) and \(X_2\), and state the proportion of total variance it explains.
(d) Verify that \(\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 = \operatorname{trace}(\Sigma)\) and \(\lambda_1 \lambda_2 = \det(\Sigma)\), and explain why \(PC_1\) and \(PC_2\) are uncorrelated.

The characteristic equation is \((5 - \lambda)(2 - \lambda) - 4 = 0\).

(a)

\[ \lambda^2 - 7\lambda + 6 = 0 \]

so \(\lambda_1 = 6\) and \(\lambda_2 = 1\).

(b) For \(\lambda = 6\): \(-x_1 + 2x_2 = 0\), so \(v_1 = \frac{(2, 1)}{\sqrt{5}}\). For \(\lambda = 1\): \(4x_1 + 2x_2 = 0\), so \(v_2 = \frac{(1, -2)}{\sqrt{5}}\).

(c)

\[ PC_1 = \frac{2X_1 + X_2}{\sqrt{5}} \]

It explains \(\frac{6}{7} \approx 85.7\%\) of the total variance.

(d) \(6 + 1 = 7 = 5 + 2\) and \(6 \times 1 = 6 = 10 - 4\). The eigenvectors of a symmetric matrix are orthogonal, so in the rotated coordinates \(\Sigma\) becomes \(\operatorname{diag}(6, 1)\). The zero off-diagonal entries mean the PCs have zero covariance. This is the slides' phrase "diagonalising the covariance matrix".

Key insight: PCA re-expresses the data along directions with no covariance between them. Each eigenvalue is the variance captured along its eigenvector, so the eigenvalue ratios tell you how many dimensions you actually need.

tests: 5.1 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP5.7

Reading multicollinearity and explained variance

(a) A house-price model uses the following correlation matrix:

Size (sqft) Rooms Age Price
Size 1 0.96 −0.10 0.82
Rooms 0.96 1 −0.05 0.79
Age −0.10 −0.05 1 −0.35
Price 0.82 0.79 −0.35 1

An analyst fits Price on Size and Rooms and is puzzled to find that the coefficient of Rooms is negative and unstable. Use the matrix to explain why, and propose two remedies.

(b) PCA on four standardised sensor features gives eigenvalues 2.6, 1.0, 0.3 and 0.1. Give the proportion of variance explained by each component and the cumulative proportions. How many components keep at least 90% of the variance? Why must the eigenvalues sum to 4?

\(r = 0.96\) between two predictors means they carry almost the same information. For standardised data, \(\operatorname{trace}(R) = \text{number of features}\).

(a) Size and Rooms are almost collinear (\(r = 0.96\)). The regression cannot separate their individual effects, so many combinations of the two coefficients fit nearly equally well. The coefficient estimates have huge variance and can flip sign. This is multicollinearity. Both predictors still correlate strongly and positively with Price. Remedies: drop one of the pair (keep Size); combine them into a single feature such as \(PC_1\) or rooms-per-sqft; or use ridge regularisation.

(b) The proportions are 65%, 25%, 7.5% and 2.5%, with cumulative values 65%, 90%, 97.5% and 100%. Two components reach 90%. Standardised data has covariance matrix = correlation matrix \(R\), whose trace is \(1 + 1 + 1 + 1 = 4\), and the trace equals the sum of the eigenvalues.

Key insight: Before modelling, scan the correlation matrix. High predictor–predictor correlation is a warning about unstable coefficients. Large unequal eigenvalues show that the data really lives in fewer dimensions.

tests: 5.1, 5.2, 5.4 · L5 Mastery

8.B Pearson & Spearman Correlation (5.2–5.3)

L1 · FoundationP5.8

Correlation truths and myths

Mark each statement True or False and justify it.

(a) \(r = -0.90\) indicates a weaker relationship than \(r = 0.50\).
(b) \(r\) has no units.
(c) If \(r = 0\), \(X\) and \(Y\) are unrelated.
(d) Using the slide bands, \(r = 0.30\) is a "moderate positive" correlation.
(e) A strong correlation between two variables proves that one causes the other.
(f) A scatter of points lying exactly on a line falling from top-left to bottom-right gives \(r = -1\).
(g) \(r(X, Y) = r(Y, X)\).

The sign of \(r\) gives direction and \(\lvert r \rvert\) gives strength. "Linear" is the key qualifier in everything \(r\) measures.

(a) False. \(\lvert -0.90 \rvert > 0.50\), so it is the stronger association, just in the negative direction.
(b) True. It is covariance divided by \(\sigma_x \sigma_y\).
(c) False. \(r = 0\) rules out only a linear relationship (see P5.14).
(d) True. The band 0.26–0.75 is "intermediate/moderate".
(e) False. A confounder could drive both variables (see P5.16).
(f) True. This is perfect negative linear dependence.
(g) True. Correlation is symmetric, unlike the two regression slopes.

Key insight: \(r\) is a symmetric, unit-free measure of linear association. Strength is \(\lvert r \rvert\) and direction is the sign. Causation is outside what \(r\) can show.

tests: 5.2 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP5.9

Pearson r from raw sums

A small study records \(X\) = years of experience and \(Y\) = monthly output (units):

\(X\) 2 3 5 7 8
\(Y\) 4 5 9 12 14

Use

\[ r = \frac{n\sum XY - \sum X \sum Y}{\sqrt{\left[n\sum X^2 - \left(\sum X\right)^2\right]\left[n\sum Y^2 - \left(\sum Y\right)^2\right]}} \]

to compute \(r\), and classify it using the slide bands.

Compute the five sums first: \(\sum X\), \(\sum Y\), \(\sum X^2\), \(\sum Y^2\), \(\sum XY\).

The sums are \(\sum X = 25\), \(\sum Y = 44\), \(\sum X^2 = 151\), \(\sum Y^2 = 462\) and \(\sum XY = 264\), with \(n = 5\).

  • Numerator: \(5(264) - 25(44) = 1320 - 1100 = 220\).
  • Denominator: \(\sqrt{(755 - 625)(2310 - 1936)} = \sqrt{130 \times 374} = \sqrt{48620} = 220.50\).

\(r = 0.9977\), which is a strong positive correlation.

Key insight: The raw-sum formula is the deviation formula, \(\frac{S_{xy}}{\sqrt{S_{xx} S_{yy}}}\), with every term multiplied by \(n\). Keep the five sums organised and the rest is arithmetic.

tests: 5.2 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP5.10

Spearman's rank correlation (no ties)

Two judges rank 8 contestants:

Contestant A B C D E F G H
Judge 1 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8
Judge 2 2 1 4 3 7 5 8 6

Compute Spearman's \(R\) and interpret it.

\(R = 1 - \frac{6\sum d^2}{n(n^2 - 1)}\).

The rank differences are \(d = -1, 1, -1, 1, -2, 1, -1, 2\), so \(\sum d^2 = 1 + 1 + 1 + 1 + 4 + 1 + 1 + 4 = \mathbf{14}\).

\[ R = 1 - \frac{6(14)}{8 \times 63} = 1 - \frac{84}{504} = \mathbf{0.833} \]

The judges agree strongly.

Key insight: Spearman's \(R\) needs only the order of the observations. That makes it the natural tool for ordinal judgements such as beauty contests, ratings or honesty, where Pearson's \(r\) would be meaningless.

tests: 5.3 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP5.11

Spearman with tied ranks (exam style)

Marks of 10 students in Statistics (\(X\)) and Machine Learning (\(Y\)):

\(X\) 68 64 75 50 64 80 75 40 55 64
\(Y\) 62 58 68 45 81 60 68 48 50 70

Rank each variable with rank 1 for the highest score, giving tied values the average of the ranks they occupy. Compute Spearman's \(R\) with the tie correction:

\[ R = 1 - \frac{6\left[\sum d^2 + \sum \frac{m(m^2 - 1)}{12}\right]}{n(n^2 - 1)} \]

In \(X\), 75 appears twice (ranks 2 and 3, so each gets 2.5) and 64 appears three times (ranks 5, 6 and 7, so each gets 6). In \(Y\), 68 appears twice.

  • \(R_X = 4, 6, 2.5, 9, 6, 1, 2.5, 10, 8, 6\)
  • \(R_Y = 5, 7, 3.5, 10, 1, 6, 3.5, 9, 8, 2\)
  • \(d = -1, -1, -1, -1, 5, -5, -1, 1, 0, 4\), so \(\sum d^2 = 72\)

Tie groups: \(m = 2\) (\(X\): 75) contributes \(\frac{2 \cdot 3}{12} = 0.5\); \(m = 3\) (\(X\): 64) contributes \(\frac{3 \cdot 8}{12} = 2\); \(m = 2\) (\(Y\): 68) contributes 0.5. The correction factor is 3.

\[ R = 1 - \frac{6(72 + 3)}{10 \times 99} = 1 - \frac{450}{990} = \mathbf{0.545} \]

a moderate positive agreement.

A library that computes Pearson's \(r\) on the averaged ranks (for example scipy) gives 0.556. The textbook correction formula approximates that exact value.

Key insight: Ties reduce rank variance. The \(\frac{m(m^2 - 1)}{12}\) terms put that variance back, so \(R\) is not overstated. Add one term per tie group, in both variables.

tests: 5.3 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP5.12

Why |r| ≤ 1, and why r ignores units

(a) Using standardised scores \(z_x = \frac{x - \bar{x}}{s_x}\) and \(z_y = \frac{y - \bar{y}}{s_y}\), so that \(r = \frac{\sum z_x z_y}{n - 1}\), show that \(\sum (z_x \pm z_y)^2 \ge 0\) implies \(-1 \le r \le 1\). State when equality holds.
(b) Show that the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality \(\left(\sum uv\right)^2 \le \sum u^2 \cdot \sum v^2\), applied with \(u = x - \bar{x}\) and \(v = y - \bar{y}\), gives the same result.
(c) Show that \(r(aX + b, cY + d) = \operatorname{sign}(ac) \cdot r(X, Y)\), and so explain the slides' "change of origin and scale" (coded-variable) method \(u = \frac{x - A}{h}\).

\(\sum z_x^2 = \sum z_y^2 = n - 1\).

(a)

\[ 0 \le \sum (z_x \pm z_y)^2 = (n - 1) + (n - 1) \pm 2(n - 1)r = 2(n - 1)(1 \pm r) \]

Therefore \(1 + r \ge 0\) and \(1 - r \ge 0\), which gives \(-1 \le r \le 1\). Equality \(r = \pm 1\) holds exactly when \(z_y = \pm z_x\) for every point, so all points lie on a straight line.

(b) \(r^2 = \frac{\left(\sum uv\right)^2}{\sum u^2 \sum v^2} \le 1\) directly from the inequality. Equality holds iff \(v = ku\), which is again a perfect line.

(c) Using P5.5, \(\operatorname{Cov}(aX + b, cY + d) = ac \cdot \operatorname{Cov}\), and the standard deviations become \(\lvert a \rvert s_x\) and \(\lvert c \rvert s_y\). So

\[ r' = \frac{ac \cdot \operatorname{Cov}}{\lvert a \rvert \lvert c \rvert s_x s_y} = \mathbf{\operatorname{sign}(ac) \cdot r} \]

Coding \(u = \frac{x - A}{h}\) with \(h > 0\) leaves \(r\) unchanged, which is why the hand computations on the slides can use small coded numbers.

Key insight: \(r\) is the average product of z-scores. Its bound is a geometric fact (the cosine of the angle between the deviation vectors), and it is unit-free by construction.

tests: 5.2 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP5.13

Spearman is Pearson on ranks

With no ties, both variables have ranks \(1, 2, \ldots, n\). Using \(\sum r = \frac{n(n + 1)}{2}\) and \(\sum r^2 = \frac{n(n + 1)(2n + 1)}{6}\):

(a) Show that the sum of squared deviations of the ranks is \(S = \frac{n(n^2 - 1)}{12}\) for each variable.
(b) Writing \(d = r_x - r_y\), show that \(\sum (r_x - \bar{r})(r_y - \bar{r}) = S - \frac{\sum d^2}{2}\).
(c) Conclude that Pearson's \(r\) on the ranks equals \(1 - \frac{6\sum d^2}{n(n^2 - 1)}\).

\(\sum d^2 = \sum \left[(r_x - \bar{r}) - (r_y - \bar{r})\right]^2\). Expand the square.

(a)

\[ S = \sum r^2 - n\bar{r}^2 = \frac{n(n + 1)(2n + 1)}{6} - \frac{n(n + 1)^2}{4} = \frac{n(n + 1)\left[2(2n + 1) - 3(n + 1)\right]}{12} = \mathbf{\frac{n(n^2 - 1)}{12}} \]

(b) \(\sum d^2 = S + S - 2\sum (r_x - \bar{r})(r_y - \bar{r})\), so the cross-product sum is \(S - \frac{\sum d^2}{2}\).

(c)

\[ r_{\text{ranks}} = \frac{S - \sum d^2/2}{S} = 1 - \frac{\sum d^2}{2S} = 1 - \frac{\sum d^2}{n(n^2 - 1)/6} = \mathbf{1 - \frac{6\sum d^2}{n(n^2 - 1)}} \]

∎

Key insight: Spearman's \(R\) is Pearson's \(r\) applied to ranks. It inherits all of \(r\)'s properties but measures monotone rather than linear association. Ties break the identity in (a), and that is why a correction is needed.

tests: 5.3, 5.2 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP5.14

Perfect dependence, zero correlation

Let \(x = -3, -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, 3\) and \(y = x^2\).

(a) Compute Pearson's \(r\) and Spearman's \(R\).
(b) \(Y\) is a deterministic function of \(X\). Reconcile that with your answers.
(c) A data scientist screens features by keeping only those with \(\lvert r \rvert > 0.3\) against the target. Explain what this filter can throw away, and name a tool that would catch this kind of relationship.

Compute \(\sum (x - \bar{x})(y - \bar{y})\) with \(\bar{x} = 0\). Think of the left and right halves of the parabola cancelling.

(a) \(\bar{x} = 0\), so

\[ \sum (x - \bar{x})(y - \bar{y}) = \sum x^3 - \bar{y}\sum x = 0 - 0 = 0 \]

giving \(r = 0\). \(R = 0\) as well, because the ranks of \(y\) rise and fall symmetrically.

(b) Both coefficients measure a specific kind of association: linear for \(r\), monotone for \(R\). The parabola is neither. The left half contributes negative cross-products and the right half equal positive ones, so they cancel.

(c) The filter discards strong non-monotone signals such as U-shapes or periodic patterns. Better tools are a scatter plot, binned means, mutual information, or adding \(x^2\) as a feature.

Key insight: \(r = 0\) means "no straight-line trend", not "no relationship". Always look at the scatter plot.

tests: 5.2, 5.3 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP5.15

One point can manufacture a correlation

Eight observations:

\(x\) 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8
\(y\) 5 3 6 2 5 4 6 3

(a) Compute Pearson's \(r\) and Spearman's \(R\).
(b) A ninth point, \((25, 30)\), is added, perhaps a data-entry error. Recompute both.
(c) What does this show about leverage, and which coefficient is more robust? What should a preprocessing pipeline do?

The new point is far from \(\bar{x}\) and far from \(\bar{y}\), so its single cross-product dominates \(\sum (x - \bar{x})(y - \bar{y})\).

(a) \(r \approx -0.039\) and \(R \approx -0.024\): essentially no association.

(b) With \((25, 30)\) added, \(r \approx 0.934\) but \(R \approx 0.287\).

(c) A single high-leverage point (extreme in \(x\)) that also has an extreme \(y\) can create a "strong" Pearson correlation out of noise. Rank-based \(R\) limits that point's influence to "largest rank in both variables", so it moves far less. A pipeline should plot the data, flag points outside the IQR fences (1.1.5), investigate them before deleting, and report robust measures next to \(r\).

Key insight: Pearson's \(r\) is a mean of products, so it inherits the mean's sensitivity to outliers. When \(R\) and \(r\) disagree sharply, look for influential points.

tests: 5.2, 5.3 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP5.16

Confounding: ice cream and drownings

City data across 36 months shows \(r(\text{ice-cream sales}, \text{drownings}) = 0.85\). Both variables also correlate with temperature: \(r(\text{temp}, \text{ice-cream}) = 0.90\) and \(r(\text{temp}, \text{drownings}) = 0.90\).

(a) A journalist writes "ice cream causes drowning". Critique this.
(b) The partial correlation of \(X\) and \(Y\) controlling for \(Z\) is

\[ r_{xy\cdot z} = \frac{r_{xy} - r_{xz} r_{yz}}{\sqrt{(1 - r_{xz}^2)(1 - r_{yz}^2)}} \]

Compute it and interpret.
(c) Which time-series tool in Module 6 is built on this same "remove the intermediate influence" idea?

Substitute directly: the numerator is \(0.85 - 0.81\).

(a) Correlation is not causation. Temperature is a plausible confounder that drives both ice-cream sales and swimming, and swimming leads to drownings.

(b)

\[ r_{xy\cdot z} = \frac{0.85 - 0.81}{\sqrt{0.19 \times 0.19}} = \frac{0.04}{0.19} \approx \mathbf{0.21} \]

Once temperature is held fixed, only a weak association remains. Most of the 0.85 was due to the common cause.

(c) The PACF (6.5): the correlation between \(Y_t\) and \(Y_{t-k}\) after removing the influence of the intermediate lags.

Key insight: A high correlation can be entirely inherited from a third variable. Partial correlation, and later multiple regression, ask what is left after the confounder is accounted for.

tests: 5.2 · L5 Mastery

8.C Simple Linear Regression & Regression Lines (5.4)

L1 · FoundationP5.17

Anatomy of a regression model

For the model \(\text{Revenue} = a + b \cdot (\text{TV ad spend}) + \varepsilon\):

(a) Which variable is the response and which is the predictor?
(b) Interpret \(a\) and \(b\) in words.
(c) List the four assumptions placed on \(\varepsilon\) (CS-11).
(d) True or False: the regression line of \(Y\) on \(X\) and the regression line of \(X\) on \(Y\) are the same line unless \(r = \pm 1\).
(e) True or False: least squares minimises \(\sum \lvert y_i - \hat{y}_i \rvert\).

Recall the CS-11 list of assumptions on the error term, and the name of the method.

(a) Revenue is the response (dependent) variable; TV ad spend is the predictor (independent) variable.
(b) \(a\) is the expected revenue at zero TV spend (the baseline). \(b\) is the expected change in revenue per one-unit increase in TV spend.
(c) \(\varepsilon\) has mean 0; \(\varepsilon\) has constant variance \(\sigma^2\) for all \(X\); the errors are independent; \(\varepsilon\) is normally distributed.
(d) True. The two lines coincide only when every point lies on one line.
(e) False. Least squares minimises \(\sum (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2\). Minimising absolute deviations is a different method (LAD regression).

Key insight: Regression is asymmetric. It predicts \(Y\) from \(X\) by minimising vertical squared errors, and that asymmetry is why there are two different regression lines.

tests: 5.4 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP5.18

Fitting a least-squares line

\(X\) (ads, ₹ lakh) 1 2 3 4 5 6
\(Y\) (sales, ₹ lakh) 3 5 6 8 11 12

(a) Compute \(b = \frac{\sum xy}{\sum x^2}\) using deviations \(x = X - \bar{X}\) and \(y = Y - \bar{Y}\), then \(a = \bar{Y} - b\bar{X}\).
(b) Predict sales when \(X = 7\).
(c) Interpret the slope.

\(\bar{X} = 3.5\) and \(\bar{Y} = 7.5\).

\(x = -2.5, -1.5, -0.5, 0.5, 1.5, 2.5\) and \(y = -4.5, -2.5, -1.5, 0.5, 3.5, 4.5\).
\(\sum xy = 32.5\) and \(\sum x^2 = 17.5\).

(a)

\[ b = \frac{32.5}{17.5} = \mathbf{1.857}, \qquad a = 7.5 - 1.857 \times 3.5 = \mathbf{1.0} \]

so \(\hat{Y} = 1.0 + 1.857X\).
(b) \(\hat{Y}(7) = 1 + 13 = 14.0\) lakh. \(X = 7\) lies just outside the observed range 1–6, a mild extrapolation.
(c) Each extra ₹1 lakh of advertising is associated with about ₹1.86 lakh more sales.

Key insight: The slope is a ratio of co-variation to \(X\)-variation (\(\frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}}\)). The intercept is chosen so that the line passes through the point of means.

tests: 5.4 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP5.19

Both regression lines from summary statistics

For weekly data on advertising spend \(X\) and sales \(Y\): \(\bar{X} = 60\), \(\sigma_x = 8\), \(\bar{Y} = 150\), \(\sigma_y = 20\) and \(r = 0.75\).

(a) Find the regression line of \(Y\) on \(X\) and the regression line of \(X\) on \(Y\).
(b) Estimate \(Y\) when \(X = 70\).
(c) Estimate \(X\) when \(Y = 170\).
(d) Why is it wrong to answer (c) by solving the \(Y\)-on-\(X\) line for \(X\)?

\(b_{yx} = r \cdot \frac{\sigma_y}{\sigma_x}\) and \(b_{xy} = r \cdot \frac{\sigma_x}{\sigma_y}\).

\[ b_{yx} = 0.75 \times \frac{20}{8} = \mathbf{1.875}, \qquad b_{xy} = 0.75 \times \frac{8}{20} = \mathbf{0.3} \]

(a) \(Y - 150 = 1.875(X - 60)\), i.e. \(Y = 37.5 + 1.875X\).
\(X - 60 = 0.3(Y - 150)\), i.e. \(X = 15 + 0.3Y\).
(b) \(\hat{Y} = 37.5 + 131.25 = 168.75\).
(c) \(\hat{X} = 15 + 51 = 66\).
(d) Inverting the \(Y\)-on-\(X\) line gives \(X = \frac{170 - 37.5}{1.875} = 70.67\), which is a different answer. The \(Y\)-on-\(X\) line minimises errors in \(Y\), so it is the wrong tool for predicting \(X\). Use the \(X\)-on-\(Y\) line, which minimises horizontal errors.

Key insight: Each regression line answers one direction of question. Choose the line whose dependent variable is the one you are predicting.

tests: 5.4 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP5.20

Two regression lines given as equations (exam style)

The two regression lines for a bivariate dataset are
\(L_1: 3x + 2y = 26\) and \(L_2: 6x + y = 31\).

(a) Find \(\bar{x}\) and \(\bar{y}\).
(b) Decide which line is \(Y\) on \(X\) and which is \(X\) on \(Y\), and justify your choice.
(c) Find \(r\).
(d) If \(\sigma_x = 2\), find \(\sigma_y\).

Try one assignment, compute \(b_{yx} b_{xy}\), and reject the assignment if the product exceeds 1.

(a) Solve the pair: from \(L_2\), \(y = 31 - 6x\). Substituting into \(L_1\) gives

\[ 3x + 62 - 12x = 26 \]

so \(\bar{x} = 4\) and \(\bar{y} = 7\). The regression lines always intersect at the means.

(b) Assignment 1: \(L_1\) is \(Y\) on \(X\), giving \(y = 13 - 1.5x\) (\(b_{yx} = -1.5\)). \(L_2\) is \(X\) on \(Y\), giving \(x = \frac{31 - y}{6}\) (\(b_{xy} = -\frac{1}{6}\)). The product is \(0.25 \le 1\). ✓
Assignment 2: \(L_1\) is \(X\) on \(Y\), giving \(b_{xy} = -\frac{2}{3}\). \(L_2\) is \(Y\) on \(X\), giving \(b_{yx} = -6\). The product is \(4 > 1\), which is impossible because \(r^2 \le 1\). ✗
So \(L_1\) is the \(Y\)-on-\(X\) line and \(L_2\) is the \(X\)-on-\(Y\) line.

(c) \(r^2 = 0.25\) and both slopes are negative, so \(r = -0.5\).

(d)

\[ b_{yx} = r \cdot \frac{\sigma_y}{\sigma_x} \;\Rightarrow\; -1.5 = -0.5 \cdot \frac{\sigma_y}{2} \]

so \(\sigma_y = 6\). Check: \(b_{xy} = -0.5 \times \frac{2}{6} = -\frac{1}{6}\) ✓.

Key insight: Three facts solve every "two regression lines" question. The lines meet at \((\bar{x}, \bar{y})\). \(b_{yx} b_{xy} = r^2\) must be \(\le 1\). \(r\) takes the common sign of the two slopes.

tests: 5.4, 5.2 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP5.21

Full analysis from computational sums (exam style)

For 8 weeks of advertising (\(X\)) and sales (\(Y\)), the following sums are given: \(n = 8\), \(\sum X = 150\), \(\sum Y = 368\), \(\sum X^2 = 3076\), \(\sum Y^2 = 17814\), \(\sum XY = 7381\).

(a) Compute \(S_{xy}\), \(S_{xx}\) and \(S_{yy}\), and from them \(r\).
(b) Find the regression line of \(Y\) on \(X\) and the regression line of \(X\) on \(Y\).
(c) Estimate \(Y\) when \(X = 22\), and \(X\) when \(Y = 55\).
(d) Verify that \(b_{yx} b_{xy} = r^2\), and state the proportion of variation in sales explained by advertising.

\(S_{xy} = \sum XY - \frac{\sum X \sum Y}{n}\), and similarly for \(S_{xx}\) and \(S_{yy}\).

(a)

\[ S_{xy} = 7381 - \frac{150 \times 368}{8} = \mathbf{481}, \qquad S_{xx} = 3076 - \frac{150^2}{8} = \mathbf{263.5}, \qquad S_{yy} = 17814 - \frac{368^2}{8} = \mathbf{886} \]

\[ r = \frac{481}{\sqrt{263.5 \times 886}} = \mathbf{0.9955} \]

(b) The means are \(\bar{X} = 18.75\) and \(\bar{Y} = 46\).

  • \(b_{yx} = \frac{481}{263.5} = 1.8254\), so \(\hat{Y} = 11.77 + 1.825X\).
  • \(b_{xy} = \frac{481}{886} = 0.5429\), so \(\hat{X} = -6.22 + 0.543Y\).

(c) \(\hat{Y}(22) = 46 + 1.8254 \times 3.25 \approx 51.93\). \(\hat{X}(55) = 18.75 + 0.5429 \times 9 \approx 23.64\).

(d) \(1.8254 \times 0.5429 = \mathbf{0.991 = r^2}\). About 99.1% of the variation in sales is explained by the linear relation with advertising.

Key insight: \(S_{xx}\), \(S_{yy}\) and \(S_{xy}\) give you everything at once: \(r = \frac{S_{xy}}{\sqrt{S_{xx} S_{yy}}}\), \(b_{yx} = \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}}\) and \(b_{xy} = \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{yy}}\).

tests: 5.2, 5.4, 5.5 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP5.22

Deriving the normal equations and their consequences

(a) Minimise \(S(a, b) = \sum (y_i - a - bx_i)^2\). Set \(\frac{\partial S}{\partial a} = 0\) and \(\frac{\partial S}{\partial b} = 0\), and show that they give the normal equations
\(\sum y = na + b\sum x\) and \(\sum xy = a\sum x + b\sum x^2\).
(b) From the first equation, show that the fitted line passes through \((\bar{x}, \bar{y})\) and that the residuals sum to zero, \(\sum e_i = 0\).
(c) From the second equation, show that \(\sum x_i e_i = 0\).
(d) Solve the equations to show that \(b = \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}}\).
(e) Check (b) and (c) on the residuals of P5.18: 0.143, 0.286, −0.571, −0.429, 0.714, −0.143.

\(\frac{\partial S}{\partial a} = -2\sum (y_i - a - bx_i) = -2\sum e_i\).

(a)

\[ \frac{\partial S}{\partial a} = -2\sum (y_i - a - bx_i) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; \mathbf{\sum y = na + b\sum x} \]

\[ \frac{\partial S}{\partial b} = -2\sum x_i(y_i - a - bx_i) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; \mathbf{\sum xy = a\sum x + b\sum x^2} \]

(b) Dividing the first equation by \(n\) gives \(\bar{y} = a + b\bar{x}\), so the line passes through \((\bar{x}, \bar{y})\). The derivative condition is literally \(\sum e_i = 0\).

(c) The second condition is literally \(\sum x_i e_i = 0\): the residuals are uncorrelated with \(x\).

(d) Substitute \(a = \bar{y} - b\bar{x}\) into the second equation:

\[ \sum xy - \bar{x}\sum y = b\left(\sum x^2 - \bar{x}\sum x\right) \]

so \(b = \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}}\).

(e) The residuals sum to \(0.143 + 0.286 - 0.571 - 0.429 + 0.714 - 0.143 = \mathbf{0}\) ✓.
\(\sum x e = 1(0.143) + 2(0.286) + 3(-0.571) + 4(-0.429) + 5(0.714) + 6(-0.143) = \mathbf{0}\) ✓, up to rounding.

Key insight: The fitted line is defined by two orthogonality conditions: the residuals are orthogonal to the constant 1 and to \(x\). This geometric view extends directly to multiple regression and to AR models fitted by least squares (6.6).

tests: 5.4 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP5.23

Properties of the regression coefficients

(a) Prove that \(b_{yx} b_{xy} = r^2\).
(b) Deduce that \(b_{yx}\) and \(b_{xy}\) always have the same sign as \(r\), and that both cannot exceed 1 in absolute value.
(c) For \(r > 0\), prove that \(\frac{b_{yx} + b_{xy}}{2} \ge r\), i.e. the arithmetic mean of the slopes is at least \(r\).
(d) A student reports \(b_{yx} = 2.5\) and \(b_{xy} = 0.6\). Is this possible? What about \(b_{yx} = 1.2\) and \(b_{xy} = -0.4\)?

Write \(b_{yx} = \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}}\) and \(b_{xy} = \frac{S_{xy}}{S_{yy}}\). For (c), use AM \(\ge\) GM.

(a)

\[ b_{yx} b_{xy} = \frac{S_{xy}^2}{S_{xx} S_{yy}} = \mathbf{r^2} \]

(b) Both slopes share the sign of \(S_{xy}\), which is the sign of \(r\). If \(\lvert b_{yx} \rvert > 1\) and \(\lvert b_{xy} \rvert > 1\), the product would exceed 1, contradicting \(r^2 \le 1\).

(c) For \(r > 0\) both slopes are positive, so AM \(\ge\) GM gives

\[ \frac{b_{yx} + b_{xy}}{2} \ge \sqrt{b_{yx} b_{xy}} = \sqrt{r^2} = r \]

∎

(d) \(2.5 \times 0.6 = 1.5 > 1\), which is impossible. 1.2 and −0.4 have opposite signs, which is impossible.

Key insight: \(r\) is the geometric mean of the two regression slopes. That single identity polices the consistency of any reported pair of slopes.

tests: 5.4, 5.2 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP5.24

The danger of extrapolation

Average weights of children by age:

Age (years) 1 2 3 4 5 6
Weight (kg) 10.0 12.2 14.1 16.3 18.2 20.4

(a) Fit the least-squares line and report \(r\).
(b) Predict the weight at age 3.5 and at age 40.
(c) Which prediction is trustworthy, and why? Relate your answer to the scope of a regression model and to what "\(r = 0.9998\)" does not guarantee.

The fitted slope is a little over 2 kg per year.

(a)

\[ \hat{W} = 7.98 + 2.063 \cdot \text{Age} \]

with \(r = 0.9998\).

(b) Age 3.5: about 15.2 kg. Age 40: about 90.5 kg.

(c) The age-3.5 value is an interpolation inside the data range and is reliable. The age-40 value is an extrapolation: growth stops in adulthood, so the linear relationship does not hold beyond the observed range of 1–6 years. A near-perfect \(r\) certifies linearity only within the sampled range.

Key insight: A regression model describes the data it was fitted on. Outside the range of \(X\), predictions rest on an untested assumption, and in ML this shows up as the failure of models under distribution shift.

tests: 5.4 · L5 Mastery

8.D Goodness of Fit r² (5.5)

L2 · ComputationP5.25

Decomposing variation

Using the data and fitted line of P5.18 (\(\hat{Y} = 1 + 1.857X\)):

(a) Compute \(\text{SST} = \sum (Y - \bar{Y})^2\), \(\text{SSR} = \sum (\hat{Y} - \bar{Y})^2\) and \(\text{SSE} = \sum (Y - \hat{Y})^2\).
(b) Verify that \(\text{SST} = \text{SSR} + \text{SSE}\), and compute \(r^2 = \frac{\text{SSR}}{\text{SST}}\).
(c) Check that \(r^2\) equals the square of Pearson's \(r\) for the same data.
(d) Compute the standard error of the estimate, \(s_e = \sqrt{\frac{\text{SSE}}{n - 2}}\).

\(\text{SST} = \sum y^2\) in deviation form, which equals 61.5 here.

(a) \(\text{SST} = 61.5\), \(\text{SSR} = 60.357\) and \(\text{SSE} = 1.143\). SSE is the sum of the squared residuals from P5.22(e).

(b) \(60.357 + 1.143 = 61.5\) ✓. \(r^2 = 0.9814\).

(c)

\[ r = \frac{32.5}{\sqrt{17.5 \times 61.5}} = 0.9907, \qquad 0.9907^2 = \mathbf{0.9814} \]

✓.

(d)

\[ s_e = \sqrt{\frac{1.143}{4}} \approx \mathbf{0.535 \text{ lakh}} \]

Key insight: \(r^2\) is the share of \(Y\)'s variation that the line accounts for. In simple linear regression it equals Pearson's \(r\) squared, so it inherits \(r\)'s limits: it measures linear fit only.

tests: 5.5, 5.4 · L2 Computation
L1 · FoundationP5.26

Reading r² correctly

A regression of exam score on study hours (\(n = 20\)) gives \(\text{SST} = 500\) and \(\text{SSE} = 180\).

(a) Compute \(r^2\), and state \(\lvert r \rvert\). What decides the sign of \(r\)?
(b) True or False: "64% of students' scores are predicted correctly".
(c) True or False: adding any extra predictor can never decrease \(r^2\).
(d) True or False: a high \(r^2\) proves that the linear model is appropriate.
(e) Compute \(s_e = \sqrt{\frac{\text{SSE}}{n - 2}}\).

\(r^2 = 1 - \frac{\text{SSE}}{\text{SST}}\).

(a)

\[ r^2 = 1 - \frac{180}{500} = \mathbf{0.64} \]

so \(\lvert r \rvert = 0.8\). The sign of \(r\) is the sign of the slope \(b\).

(b) False. 64% of the variation in scores is explained by the linear relation. The statement is not about individual students.

(c) True for least squares on the same data. That is why adjusted \(R^2\) exists.

(d) False. A curved pattern can still give a high \(r^2\) (P5.28, P5.29). Check the residuals.

(e)

\[ s_e = \sqrt{\frac{180}{18}} \approx \mathbf{3.16 \text{ marks}} \]

Key insight: \(r^2\) describes how much variance is explained, not how often predictions are correct, and not whether the model's form is correct.

tests: 5.5 · L1 Foundation
L4 · AnalysisP5.27

Proving the variance decomposition

(a) Write \(y_i - \bar{y} = (\hat{y}_i - \bar{y}) + (y_i - \hat{y}_i)\). Square and sum, and show that the cross term \(\sum (\hat{y}_i - \bar{y})(y_i - \hat{y}_i) = 0\) using the results of P5.22. Conclude that \(\text{SST} = \text{SSR} + \text{SSE}\).
(b) Using \(\hat{y}_i - \bar{y} = b(x_i - \bar{x})\), show that \(\text{SSR} = b^2 S_{xx} = \frac{S_{xy}^2}{S_{xx}}\), and hence that \(\frac{\text{SSR}}{\text{SST}} = r^2\).
(c) Explain the connection to one-way ANOVA (4.7): what plays the role of SSTR and SSE there?

\(\hat{y}_i - \bar{y} = b(x_i - \bar{x})\). Then the cross term equals \(b \cdot \sum (x_i - \bar{x})e_i\).

(a)

\[ \text{Cross term} = b \cdot \sum (x_i - \bar{x})e_i = b\left[\sum x_i e_i - \bar{x} \cdot \sum e_i\right] = b[0 - 0] = 0 \]

by P5.22(b) and (c). So \(\text{SST} = \text{SSR} + \text{SSE}\).

(b)

\[ \text{SSR} = \sum b^2(x_i - \bar{x})^2 = b^2 S_{xx} = \left(\frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}}\right)^2 \cdot S_{xx} = \frac{S_{xy}^2}{S_{xx}} \]

Then \(\frac{\text{SSR}}{\text{SST}} = \frac{S_{xy}^2}{S_{xx} S_{yy}} = r^2\). ∎

(c) In ANOVA, \(\text{SST} = \text{SSTR} + \text{SSE}\). "Treatment" (group membership) plays the role of the regressor, so SSTR is the variation explained by group means, just as SSR is the variation explained by the line. Both are partitions of total squared deviation, which is Big Idea B in Part 2.

Key insight: The decomposition works because the residuals are orthogonal to the fitted values. Every "explained versus unexplained" ratio in the course, including \(r^2\), the \(F\) in ANOVA and forecast MSE, relies on this structure.

tests: 5.5, 5.4 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP5.28

A residual plot that exposes curvature

\(x\) 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8
\(y\) 2.1 2.9 4.2 6.1 8.8 12.2 16.1 20.9

(a) Fit a straight line and report \(r^2\).
(b) Compute the residuals and describe the sign pattern.
(c) Fit a quadratic \(y = a + bx + cx^2\) and compare its SSE with the line's SSE.
(d) A colleague says "\(r^2 = 0.94\), ship it." Respond.

Look at the signs of the residuals across \(x\): + + then − − − − then + +.

(a)

\[ \hat{y} = -2.854 + 2.670x \]

with \(r^2 = 0.941\).

(b) The residuals are 2.28, 0.41, −0.96, −1.73, −1.70, −0.97, 0.26 and 2.39. They are positive at both ends and negative in the middle: a U-shape. This is systematic curvature, not random noise.

(c) The quadratic

\[ \hat{y} = 2.173 - 0.346x + 0.335x^2 \]

has \(\text{SSE} = 0.024\), compared with 18.89 for the line, a reduction of more than 99%.

(d) A high \(r^2\) does not show the model is right. The residual pattern violates the assumption that the errors are random with mean 0 at every \(x\). The line will under-predict at large \(x\), so any forecast in the growing region will be biased. Use the curved model.

Key insight: Always diagnose a fit with residuals. Structure left in the residuals is signal the model failed to capture.

tests: 5.5, 5.6, 5.4 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP5.29

Same statistics, different stories (Anscombe trap)

Two datasets share the same \(x\) values: \(x = 10, 8, 13, 9, 11, 14, 6, 4, 12, 7, 5\).

  • \(y(\text{I}) = 8.04, 6.95, 7.58, 8.81, 8.33, 9.96, 7.24, 4.26, 10.84, 4.82, 5.68\)
  • \(y(\text{II}) = 9.14, 8.14, 8.74, 8.77, 9.26, 8.10, 6.13, 3.10, 9.13, 7.26, 4.74\)

(a) Show that both datasets give (to 2 decimals) \(\bar{y} = 7.50\), the same regression line \(\hat{y} = 3.00 + 0.50x\), and \(r = 0.82\).
(b) Compute the residuals for dataset II at \(x = 4, 8, 11, 14\), and describe the pattern you would see in a scatter plot.
(c) What lesson does this give about automated pipelines that report only summary statistics?

For dataset II, plot \(y\) against \(x\). The points trace a curve.

(a) For both datasets, \(\bar{y} \approx 7.50\), \(\hat{y} \approx 3.00 + 0.500x\) and \(r \approx 0.816\). The sample variances of \(y\) are also both \(\approx 4.13\).

(b) For dataset II the residuals are \(x = 4\): −1.90; \(x = 8\): +1.14; \(x = 11\): +0.76; \(x = 14\): −1.90. They are negative at both ends and positive in the middle, which is an inverted U. Dataset II is a smooth parabola: a curved relationship with essentially no noise. Dataset I is a genuinely linear relationship with random scatter.

(c) Identical means, variances, \(r\), line and \(r^2\) can come from completely different structures. A pipeline that reports only numbers can approve a misspecified model. Plot the scatter and the residuals before trusting any model.

Key insight: Summary statistics compress the data and discard its shape. Plot first, then summarise. These datasets are Anscombe's (1973) quartet I and II.

tests: 5.2, 5.4, 5.5 · L5 Mastery

8.E Curve Fitting (5.6)

L1 · FoundationP5.30

Which transformation linearises which model?

For each model, give the transformed variables \((U, V)\) in which it becomes the straight line \(V = A + BU\), and say how \(a\) and \(b\) are recovered from \(A\) and \(B\).

(a) \(y = a x^b\) (power)
(b) \(y = a e^{bx}\) (exponential)
(c) \(y = a b^x\)
(d) \(y = a + \frac{b}{x}\)
(e) \(y = a + bx + cx^2\): can it be linearised by a log transform?

Take logs where a product or power appears. Substitute where a reciprocal appears.

(a) \(\log y = \log a + b \log x\). \(U = \log x\), \(V = \log y\), \(a = 10^A\) and \(b = B\).
(b) \(\ln y = \ln a + b x\). \(U = x\), \(V = \ln y\), \(a = e^A\) and \(b = B\). With \(\log_{10}\): \(B = b \log_{10}(e)\), so \(b = \frac{B}{0.4343}\).
(c) \(\log y = \log a + x \log b\). \(U = x\), \(V = \log y\), \(a = 10^A\) and \(b = 10^B\).
(d) \(U = \frac{1}{x}\), \(V = y\). The model is linear directly, with \(a = A\) and \(b = B\).
(e) No log transform is needed or helpful. The parabola is already linear in its parameters, so fit it with three normal equations (P5.31).

Key insight: Least squares needs linearity in the parameters, not in \(x\). A log transform is a way to reach that form, but it changes the error structure (P5.34).

tests: 5.6 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP5.31

Fitting a parabola by normal equations

\(x\) −2 −1 0 1 2
\(y\) 9.2 3.8 1.1 2.9 8.8

Fit \(y = a + bx + cx^2\) using

\[ \begin{aligned} \sum y &= na + b\sum x + c\sum x^2 \\ \sum xy &= a\sum x + b\sum x^2 + c\sum x^3 \\ \sum x^2 y &= a\sum x^2 + b\sum x^3 + c\sum x^4 \end{aligned} \]

The \(x\) values are symmetric about 0, so \(\sum x = \sum x^3 = 0\). \(\sum x^2 = 10\) and \(\sum x^4 = 34\).

The sums are \(\sum y = 25.8\), \(\sum xy = -1.7\) and \(\sum x^2 y = 78.7\). The equations become

\[ 5a + 10c = 25.8, \qquad 10b = -1.7, \qquad 10a + 34c = 78.7 \]

So \(b = -0.17\). Solving the other two: from the first, \(a = 5.16 - 2c\). Then \(51.6 - 20c + 34c = 78.7\), so \(14c = 27.1\), giving \(c = 1.936\) and \(a = 1.289\).

\[ \hat{y} = \mathbf{1.289 - 0.170x + 1.936x^2} \]

Key insight: Coding \(x\) symmetrically about 0 makes the odd-power sums vanish and splits the system into small pieces. The same trick appears in least-squares trend fitting with coded time (6.2).

tests: 5.6 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP5.32

Exponential growth curve

A bacterial culture is counted every hour:

\(t\) (h) 0 1 2 3 4 5
\(N\) 120 190 310 480 770 1250

(a) Fit \(N = a e^{bt}\) by regressing \(\ln N\) on \(t\).
(b) Express the same model as \(N = a B^t\) and interpret \(B\).
(c) Predict \(N\) at \(t = 7\) and compute the doubling time.

The \(\ln N\) values are 4.7875, 5.2470, 5.7366, 6.1738, 6.6464, 7.1309. \(\sum t = 15\), \(\sum t^2 = 55\), \(\sum \ln N = 35.722\), \(\sum t \ln N = 97.482\).

(a) Solve

\[ 35.722 = 6A + 15b, \qquad 97.482 = 15A + 55b \]

This gives \(b \approx 0.4672\) and \(A \approx 4.7857\), so \(a = e^A \approx \mathbf{119.8}\).
\(\hat{N} = 119.8 e^{0.4672t}\).

(b) \(B = e^b = \mathbf{1.596}\). The culture grows by about 59.6% per hour. Equivalently, a \(\log_{10}\) fit gives \(\log_{10} B = 0.2029\).

(c)

\[ \hat{N}(7) \approx 119.8 e^{3.270} \approx \mathbf{3150} \]

(3153 unrounded). The doubling time is \(\frac{\ln 2}{b} \approx 1.48\) h.

Key insight: Exponential growth looks linear in the log scale. The slope there is the continuous growth rate, and the doubling time is \(\ln 2\) divided by that rate.

tests: 5.6 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP5.33

Power curve

The time (s) to train a model depends on the dataset size \(x\) (thousand rows):

\(x\) 1 2 4 8 16
\(y\) 3.0 4.9 8.1 13.0 21.5

(a) Fit \(y = a x^b\) using \(X = \log_{10} x\) and \(Y = \log_{10} y\).
(b) Interpret \(b\): by what factor does training time grow when the data doubles?
(c) Predict \(y\) at \(x = 32\).

\(\sum X = 3.0103\), \(\sum Y = 4.5222\), \(\sum X^2 = 2.7186\), \(\sum XY = 3.3651\), \(n = 5\).

(a) The normal equations are

\[ 4.5222 = 5A + 3.0103b, \qquad 3.3651 = 3.0103A + 2.7186b \]

This gives \(b \approx 0.709\) and \(A \approx 0.4776\), so \(a = 10^A \approx \mathbf{3.00}\).
\(\hat{y} = 3.00 x^{0.709}\).

(b) Doubling \(x\) multiplies \(y\) by \(2^{0.709} \approx \mathbf{1.63}\). The growth is sub-linear, since \(b < 1\).

(c)

\[ \hat{y}(32) \approx 3.00 \times 32^{0.709} \approx \mathbf{35.1 \text{ s}} \]

Key insight: In a power law, \(b\) is an elasticity: a 1% increase in \(x\) gives about a \(b\)% increase in \(y\). That is why scaling laws in ML are drawn on log-log axes.

tests: 5.6 · L3 Application
L5 · MasteryP5.34

Linear or exponential? Let the errors decide

The monthly active users (thousand) of an app:

\(t\) (month) 1 2 3 4 5 6
\(y\) 5.0 7.1 10.3 14.8 21.0 30.2

(a) Fit a linear model and an exponential model (via \(\ln y\)). Compute each model's SSE in the original units of \(y\).
(b) Forecast month 8 with both models.
(c) Which model do you choose? Point out the subtlety that the log-fit minimises SSE on the log scale, not in \(y\)-units.
(d) Is choosing the model by in-sample SSE alone a sound practice? What would you do instead (link to 6.3)?

Compute \(\hat{y}\) from each model at \(t = 1 \ldots 6\), then \(\sum (y - \hat{y})^2\).

(a) Linear: \(\hat{y} = -2.487 + 4.920t\), with \(\text{SSE} \approx 27.54\).
Exponential: \(\hat{y} = 3.481 e^{0.3602t}\), with \(\text{SSE} \approx 0.021\). Users grow by about 43% per month.

(b) Month 8: linear ≈ 36.9 thousand; exponential ≈ 62.1 thousand. The two forecasts differ greatly, and the gap widens with the horizon.

(c) Choose the exponential model. Its errors are about 1300× smaller and the ratios \(\frac{y(t+1)}{y(t)}\) are roughly constant (\(\approx 1.43\)), which is the signature of exponential growth. The fit on \(\ln y\) is not the least-squares fit in \(y\)-units, because it down-weights large values. It is still a valid candidate, but the two models must be compared on the same scale (here, \(y\)-units).

(d) In-sample SSE favours flexible models and says nothing about the future. Hold out the last few months, compare out-of-sample MSE, MAD or MAPE (6.3), and ask whether exponential growth is plausible, since market saturation eventually bends the curve.

Key insight: Choose the functional form using diagnostics (constant ratios versus constant differences) and validation error measured on the original scale. When forecasts diverge this much, the choice of form matters more than the parameter values.

tests: 5.6, 5.5, 5.4 · L5 Mastery

8.F Module 5 Capstone

L5 · MasteryP5.35

Study hours and exam scores: complete bivariate analysis

Student 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8
Hours (\(h\)) 2 3 4 5 6 7 8 9
Score (\(s\)) 52 55 61 60 68 71 75 82

(a) Compute the sample covariance and Pearson's \(r\). Interpret both.
(b) Compute Spearman's \(R\). Why is it slightly below 1 although the scores almost always rise?
(c) Fit the regression of score on hours, and the regression of hours on score. Verify that their slopes multiply to \(r^2\).
(d) Compute \(r^2\) and interpret it. List the residuals and name the student whose score is most below the model's expectation.
(e) Predict the score for 6.5 hours. Should you predict for 20 hours?
(f) The principal concludes "forcing every student to study 9 hours will raise the average to 80+". Give two statistical objections.

\(\bar{h} = 5.5\) and \(\bar{s} = 65.5\). Only one adjacent pair of scores is out of order.

(a) \(\operatorname{Cov}(h, s) = 24.86\) hour·marks (positive). \(r = 0.986\), a strong positive linear association.

(b) \(R = 0.976\). Students 3 and 4 swap order (61 > 60), so \(\sum d^2 = 2\) and

\[ R = 1 - \frac{12}{504} \]

The relationship is monotone except for that one pair.

(c) \(\hat{s} = 42.71 + 4.143h\): each extra hour is associated with about 4.1 more marks.
\(\hat{h} = -9.86 + 0.2345s\).
Product of slopes: \(4.143 \times 0.2345 = \mathbf{0.9715 = r^2}\) ✓.

(d) \(r^2 = 0.9715\): about 97% of the score variation is explained linearly by hours.
The residuals are 1.00, −0.14, 1.71, −3.43, 0.43, −0.71, −0.86 and 2.00. Student 4 (−3.43) is furthest below expectation.

(e) \(\hat{s}(6.5) \approx 69.6\). Do not predict for 20 hours. That is far outside the 2–9 range, the line would give about 125 (above any possible maximum), and returns from study diminish.

(f) First, this is observational correlation, not causation: motivation and ability confound hours and scores (P5.16). Second, forcing 9 hours applies the cross-sectional slope as an intervention. Even the model's predicted mean at 9 hours is \(\hat{s}(9) \approx 80\), and that is a mean: individual scores scatter around it (\(s_e \approx 1.9\)), so "everyone 80+" does not follow. In addition, students who currently study 9 hours may differ systematically from those who would be forced to.

Key insight: A complete bivariate analysis moves through direction (covariance), strength (\(r\) and \(R\)), a predictive equation (regression), adequacy (\(r^2\) and residuals), and the limits of interpretation (extrapolation and causation). The statistics can be flawless while the decision drawn from them is wrong.

tests: 5.1, 5.2, 5.3, 5.4, 5.5 · L5 Mastery
Module 6: Time Series Forecasting; Gaussian Mixtures & EM · 46 questions

Covers tree nodes 6.1 – 6.7 (CS-12, CS-13, CS-14, CS-15, W-4 time-series part).
Conventions used throughout: \(Y_t\) is the observation at time \(t\), \(F_t\) is the forecast for period \(t\), and \(e_t = Y_t - F_t\). For SES the course initialisation is \(F_2 = Y_1\). ACF uses \(r_k = \sum_{t=1}^{T-k}(Y_t - \bar{Y})(Y_{t+k} - \bar{Y}) \,/\, \sum_{t=1}^{T}(Y_t - \bar{Y})^2\).

9.A Foundations & Components (6.1)

L1 · FoundationP6.1

Name that component

For each description, state whether it is mainly the Trend (T), Seasonal (S), Cyclical (C) or Irregular (I) component.

  1. Air-conditioner sales peak every May–June.
  2. India's mobile-data consumption has risen every year since 2015.
  3. A sudden one-week drop in car sales caused by a transport strike.
  4. Construction activity expands for 3–5 years, contracts for 2–4 years, then repeats with no fixed period.
  5. Restaurant footfall is high every Saturday and Sunday.
  6. Petrol prices jump for two weeks after an unexpected Middle-East flare-up.
  7. A slow decline in landline subscriptions over two decades.
  8. Unemployment rises during recessions and falls during expansions.

The difference between seasonal and cyclical is whether the period is fixed and known (seasonal) or irregular and longer than a year (cyclical). Irregular means unpredictable and non-recurring.

1-S, 2-T, 3-I, 4-C, 5-S (period of 7 days), 6-I, 7-T, 8-C.

Item 5 shows that seasonality does not have to be annual. Any fixed calendar period counts, whether daily, weekly or quarterly. Item 4 is cyclical because the length of each phase is unpredictable.

Key insight: To classify a component, ask two questions. Does it recur? Is its period fixed? Recurring with a fixed period is S. Recurring with a variable period longer than a year is C. Persistent direction is T. Everything left over is I.

tests: 6.1 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP6.2

Time series or cross-section? Forecast, plan or goal?

(a) A teacher records (study hours, marks) for 40 students. A college records its number of admissions every year from 2005 to 2024. Which dataset calls for correlation/regression and which calls for time-series analysis? Why can the two not simply be swapped?

(b) Classify each statement as a forecast, a plan or a goal:
(i) "We want 20% market share by 2027."
(ii) "Demand next quarter is expected to be 12,400 units \(\pm\) 600."
(iii) "We will hire 15 extra call-centre staff in December to meet the expected volume."

(c) True or False: "Forecasting is easiest when we have plenty of data, even if we don't understand what drives the variable." Justify using the exchange-rate example.

In a time series the order of the observations carries information. A plan is the action that closes the gap between a forecast and a goal.

(a) Study hours vs marks is cross-sectional data, so use correlation and regression. Each student is an independent unit, and shuffling the rows changes nothing. Admissions by year is a time series. Successive observations are dependent, ordered and equally spaced, and shuffling the rows destroys the information. Regression assumes independent errors, an assumption a time series usually violates through autocorrelation.

(b) (i) Goal (ii) Forecast (iii) Plan

(c) False. Forecastability depends on three things: (1) how well we understand the drivers, (2) how much data we have, and (3) whether the forecast itself changes the outcome. Exchange rates satisfy only (2). The drivers are poorly understood, and published forecasts move the market. So abundant data alone does not make a series forecastable.

Key insight: In time-series data, the dependence on order is the very thing we model. Cross-sectional regression usually ignores it.

tests: 6.1 · L1 Foundation
L1 · FoundationP6.3

Method and horizon matching

(a) Classify each method as qualitative or quantitative. For quantitative methods, also say whether it is time-series or causal: Delphi method · simple exponential smoothing · regression of sales on advertising spend · panel consensus · least-squares trend projection · market research survey · moving average.

(b) Assign a forecasting horizon (short, medium or long) to each task, and name the component that dominates at that horizon:
(i) scheduling call-centre staff next week, (ii) buying raw material and hiring for next year, (iii) deciding whether to build a power plant in 5 years.

Causal methods use other variables to explain \(Y\). Time-series methods use \(Y\)'s own past. Over longer horizons the trend matters more and more.

(a) Qualitative: Delphi, panel consensus, market research. Quantitative time-series: SES, trend projection, moving average. Quantitative causal: regression on advertising.

(b) (i) Short-term. Scheduling dominates and seasonal patterns (day of week, hour) matter most. The slides say cyclical/seasonal factors are less critical here, but day-of-week effects are still used. (ii) Medium-term. Trend and cyclical components drive resource planning. (iii) Long-term. Trend dominates. Use econometric or life-cycle analysis and revise the forecast every 2–3 years.

Key insight: The forecasting horizon determines which component you need to get right. A weekly forecast that misses the trend does little harm. A 5-year forecast that misses the trend is a disaster.

tests: 6.1 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP6.4

Additive or multiplicative?

Quarterly sales (₹ lakh):

Year Q1 Q2 Q3 Q4
1 80 120 100 60
2 120 180 150 90
3 160 240 200 120

(a) For each year, compute the annual mean. Then compute each quarter's difference from the mean and its ratio to the mean.
(b) Which model, \(Y = T + S + I\) or \(Y = T \times S \times I\), describes the data? Give the seasonal indices.
(c) Deseasonalise Year 3, Q2.
(d) What transformation would turn this series into one suited to an additive model?

If the seasonal swings grow with the level of the series, the ratios stay constant and the differences do not.

(a) Annual means: 90, 135, 180.
Differences: Year 1 (−10, +30, +10, −30), Year 2 (−15, +45, +15, −45), Year 3 (−20, +60, +20, −60). The differences grow each year.
Ratios: 0.889, 1.333, 1.111, 0.667 in every year. The ratios are constant.

(b) Multiplicative. Seasonal indices are \(S = (0.889, 1.333, 1.111, 0.667)\). They sum to 4.0, as indices for 4 quarters should.

(c) Deseasonalised value \(= 240 / 1.333 =\) 180, which equals the Year 3 level.

(d) Take logs: \(\log Y = \log T + \log S + \log I\), which is additive.

Key insight: When the seasonal amplitude is proportional to the level, use a multiplicative model. When the amplitude is constant, use an additive model. A quick test is to check whether the ratios or the differences are stable across years.

tests: 6.1 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP6.5

Composing and decomposing a series

A retailer's analyst estimates the following for the coming Q4: trend \(T = 500\) units, seasonal factor \(S = 1.15\), cyclical factor \(C = 0.96\) (mild slowdown), irregular \(I = 1.02\).

(a) Compute the multiplicative-model value \(Y = T \times C \times S \times I\).
(b) A colleague uses an additive model with \(T = 500\), \(S = +75\), \(C = -20\), \(I = +8\). Compute \(Y\). Explain why the two models can give similar numbers here but will diverge when \(T\) doubles.
(c) Actual Q4 sales were 690. Deseasonalise this value using the multiplicative index.
(d) Next year's Q1 trend is projected at 520, with seasonal index 0.85. Assuming \(C = I = 1\), forecast Q1.

To deseasonalise in a multiplicative model, divide by \(S\). In an additive model you would subtract \(S\).

(a)

\[ Y = 500 \times 0.96 \times 1.15 \times 1.02 = \mathbf{563.04} \]

(b)

\[ Y = 500 + 75 - 20 + 8 = \mathbf{563} \]

The numbers agree because the additive effects were chosen to match the multiplicative ones at \(T = 500\) (\(75 = 15\%\) of 500, and so on). If \(T\) doubles to 1000, the multiplicative seasonal effect becomes +150, while the additive one stays at +75.

(c)

\[ \text{Deseasonalised} = \frac{690}{1.15} = \mathbf{600} \]

Underlying demand, after removing the Q4 boost, was 600.

(d)

\[ F = 520 \times 0.85 = \mathbf{442} \]

Key insight: Deseasonalising means dividing (multiplicative) or subtracting (additive). It exposes the underlying level, so quarters can be compared fairly.

tests: 6.1 · L3 Application

9.B Moving Averages & Trend (6.2)

L1 · FoundationP6.6

Moving-average facts

True or False, with a one-line reason:

  1. A larger window \(k\) gives a smoother moving average.
  2. A trailing moving average used as a forecast lags behind a trending series.
  3. A 4-period moving average is automatically centred on an actual time point.
  4. A moving average whose window equals the seasonal period removes that seasonality.
  5. A weighted moving average must have weights that sum to 1.
  6. A moving average is a "high-pass filter".

Averaging cancels rapid wiggles and keeps slow movements. Ask yourself what "low-pass" means.

  1. True. More points are averaged, so more noise cancels.
  2. True. It averages past values only, so it trails an upward trend.
  3. False. The average of \(t = 1, \dots, 4\) sits at \(t = 2.5\). You need a \(2 \times 4\) centred MA.
  4. True. Each window contains exactly one full cycle, and the seasonal effects sum to about zero.
  5. True. Otherwise the forecast is biased up or down.
  6. False. It is a low-pass filter. It passes slow trend and cycle and blocks high-frequency noise.

Key insight: A moving average trades responsiveness for smoothness. Wider windows give less noise but more lag.

tests: 6.2 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP6.7

3-year moving average

Production (thousand tonnes), 2015–2021: 42, 48, 45, 51, 57, 54, 60.
Compute the 3-year moving totals and moving averages. Place each average against its correct year. Describe the trend.

With an odd window there is no centring problem. The first average belongs to the middle year, 2016.

Year \(Y\) 3-yr total 3-yr MA
2015 42 – –
2016 48 135 45
2017 45 144 48
2018 51 153 51
2019 57 162 54
2020 54 171 57
2021 60 – –

The zig-zag in the raw data disappears. The trend rises by exactly 3 thousand tonnes per year. One value is lost at each end of the series.

Key insight: An MA(3) wipes out alternating up/down noise and leaves a clean linear trend. The cost is \((k-1)/2\) missing values at each end.

tests: 6.2 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP6.8

4-quarter centred moving average

Quarterly demand for \(t = 1, \dots, 8\): 30, 42, 50, 38, 34, 46, 54, 42.

(a) Compute the 4-quarter moving totals and averages, and state where each average sits in time.
(b) Compute the centred moving average (CMA) and attach each value to an actual quarter.
(c) Compute the ratio \(Y_t / \text{CMA}_t\) for \(t = 3, 4, 5, 6\).

The averages sit at \(t = 2.5, 3.5, \dots\). Average adjacent pairs of them to land on whole periods.

(a) Totals: 160, 164, 168, 172, 176. MA(4): 40, 41, 42, 43, 44, located at \(t = 2.5, 3.5, 4.5, 5.5, 6.5\).

(b) CMA: \(t=3\): 40.5, \(t=4\): 41.5, \(t=5\): 42.5, \(t=6\): 43.5.

(c) Ratios:

\[ \frac{50}{40.5} = \mathbf{1.235}, \quad \frac{38}{41.5} = \mathbf{0.916}, \quad \frac{34}{42.5} = \mathbf{0.800}, \quad \frac{46}{43.5} = \mathbf{1.057} \]

Key insight: With an even window you must centre, by taking a \(2 \times k\) MA. The ratios \(Y/\text{CMA}\) are the raw seasonal-index estimates used in the ratio-to-moving-average method. Q3 is about 23% above trend and Q1 is about 20% below.

tests: 6.2 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP6.9

Weighted vs simple moving-average forecast

Monthly sales were 120 (two months ago), 132 (last month) and 141 (this month). The series is rising.

(a) Compute the next-month forecast with SMA(3).
(b) Compute the next-month forecast with WMA(3), using weights 0.5 on the most recent month, 0.3 on the previous and 0.2 on the oldest.
(c) Actual next-month sales = 150. Compute both errors. Which method is better, and why?
(d) A junior analyst reverses the weights by mistake (0.2 on the most recent). Compute that forecast and comment.

When a series is trending, recent observations are better guides to the next value.

(a)

\[ \text{SMA} = \frac{120 + 132 + 141}{3} = \mathbf{131.0} \]

(b)

\[ \text{WMA} = 0.5(141) + 0.3(132) + 0.2(120) = 70.5 + 39.6 + 24 = \mathbf{134.1} \]

(c) Errors: SMA 19.0, WMA 15.9. The WMA is better because it gives more weight to recent values, so it lags the rising trend less.

(d) \(0.2(141) + 0.3(132) + 0.5(120) =\) 127.8, which is worse than the SMA. Reversing the weights puts the emphasis on stale data and makes the lag worse.

Key insight: Weighting recent values more reduces lag. But a moving average of any kind still cannot extrapolate a trend: every forecast here is below 141. That limitation is the motivation for Holt's method.

tests: 6.2, 6.3 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP6.10

Least-squares trend with coded time

Annual sales (₹ crore): 2018: 120, 2019: 128, 2020: 135, 2021: 141, 2022: 150, 2023: 158, 2024: 163.

(a) Code \(X = \text{year} - 2021\), so that \(X = -3, \dots, 3\). Fit \(Y = a + bX\) using the normal equations \(\sum Y = na + b\sum X\) and \(\sum XY = a\sum X + b\sum X^2\).
(b) Compute the trend value for 2018 and the forecast for 2027.
(c) Now use only the 6 years 2019–2024. With an even count, code \(X = -5, -3, -1, 1, 3, 5\), where each unit is half a year. Fit the trend, convert \(b\) to a per-year slope, and forecast 2027.

Coding makes \(\sum X = 0\), so \(a = \bar{Y}\) and \(b = \sum XY / \sum X^2\). With half-year units, 2027 sits at \(X = 11\).

(a) \(\sum Y = 995\), \(\sum XY = 204\), \(\sum X^2 = 28\). So \(a = 142.14\), \(b = 7.286\), giving

\[ \hat{Y} = \mathbf{142.14 + 7.286X} \]
