(b) For 2018 (\(X = -3\)): \(142.14 - 21.86 =\) 120.29. For 2027 (\(X = 6\)): 185.86 ₹ crore.

(c) \(\sum Y = 875\), so \(a =\) 145.83. \(\sum XY = 253\) and \(\sum X^2 = 70\), so \(b =\) 3.614 per half-year = 7.229 per year. For 2027, \(X = 11\):

\[ 145.83 + 3.614 \times 11 = \mathbf{185.59} \]

Key insight: Coding time so that \(\sum X = 0\) decouples the normal equations: \(a\) becomes the mean and \(b\) becomes \(\sum XY / \sum X^2\). With an even number of years, the odd-number coding is in half-year units, so double \(b\) to get the yearly slope.

tests: 6.2 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP6.11

Why moving averages lag and act as low-pass filters

(a) Let \(Y_t = 10 + 2t\) with no noise. Show that the centred MA(3) at time \(t\) equals \(Y_t\) exactly. Then show that the trailing MA(3) used as a forecast for \(t + 1\) has error 4.
(b) Generalise: for a linear trend with slope \(b\), show that the trailing MA(\(k\)) forecast of \(Y_{t+1}\) has error \(b(k+1)/2\).
(c) A quarterly series is \(Y_t = 100 + s_t\), where the seasonal pattern repeats (+6, −2, +4, −8). Show that MA(4) returns exactly 100 for every window.
(d) If \(Y_t = \mu + \varepsilon_t\) with independent \(\varepsilon_t\) of variance \(\sigma^2\), find the variance of MA(\(k\)). Use your results to explain the phrase "low-pass filter".

The average of equally spaced points on a straight line is the value at their middle time point.

(a) Centred:

\[ \frac{Y_{t-1} + Y_t + Y_{t+1}}{3} = 10 + 2t = \mathbf{Y_t} \]

because the \(\pm 2\) terms cancel. Trailing:

\[ \frac{Y_t + Y_{t-1} + Y_{t-2}}{3} = Y_{t-1} \]

As a forecast of \(Y_{t+1}\) the error is \(Y_{t+1} - Y_{t-1} = 2b =\) 4.

(b) The trailing MA(\(k\)) equals the trend value at the window's midpoint, \(t - (k-1)/2\). The target is \(t + 1\), so the error is

\[ b\left[1 + \frac{k-1}{2}\right] = \boldsymbol{\frac{b(k+1)}{2}} \]

The lag grows linearly with \(k\).

(c) Every window of 4 consecutive quarters contains each seasonal effect exactly once, and \(6 - 2 + 4 - 8 = 0\). So \(\text{MA} = 100\) for every window.

(d) \(\operatorname{Var}(\text{MA}) = \sigma^2/k\). So the MA (i) passes slow components (trend) unchanged, (ii) annihilates fluctuations whose period divides \(k\), and (iii) shrinks fast random noise by a factor of \(1/k\). Low frequencies pass and high frequencies are blocked, which is exactly a low-pass filter.

Key insight: A moving average makes a precise trade. A wider window reduces noise variance by \(1/k\), but it adds a forecast lag of \(b(k+1)/2\) on trending data. Centred MAs describe the past well. Trailing MAs forecast trends badly.

tests: 6.2 · L4 Analysis

9.C Forecast Accuracy (6.3)

L1 · FoundationP6.12

Know your error measures

Match each statement to MSE, MAD, MAPE or LAD:

  1. Penalises one large error much more than several small ones.
  2. Scale-free, so it can compare forecasts of rupees and of tonnes.
  3. Tells you whether every error stayed below a tolerance.
  4. Has the same units as the data and treats all errors linearly.
  5. Undefined if any actual value is 0.

Also: why can't we just average the raw errors \(e_t\)?

Squaring magnifies large errors. Percentages divide by \(Y_t\). The "largest" measure looks only at the worst case.

1-MSE, 2-MAPE, 3-LAD, 4-MAD, 5-MAPE.

Positive and negative raw errors cancel, so their mean only measures bias, not accuracy. A forecast that is wrong by +10 and −10 has mean error 0.

Key insight: Each measure encodes a different loss. Choose the one that matches the business cost of being wrong. Use MSE when large misses are catastrophic, and MAD when cost is linear in the error.

tests: 6.3 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP6.13

Computing all four measures

\(t\) 1 2 3 4 5
Actual \(Y\) 50 54 60 58 65
Forecast \(F\) 52 51 57 62 61

Compute MSE, MAD, MAPE and LAD.

\(e = Y - F\). \(\text{MAPE} = \frac{1}{n}\sum \frac{\lvert e_t \rvert}{Y_t} \times 100\%\).

Errors: −2, 3, 3, −4, 4.

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{MSE} &= \frac{4 + 9 + 9 + 16 + 16}{5} = \mathbf{10.8} \\ \text{MAD} &= \frac{16}{5} = \mathbf{3.2} \\ \text{MAPE} &= \frac{2/50 + 3/54 + 3/60 + 4/58 + 4/65}{5} = \mathbf{5.52\%} \\ \text{LAD} &= \mathbf{4} \end{aligned} \]

Key insight: The raw errors sum to 4, so the bias is small (mean error 0.8). Accuracy measures look at the size of the errors, not their sign.

tests: 6.3 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP6.14

When MSE and MAD disagree

Actual demand for 5 days: 100, 102, 98, 105, 110.
Method A forecasts: 100, 102, 98, 105, 98.
Method B forecasts: 96, 98, 94, 101, 106.

(a) Compute MSE, MAD, MAPE and LAD for both methods.
(b) Which method does each measure prefer?
(c) Which would you choose for (i) a hospital's oxygen-cylinder stocking, where one big shortfall is life-threatening, and (ii) a bakery, where the loss is proportional to the units wasted?

Method A is perfect on 4 days and badly wrong on 1. Method B is moderately wrong every day.

(a)

MSE MAD MAPE LAD
A (errors 0,0,0,0,12) 28.8 2.4 2.18% 12
B (errors 4,4,4,4,4) 16.0 4.0 3.89% 4

(b) MAD and MAPE prefer A. MSE and LAD prefer B.

(c) (i) Hospital: choose B, the MSE/LAD choice, because a single large miss is unacceptable. (ii) Bakery: choose A, the MAD choice, because the cost is linear.

Key insight: "Best forecast" is undefined until you choose a loss function. MSE is sensitive to outliers. MAD is robust to them.

tests: 6.3 · L3 Application
L5 · MasteryP6.15

Why MAPE breaks on intermittent demand

A spare-parts store has weekly demand of 2, 1, 0.5 (in hundreds) and 40. Forecasts are 3, 2, 1.5 and 38.

(a) Compute MAD and MAPE. What does MAPE suggest about forecast quality, and is that fair?
(b) What happens to MAPE if a week has actual demand 0?
(c) Propose two better metrics and explain why each fixes the problem.
(d) A manager sees MAPE drop from 40% to 12% after the team switched to forecasting sales of a high-volume SKU instead of a low-volume one. Is the model better?

MAPE divides by \(Y_t\). Look at what happens when \(Y_t\) is small.

(a) Errors are −1, −1, −1, +2, so \(\text{MAD} = 1.25\). APEs are 50%, 100%, 200% and 5%, so \(\text{MAPE} = 88.75\%\). MAPE suggests terrible forecasting, but every miss was only 1–2 units. Three tiny-denominator weeks dominate the measure, so it is not a fair picture.

(b) Division by zero. MAPE is undefined or infinite.

(c) (1) MAD/MAE, which has no denominator, is in units and is interpretable. (2) MASE, which scales MAE by the in-sample MAE of a naive forecast. It is scale-free and has no division by \(Y_t\). Weighted MAPE (\(\sum \lvert e \rvert / \sum Y\)) is also acceptable.

(d) Not necessarily. MAPE is scale-dependent in a hidden way. Higher volumes shrink the percentage error even if the absolute errors are identical. Compare models on the same series.

Key insight: MAPE is asymmetric and explodes near zero, so it is inappropriate for intermittent or low-count series. Check the denominator before trusting a percentage.

tests: 6.3 · L5 Mastery

9.D Exponential Smoothing: SES, Holt, Holt–Winters (6.4)

L1 · FoundationP6.16

Smoothing constants and the method taxonomy

(a) In SES, \(F_{t+1} = \alpha Y_t + (1 - \alpha)F_t\). What does the forecast become when \(\alpha = 1\)? When \(\alpha = 0\)? Does a small \(\alpha\) give a smoother or a more responsive forecast?

(b) Match each data pattern to a method: SES, Holt (double), Holt–Winters additive, Holt–Winters multiplicative.
(i) Flat level with noise. (ii) Steady upward trend, no seasonality. (iii) Upward trend with seasonal swings of constant size. (iv) Upward trend with seasonal swings that grow with the level.

(c) In Holt's method, what does \(\beta = 0\) mean? What does \(\beta = 1\) mean?

Each added component (level → trend → season) needs its own smoothing constant (\(\alpha \to \beta \to \gamma\)).

(a) \(\alpha = 1\) gives \(F_{t+1} = Y_t\), the naive forecast. \(\alpha = 0\) gives \(F_{t+1} = F_t\), a frozen forecast that never learns. A small \(\alpha\) gives a smoother forecast, slow to react. A large \(\alpha\) is responsive but noisy.

(b) (i) SES, (ii) Holt, (iii) HW additive, (iv) HW multiplicative (ratio seasonality).

(c) \(\beta = 0\): \(T_t = T_{t-1}\), so the trend is frozen at its initial value. \(\beta = 1\): \(T_t = L_t - L_{t-1}\), so the trend is the latest change in level, with no smoothing at all.

Key insight: The exponential-smoothing family is a \(3 \times 3\) grid of {no, linear, ratio} trend \(\times\) {no, linear, ratio} season. Choose the cell from a plot of the series.

tests: 6.4 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP6.17

SES recursion with initialisation

Weekly orders: \(Y_1, \dots, Y_6 = 40, 44, 41, 47, 50, 46\). Use \(\alpha = 0.3\) and \(F_2 = Y_1\).
Compute \(F_3\), \(F_4\), \(F_5\), \(F_6\) and the forecast for week 7.

\(F_{t+1} = 0.3Y_t + 0.7F_t\). Start from \(F_2 = 40\).

\[ \begin{aligned} F_2 &= 40 \\ F_3 &= 0.3(44) + 0.7(40) = \mathbf{41.20} \\ F_4 &= 0.3(41) + 0.7(41.2) = \mathbf{41.14} \\ F_5 &= 0.3(47) + 0.7(41.14) = \mathbf{42.90} \\ F_6 &= 0.3(50) + 0.7(42.898) = \mathbf{45.03} \\ F_7 &= 0.3(46) + 0.7(45.0286) = \mathbf{45.32} \end{aligned} \]

Key insight: Unlike an MA(\(k\)), SES produces a forecast from period 2 onward, so no observations are lost. Each forecast is the previous forecast nudged by a fraction \(\alpha\) of the last error: \(F_{t+1} = F_t + \alpha(Y_t - F_t)\).

tests: 6.4 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP6.18

One Holt update

At the end of period \(t - 1\), Holt's method has level \(L_{t-1} = 200\) and trend \(T_{t-1} = 8\). The new observation is \(Y_t = 215\). With \(\alpha = 0.4\) and \(\beta = 0.3\), compute \(L_t\), \(T_t\), the forecast \(F_{t+1}\), and the 3-step-ahead forecast \(F_{t+3}\).

\(L_t = \alpha Y_t + (1-\alpha)(L_{t-1} + T_{t-1})\); \(T_t = \beta(L_t - L_{t-1}) + (1-\beta)T_{t-1}\); \(F_{t+k} = L_t + kT_t\).

\[ \begin{aligned} L_t &= 0.4(215) + 0.6(208) = 86 + 124.8 = \mathbf{210.8} \\ T_t &= 0.3(210.8 - 200) + 0.7(8) = 3.24 + 5.6 = \mathbf{8.84} \\ F_{t+1} &= \mathbf{219.64}; \quad F_{t+3} = 210.8 + 3(8.84) = \mathbf{237.32} \end{aligned} \]

Key insight: The level update blends the new data with the one-step forecast \(L_{t-1} + T_{t-1}\). The trend update blends the observed change in level with the old trend. Forecasts many steps ahead are a straight line, \(L_t + kT_t\).

tests: 6.4 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP6.19

Choosing α by MSE

Monthly subscriptions (hundreds): 20, 24, 22, 27, 30, 28, 33.

(a) Apply SES with \(\alpha = 0.2\) and with \(\alpha = 0.6\), using \(F_2 = Y_1\). Tabulate \(F_2, \dots, F_7\).
(b) Compute the MSE and MAD over \(t = 2, \dots, 7\) for each \(\alpha\).
(c) Which \(\alpha\) would you choose? What is its forecast for month 8? Why does the series favour that \(\alpha\), and what better method exists?

The series trends upward, so ask which \(\alpha\) lets the forecast "keep up".

\(t\) \(Y\) \(F\) (\(\alpha=0.2\)) \(F\) (\(\alpha=0.6\))
2 24 20.00 20.00
3 22 20.80 22.40
4 27 21.04 22.16
5 30 22.23 25.06
6 28 23.79 28.03
7 33 24.63 28.01
8 – 26.30 31.00

(b) \(\alpha = 0.2\): \(\text{MSE} = 33.52\), \(\text{MAD} = 5.25\). \(\alpha = 0.6\): \(\text{MSE} = 14.81\), \(\text{MAD} = 3.20\).

(c) Choose \(\alpha = 0.6\), with \(F_8 =\) 31.00. With a trending series, a low \(\alpha\) lags further behind (compare P6.23). Even \(\alpha = 0.6\) is mostly below the actual values, which means systematic bias. Holt's method models the trend explicitly and would do better.

Key insight: Pick \(\alpha\) by minimising one-step-ahead error on historical data. If the errors stay mostly the same sign, SES is the wrong model and a trend component is missing.

tests: 6.4, 6.3 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP6.20

A full Holt run

Quarterly sales (thousand units): Q1..Q5 = 100, 108, 113, 121, 130. Initialise \(L_1 = Y_1 = 100\) and \(T_1 = Y_2 - Y_1 = 8\). Use \(\alpha = 0.5\) and \(\beta = 0.4\).

(a) For \(t = 2, \dots, 5\), compute the one-step forecast \(F_t = L_{t-1} + T_{t-1}\), then \(L_t\) and \(T_t\).
(b) Forecast Q6 and Q8.

Compute the forecast first, then update the level using that forecast, then update the trend.

\(t\) \(Y\) \(F_t\) \(L_t\) \(T_t\)
1 100 – 100 8
2 108 108.00 108.00 8.00
3 113 116.00 114.50 7.40
4 121 121.90 121.45 7.22
5 130 128.67 129.335 7.486

(b)

\[ \begin{aligned} F_6 &= 129.335 + 7.486 = \mathbf{136.82} \\ F_8 &= 129.335 + 3(7.486) = \mathbf{151.79} \text{ thousand units} \end{aligned} \]

Key insight: Holt's forecasts keep pace with the trend. Compare \(F_5 = 128.67\) with \(Y_5 = 130\). Multi-step forecasts extrapolate the current smoothed slope, which SES cannot do.

tests: 6.4 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP6.21

Holt–Winters multiplicative update

(a) A quarterly product's first-year sales are 400, 520, 610, 470. Compute the initial seasonal factors \(S_i = Y_i / \text{average}\).

(b) Later in the series, at quarter \(t\), the state is: \(L_{t-1} = 500\), \(T_{t-1} = 10\), the seasonal factor for this quarter from a year ago \(S_{t-4} = 1.20\), the factor for next quarter \(S_{t-3} = 0.85\), and the factor for the quarter after that \(S_{t-2} = 1.05\). The new observation is \(Y_t = 630\). Use \(\alpha = 0.3\), \(\beta = 0.2\), \(\gamma = 0.1\) with

\[ \begin{aligned} L_t &= \alpha(Y_t / S_{t-4}) + (1-\alpha)(L_{t-1} + T_{t-1}) \\ T_t &= \beta(L_t - L_{t-1}) + (1-\beta)T_{t-1} \\ S_t &= \gamma(Y_t / L_t) + (1-\gamma)S_{t-4} \end{aligned} \]

Compute \(L_t\), \(T_t\), \(S_t\), and the forecasts for the next two quarters, \(F_{t+k} = (L_t + kT_t) \cdot S_{t-4+k}\).

Deseasonalise \(Y_t\) by dividing by last year's factor before blending it into the level.

(a) Average = 500, so \(S = 0.80, 1.04, 1.22, 0.94\). They sum to 4.

(b)

\[ \begin{aligned} L_t &= 0.3(630/1.2) + 0.7(510) = 157.5 + 357 = \mathbf{514.5} \\ T_t &= 0.2(14.5) + 0.8(10) = \mathbf{10.9} \\ S_t &= 0.1(630/514.5) + 0.9(1.20) = 0.1224 + 1.08 = \mathbf{1.2024} \\ F_{t+1} &= (514.5 + 10.9)(0.85) = \mathbf{446.59}; \quad F_{t+2} = (514.5 + 21.8)(1.05) = \mathbf{563.12} \end{aligned} \]

Key insight: Holt–Winters runs three coupled exponential smoothers: level on deseasonalised data, trend on changes in level, and season on the ratio of data to level. The forecast re-applies the seasonal factor of the target quarter. (The slides misprint the trend formula. The correct form is \(\beta(L_t - L_{t-1})\).)

tests: 6.4 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP6.22

The exponential weights of SES

(a) By repeatedly substituting \(F_t = \alpha Y_{t-1} + (1-\alpha)F_{t-1}\), show that

\[ F_{t+1} = \sum_{j=0}^{t-2} \alpha(1-\alpha)^j Y_{t-j} + (1-\alpha)^{t-1} F_2, \]

and that the weights sum to exactly 1.
(b) For \(\alpha = 0.4\), list the weights on \(Y_t\), \(Y_{t-1}\), \(Y_{t-2}\), \(Y_{t-3}\). After how many lags does a weight first drop below 1%?
(c) The "mean age" of the data in a forecast is \(\sum j \cdot w_j\). Show that it equals \((1-\alpha)/\alpha\) for SES (large \(t\)). An SMA(\(k\)) has mean age \((k-1)/2\). Find the SMA equivalent to \(\alpha = 0.4\), and the general rule.

Use the geometric series \(\sum r^j = 1/(1-r)\) and \(\sum j r^j = r/(1-r)^2\).

(a) Unrolling the recursion gives the expression. The weights sum to

\[ \frac{\alpha[1 - (1-\alpha)^{t-1}]}{1 - (1-\alpha)} + (1-\alpha)^{t-1} = 1 - (1-\alpha)^{t-1} + (1-\alpha)^{t-1} = \mathbf{1} \]

(b) Weights: 0.400, 0.240, 0.144, 0.0864. We need \(0.4(0.6)^j < 0.01\), so \(j > \ln(0.025)/\ln(0.6) = 7.22\). The first weight below 1% is at lag 8 (0.0067).

(c)

\[ \sum j \alpha(1-\alpha)^j = \frac{\alpha(1-\alpha)}{\alpha^2} = \boldsymbol{\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}} \]

For \(\alpha = 0.4\) this is 1.5. Setting \((k-1)/2 = 1.5\) gives \(k = 4\). In general, \(\alpha = 2/(k+1)\).

Key insight: SES is a weighted average of all past observations, with geometrically decaying weights. The name "exponential" comes from these weights. The constant \(\alpha\) sets the effective memory, about \(2/\alpha - 1\) periods.

tests: 6.4 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP6.23

Steady-state lag of SES on a trend

Let \(Y_t = a + bt\) with no noise, and apply SES.

(a) Show that the one-step error \(e_t = Y_t - F_t\) converges to a constant \(e^*\), and find \(e^*\) in terms of \(b\) and \(\alpha\).
(b) For \(b = 5\) and \(\alpha = 0.25\), compute \(e^*\). What happens to \(e^*\) as \(\alpha \to 1\)?
(c) Explain how Holt's trend term removes this bias.

Write \(e_{t+1} = Y_{t+1} - F_{t+1}\) using \(F_{t+1} = F_t + \alpha e_t\) and \(Y_{t+1} = Y_t + b\).

(a)

\[ e_{t+1} = (Y_t + b) - (F_t + \alpha e_t) = (1-\alpha)e_t + b \]

This is a contraction for \(0 < \alpha \le 1\), with fixed point \(e^* = (1-\alpha)e^* + b\), so \(e^* = b/\alpha\).

(b) \(e^* = 5/0.25 =\) 20 units, a permanent under-forecast. (A numerical run of 60 periods confirms 20.) As \(\alpha \to 1\), \(e^* \to b\), which is the naive forecast's lag of one period's growth.

(c) Holt adds \(T_t\), which converges to \(b\). The forecast \(L_t + T_t\) then contains the slope, and the steady-state error goes to 0.

Key insight: SES is unbiased only when the level is constant. On a trend it settles into a permanent lag of \(b/\alpha\). If you see systematic same-sign errors, the model is missing a trend term.

tests: 6.4 · L4 Analysis

9.E Stationarity, ACF/PACF, White Noise, Dickey–Fuller (6.5)

L1 · FoundationP6.24

Stationary or not?

Decide whether each process is (weakly) stationary, with a reason (\(\varepsilon_t\) is white noise):
(a) \(Y_t = \varepsilon_t\)
(b) \(Y_t = Y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t\) (random walk)
(c) \(Y_t = 5 + 0.3t + \varepsilon_t\)
(d) \(Y_t = 2 + 0.5Y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t\)
(e) \(Y_t = 1.02Y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t\)
(f) monthly ice-cream sales with summer peaks
(g) \(Y_t = \varepsilon_t + 3\varepsilon_{t-1}\)

Also state the three conditions for weak stationarity.

Weak stationarity requires: a constant mean, a constant variance, and an autocovariance \(\gamma(h)\) that depends only on the lag \(h\), not on \(t\).

Conditions: \(E[Y_t] = \mu\) for all \(t\); \(\operatorname{Var}(Y_t) = \sigma^2 < \infty\) for all \(t\); \(\operatorname{Cov}(Y_t, Y_{t+h}) = \gamma(h)\) depends only on \(h\).

(a) Stationary (white noise).

(b) Not stationary: \(\operatorname{Var} = t\sigma^2\) grows with \(t\).

(c) Not stationary: the mean grows with \(t\). It is "trend-stationary" (detrending makes it stationary).

(d) Stationary: \(\lvert \phi \rvert = 0.5 < 1\), with mean \(2/0.5 = 4\).

(e) Not stationary: \(\lvert \phi \rvert > 1\) makes it explosive.

(f) Not stationary: the mean depends on the month.

(g) Stationary: every finite MA is stationary. With \(\theta = 3\) it is not invertible, but that is a separate property.

Key insight: Trends, seasonality, unit roots and explosive roots all break stationarity. AR models need \(\lvert \phi \rvert < 1\). MA models are always stationary.

tests: 6.5 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP6.25

Autocorrelation by hand

Compute \(r_1\), \(r_2\) and \(r_3\) for the series 3, 5, 4, 6, 8, 7.

Mean = 5.5. Use the deviations \(d_t\). \(r_k = \sum d_t d_{t+k} / \sum d_t^2\), where the numerator has \(T - k\) terms.

Deviations: −2.5, −0.5, −1.5, 0.5, 2.5, 1.5. \(\sum d^2 =\) 17.5.

Lag 1:

\[ (-2.5)(-0.5) + (-0.5)(-1.5) + (-1.5)(0.5) + (0.5)(2.5) + (2.5)(1.5) = 1.25 + 0.75 - 0.75 + 1.25 + 3.75 = 6.25, \text{ so } \mathbf{r_1 = 0.357} \]

Lag 2:

\[ (-2.5)(-1.5) + (-0.5)(0.5) + (-1.5)(2.5) + (0.5)(1.5) = 3.75 - 0.25 - 3.75 + 0.75 = 0.5, \text{ so } \mathbf{r_2 = 0.029} \]

Lag 3:

\[ (-2.5)(0.5) + (-0.5)(2.5) + (-1.5)(1.5) = -1.25 - 1.25 - 2.25 = -4.75, \text{ so } \mathbf{r_3 = -0.271} \]

Key insight: The denominator always uses all \(T\) terms, while the numerator shrinks to \(T - k\) terms. This keeps \(\lvert r_k \rvert \le 1\) and pulls high-lag estimates toward 0. With \(T = 6\), none of these values is reliable, since the white-noise band is \(\pm 2/\sqrt{6} \approx \pm 0.82\).

tests: 6.5 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP6.26

Is it white noise?

Two residual series, each of length \(T = 100\), have these sample autocorrelations:

Lag 1 2 3 4 5
Series P 0.45 0.31 0.12 −0.05 0.08
Series Q 0.11 −0.15 0.06 0.18 −0.09

Using the approximate 95% band \(\pm 2/\sqrt{T}\), decide whether each series looks like white noise. What does the result mean for a forecasting model whose residuals are P?

For white noise, \(r_k \approx N(0, 1/T)\).

\[ \text{Band} = \pm \frac{2}{\sqrt{100}} = \mathbf{\pm 0.20} \]

P is not white noise: \(r_1 = 0.45\) and \(r_2 = 0.31\) lie outside the band. Q is consistent with white noise: every \(r_k\) lies inside the band.

Residuals like P still contain predictable structure, so the model is under-fitted. Add AR terms (the decaying ACF suggests AR) and refit.

Key insight: A good forecasting model leaves white-noise residuals. Any significant ACF spike in the residuals is information the model has not yet used. Even true white noise puts about 1 lag in 20 outside the band, so do not over-react to a single marginal spike.

tests: 6.5 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP6.27

PACF at lag 2

The lag-2 partial autocorrelation is

\[ \phi_{22} = \frac{\rho_2 - \rho_1^2}{1 - \rho_1^2} \]

(a) A stock-return series has \(\rho_1 = 0.8\) and \(\rho_2 = 0.64\). Compute \(\phi_{22}\) and interpret it using the slide's example of "today, yesterday and the day before yesterday".
(b) Another series has \(\rho_1 = 0.6\) and \(\rho_2 = 0.5\). Compute \(\phi_{22}\).
(c) Which series is consistent with an AR(1) model? Why?

For an AR(1), \(\rho_2 = \rho_1^2\), so all of the lag-2 correlation is inherited through lag 1.

(a)

\[ \phi_{22} = \frac{0.64 - 0.64}{1 - 0.64} = \mathbf{0} \]

Today is correlated with the day before yesterday (0.64) only because both are correlated with yesterday. After removing yesterday's influence, no direct link remains.

(b)

\[ \phi_{22} = \frac{0.5 - 0.36}{0.64} = \mathbf{0.219} \]

There is a direct lag-2 effect.

(c) Series (a). Its PACF cuts off after lag 1, which is the signature of AR(1). Series (b) needs at least AR(2).

Key insight: The ACF measures total correlation, including correlation passed through intermediate lags. The PACF measures direct correlation. That is why the PACF identifies the AR order \(p\).

tests: 6.5, 6.6 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP6.28

Dickey–Fuller unit-root test

For two series of length about 100, the DF regression \(\Delta Y_t = \mu + \delta Y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t\), where \(\delta = \phi - 1\), gives:

  • Series A: \(\hat{\mu} = 1.2\), \(\hat{\delta} = -0.42\), \(\text{SE}(\hat{\delta}) = 0.11\)
  • Series B: \(\hat{\mu} = 0.3\), \(\hat{\delta} = -0.04\), \(\text{SE}(\hat{\delta}) = 0.03\)

The 5% Dickey–Fuller critical value (with constant, \(n \approx 100\)) is −2.89.

(a) State \(H_0\) and \(H_1\) in terms of \(\phi\) and \(\delta\).
(b) Compute the test statistic for each series and give your decision.
(c) Recover \(\hat{\phi}\) for each series. What should you do with Series B before fitting an ARMA model?
(d) Why is the Student-t critical value (\(\approx -1.66\)) not appropriate here?

\(\tau = \hat{\delta}/\text{SE}(\hat{\delta})\). Reject the unit root if \(\tau <\) critical value (left-tailed test).

(a) \(H_0: \phi = 1\) (\(\delta = 0\)), a unit root, non-stationary. \(H_1: \phi < 1\) (\(\delta < 0\)), stationary.

(b) A: \(\tau = -0.42/0.11 =\) \(-3.82 < -2.89\), so reject \(H_0\). Series A is stationary.
B: \(\tau = -0.04/0.03 =\) \(-1.33 > -2.89\), so fail to reject \(H_0\). Series B has a unit root.

(c) \(\hat{\phi}_A = 1 - 0.42 =\) 0.58; \(\hat{\phi}_B =\) 0.96. Difference Series B (\(d = 1\)), check again, then fit ARMA to the differences. That is \(\text{ARIMA}(p, 1, q)\).

(d) Under \(H_0\) the series is non-stationary, so \(\tau\) does not follow a \(t\)-distribution. The DF distribution is shifted left, which makes the critical values more negative. Using −1.66 would reject the unit root far too often.

Key insight: Test for a unit root before fitting an ARMA model. Regressing on a random walk produces spurious significance. Differencing is the cure.

tests: 6.5 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP6.29

Random walk, differencing and autocovariance properties

Let \(Y_t = Y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t\) with \(Y_0 = 0\) and \(\varepsilon_t\) white noise with variance \(\sigma^2\).

(a) Show that \(E[Y_t] = 0\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(Y_t) = t\sigma^2\). For \(\sigma^2 = 4\), find the SD of \(Y_{25}\) and \(Y_{100}\).
(b) Show that \(\operatorname{Cov}(Y_t, Y_{t+h}) = t\sigma^2\). Which stationarity condition fails?
(c) Show that \(\Delta Y_t = Y_t - Y_{t-1}\) is stationary.
(d) For the trend model \(Y_t = 3 + 2t + \varepsilon_t\), show that \(\Delta Y_t\) has constant mean 2 and variance \(2\sigma^2\).
(e) For any stationary series, prove \(\gamma(-h) = \gamma(h)\) and \(\gamma(0) = \operatorname{Var}(Y_t)\), and state why \(\rho(0) = 1\).

\(Y_t = \varepsilon_1 + \dots + \varepsilon_t\). The covariance of two sums counts only the shared terms.

(a) \(E[Y_t] = \sum E[\varepsilon_i] = 0\). \(\operatorname{Var}(Y_t) = \sum \operatorname{Var}(\varepsilon_i) = t\sigma^2\), because the \(\varepsilon\) are uncorrelated. \(\text{SD}(Y_{25}) = \sqrt{100} = 10\); \(\text{SD}(Y_{100}) = \sqrt{400} = 20\).

(b) \(Y_t\) and \(Y_{t+h}\) share \(\varepsilon_1, \dots, \varepsilon_t\), so the covariance is \(t\sigma^2\). It depends on \(t\), so constant variance and lag-only autocovariance both fail.

(c) \(\Delta Y_t = \varepsilon_t\), which is white noise and therefore stationary. A random walk is ARIMA(0,1,0).

(d) \(\Delta Y_t = 2 + \varepsilon_t - \varepsilon_{t-1}\). \(E =\) 2 (constant), \(\operatorname{Var} =\) \(2\sigma^2\) (constant). Differencing turns a linear trend into a constant.

(e) \(\gamma(-h) = \operatorname{Cov}(Y_t, Y_{t-h}) = \operatorname{Cov}(Y_{t-h}, Y_t) = \gamma(h)\) by stationarity (shift by \(h\)). \(\gamma(0) = \operatorname{Cov}(Y_t, Y_t) = \operatorname{Var}\). \(\rho(0) = \gamma(0)/\gamma(0) = 1\).

Key insight: The variance of a random walk grows without bound, which is why the ACF of a non-stationary series decays very slowly. One difference makes it white noise. In ARIMA, the "I" stands for undoing that difference when forecasting.

tests: 6.5 · L4 Analysis

9.F AR / MA / ARMA / ARIMA / SARIMA(X) / VAR(MAX) (6.6)

L1 · FoundationP6.30

Identify the model from the ACF/PACF

Suggest a model for each pattern:
(a) ACF decays geometrically. PACF has significant spikes at lags 1 and 2 only.
(b) ACF has a significant spike at lag 1 only. PACF decays gradually.
(c) Both the ACF and the PACF tail off gradually.
(d) The ACF starts near 0.98 and declines very slowly over 30 lags.
(e) The ACF has spikes at lags 12, 24, 36 in monthly data.
(f) Every ACF and PACF value lies within \(\pm 2/\sqrt{T}\).

AR(\(p\)) is revealed by a PACF cut-off. MA(\(q\)) is revealed by an ACF cut-off.

(a) AR(2)

(b) MA(1)

(c) \(\text{ARMA}(p, q)\)

(d) Non-stationary (unit root): difference first, \(d = 1\)

(e) Seasonality with \(m = 12\), so use a SARIMA seasonal term or seasonal differencing

(f) White noise, nothing left to model.

Key insight: Remember this table. AR: ACF tails off, PACF cuts off at \(p\). MA: ACF cuts off at \(q\), PACF tails off. ARMA: both tail off. Check stationarity before reading either plot.

tests: 6.6, 6.5 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP6.31

AR(1) forecasting

The fitted model is \(y_t = 5 + 0.6y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t\) with \(\sigma_\varepsilon^2 = 9\). The last observation is \(y_{100} = 20\).
(a) Forecast \(y_{101}\), \(y_{102}\) and \(y_{103}\).
(b) Find the long-run mean and the long-run variance.
(c) Is the model stationary?

Set future \(\varepsilon\) to their expectation, 0, and iterate. The long-run mean is \(c/(1-\phi)\).

(a)

\[ \begin{aligned} \hat{y}_{101} &= 5 + 0.6(20) = \mathbf{17} \\ \hat{y}_{102} &= 5 + 0.6(17) = \mathbf{15.2} \\ \hat{y}_{103} &= 5 + 0.6(15.2) = \mathbf{14.12} \end{aligned} \]

(b)

\[ \mu = \frac{5}{1 - 0.6} = \mathbf{12.5}; \quad \operatorname{Var} = \frac{9}{1 - 0.36} = \mathbf{14.06} \]

(c) Yes, since \(\lvert \phi \rvert = 0.6 < 1\). The forecasts decay geometrically toward 12.5, and the gap shrinks by a factor of 0.6 each step.

Key insight: AR forecasts mean-revert. The further ahead you forecast, the closer the forecast gets to the unconditional mean. The speed of reversion is set by \(\phi\).

tests: 6.6 · L2 Computation
L2 · ComputationP6.32

ARMA(1,1) forecasting

The fitted model is \(y_t = 4 + 0.5y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t + 0.3\varepsilon_{t-1}\). The last values are \(y_{50} = 12\) and residual \(\varepsilon_{50} = -2\).
Forecast \(y_{51}\), \(y_{52}\) and \(y_{53}\), and give the long-run mean. Explain why the MA term affects only the first forecast.

\(\varepsilon_{50}\) is known. \(\varepsilon_{51}, \varepsilon_{52}, \dots\) are future shocks with expectation 0.

\[ \begin{aligned} \hat{y}_{51} &= 4 + 0.5(12) + 0.3(-2) = 4 + 6 - 0.6 = \mathbf{9.4} \\ \hat{y}_{52} &= 4 + 0.5(9.4) + 0.3 \cdot E[\varepsilon_{51}] = \mathbf{8.7} \\ \hat{y}_{53} &= 4 + 0.5(8.7) = \mathbf{8.35} \\ \text{Long-run mean} &= \frac{4}{1 - 0.5} = \mathbf{8} \end{aligned} \]

An MA(\(q\)) term involves the last \(q\) observed shocks. Beyond \(q\) steps ahead, every shock involved lies in the future, with expectation 0.

Key insight: An MA(\(q\)) part has a memory of exactly \(q\) periods for forecasting. The AR part carries the dynamics on from there.

tests: 6.6 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP6.33

ARIMA(1,1,1): forecast and un-difference

A non-stationary series has \(y_{98} = 200\), \(y_{99} = 206\), \(y_{100} = 215\). After first differencing, \(w_t = y_t - y_{t-1}\), the fitted model is \(w_t = 0.5w_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t - 0.4\varepsilon_{t-1}\), with last residual \(\varepsilon_{100} = 1.5\).

(a) Compute \(w_{100}\). Forecast \(w_{101}\), \(w_{102}\) and \(w_{103}\).
(b) Convert these to forecasts of \(y_{101}\), \(y_{102}\) and \(y_{103}\).
(c) To what level do the \(y\)-forecasts converge?
(d) Why could we not fit the ARMA model directly to \(y\)?

\(\hat{y}_{t+1} = \hat{y}_t + \hat{w}_{t+1}\). Integrating means taking a cumulative sum.

(a) \(w_{100} = 215 - 206 = 9\).

\[ \hat{w}_{101} = 0.5(9) - 0.4(1.5) = \mathbf{3.9}; \quad \hat{w}_{102} = \mathbf{1.95}; \quad \hat{w}_{103} = \mathbf{0.975} \]

(b)

\[ \hat{y}_{101} = 215 + 3.9 = \mathbf{218.9}; \quad \hat{y}_{102} = \mathbf{220.85}; \quad \hat{y}_{103} = \mathbf{221.825} \]

(c) The remaining increments sum geometrically: \(3.9/(1 - 0.5) = 7.8\). So \(\hat{y} \to\) 222.8.

(d) \(y\) has a unit root, so the ARMA stationarity assumption fails. The fitted coefficients and their significance would be spurious.

Key insight: In ARIMA you forecast the changes and then add them back cumulatively. A mean-reverting difference series produces level forecasts that flatten out at a new plateau.

tests: 6.6 · L3 Application
L3 · ApplicationP6.34

Lag selection, seasonal, exogenous and vector models

(a) A regression of \(y_t\) on its first three lags gives:

Term Coef p-value
Intercept 12.0 0.001
lag 1 0.55 <0.001
lag 2 −0.08 0.41
lag 3 0.20 0.012

Which lags would you keep at \(\alpha = 0.05\)? After refitting with lags 1 and 3, the model is \(y_t = 10 + 0.6y_{t-1} + 0.18y_{t-3}\). With \(y_{t-1} = 50\), \(y_{t-2} = 46\) and \(y_{t-3} = 44\), forecast \(y_t\).

(b) \(\text{SARIMA}(0,0,0)(1,0,0)_{12}\): \(y_t = 20 + 0.75y_{t-12}\). Last January's value was 240. Forecast this January, and also January next year.

(c) SARIMAX core: \(y_t = 15 + 0.5y_{t-1} + 12X_t\), where \(X_t = 1\) if a promotion runs. Given \(y_{t-1} = 60\), forecast \(y_t\) with and without a promotion.

(d) VAR(1): \(x_t = 1 + 0.4x_{t-1} + 0.2z_{t-1}\) and \(z_t = 0.5 + 0.1x_{t-1} + 0.7z_{t-1}\). Given \(x = 5\) and \(z = 10\), forecast both variables one and two steps ahead.

In SARIMA the relevant lag is the same season last year. In a VAR, each equation uses the lags of every variable.

(a) Keep lags 1 and 3 (\(p < 0.05\)). Drop lag 2.

\[ \hat{y} = 10 + 0.6(50) + 0.18(44) = 10 + 30 + 7.92 = \mathbf{47.92} \]

The lag-2 value of 46 is not used.

(b)

\[ \hat{y}(\text{Jan}) = 20 + 0.75(240) = \mathbf{200}; \quad \text{next year} = 20 + 0.75(200) = \mathbf{170} \]

(c) With promotion: \(15 + 30 + 12 =\) 57; without: 45. The coefficient \(\beta = 12\) is exactly the promotion's lift.

(d)

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{Step 1: } \hat{x} &= 1 + 2 + 2 = \mathbf{5.0}, & \hat{z} &= 0.5 + 0.5 + 7 = \mathbf{8.0} \\ \text{Step 2: } \hat{x} &= 1 + 0.4(5) + 0.2(8) = \mathbf{4.6}, & \hat{z} &= 0.5 + 0.1(5) + 0.7(8) = \mathbf{6.6} \end{aligned} \]

Key insight: All these models extend one recipe: a linear combination of relevant pasts. The pasts can be recent lags (AR), seasonal lags (SAR), external drivers (X), or other series' lags (VAR). AR orders can be chosen by the significance of lag terms in a regression.

tests: 6.6 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP6.35

Deriving the AR(1) moments

For a stationary AR(1), \(y_t = c + \phi y_{t-1} + \varepsilon_t\) with \(\lvert \phi \rvert < 1\) and white noise \(\varepsilon\) of variance \(\sigma^2\) that is independent of past \(y\):

(a) Derive \(\mu = c/(1-\phi)\).
(b) Derive \(\gamma(0) = \sigma^2/(1-\phi^2)\).
(c) Derive \(\gamma(h) = \phi\gamma(h-1)\), and hence \(\rho(h) = \phi^h\).
(d) Evaluate these for \(c = 3\), \(\phi = 0.8\), \(\sigma^2 = 1.8\). Find \(\mu\), \(\gamma(0)\) and \(\rho(3)\).
(e) What happens to \(\mu\) and \(\gamma(0)\) as \(\phi \to 1\)? Connect this to the Dickey–Fuller \(H_0\).

Stationarity means \(E[y_t] = E[y_{t-1}]\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(y_t) = \operatorname{Var}(y_{t-1})\). For (c), multiply the centred equation by \((y_{t-h} - \mu)\) and take expectations.

(a) \(\mu = c + \phi\mu\) gives \(\mu = c/(1-\phi)\).

(b) \(\operatorname{Var}(y_t) = \phi^2\operatorname{Var}(y_{t-1}) + \sigma^2\) gives \(\gamma(0)(1-\phi^2) = \sigma^2\), so \(\gamma(0) = \sigma^2/(1-\phi^2)\).

(c) \((y_t - \mu) = \phi(y_{t-1} - \mu) + \varepsilon_t\). Multiplying by \((y_{t-h} - \mu)\) and taking expectations gives \(\gamma(h) = \phi\gamma(h-1) + 0\), so \(\rho(h) = \phi^h\), a geometric decay. That is the AR signature in the ACF.

(d)

\[ \mu = \frac{3}{0.2} = \mathbf{15}; \quad \gamma(0) = \frac{1.8}{0.36} = \mathbf{5}; \quad \rho(3) = 0.8^3 = \mathbf{0.512} \]

(e) Both blow up, because the denominators go to 0. At \(\phi = 1\) there is no finite mean or variance: this is the random walk and the unit-root \(H_0\).

Key insight: The condition \(\lvert \phi \rvert < 1\) is precisely what makes the mean, the variance and a geometrically decaying ACF exist. Every AR property follows from the stationarity identities.

tests: 6.6, 6.5 · L4 Analysis
L4 · AnalysisP6.36

MA(1) autocorrelation

For \(X_t = \varepsilon_t + \theta\varepsilon_{t-1}\), with \(\varepsilon\) white noise of variance \(\sigma^2\):

(a) Derive \(\gamma(0)\), \(\gamma(1)\), and \(\gamma(h)\) for \(h \ge 2\). Hence show \(\rho_1 = \theta/(1+\theta^2)\) and \(\rho_h = 0\) for \(h \ge 2\).
(b) Show that \(\lvert \rho_1 \rvert \le 0.5\), and find the \(\theta\) that achieves the maximum.
(c) Compute \(\rho_1\) for \(\theta = 0.5\) and \(\theta = 2\). What do you notice? Which \(\theta\) would you report, and why?
(d) A sample ACF shows \(r_1 = 0.7\) with all other lags insignificant. Can an MA(1) explain this?

Count the shared \(\varepsilon\) terms in \(X_t\) and \(X_{t+h}\). For (b), \((1 - \lvert \theta \rvert)^2 \ge 0\).

(a) \(\gamma(0) = \sigma^2(1+\theta^2)\); \(\gamma(1) = \operatorname{Cov}(\varepsilon_t + \theta\varepsilon_{t-1}, \varepsilon_{t+1} + \theta\varepsilon_t) = \theta\sigma^2\); for \(h \ge 2\) there are no shared terms, so \(\gamma(h) = 0\). Hence \(\rho_1 = \theta/(1+\theta^2)\) and the ACF cuts off after lag 1.

(b) \(1 + \theta^2 \ge 2\lvert \theta \rvert\) gives \(\lvert \rho_1 \rvert \le 1/2\), with equality at \(\theta = \pm 1\).

(c) Both give \(\rho_1 = 0.4\). Solving \(0.4\theta^2 - \theta + 0.4 = 0\) gives \(\theta = 2\) or 0.5, so \(\theta\) and \(1/\theta\) are indistinguishable from the ACF. Report \(\theta = 0.5\), the invertible choice (\(\lvert \theta \rvert < 1\)), so that the shocks can be recovered from the past data.

(d) No. An MA(1) can never produce \(\lvert \rho_1 \rvert > 0.5\). Consider a higher-order or mixed model, or check for non-stationarity.

Key insight: An MA(\(q\)) has finite memory. Its ACF is exactly zero beyond lag \(q\). That is why the ACF cut-off identifies \(q\).

tests: 6.6, 6.5 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP6.37

Pick the model for the business case

Recommend a model family, with orders or periods where you can, and justify each choice in one line:
(a) Daily visits to an e-commerce site: strong weekday/weekend pattern, slow upward drift, and marketing knows promotion dates in advance.
(b) A temperature sensor's baseline that drifts steadily upward. There is no seasonality, and the ADF test fails to reject a unit root.
(c) A central bank wants joint forecasts of GDP growth, inflation and the policy rate, which influence one another.
(d) The same as (c), but the global oil price must also be fed in as an external driver.
(e) Quarterly retail sales whose festival-season spike grows every year. The manager wants a simple, explainable method.
(f) Weekly demand for a staple, with no trend or season, and a forecast is needed every Monday morning with minimal computation.

Ask four questions: one series or several? Seasonal, and with what period? Exogenous drivers? Stationary?

(a) \(\text{SARIMAX}(p,1,q)(P,1,Q)_7\) with \(X =\) promotion flag. \(m = 7\) for the weekly cycle, \(d = 1\) for the drift, and \(X\) because promotions are known in advance. Holt–Winters cannot include the promotion effect.
(b) \(\text{ARIMA}(p,1,q)\), or Holt's method. There is a unit root and no season.
(c) \(\text{VAR}(p)\). The variables are treated symmetrically and the cross-lags capture feedback. The series must be jointly stationary, so difference them if needed.
(d) VARMAX / VARX with the oil price as the exogenous \(X\).
(e) Holt–Winters multiplicative (\(m = 4\)), or SARIMA on \(\log(\text{sales})\). The spike growing with the level means ratio seasonality.
(f) SES: a single parameter, robust and cheap.

Key insight: Choosing a model is a series of checks: stationarity determines \(d\), seasonality determines \((P,D,Q)_m\), exogenous information adds \(X\), and several interacting targets call for V. The simplest model that leaves white-noise residuals wins.

tests: 6.6, 6.4 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP6.38

Leakage in time-series validation

An intern has 36 months of sales. They randomly shuffle all rows, use 80% for training and 20% for testing, and report \(\text{MAPE} = 3\%\). In production the model gets \(\text{MAPE} = 15\%\).

(a) Explain two ways the random split leaks future information into training.
(b) A feature called "7-day centred moving average of sales" was used. Why is it a leak?
(c) Design a rolling-origin evaluation: initial training window of 24 months, 1-step-ahead forecast, and the origin advanced by 1 month each time. How many forecast evaluations do you get?
(d) Also name one preprocessing step that must be fitted only on the training window.

In production, you never get to see the future. The validation scheme must imitate that.

(a) (1) Training rows from months after a test month let the model interpolate between neighbours instead of forecasting. Autocorrelation makes neighbouring months nearly identical. (2) Lag and trend information from the future shapes the fitted coefficients, so the test points are no longer genuinely out of sample.

(b) A centred MA at time \(t\) averages \(Y_{t+1}\), \(Y_{t+2}\) and \(Y_{t+3}\), which are unknown at forecast time. That is direct look-ahead leakage. Use trailing windows only.

(c) The training windows are months 1–24, 1–25, …, 1–35, forecasting months 25 through 36. That gives 12 evaluations. Average their errors (MAE or MSE).

(d) Scaling or normalisation statistics, seasonal-index estimates, imputation values, and the choice of \(\alpha\) or ARIMA orders. All of these must be computed from the training window only.

Key insight: In time series, validation must respect time order (walk-forward or rolling origin). Any statistic that touches the future inflates accuracy in a way production will not reproduce.

tests: 6.6, 6.3 · L5 Mastery

9.G Gaussian Mixture Models & EM (6.7)

L1 · FoundationP6.39

GMM vocabulary

True or False, with a reason:

  1. A GMM assigns each point to exactly one cluster.
  2. The mixing coefficients \(\pi_k\) must be non-negative and sum to 1.
  3. For a GMM, the log-likelihood has a closed-form maximiser, just as it does for a single Gaussian.
  4. In the E-step, the parameters are re-estimated.
  5. The mixing coefficient \(\pi_k\) can be read as the prior probability that a random point comes from component \(k\).
  6. The number of components \(K\) is learned automatically by EM.
  7. A two-company share-price dataset (means 90 and 100, SD 2) is a natural GMM example.

E = expectation, which here means computing responsibilities. M = maximisation, which means updating the parameters.

  1. False. It is soft clustering: each point receives a probability for every component.
  2. True. They form a probability distribution over the latent \(z\).
  3. False. The sum sits inside the log, \(\ln \sum_k \pi_k \mathcal{N}(\cdot)\), so the stationarity equations are coupled and must be solved iteratively.
  4. False. The E-step computes responsibilities. The M-step updates \(\mu\), \(\Sigma\) and \(\pi\).
  5. True. \(p(z_k = 1) = \pi_k\).
  6. False. \(K\) must be fixed beforehand, or chosen by a criterion such as BIC (see P6.44).
  7. True. Pooling two normal populations gives a bimodal mixture, and the points near 95 are ambiguous.

Key insight: A GMM is a generative story. First pick a component with probability \(\pi_k\), then draw \(x\) from its Gaussian. Clustering means inverting that story with Bayes' theorem.

tests: 6.7 · L1 Foundation
L2 · ComputationP6.40

Mixture density and responsibilities

A 1-D GMM has \(\pi_1 = 0.7\), \(\mu_1 = 10\), \(\sigma_1 = 2\) and \(\pi_2 = 0.3\), \(\mu_2 = 16\), \(\sigma_2 = 3\).
For \(x = 13\):
(a) compute \(\mathcal{N}(13 \mid \mu_k, \sigma_k^2)\) for each component;
(b) compute \(p(13)\);
(c) compute the responsibilities \(\gamma_1\) and \(\gamma_2\);
(d) \(x = 13\) lies \(1.5\sigma\) from \(\mu_1\) and \(1\sigma\) from \(\mu_2\), and component 2 has the higher density at \(x\). Explain why component 1 still "wins".

\(\mathcal{N}(x \mid \mu, \sigma^2) = \frac{1}{\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}} \exp\left(-\frac{(x-\mu)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)\), and \(\gamma_k = \pi_k \mathcal{N}_k / \sum_j \pi_j \mathcal{N}_j\).

(a)

\[ \mathcal{N}_1 = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\pi}} e^{-9/8} = \mathbf{0.06476}; \quad \mathcal{N}_2 = \frac{1}{3\sqrt{2\pi}} e^{-1/2} = \mathbf{0.08066} \]

(b)

\[ p(13) = 0.7(0.06476) + 0.3(0.08066) = 0.04533 + 0.02420 = \mathbf{0.06953} \]

(c)

\[ \gamma_1 = \mathbf{0.652}, \quad \gamma_2 = \mathbf{0.348} \]

(d) The likelihood favours component 2 (0.081 vs 0.065), but the prior favours component 1 (0.7 vs 0.3). The posterior combines both, and the prior tips the balance.

Key insight: A responsibility is the Bayes posterior \(P(\text{component} \mid x)\), proportional to prior \(\times\) likelihood. This is exactly Module 2's Bayes theorem.

tests: 6.7, 2.2 · L2 Computation
L3 · ApplicationP6.41

One complete EM iteration (1-D, two components)

Data: \(x = \{1, 2, 4, 7, 8\}\). Initial parameters: \(\pi = (0.5, 0.5)\), \(\mu = (2, 7)\), \(\sigma_1^2 = \sigma_2^2 = 4\).

(a) Compute the initial log-likelihood \(\ell_0 = \sum \ln p(x_n)\).
(b) E-step: compute \(\gamma_{n1}\) and \(\gamma_{n2}\) for each point.
(c) M-step: compute

\[ N_k = \sum_n \gamma_{nk}, \quad \mu_k^{\text{new}} = \frac{\sum \gamma_{nk} x_n}{N_k}, \quad (\sigma_k^2)^{\text{new}} = \frac{\sum \gamma_{nk} (x_n - \mu_k^{\text{new}})^2}{N_k}, \quad \text{and} \quad \pi_k^{\text{new}} = \frac{N_k}{N} \]

(d) Compute the new log-likelihood \(\ell_1\) and comment.

With equal \(\pi\) and \(\sigma\), \(\gamma_{n1} = 1 / \left(1 + \exp\left(-(x-7)^2/8 + (x-2)^2/8\right)\right)\). For \(x = 4\) the exponents are −0.5 and −1.125.

(a) \(\ell_0 = -11.736\)

(b)

\(x\) \(\gamma_1\) \(\gamma_2\)
1 0.9876 0.0124
2 0.9579 0.0421
4 0.6514 0.3486
7 0.0421 0.9579
8 0.0124 0.9876

(c)

\[ \begin{aligned} N_1 &= \mathbf{2.651}, & N_2 &= \mathbf{2.349} \\ \mu_1 &= \mathbf{2.226}, & \mu_2 &= \mathbf{6.854} \\ \sigma_1^2 &= \mathbf{1.870}, & \sigma_2^2 &= \mathbf{2.374} \\ \pi_1 &= \mathbf{0.530}, & \pi_2 &= \mathbf{0.470} \end{aligned} \]

(d) \(\ell_1 = -11.057 > \ell_0\). The likelihood increased, as EM guarantees. The point \(x = 4\) is shared about 65/35, which pulls \(\mu_1\) up from 2 and \(\mu_2\) down from 7. Both variances shrink because the components now fit their own points more tightly.

Key insight: The M-step is just "weighted MLE". Each point contributes to each component in proportion to its responsibility. If the \(\gamma\)'s were all 0 or 1, this would be ordinary per-cluster mean and variance.

tests: 6.7, 4.5 · L3 Application
L4 · AnalysisP6.42

Deriving EM for a 1-D GMM, and its k-means limit

(a) Using Bayes' theorem with \(p(z_k = 1) = \pi_k\) and \(p(x \mid z_k = 1) = \mathcal{N}(x \mid \mu_k, \sigma_k^2)\), derive \(\gamma(z_{nk})\).
(b) Differentiate \(\ell = \sum_n \ln \sum_k \pi_k \mathcal{N}(x_n \mid \mu_k, \sigma_k^2)\) with respect to \(\mu_k\), set the result to 0, and show \(\mu_k = \sum_n \gamma_{nk} x_n / \sum_n \gamma_{nk}\).
(c) Using a Lagrange multiplier for \(\sum \pi_k = 1\), show \(\pi_k = N_k/N\).
(d) Explain why each EM iteration cannot decrease \(\ell\).
(e) Let all components share variance \(\sigma^2\), and let \(\sigma \to 0\). Show that the responsibilities become hard 0/1 assignments to the nearest mean, and that EM becomes k-means.

\(\frac{\partial}{\partial \mu_k} \ln \sum_j \pi_j \mathcal{N}_j = \left[\frac{\pi_k \mathcal{N}_k}{\sum_j \pi_j \mathcal{N}_j}\right] \cdot \frac{x - \mu_k}{\sigma_k^2}\). The first factor is \(\gamma_{nk}\). For (e), compare \(\exp(-d_k^2/2\sigma^2)\) across \(k\) as \(\sigma \to 0\).

(a)

\[ \gamma(z_{nk}) = p(z_k=1 \mid x_n) = \frac{p(z_k=1)\,p(x_n \mid z_k=1)}{\sum_j p(z_j=1)\,p(x_n \mid z_j=1)} = \boldsymbol{\frac{\pi_k \mathcal{N}(x_n \mid \mu_k, \sigma_k^2)}{\sum_j \pi_j \mathcal{N}(x_n \mid \mu_j, \sigma_j^2)}} \]

(b)

\[ \frac{\partial \ell}{\partial \mu_k} = \sum_n \gamma_{nk} \frac{x_n - \mu_k}{\sigma_k^2} = 0, \text{ so } \boldsymbol{\mu_k = \frac{\sum \gamma_{nk} x_n}{N_k}} \]

The equation is not closed-form because \(\gamma_{nk}\) itself depends on \(\mu_k\). Hence the iteration. (A similar derivation gives \(\sigma_k^2 = \sum \gamma_{nk} (x_n - \mu_k)^2 / N_k\).)

(c) Maximise \(\ell + \lambda(\sum \pi_k - 1)\). Then \(\sum_n \mathcal{N}_{nk} / \sum_j \pi_j \mathcal{N}_{nj} + \lambda = 0\). Multiplying by \(\pi_k\) gives \(N_k + \lambda\pi_k = 0\). Summing over \(k\) gives \(\lambda = -N\), so \(\pi_k = N_k/N\).

(d) With the current parameters fixed, the E-step builds a lower bound on \(\ell\) (via Jensen's inequality) that touches \(\ell\) at the current parameters. The M-step maximises that bound. So the new \(\ell \ge\) the new bound \(\ge\) the old bound = the old \(\ell\). The likelihood is monotone non-decreasing, but it can converge to a local maximum.

(e)

\[ \gamma_{nk} \propto \pi_k \exp\left(-\frac{\lVert x_n - \mu_k \rVert^2}{2\sigma^2}\right) \]

As \(\sigma \to 0\), the term with the smallest distance dominates exponentially, so \(\gamma \to 1\) for the nearest mean and 0 for the others. The M-step mean then becomes the plain average of the assigned points. Together, these are k-means' assignment and update steps.

Key insight: EM for a GMM is Bayes (E-step) plus weighted MLE (M-step). k-means is its "zero-temperature" special case, with hard assignments and spherical, equal clusters.

tests: 6.7, 4.5, 2.2 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryP6.43

Anomaly detection with a fitted GMM

A payment-latency model is a fitted 1-D GMM with \(\pi = (0.6, 0.4)\), \(\mu = (50, 80)\) ms and \(\sigma = (5, 8)\) ms. A transaction is flagged as anomalous if \(\log p(x) < -7\).

(a) Compute \(\log p(x)\) for \(x = 52, 65, 100\) and 30 ms, and decide which to flag.
(b) Point 65 lies between the clusters, far from both means. Why is it not flagged? Point 30 is closer to a mean (20 ms from \(\mu_1\)) than 100 is to \(\mu_2\) (20 ms). Why is 30 so much more anomalous?
(c) How would you choose the threshold −7 in practice?

Measure distance in units of each component's \(\sigma\), and remember to weight by \(\pi\).

(a)

\[ \begin{aligned} \log p(52) &= \mathbf{-3.12} \ \text{(normal)} \\ \log p(65) &= \mathbf{-5.53} \ \text{(normal)} \\ \log p(100) &= \mathbf{-7.04} \ \text{(flag, marginally)} \\ \log p(30) &= \mathbf{-11.04} \ \text{(flag)} \end{aligned} \]

(b) At 65 both components contribute appreciable density (\(3\sigma\) from \(\mu_1\) and about \(1.9\sigma\) from \(\mu_2\), the wider component), so the mixture density is moderate. The point 30 is \(4\sigma\) below the narrow component 1 (\(\sigma = 5\)). The point 100 is only \(2.5\sigma\) above the wide component 2 (\(\sigma = 8\)). Density depends on standardised distance, not raw distance.

(c) Fit the model on clean historical data. Then set the threshold at a low percentile of the training log-likelihoods (for example the 0.5th–1st percentile), or tune it on labelled incidents for an acceptable false-alarm rate.

Key insight: A GMM turns "is this unusual?" into "is this unlikely under the learned density?". Unlike distance to the nearest centroid, it respects each cluster's spread and weight.

tests: 6.7 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP6.44

Choosing K with BIC

A 1-D GMM was fitted with EM to \(n = 400\) points for \(K = 1, \dots, 4\). The maximised log-likelihoods were −1520, −1405, −1398, −1393.

(a) How many free parameters does a 1-D, \(K\)-component GMM have?
(b) Compute \(\text{BIC} = -2\ell + p \ln n\) for each \(K\), and choose \(K\).
(c) Compute \(\text{AIC} = -2\ell + 2p\) as well. Which \(K\) does AIC choose? Explain the difference.
(d) Why can't you simply choose the \(K\) with the largest likelihood?

Each component has a mean and a variance, plus \(K\) weights constrained to sum to 1. \(\ln 400 \approx 5.991\).

(a) \(p = K \text{ means} + K \text{ variances} + (K-1) \text{ weights} =\) \(3K - 1\), so 2, 5, 8, 11.

(b) BIC: \(K=1\): 3051.98, \(K=2\): 2839.96, \(K=3\): 2843.93, \(K=4\): 2851.91. The minimum is at \(K = 2\).

(c) AIC: 3044, 2820, 2812, 2808, so AIC picks \(K = 4\). AIC's penalty (2 per parameter) is lighter than BIC's (\(\ln n \approx 6\) per parameter), so it tolerates small gains in likelihood from extra components. BIC favours parsimony.

(d) The likelihood never decreases as \(K\) grows, because a larger model contains the smaller one. With \(K = n\), each point can get its own spike (P6.45). Raw likelihood always prefers over-fitting.

Key insight: Model selection balances fit against complexity. The jump from \(K = 1\) to \(K = 2\) gains 115 log-units, which is clearly real. The later gains of 5–7 log-units do not justify 3 more parameters each.

tests: 6.7, 4.5 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryP6.45

Elongated clusters, singularities and k-means vs GMM

(a) Two clusters in 2-D are long, thin and parallel, like two cigars side by side. Why does k-means split them wrongly while a full-covariance GMM can separate them?
(b) During EM, one component's mean lands exactly on a single data point \(x_n\), and its variance shrinks. Compute \(\mathcal{N}(x_n \mid x_n, \sigma^2)\) for \(\sigma = 1\), 0.01 and \(10^{-6}\), together with the log-density. What happens to the log-likelihood?
(c) Give two practical remedies for (b) and one for EM converging to a poor local optimum.

k-means implicitly assumes spherical, equal-size clusters. The peak of a Gaussian density is \(1/(\sigma\sqrt{2\pi})\).

(a) k-means assigns points by Euclidean distance to the centroids, so its boundaries are straight perpendicular bisectors that favour round clusters. It cuts across the cigars. A GMM with a full \(\Sigma_k\) learns each cluster's orientation and elongation through its covariance, and uses the Mahalanobis distance. It also gives soft probabilities near the boundary.

(b) Peak density: \(\sigma = 1\): 0.399 (log −0.92); \(\sigma = 0.01\): 39.89 (log 3.69); \(\sigma = 10^{-6}\): 398,942 (log 12.90). As \(\sigma \to 0\) the density goes to \(\infty\), so \(\ell \to \infty\). This is a degenerate "maximum" that explains nothing.

(c) Singularities: put a floor on the variance (\(\sigma^2 \ge \varepsilon\), or add \(\varepsilon I\) to \(\Sigma_k\)), use a Bayesian prior on \(\Sigma\), or detect a collapse and re-initialise that component. Local optima: run several random restarts or initialise with k-means, then keep the solution with the highest likelihood.

Key insight: The GMM likelihood is unbounded, so "maximum likelihood" needs guard-rails. Flexible covariances are the GMM's strength over k-means, and also the source of these pathologies.

tests: 6.7 · L5 Mastery

9.H Module 6 Capstone

L5 · MasteryP6.46

End-to-end: decompose, diagnose, model, evaluate

Quarterly sales (₹ lakh) over three years:

Year Q1 Q2 Q3 Q4
1 52 68 80 60
2 60 78 92 70
3 68 88 104 80

(a) Compute the annual means, the seasonal differences and the seasonal ratios. Is the evidence stronger for an additive or a multiplicative model?
(b) Compute the 4-quarter centred moving average for \(t = 3, \dots, 10\).
(c) Using the ratio-to-moving-average method, estimate the seasonal indices for Q1..Q4, normalised so that they sum to 4.
(d) Deseasonalise the series, fit a least-squares trend against \(t = 1, \dots, 12\), and forecast Q1 and Q2 of Year 4.
(e) Compute the seasonal (lag-4) differences \(Y_t - Y_{t-4}\). What do they suggest for a SARIMA specification?
(f) Compare two benchmark forecasts on Year 3, naive (\(F_t = Y_{t-1}\)) and seasonal naive (\(F_t = Y_{t-4}\)), using MAD and MSE. What does the comparison teach you?

The CMA for \(t = 3\) is the average of the first two MA(4) values. Ratio for \(t = 3\) is \(Y_3/\text{CMA}_3\). Average the ratios for each quarter (2 per quarter) before normalising.

(a) Means: 65, 75, 85.
Differences: Q1 −13, −15, −17; Q2 +3, +3, +3; Q3 +15, +17, +19; Q4 −5, −5, −5.
Ratios: Q1 0.80, 0.80, 0.80; Q2 1.046, 1.040, 1.035; Q3 1.231, 1.227, 1.224; Q4 0.923, 0.933, 0.941.
The large seasonal effects (Q1 and Q3) grow with the level in absolute terms while their ratios stay constant. That favours a multiplicative model. The small effects (Q2, Q4) are ambiguous, which is common in real data.

(b) MA(4): 65, 67, 69.5, 72.5, 75, 77, 79.5, 82.5, 85. CMA (\(t = 3, \dots, 10\)): 66, 68.25, 71, 73.75, 76, 78.25, 81, 83.75.

(c) Ratios \(Y/\text{CMA}\): \(t=3\) (Q3) 1.212, \(t=4\) (Q4) 0.879, \(t=5\) (Q1) 0.845, \(t=6\) (Q2) 1.058, \(t=7\) (Q3) 1.211, \(t=8\) (Q4) 0.895, \(t=9\) (Q1) 0.840, \(t=10\) (Q2) 1.051.
Average indices: Q1 0.8423, Q2 1.0542, Q3 1.2113, Q4 0.8868 (sum 3.9946).
Normalised: Q1 0.843, Q2 1.056, Q3 1.213, Q4 0.888.

(d) Deseasonalised series: 61.65, 64.42, 65.96, 67.56, 71.14, 73.89, 75.85, 78.83, 80.62, 83.36, 85.74, 90.09.
Trend: \(\hat{T} = 58.63 + 2.506t\).
Forecasts:

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{Year 4 Q1 } (t = 13)&: (58.63 + 32.58)(0.843) = \mathbf{76.9} \\ \text{Q2 } (t = 14)&: (58.63 + 35.09)(1.056) = \mathbf{98.9} \end{aligned} \]

(e) Lag-4 differences: 8, 10, 12, 10, 8, 10, 12, 10. The seasonal pattern disappears and leaves a roughly stationary series around 10 (the yearly growth). This suggests seasonal differencing \(D = 1\), \(m = 4\): \(\text{SARIMA}(p,0,q)(P,1,Q)_4\), possibly with a constant.

(f) Errors on Year 3, naive: −2, +20, +16, −24, so \(\text{MAD} = 15.5\), \(\text{MSE} = 309\). Seasonal naive: +8, +10, +12, +10, so \(\text{MAD} = 10\), \(\text{MSE} = 102\). The seasonal naive forecast wins by a wide margin, but its errors are all positive, so it is biased by the trend of about 10 per year. Adding drift (seasonal naive + 10) would almost remove the error. Any proposed model should beat these benchmarks.

Key insight: A complete forecasting workflow runs: plot, choose additive or multiplicative, decompose (CMA → indices → trend), check stationarity and differencing, model, and benchmark against naive and seasonal-naive forecasts. Seasonality and trend are the two components that matter most in this data, and every good method here is a way of handling both.

tests: 6.1, 6.2, 6.3, 6.4, 6.5, 6.6 · L5 Mastery
Cross-Module Capstones · 6 questions

Each capstone draws on three or more modules, the way a real data-science task does. Attempt these only after finishing the module sets. They are written to feel like an EC-3 open-book question.

L5 · MasteryC.1

Shipping a spam filter (M2 + M3 + M4)

A team trains a Naive Bayes spam filter. In the training corpus 40% of e-mails are spam. Use only two word features:

\(P(\text{word present} \mid \text{spam})\) \(P(\text{word present} \mid \text{ham})\)
"free" 0.50 0.05
"meeting" 0.02 0.20

(a) A new e-mail contains both "free" and "meeting". Under the naive assumption, and considering only these two features, find \(P(\text{spam} \mid \text{e-mail})\).

(b) Filter A classifies 460 of 500 held-out e-mails correctly. Give a 95% confidence interval for its true accuracy.

(c) Filter B classifies 445 of a different, independent set of 500 e-mails correctly. At \(\alpha = 0.05\), is there evidence that A is more accurate than B?

(d) Suppose instead that A and B were evaluated on the same 500 e-mails. Explain why the test in (c) is no longer the right one, and name the kind of test that should replace it.

(a) Compare \(P(\text{spam}) \cdot \prod P(\text{word} \mid \text{spam})\) against \(P(\text{ham}) \cdot \prod P(\text{word} \mid \text{ham})\), then normalise. (c) This is a one-tailed two-proportion z-test with a pooled \(\hat{p}\).

(a) Spam score \(= 0.4 \times 0.50 \times 0.02 = 0.004\). Ham score \(= 0.6 \times 0.05 \times 0.20 = 0.006\).

\[ P(\text{spam} \mid \text{e-mail}) = \frac{0.004}{0.010} = \mathbf{0.40} \]

The two words pull in opposite directions by the same factor: \(0.50/0.05 = 10\) and \(0.02/0.20 = 0.1\). Their product is 1, so the posterior equals the prior.

(b) \(\hat{p} = 0.92\),

\[ \text{SE} = \sqrt{\frac{0.92 \times 0.08}{500}} = 0.01213 \]

\[ \text{CI} = 0.92 \pm 1.96 \times 0.01213 = \mathbf{(0.896,\ 0.944)} \]

(c) \(H_0: p_A = p_B\) vs \(H_1: p_A > p_B\). Pooled \(\hat{p} = 905/1000 = 0.905\).

\[ z = \frac{0.92 - 0.89}{\sqrt{0.905 \times 0.095 \times (2/500)}} = \mathbf{1.618}, \qquad \text{one-tailed } p = \mathbf{0.0529} \]

The critical value is 1.645, so we fail to reject \(H_0\) at 5%. A 3-point accuracy gap on 500 e-mails each is not quite conclusive.

(d) On the same e-mails the two proportions are dependent: each e-mail is scored by both filters. The independent-samples formula would then overstate the variance of the difference. A paired analysis is needed, such as McNemar's test on the discordant pairs. This is the same reasoning that makes a paired t-test better than an unpaired one when the measurements are before/after on the same subjects (4.3).

Key insight: Naive Bayes multiplies likelihood ratios, so features can cancel each other exactly. An "accuracy" from a test set is an estimate with a standard error, and comparing two models is a hypothesis test whose form depends on the sampling design.

tests: 2.2.6, 3.3.8, 4.2, 4.1 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryC.2

Quality control on a bolt line (M3 + M4)

A line produces bolts with a 2% defect rate. Bolt length is \(N(50, 0.2^2)\) mm.

(a) A box holds 200 bolts. Find \(P(\text{at most 2 defective})\) exactly (binomial) and by the Poisson approximation. Is the approximation justified by the course rule?

(b) The specification is 49.6–50.4 mm. What fraction of bolts meets it? Which rule from the slides gives this value instantly?

(c) A random sample of 16 bolts has mean length 50.12 mm. \(\sigma = 0.2\) is known. Test \(H_0: \mu = 50\) against a two-sided alternative at \(\alpha = 0.05\) and report the p-value.

(d) Lengths from three machines (5 bolts each):
A: 50.1, 50.3, 49.9, 50.2, 50.0 · B: 50.4, 50.6, 50.3, 50.5, 50.7 · C: 49.8, 50.0, 49.9, 50.1, 49.7
Build the one-way ANOVA table using the correction-factor method and test at 5%. Then use LSD to find which machines differ.

(a) \(\lambda = np\). (b) \(\pm 0.4\) mm is \(\pm 2\sigma\). (d) \(\text{CF} = G^2/n\); \(\text{LSD} = t(0.025,\ n-k) \cdot \sqrt{2 \cdot \text{MSE}/r}\).

(a) Binomial: \(P(X \le 2) = 0.2351\). Poisson with \(\lambda = 4\): 0.2381. The slide rule asks for \(n \ge 100\) and \(np \le 10\), and here \(n = 200\) and \(np = 4\), so the approximation is justified. It is off by only 0.003.

(b) \(\pm 0.4\) mm is \(\pm 2\sigma\), so the fraction is 0.9545, the "95" of the 68-95-99.7 rule.

(c)

\[ z = \frac{50.12 - 50}{0.2/\sqrt{16}} = \mathbf{2.40} \]

The two-sided p-value is 0.0164 \(< 0.05\), so reject \(H_0\): the line's mean has drifted.

(d) \(G = 752.5\), \(\text{CF} = 37750.4167\), \(\text{SST} = 1.2333\), \(\text{SSTR} = 0.9333\), \(\text{SSE} = 0.3000\).

Source SS df MS F
Machines 0.9333 2 0.4667 18.67
Error 0.3000 12 0.0250
Total 1.2333 14

\(F_{0.05}(2, 12) = 3.885\), and \(p = 0.0002\), so reject \(H_0\). The means are \(A = 50.1\), \(B = 50.5\), \(C = 49.9\).

\[ \text{LSD} = 2.179 \times \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 0.025}{5}} = \mathbf{0.218} \]

\(\lvert B - A \rvert = 0.4\) and \(\lvert B - C \rvert = 0.6\) are both larger than 0.218, so they are significant. \(\lvert A - C \rvert = 0.2\) is smaller, so it is not. Machine B is the one that is off.

Key insight: The same physical process gives a count model (binomial/Poisson) for defects and a continuous model (normal) for dimensions. ANOVA only says some machine differs. A post-hoc comparison is needed to say which.

tests: 3.2.2, 3.2.3, 3.2.4, 4.2, 4.7 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryC.3

Sensor anomaly detection: box plot vs GMM (M1 + M6 + M2)

Ten temperature readings (°C): 21.4, 22.0, 22.3, 22.8, 23.1, 23.5, 24.0, 24.4, 25.2, 31.9

(a) Using quartile positions \(k(n+1)/4\), find \(Q_1\), \(Q_2\), \(Q_3\), the IQR and the 1.5-IQR fences. Which readings are flagged?

(b) Compute the mean and sample SD with and without 31.9. Which summary statistics are robust here?

(c) Engineers know the sensor has a normal mode \(N(23, 1^2)\) with weight 0.9 and a known fault mode \(N(30, 2^2)\) with weight 0.1. For \(x = 24.4\), 27.0 and 31.9, compute the fault-mode responsibility \(\gamma_{\text{fault}}(x)\) and the mixture log-density \(\ln p(x)\).

(d) An anomaly is defined as "\(\ln p(x) < -4.5\)". Which of the three readings are anomalies? Explain why the result differs from the box-plot verdict, and what each method is really detecting.

(c)

\[ \gamma_{\text{fault}} = \frac{0.1 \cdot N(x \mid 30, 2)}{0.9 \cdot N(x \mid 23, 1) + 0.1 \cdot N(x \mid 30, 2)} \]

This is Bayes' theorem with the mixing weights as priors.

(a) \(Q_1 = \mathbf{22.225}\), \(Q_2 = \mathbf{23.3}\), \(Q_3 = \mathbf{24.6}\), \(\text{IQR} = \mathbf{2.375}\). The fences are 18.66 and 28.16. Only 31.9 is flagged.

(b) With 31.9: mean 24.06, \(s = \mathbf{2.98}\). Without it: mean 23.19, \(s = \mathbf{1.22}\). The median moves only from 23.3 to 23.1. The median and IQR are robust here; the mean and SD are not.

(c)

\(x\) \(\gamma_{\text{fault}}(x)\) \(\ln p(x)\)
24.4 0.0029 \(-2.00\)
27.0 0.9817 \(-5.02\)
31.9 \(\approx 1.0000\) \(-4.37\)

(d) Only 27.0 is an anomaly under the GMM rule. 31.9 is well explained by the known fault cluster: it is a recognised failure, not a surprise. 27.0 falls in the low-density gap between the two modes, where neither component expects data. The box plot detects points that are far from the bulk. The GMM detects points that are unlikely under any known regime. An operational system would probably use both: \(\gamma_{\text{fault}}\) for "fault detected" and \(\ln p\) for "something new".

Key insight: "Outlier" depends on the model. The responsibility in a GMM is Bayes' posterior with the mixing coefficients acting as priors, the same formula as "which machine made this defective?" in Module 2.

tests: 1.1.5, 1.1.4, 6.7, 2.2.2 · L5 Mastery
L5 · MasteryC.4

Forecasting quarterly demand: regression vs smoothing (M5 + M6)

Quarterly demand: 120, 126, 129, 137, 140, 147, 151, 158 (\(t = 1, \ldots, 8\)).

(a) Fit a least-squares linear trend \(\hat{Y} = a + bt\) and forecast \(t = 9\). Report the in-sample MSE.

(b) Apply simple exponential smoothing with \(\alpha = 0.3\) and \(F_2 = Y_1\). Report \(F_9\) and the MSE and MAD over \(t = 2, \ldots, 8\).

(c) Apply Holt's method with \(\alpha = 0.5\), \(\beta = 0.3\), \(L_1 = Y_1\), \(T_1 = Y_2 - Y_1\). Report \(F_9\), \(F_{10}\) and the MSE over \(t = 2, \ldots, 8\).

(d) Rank the three methods. Explain structurally why SES does so badly on this series. What sign do all its errors have, and why?

(e) The lag-1 autocorrelation of the regression residuals is about \(-0.81\). What does this suggest about using the trend line's residuals as white noise? Which model family from CS-13 would you try for the residuals?

\(b = S_{xy}/S_{xx}\) with \(\bar{t} = 4.5\). SES has no trend term, so it is always "catching up".

(a)

\[ b = \mathbf{5.3333}, \qquad a = \mathbf{114.5}, \qquad F_9 = \mathbf{162.5} \]

In-sample \(\text{MSE} = \mathbf{0.917}\).

(b) The SES forecasts are 120, 121.8, 123.96, 127.87, 131.51, 136.16, 140.61. \(F_9 = 145.83\). \(\text{MSE} = 166.80\), \(\text{MAD} = 12.30\).

(c) \(F_2, \ldots, F_8\) = 126, 132, 136.05, 142.22, 146.47, 152.17, 156.85. \(L_8 = 157.43\) and \(T_8 = 5.44\), so

\[ F_9 = \mathbf{162.86} \quad \text{and} \quad F_{10} = \mathbf{168.30} \]

\(\text{MSE} = 2.54\).

(d) Ranking: regression (0.92, but in-sample) \(<\) Holt (2.54, one-step-ahead) \(\ll\) SES (166.8). The regression MSE is in-sample, so it is optimistic. Holt's errors are genuine one-step-ahead forecast errors, which makes the comparison more honest than it looks. Every SES error is positive (6.0, 7.2, 13.0, …): with no trend component, SES behaves like a weighted average of past values, and on a rising series that average always lags behind. This is exactly the lag that Holt's trend term removes.

(e) Strong negative lag-1 autocorrelation means the residuals are not white noise: a positive residual tends to be followed by a negative one. The trend line leaves structure behind. Fit an MA(1) or AR(1) to the residuals and identify which one using the ACF/PACF cut-offs. Equivalently, model the original series with an \(\text{ARIMA}(p, 1, q)\), since differencing removes a linear trend. With only 8 points, any such estimate is very noisy, and you should say so in an answer.

Key insight: Choose a forecaster to match the structure of the series (level, trend, season). Compare forecasters on out-of-sample, one-step-ahead errors, not on in-sample fit. Residual autocorrelation is the diagnostic that says a model has missed something.

tests: 5.4, 6.2, 6.3, 6.4, 6.5 · L5 Mastery
L4 · AnalysisC.5

Least squares is maximum likelihood (M4 + M5)

Assume \(Y_i = a + bx_i + \varepsilon_i\) with \(\varepsilon_i\) i.i.d. \(N(0, \sigma^2)\).

(a) Write down the likelihood \(L(a, b, \sigma^2)\) and the log-likelihood.

(b) Show that maximising \(\ln L\) over \((a, b)\) is equivalent to minimising \(\sum (Y_i - a - bx_i)^2\), so the MLEs of \(a\) and \(b\) are exactly the least-squares estimates from CS-11.

(c) Derive the MLE of \(\sigma^2\) and compare it with \(\text{SSE}/(n - 2)\). Which is unbiased? Relate this to the \(n\) versus \(n - 1\) discussion in CS-1.

(d) Suppose instead that \(Y_i\) is a count and \(Y_i \sim \text{Poisson}(\lambda_i)\) with \(\log \lambda_i = a + bx_i\). Write the log-likelihood. Explain why least squares is no longer the MLE, and what this means for using linear regression on count data.

\[ \ln L = -\frac{n}{2} \ln(2\pi\sigma^2) - \frac{1}{2\sigma^2} \sum (Y_i - a - bx_i)^2 \]

Only the second term depends on \((a, b)\).

(a)

\[ L = \prod (2\pi\sigma^2)^{-1/2} \exp\left[-\frac{(Y_i - a - bx_i)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right] \]

so

\[ \ln L = -\frac{n}{2} \ln(2\pi\sigma^2) - \frac{\text{SSE}(a, b)}{2\sigma^2} \]

(b) For any fixed \(\sigma^2\), the first term is constant and the second is \(-\text{SSE}/(2\sigma^2)\) with \(\sigma^2 > 0\). So \(\ln L\) is largest exactly where \(\text{SSE}(a, b)\) is smallest. Setting \(\partial/\partial a = \partial/\partial b = 0\) gives the normal equations

\[ \sum Y = na + b\sum x \quad \text{and} \quad \sum xY = a\sum x + b\sum x^2 \]

so the MLEs are the least-squares estimates \(b = S_{xy}/S_{xx}\) and \(a = \bar{y} - b \cdot \bar{x}\).

(c)

\[ \frac{\partial \ln L}{\partial \sigma^2} = -\frac{n}{2\sigma^2} + \frac{\text{SSE}}{2\sigma^4} = 0 \]

gives \(\hat{\sigma}^2_{\text{MLE}} = \text{SSE}/n\). This is biased, because two parameters \((a, b)\) were estimated from the same data, and that uses up 2 degrees of freedom. \(\text{SSE}/(n - 2)\) is unbiased. It is the same story as \(\hat{\sigma}^2 = \text{SS}/n\) (MLE, biased) versus \(s^2 = \text{SS}/(n - 1)\) (unbiased) for a single mean, where one degree of freedom is used by \(\bar{x}\).

(d)

\[ \ln L = \sum \left[ Y_i (a + bx_i) - e^{a + bx_i} - \ln Y_i! \right] \]

This is not a sum of squared errors, so the minimiser of SSE is not the maximiser of this likelihood. Counts also have variance equal to their mean (3.2.3), which violates the constant-variance assumption of CS-11. Ordinary least squares on counts is therefore misspecified. The fix is Poisson (generalised linear) regression.

Key insight: A loss function is a hidden distributional assumption. Squared error corresponds to Gaussian noise, and changing the noise model changes the "right" loss. Mean squared error, cross-entropy and Poisson deviance all come from the same principle: maximum likelihood.

tests: 4.5, 5.4, 3.2.4 · L4 Analysis
L5 · MasteryC.6

Population screening programme (M2 + M3 + M4)

A disease has prevalence 2%. A screening test has sensitivity 95% and specificity 90%.

(a) Find \(P(\text{test } +)\), the PPV \(P(\text{disease} \mid +)\) and the NPV \(P(\text{no disease} \mid -)\).

(b) A person who tests positive takes a second, conditionally independent test with the same characteristics, and it is also positive. Find the new posterior. Show that updating twice in sequence (using the first posterior as the new prior) gives the same answer as treating both results at once.

(c) 1000 people are screened. Find the expected numbers of true and false positives. Use the normal approximation with continuity correction to find \(P(\text{at least 130 positives})\), and compare with the exact binomial.

(d) 125 of the 1000 actually test positive. A naive analyst reports a prevalence of 12.5%. Correct this using \(P(+) = \pi \cdot \text{Se} + (1 - \pi)(1 - \text{Sp})\) and solve for \(\pi\).

(e) How many people must be sampled, with a perfect test, to estimate the prevalence within \(\pm 1\) percentage point at 95% confidence, using the anticipated \(\pi = 0.02\)? How many if nothing is known about \(\pi\)?

(c) The count of positives is \(\text{Binomial}(1000, P(+))\). (e) \(n = z^2 \cdot p(1-p)/E^2\), rounded up. Worst case \(p = 0.5\).

(a)

\[ P(+) = 0.02 \times 0.95 + 0.98 \times 0.10 = \mathbf{0.117} \]

\[ \text{PPV} = \frac{0.019}{0.117} = \mathbf{0.1624} \]

\(\text{NPV} = 0.9989\). A positive result raises the risk from 2% to only about 16%.

(b) Both tests at once:

\[ \frac{0.02 \times 0.95^2}{0.02 \times 0.95^2 + 0.98 \times 0.10^2} = \mathbf{0.6481} \]

In sequence: start from the prior 0.1624 and apply one more positive test, which gives 0.6481, the same value. Under conditional independence, Bayesian updating can be done in sequence or in batch.

(c) Expected \(\text{TP} = 1000 \times 0.02 \times 0.95 = \mathbf{19}\). Expected \(\text{FP} = 1000 \times 0.98 \times 0.10 = \mathbf{98}\). Most positives are false. \(\mu = 117\) and \(\sigma = \sqrt{1000 \times 0.117 \times 0.883} = 10.16\).

\[ P(X \ge 130) \approx P\left(Z \ge \frac{129.5 - 117}{10.16}\right) = \mathbf{0.1094} \]

The exact binomial gives 0.1105.

(d) \(0.125 = \pi(0.95) + (1 - \pi)(0.10)\), so

\[ \pi = \frac{0.125 - 0.10}{0.95 - 0.10} = \mathbf{0.0294} \]

about 2.9%, not 12.5%.

(e)

\[ n = \frac{1.96^2 \times 0.02 \times 0.98}{0.01^2} = 752.9 \]

so \(n = 753\). Worst case:

\[ \frac{1.96^2 \times 0.25}{0.01^2} = 9603.6 \]

so \(n = 9604\). Prior knowledge of \(\pi\) cuts the required sample size by a factor of about 13.

Key insight: In low-prevalence settings a "good" test still produces mostly false positives. This is the base-rate effect, and the same effect explains why fraud and anomaly classifiers have poor precision. Apparent rates must be corrected for measurement error before they are reported as true rates.

tests: 2.2.3, 2.2.5, 3.2.2, 3.2.5, 3.3.8 · L5 Mastery

10.7 Final self-assessment: can you do each of these from memory?

Tick a box only if you can do it without notes in under 5 minutes.

  • [ ] Classify any variable by type and level of measurement, and choose the right centre and spread for it (1.1)
  • [ ] Compute a five-number summary and fences, and read the skew of a box plot (1.1.5)
  • [ ] Explain why \(s^2\) divides by \(n - 1\) and \(\hat{\sigma}^2_{\text{MLE}}\) divides by \(n\) (1.1.4, 4.5)
  • [ ] Prove that mutually exclusive events with non-zero probability are dependent (1.2.6)
  • [ ] Draw the tree and compute any total-probability / Bayes question (2.1.5, 2.2.1)
  • [ ] Train a Naive Bayes classifier by hand, with Laplace smoothing (2.2.6–2.2.7)
  • [ ] Find \(k\), \(E[X]\), \(\operatorname{Var}(X)\) and the CDF for any given pmf or pdf (3.1)
  • [ ] From a joint table: marginals, conditionals, independence check, \(\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)\) (3.1.7)
  • [ ] Recognise binomial, Poisson and normal settings from the story alone, and check approximation conditions (3.2)
  • [ ] State the CLT, compute an SE with or without the FPC, and build \(z\) and \(t\) CIs plus a sample-size calculation (3.3)
  • [ ] Run any \(z\), \(t\) (one-sample, paired, pooled), \(\chi^2\) (independence or goodness of fit), \(F\) or ANOVA test end-to-end with a p-value (4.1–4.7)
  • [ ] Derive the MLE for Bernoulli, Poisson and Normal (4.5)
  • [ ] Compute \(r\), Spearman's \(R\) and both regression lines; recover the means and \(r\) from two given regression equations (5.2–5.4)
  • [ ] Compute \(\text{SST} = \text{SSR} + \text{SSE}\) and \(r^2\), and linearise a power or exponential curve (5.5–5.6)
  • [ ] Compute a centred MA, SES, Holt, MSE, MAD and MAPE by hand (6.2–6.4)
  • [ ] Identify AR versus MA from ACF and PACF, and forecast AR(1), ARMA(1,1) and ARIMA(1,1,1) by hand (6.5–6.6)
  • [ ] Perform one full EM iteration for a 1-D two-component GMM (6.7)
